\min\!\big(1\mathchar 59\relax A(x^{(t)}\mathchar 59\relax x^{\prime})\big)
, where

(2.1.5)

A(x^{(t)}\mathchar 59\relax x^{\prime})\triangleq\frac{P^{\star}(x^{\prime})Q(x^{(t)}\mid x^{\prime})}{P^{\star}(x^{(t)})Q(x^{\prime}\mid x^{(t)})}=\frac{Q(x^{(t)}\mid x^{\prime})}{Q(x^{\prime}\mid x^{(t)})}\exp\!\big({\mathcal{E}}(x^{(t)})-{\mathcal{E}}(x^{\prime})\big)\mathchar 59\relax

and otherwise, the state remains unchanged (
x^{(t+1)}=x^{(t)}
). We can observe that the acceptance probability
A
only depends on the difference in energies between
x^{(t)}
and
x^{\prime}
, and not on the intractable partition function
Z
. For large enough
t\gg 1
,
x^{(t)}
sampled using this process is approximately distributed as
P^{\star}
. The pseudo-code for the Metropolis-Hastings algorithm is available in
Algorithm

1
.

Algorithm 1

Metropolis-Hastings algorithm

1:

An initial state
x^{(0)}\in{\mathcal{X}}
, the proposal distribution
Q(x^{\prime}\mid x)
.

2:

for

t\geq 0

do

3:

Sample a proposed move:
x^{\prime}\sim Q(x^{\prime}\mid x^{(t)})

4:

Compute the the acceptance probability
A\!\big(x^{(t)}\mathchar 59\relax x^{\prime}\big)

Equation

2.1.5

5:

Sample
u\sim U(0\mathchar 59\relax 1)

6:

Update the state:

7:

\displaystyle x^{(t+1)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}x^{\prime}&\textrm{if $u\leq A\!\big(x^{(t)}\mathchar 59\relax x^{\prime}\big)$}&\textrm{(accept)}\\
x^{(t)}&\textrm{otherwise}&\textrm{(reject)}\end{array}\right.

It is important to note that the quality of the samples obtained with MCMC highly depends on how close the Markov chain is to stationarity. The time it takes to reach this state, called the
mixing time
, depends on both the target distribution
Equation

2.1.1
(and by extension, the energy function
{\mathcal{E}}(x)
) and on the proposal distribution. For example, MCMC is known to converge slowly when the target distribution has multiple modes separated by large regions of low probability and
Q(x^{\prime}\mid x)
only proposes local moves around
x

(
Roberts & Tweedie 2008
)
. To maximize the chances of getting approximate samples of
P^{\star}
, it is common practice to discard early samples for a “burn-in” period. Although there is a vast literature on analysing and improving the convergence of MCMC methods
(
Swendsen & Wang 1986
;
Robert et al. 2018
)
, an exhaustive treatment is outside the scope of this thesis. Besides mixing, MCMC also suffers from
autocorrelation
: due to its sequential nature, samples may be highly correlated, making the estimation of quantities in
Equation

2.1.2
less efficient.

It cannot be understated how influential this algorithm has been (and MCMC in general). It has been considered one of the top 10 algorithms of the 20th century by the IEEE magazine
Computing in Science & Engineering

(
Beichl & Sullivan 2000
)
, and has been the cornerstone of major scientific discoveries, including in high-energy physics and lattice field theory
(
Lautrup 1985
)
, the estimation of the effects of monetary policies in macroeconomy (which won the Nobel prize in economics in 2005;
Sims 2012
), or the modeling of complex chemical systems at different scales (
Minary & Levitt 2010
; which once again won the Nobel prize in chemistry in 2013;
Levitt 2014
).

Using gradient information

In the standard version of the Metropolis-Hastings algorithm presented above, the proposal distribution
Q(x^{\prime}\mid x)
is not aware of the energy “surface” around the current state
x
(the difference in energies only appearing in
Equation

2.1.5
), meaning that the moves are non-adaptive. On the contrary, we could imagine proposing larger moves when
{\mathcal{E}}(x)
has larger variations. Suppose in this paragraph only that
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is a continuous sample space, and that the energy function is differentiable; this is a setting which has been, by and large, the most studied in the EBM literature. We could use the
Langevin dynamics
in order to propose new moves, with

(2.1.6)

Q(x^{\prime}\mid x)\propto\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4\beta}\|x^{\prime}-x+\beta\nabla_{x}{\mathcal{E}}(x)\|^{2}\right)\mathchar 59\relax

where
\beta>0
is a fixed step-size. Using this proposal distribution in
Algorithm

1
is called the
Metropolis-adjusted Langevin algorithm
(MALA;
Roberts & Tweedie 1996
). Langevin Monte Carlo has recently gained popularity thanks to its applications to score-based models
(
Song & Ermon 2019
)
, which are closely related to EBMs. However, while there has been some works adapting these ideas to discrete spaces
(
Grathwohl et al. 2021
;
Zhang et al. 2022b
)
, in general these methods cannot be applied to our setting where
{\mathcal{X}}
is discrete and structured since they rely on the score function
\nabla_{x}{\mathcal{E}}(x)
, which is undefined in our case a priori.

2.1.3.
Compositional objects

We focus our attention to cases where the sample space
{\mathcal{X}}
of the EBM is discrete and structured. The structure of this space will often come from some
compositional
aspect of the objects to be generated, meaning that the elements
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
can be decomposed into pieces, and those pieces can then be reassembled to create new objects. We provide multiple examples of compositional objects and the pieces they are made of in
Figure

2.1
. Of major interest for us in this thesis, we can for example view a directed acyclic graph (DAG) encoding the structure of a Bayesian network as a collection of edges connecting a fixed set of nodes. We keep this notion of compositionality rather vague on purpose to avoid being restrictive, and we may also consider somewhat loose notions of composition. For example in the context of natural language, a sentence may be viewed as being “compositional” in the sense that it is a sequence of words.

Figure 2.1
.
Examples of compositional objects. An organic molecule is a collection of molecular fragments assembled together. A protein sequence is a sequence of amino acids. An inorganic material is composed multiple elements (often organized as a crystal;
Mila et al. 2023
). A directed acyclic graph is a collection of edges, combined together so that they don’t form any cycle.

If we were to use MCMC methods in order to sample from a distribution over compositional objects, then the local “mutations” necessary to move in the sample space may correspond to replacing one piece with another, or adding / removing a piece from the object,
provided that these moves lead to another valid object
in
{\mathcal{X}}
. This is quite restrictive, and may lead to significant complexity to validate if a move is legal or not. For example, removing a fragment from a molecule may or may not be physically plausible. Applying MCMC in the context of DAGs will be detailed in
Section

7.1.1
.

2.2.
Marginal sampling as sequential decision making

Instead of sampling an object directly from
P^{\star}
, possibly by moving in
{\mathcal{X}}
with MCMC, we could leverage the compositional structure of each object
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and construct it
from scratch
by adding pieces together sequentially. In this section, we will show with multiple examples how we can sample from a complex distribution over compositional objects through a sequence of (simpler) decisions. We will also introduce notions of graph theory and probabilities which will be central in the rest of this thesis.

2.2.1.
The Galton board

In his book on
Natural Inheritance
in 1889, Francis Galton proposed a mechanical device to illustrate what he called “the curve of frequency”. This device, called the
Galton board
and illustrated in
Figure

2.2(a)
, is composed of a funnel at the top guiding beads into a series of pins disposed in a quincunx pattern. As the beads fall down due to gravity, they will bounce off each pin either to the left or to the right, going down each row to finally end up in one of the bins at the bottom of the apparatus.
Galton 1889
observed that as the numbers of beads and bins increase, the shape outlined by the beads at the bottom of the board resembles a “
Normal curve of frequency
”; this is an illustration of what we now know as the
central limit theorem
.

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 2.2
.
The Galton board. (a) Visualization of the Galton board, where blue beads fall down the apparatus into one of the five bins. (b) A bead falling into a specific bin may take multiple paths, bouncing off a different sequence of pins. (c) Representation of the Galton board as a pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
. The intermediate states
s_{i}
represent the different pins organized in quicunx, and the terminating states
x_{k}
represent the bins.

Beyond this asymptotic curiosity, and the unsettling conclusions Galton drew from it, this model is simple enough that we can analyze its behavior with a fixed number of
n+1
bins denoted by
\{0\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax n\}
. Assuming that a certain bead has probability
p
of going left and
1-p
of going right each time it bounces off a pin (in reality,
p=0.5
), it is well known that the bins in which the bead will fall is a random variable
B
that is distributed as a
binomial distribution
, where

(2.2.1)

P(B=k)=\binom{n}{k}p^{k}(1-p)^{n-k}.

While the binomial distribution could be written in terms of an energy function and its sample space
{\mathcal{X}}=\{0\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax n\}
is discrete, this example deviates from our setting highlighted in
Section

2.1.3
in that
{\mathcal{X}}
has no apparent structure. Nevertheless, this serves as an intuitive illustration of a general principle that we will use extensively in the rest of this thesis:

We have a process to get samples from a more complex distribution as a sequence of multiple simple decisions.

In our example, the distribution we sample from is arguably complex, since a bead falls in a certain bin with distribution
\mathrm{Binomial}(n\mathchar 59\relax p)
, the sequence of decisions corresponding to going left at each pin with probability
p
(or
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p)
).

A second general principle is that we are interested in the
marginal distribution
over which bins the beads fall into, rather than the distribution over the exact paths the beads may take (
i.e.
, how they get to a certain bin).

We are only interested in the outcomes of the sequential process, not in the precise sequences of decisions themselves.

As an example when
p=0.5
, beads tend to fall in the middle bins with higher probability since there are many different paths they could follow to reach those bins (see also
Figure

2.2(b)
); this is reflected by the binomial coefficient in
Equation

2.2.1
counting the number of paths to bin
k
. Playing around with the distributions of each individual decision leads to various marginals at the end of this process; our objective will be eventually to carefully tune each of these distributions so that this marginal matches a complex distribution of interest.

2.2.2.
Elements of graph theory

To formalize the example of the Galton board, and in preparation for more complex scenarios, we need to introduce a few notions of graph theory. We represent each decision point (
e.g.
, each pin) as a state
s
in a state space
{\mathcal{S}}
, connected together as a
pointed directed acyclic graph
to represent the outcomes of each decision (see also
Figure

2.2(c)
).

Definition 2.2.1

(Pointed DAG)
.

Let
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{augmentedstatespace}{{{}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}={\mathcal{S}}\cup\{\bot\}
be a state space
{\mathcal{S}}
augmented with a terminal state
\bot\notin{\mathcal{S}}
, and let
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
be a set of edges connecting these states. A (connected) directed acyclic graph (DAG)
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
is called a
pointed DAG
if there exists a unique
initial state

s_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
s_{0}
is a root of the DAG (
i.e.
, it has no parent), and
\bot
is the unique state with no child.

Unlike most works on generative flow networks
(
Bengio et al. 2023
)
, where the terminal state is denoted by “
s_{f}
”, throughout this thesis we will use “
\bot
” to represent the terminal state, as in
Lahlou et al. 2023
. We use a symbol distinct from the notation “
s
” (used for the states of
{\mathcal{S}}
) to emphasize that the terminal state is
not
an element of the state space (albeit an element of the
augmented
space
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
). We will always assume that all the states of
{\mathcal{G}}
are accessible from
s_{0}
, and that the terminal state
\bot
is also accessible from all the states of
{\mathcal{G}}
. We denote by
\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)
and
\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)
respectively the parents and the children of a state
s\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
in
{\mathcal{G}}
.

The bins of the Galton board are also represented as states in this graph (
i.e.
,
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
); in this example, those states only have a single decision leading to the terminal state, signaling the end of the sequential process. The states where termination is possible are called
terminating states
.

Definition 2.2.2

(Terminating states)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG. The set of
terminating states

{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
of
{\mathcal{G}}
is defined as the parents of the terminal state in
{\mathcal{G}}
:
{\mathcal{X}}\triangleq\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(\bot)
.

Based on our example in the previous section, we can see why the choice of
{\mathcal{X}}
, the sample space of the distribution of interest, to denote the set of terminating states is not coincidental. While in that example all the terminating states have
\bot
as their unique child (
i.e.
, we are forced to terminate once the bead reaches one of the bins), it is important to note that in general reaching a terminating state does not necessarily force termination, only taking the transition
x\rightarrow\bot
to the terminal state does. In other words, it is completely possible that a pointed DAG contains edges from a terminating state to some non-terminal
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
; see
Figure

3.1
further for an example. The sequences of decisions taken to reach a certain terminating state (
e.g.
, the pins a certain bead bounced off of) are encoded through
trajectories
in
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Definition 2.2.3

(Complete trajectories)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG, with
s_{0}
its initial state. The trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
is called a
complete trajectory
if for all
t\geq 0
,
s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1}\in{\mathcal{A}}
(with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
). The set of all the complete trajectories over
{\mathcal{G}}
is denoted by
{\mathcal{T}}
.

It is clear that for a complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
,
s_{T}\in{\mathcal{X}}
is a terminating state. In some cases, it will also be convenient to talk about
partial trajectories
, which are defined similarly except that the start and end points of those trajectories are not necessarily the initial state
s_{0}
or the terminal state
\bot
respectively. We will use “
s_{m}\rightsquigarrow s_{n}
” to denote the set of partial trajectories from
s_{m}\in{\mathcal{S}}
to
s_{n}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
(
s_{n}
may be the terminal state); in particular, it is clear that
{\mathcal{T}}\equiv s_{0}\rightsquigarrow\bot
. If
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
are two partial trajectories such that the end point of
\tau
matches the start point of
\tau^{\prime}
, we will denote by
\tau\oplus\tau^{\prime}
the concatenation of both trajectories. We will also use the abuse of notation “
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
” when the context is clear to denote
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}
, where
{\mathcal{A}}
is the set of edges of
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Notations

We will often visualize a pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
as a graphical structure like the one shown in
Figure

2.2(c)
. We will use standard circles
to denote the states in
{\mathcal{S}}
, and double circles
to highlight the terminating states in
{\mathcal{X}}
. We identify the initial state
s_{0}
with a triangular node
, and the terminal state as
\bot
. We use
to denote the terminating edges leading to
\bot
. Note that when the context is clear (
e.g.
, in
Figure

2.3
), we may duplicate the node
\bot
for visual clarity, even though it must be understood as being
one single state
\bot
. We will give a summary of these notations in the annotated
Figure

3.1(b)
. In the second part of this thesis, we will relax these notations (
e.g.
, the terminal state will be implicit, as in
Figure

7.2
).

2.2.3.
Forward transition probabilities

In addition to the structure of the state space encoding the outcomes of each decision made sequentially, we also need to specify
how
these decisions are made. For example in the Galton board, each bead had probability
p
of falling to the left if it bounced off a pin. We can formalize this with a probability distribution to transition from any state to the next states (
e.g.
, transitioning with a
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p)
).

Definition 2.2.4

(Consistent forward transition probabilities)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG. A function
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{forwardtransitionprobability}{{{}}P_{F}}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
is called a
forward transition probability
distribution consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
if for any
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
P_{F}(\cdot\mid s)
is a properly defined distribution over
\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)
,
i.e.
, for all
s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)
,
P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\geq 0
and

(2.2.2)

\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=1.

In the definition above, for some state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, we use the notation “
\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
” to denote the space of probability distributions over the children of
s
in
{\mathcal{G}}
. In the context of reinforcement learning, a forward transition probability is also called a
policy

(
Puterman 1994
)
, provided the underlying environment is deterministic (
i.e.
, there is a one-to-one mapping between actions and next states). Although we will use “policies” in
Section

2.3
, the term “forward transition probability” will be prevalent in
Chapters

3
and
4
. We can also naturally extend the definition of
P_{F}
for some partial trajectory
\tau=(s_{m}\mathchar 59\relax s_{m+1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{n})
as

(2.2.3)

P_{F}(\tau)=\prod_{t=m}^{n-1}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t}).

Consistent forward transition probabilities have the interesting property that they also induce a distribution over partial trajectories starting at any state
s
and ending at
\bot
.

Lemma 2.2.5

(Distribution over suffixes)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG, and let
P_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
be a forward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
. For any state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
P_{F}
induces a distribution over partial trajectories
\tau
starting at
s
and ending at the terminal state
\bot
, i.e.,
P_{F}(\tau)\geq 0
and

(2.2.4)

\sum_{\tau:s\rightsquigarrow\bot}P_{F}(\tau)=1.

Proof.

Since
P_{F}
is a forward transition probability, we clearly have
P_{F}(\tau)\geq 0
. We can prove
Equation

2.2.4
by strong induction on the maximum length of the partial trajectories. For any state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, let
d_{s}
denote the maximum length of a partial trajectory from
s
to the terminal state
\bot
.

Base case:
Since
{\mathcal{G}}
is a pointed DAG, there exists at least a state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
d_{s}=1
. In that case,
\bot
is the only child of
s
(otherwise there would be a longer trajectory from
s
to
\bot
, making
d_{s}>1
), and there exists a single partial trajectory of the form
s\rightsquigarrow\bot
, which is the transition
s\rightarrow\bot
. Therefore

(2.2.5)

\sum_{\tau:s\rightsquigarrow\bot}P_{F}(\tau)=P_{F}(\bot\mid s)=1.

Induction step:
Suppose that
Equation

2.2.4
is valid for all states
s^{\prime}
such that
d_{s^{\prime}}\leq d
for some
d>0
. Since
{\mathcal{G}}
is a pointed DAG, there exists a state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
d_{s}=d+1
. We can decompose the set of all partial trajectories from
s
into a disjoint union of partial trajectories going through each child
s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)
as

(2.2.6)

\{\tau:s\rightsquigarrow\bot\}=\bigcup_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\{(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})\oplus\tau^{\prime}\mid\tau^{\prime}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\bot\}.

Since all the partial trajectories of the form
s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\bot
have a maximum depth
d_{s^{\prime}}\leq d
, we can apply the induction step to them. Using the decomposition above, we get

(2.2.7)

\sum_{\tau:s\rightsquigarrow\bot}P_{F}(\tau)=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\sum_{\tau^{\prime}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\bot}P_{F}(\tau^{\prime})=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=1.

This proves that
Equation

2.2.4
is also valid for a state
s
with maximum trajectory length
d+1
.
∎

A direct consequence of this lemma is that
P_{F}
induces in particular a probability distribution over complete trajectories (
i.e.
, starting at
s_{0}
). However as we noted in
Section

2.2.1
, we are interested in a distribution over terminating states (
i.e.
, a distribution over the bins in which the beads fall) derived from this distribution over complete trajectories, and not
P_{F}(\tau)
itself. This is called the
terminating state probability
distribution.

Definition 2.2.6

(Terminating state probability)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG, and
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
be the set of terminating states in
{\mathcal{G}}
. Let
P_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
be an arbitrary forward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
. The
terminating state probability
distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
(associated with
P_{F}
) is defined for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
as

(2.2.8)

P_{F}^{\top}(x)\triangleq\sum_{\tau:x\rightarrow\bot\in\tau}P_{F}(\tau)=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\mathchar 59\relax

with the conventions
s_{T_{\tau}+1}=\bot
, and necessarily
s_{T_{\tau}}=x
for all complete trajectories
\tau
such that
x\rightarrow\bot\in\tau
.

Putting it in words, the terminating state probability distribution is the marginal distribution of
P_{F}(\tau)
over trajectories terminating at
x
(
i.e.
,
x\rightarrow\bot\in\tau
). It is important to note that this is different from the marginal over trajectories simply going through
x
, since these are not necessarily guaranteed to directly take the transition
x\rightarrow\bot
. For example in
Figure

3.1
further, although the trajectory
(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{2}\mathchar 59\relax x_{4}\mathchar 59\relax x_{6}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
passes through the terminating state
x_{4}
, it would not contribute to
P_{F}^{\top}(x_{4})
. The following proposition guarantees that for any forward transition probability
P_{F}
, its corresponding terminating state probability is a properly defined distribution over the set of terminating states
{\mathcal{X}}
.

Proposition 2.2.7
.

The terminating state probability distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
is a well-defined probability distribution over the terminating states, in the sense that for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\geq 0
, and
\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}P_{F}^{\top}(x)=1
.

Proof.

Since
P_{F}
is a forward transition probability, it is clear that
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\geq 0
for any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
. The normalization is a consequence of
Lemma

2.2.5
applied to the initial state
s_{0}
:

(2.2.9)

\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}P_{F}^{\top}(x)=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\sum_{\tau:x\rightarrow\bot\in\tau}P_{F}(\tau)=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow\bot}P_{F}(\tau)=1.

∎

Interpreting
P_{F}^{\top}
as a marginal distribution suggests a natural procedure for sampling from it, detailed in
Algorithm

2
: (1) sample a complete trajectory following the forward transition probabilities
P_{F}
, and (2) return only the terminating state reached at the end of the trajectory, right before transitioning to
\bot
. This is the same procedure as sampling from
P_{F}^{\top}=\mathrm{Binomial}(n\mathchar 59\relax p)
associated with
P_{F}(\cdot\mid s)=\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p)
by letting a bead fall down the Galton board, and returning the bin in which it eventually fell into. Since
{\mathcal{G}}
is acyclic, this procedure is guaranteed to terminate in time
O(|{\mathcal{S}}|)
(worst case). However in many interesting cases, the “depth” of all the terminating states from the initial state
s_{0}
will be significantly smaller than the total number of states, as in
Figure

2.2
, but even more so when the state space
{\mathcal{S}}
has some compositional aspect which is reflected in the structure of
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Algorithm 2

Sampling from the terminating state probability distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
–
Definition

2.2.6

1:

A pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
, a forward transition probability
P_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
.

2:

A sample
x\sim P_{F}^{\top}
of the terminating state probability distribution associated with
P_{F}
.

3:

Initialize the state:
s_{0}

4:

repeat

5:

Sample the next state:
s_{t+1}\sim P_{F}(\cdot\mid s_{t})

6:

until

s_{T+1}=\bot

7:

return

x\equiv s_{T}

2.2.4.
Generation of small organic molecules

Even though the example of the Galton board in
Section

2.2.1
is interesting to give intuitions about how a sequential process can induce a terminating state probability distribution, it is limited in that
{\mathcal{X}}
is not a structured sample space. In this section, we consider a more realistic scenario where we would like to sample from a distribution over small organic molecules (
i.e.
, molecules containing C-H or C-C bonds). Molecules have a natural compositional structure as a combination of multiple fragments together, as illustrated in
Figure

2.1
. Since the number of possible molecules is extremely large
(
Fink et al. 2005
)
, defining a distribution by assigning a probability for each individual molecule is practically impossible, due to the normalization constraint. It would be far simpler to just
ignore
the normalization, and assign a certain value for each molecule. In that case, the energy function
{\mathcal{E}}(x)
in
Equation

2.1.1
may correspond to the binding affinity with a target protein for example, in order to generate molecules that are more likely to bind to this protein (
e.g.
,
Bengio et al. 2021
used the binding affinity to the soluble epoxide hydrolase (sEH) protein, a well-studied protein playing a role in respiratory and heart disease
(
Imig & Hammock 2009
)
).

Instead of using MCMC methods for sampling from this distribution
(
Seff et al. 2019
;
Xie et al. 2021
)
, we could construct a molecule
from scratch
by adding one fragment at a time
(
You et al. 2018
;
Bengio et al. 2021
)
, as shown in
Figure

2.3
. Starting from the empty state, we choose a fragment from some vocabulary to be added to a (possibly partial) molecule at each step, until we reach the terminal state. This example highlights a major difference with MCMC: the sequence of decisions leading to the generation of a full molecule may go through partial molecules that are not physically plausible, whereas MCMC methods would only allow moves from a full molecule to another full molecule (
i.e.
, moves proposed by
Q(x^{\prime}\mid x)
in
Algorithm

1
are restricted to valid elements of
{\mathcal{X}}
). Using the terminology introduced in previous sections, the set of terminating states
{\mathcal{X}}
corresponds to full molecules, and
{\mathcal{S}}\backslash{\mathcal{X}}
is the set of partial molecules.

Figure 2.3
.
Pointed DAG for the generation of small organic molecules. At each step of generation, a new molecular fragment chosen from a fixed vocabulary of fragments is added at some end of the partial molecule. The process eventually leads to fully formed molecules (
e.g.
, aspirin), where termination is possible. Following a forward transition probability distribution
P_{F}
defined on this graph yields a terminating state probability distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
over fully formed molecules.

The advantage of having compositional objects, as noted in
Section

2.1.3
, becomes apparent here: having substructures shared between different molecules means that we have many states (decision points) along the complete trajectories to any full molecule, and therefore the complexity of the target Gibbs distribution can be broken down into a sequence of much simpler distributions (
P_{F}(\cdot\mid s)
). This also reduces significantly the number of steps required to sample a full molecule using
Algorithm

2
. However, there is a trade-off between the length of the complete trajectories and the complexity of the transition probability at each state: while we could imagine having a naive pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
where
s_{0}
is directly connected to all the elements of
{\mathcal{X}}
(
e.g.
, all the full molecules), this would make
P_{F}(\cdot\mid s_{0})
extremely complex (in fact, we would be back to finding
P^{\star}
directly). We will see in
Section

2.3
and subsequent chapters how to find a forward transition probability whose corresponding terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
matches
Equation

2.1.1
for a given energy function
{\mathcal{E}}(x)
.

2.2.5.
Autoregressive sampling from a factor graph

As a final example, this time anchored in the field of probabilistic modeling, we consider the problem of inference in a factor graph as introduced by
Buesing et al. 2020
. Suppose that we have
d
discrete random variables
(X_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax X_{d})
, each
X_{i}
taking values in
\{1\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax K\}
, meaning that the sample space
{\mathcal{X}}=\{1\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax K\}^{d}
is exponentially large. We assume that the joint distribution of these random variables is given by a
factor graph
, which can be expressed as a Gibbs distribution like in
Equation

2.1.1
, with the energy function

(2.2.10)

{\mathcal{E}}(x_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax x_{d})=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\psi_{m}(x_{[m]}).

Each factor
\psi_{m}
is a known function that only depends on a subset of random variables (the values of which are denoted by “
x_{[m]}
”). A factor graph can be represented as a bipartite graph whose nodes are the random variables
\{X_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax X_{d}\}
on the one hand, and the factors
\{\psi_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax\psi_{M}\}
on the other hand; see
Figure

2.4
for an example of a factor graph with
d=3
variables and
M=3
factors connecting them.

Inference in factor graphs is a very well studied topic, whether it is exact
(
Dechter 1999
)
or approximate
(
Murphy et al. 1999
;
Kschischang et al. 2001
;
De et al. 2015
)
. As an alternative, and similar to our previous examples,
Buesing et al. 2020
treated sampling from this factor graph as a sequential decision making problem, where the value of each variable is assigned one at a time following a fixed order.
Figure

2.4
shows the pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
for a factor graph with binary variables (
K=2
), where we first assign the value of
X_{1}
, then
X_{2}
, and finally
X_{3}
. Therefore to obtain a full sample from
P^{\star}(x)
, this process requires a sequence of exactly
d
steps. Interestingly, and this time unlike our previous examples,
{\mathcal{G}}
has a tree structure (with the exception of the terminal state
\bot
, whose parents are always all the terminating states in
{\mathcal{X}}
by
Definition

2.2.2
), which is typically the case for autoregressive sequence generation tasks
(
Bachman & Precup 2015
;
Weber et al. 2015
;
Angermueller et al. 2020
;
Jain et al. 2022
;
Feng et al. 2022
;
Rafailov et al. 2024
)
.

Figure 2.4
.
Autoregressive sampling from a discrete factor graph, viewed from the perspective of sequential generation in a pointed DAG. (Left) An example of a factor graph over 3 binary random variables
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
, and
X_{3}
, with 3 factors
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
, and
\psi_{3}
. The energy function associated with the distribution represented by this factor graph is the sum of its factors. (Right) Representation of the sequential generation of a full assignment of the variables
(x_{1}\mathchar 59\relax x_{2}\mathchar 59\relax x_{3})
as a pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
.
\bot
is displayed as separate nodes for clarity only, and should be understood as a single state.

2.3.
Probabilistic inference as a control problem

Continuing with our example in
Section

2.2.5
, if the forward transition probability
P_{F}
was known, following this distribution would yield samples from the terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
. However, we don’t want to sample from any distribution, but very specifically from the Gibbs distribution with the energy function in
Equation

2.2.10
. How can we find
P_{F}
so that
P_{F}^{\top}
matches this distribution of interest, for a given energy function
{\mathcal{E}}(x)
? In this section, we will show that we can treat this as a control problem within the framework of entropy-regularized reinforcement learning
(
Ziebart 2010
;
Fox et al. 2016
)
. We will use “policies” and denote them by “
\pi
” (instead of “forward transition probabilities” and “
P_{F}
”) to be more aligned with the naming conventions in reinforcement learning.

2.3.1.
Maximum entropy reinforcement learning

We recall some notions of (maximum entropy) reinforcement learning
(
Sutton & Barto 2018
)
, with notations and concepts adapted to our setting. We consider a finite-horizon Markov Decision Process (
MDP
)
{\mathcal{M}}=({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax r)
, where
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
is a pointed DAG specifying the structure of the MDP, and
r
is a specific reward function defined below.
{\mathcal{S}}
plays the role of the state space and
{\mathcal{A}}
the action space of the MDP, both being discrete and finite. The MDP is deterministic, in the sense that we are guaranteed to transition to a certain state
s^{\prime}
from a state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
after following an action
a=s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}
. Since
{\mathcal{G}}
is a pointed DAG, all the (complete) trajectories in this MDP start at the initial state
s_{0}
, and are guaranteed to eventually end at the terminal state
\bot
(finite-horizon). We also assume that we are in an undiscounted regime, meaning that the discount factor
\gamma=1
.

Since the MDP is deterministic, we can identify the action leading to a new state
s^{\prime}
from
s
with the transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
itself. As such, we will write all quantities involving state-action pairs
(s\mathchar 59\relax a)
where
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
(
e.g.
, the rewards, or the state-action values) directly in terms of
(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
instead. The reward function
r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
of this MDP is defined such that the sum of rewards along a complete trajectory (also called the
return
) only depends on the energy of the terminating state it reaches: for a trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
, we have

(2.3.1)

\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})\mathchar 59\relax

with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
. In particular, this covers the case of a sparse reward that is received only at the end of the trajectory (
i.e.
,
r(s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})
, and zero everywhere else). In reinforcement learning, the objective is in general to find an
optimal policy

\pi_{\mathrm{RL}}^{\star}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
that maximizes the expected return

(2.3.2)

\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{RL}}=\argmax_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\Bigg[\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})\Bigg]\mathchar 59\relax

where the expectation is taken wrt. the distribution
\pi(\tau)
over complete trajectories induced by
\pi
(see
Section

2.2.3
). With our particular choice of reward function in
Equation

2.3.1
, this maximization would correspond to finding a policy (that might be deterministic) that would lead to a state
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
with the lowest energy here, or equivalently finding a mode of the Gibbs distribution
P^{\star}
. This differs from our initial goal of
sampling
from the whole distribution
P^{\star}
, and not only getting the objects with highest probability.

To account for possibly suboptimal objects, we need to regularize the objective in
Equation

2.3.2
. In maximum entropy reinforcement learning (
MaxEnt RL
;
Ziebart 2010
;
Fox et al. 2016
), our objective is instead to search for a
stochastic
policy that also maximizes the expected return, and the entropy
{\mathcal{H}}(\pi(\cdot\mid s))=-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\pi(s^{\prime}\mid s)\log\pi(s^{\prime}\mid s)
of the policy
\pi
in state
s
, in order to encourage diversity of the actions taken:

(2.3.3)

\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}=\argmax_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\Bigg[\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})+{\mathcal{H}}(\pi(\cdot\mid s_{t}))\Bigg]\mathchar 59\relax

where the expectation is again taken wrt.
\pi(\tau)
. In general, there may be a temperature parameter
\alpha>0
that controls the importance of the entropy regularization relative to the original objective, which we assume to be equal to
1
here for simplicity; we will come back to the impact of the temperature parameter in
Chapter

5
. This type of entropy regularization falls into the broader domain of
regularized MDPs

(
Geist et al. 2019
)
, and we will see another example of regularization based on the relative entropy in
Section

5.3
. MaxEnt RL has been proven to be particularly beneficial for improving exploration
(
Haarnoja et al. 2017
)
and for robust control under model misspecification
(
Eysenbach & Levine 2022
)
.

Haarnoja et al. 2017
showed that the policy maximizing the objective in
Equation

2.3.3
can be written explicitly as
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=\exp\big(Q^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s)\big)
, where
Q^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}
is the soft state-action value function and
V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}
the soft state value function which satisfy the following
soft Bellman optimality equations
(adapted to our setting,
i.e.
, undiscounted and deterministic MDP):

(2.3.4)

\displaystyle Q^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})+V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s^{\prime})

(2.3.5)

\displaystyle V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s)

\displaystyle=\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\big(Q^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\big).

These equations differ from the standard Bellman optimality equations
(
Sutton & Barto 2018
)
in that the the greedy operation appearing in the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{RL}}
is replaced by a softmax operation in
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}
, and the “
\max
” operation in the optimal state value function
V^{\star}
is replaced by the log-sum-exp in
Equation

2.3.5
.

2.3.2.
Sampling terminating states from the soft MDP

From the literature on
control as inference

(
Ziebart et al. 2008
;
Levine 2018
)
, it can be shown that the policy maximizing the MaxEnt RL objective in
Equation

2.3.3
induces a distribution over dynamically consistent trajectories that depends on the sum of rewards obtained along the trajectory.

Proposition 2.3.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a deterministic & undiscounted finite-horizon MDP. The policy
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}
maximizing
Equation

2.3.3
induces a distribution over complete trajectories
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
such that

(2.3.6)

\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(\tau)=\prod_{t=0}^{T}\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\propto\exp\Bigg(\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})\Bigg)\mathchar 59\relax

with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
.

Proof.

Using the soft Bellman optimality equation
Equation

2.3.4
in
\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}
, we have

(2.3.7)

\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=\exp\big(Q^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s)\big)=\exp\big(r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})+V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s^{\prime})-V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s)\big).

By telescoping the state value functions, we can conclude that

(2.3.8)

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(\tau)=\prod_{t=0}^{T}\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

\displaystyle=\prod_{t=0}^{T}\exp\big(r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})+V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s_{t+1})-V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s_{t})\big)

(2.3.9)

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigg(\sum_{t=0}^{T}\big(r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})+V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s_{t+1})-V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s_{t})\big)\Bigg)

(2.3.10)

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigg(\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})+V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(\bot)-V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s_{0})\Bigg)

(2.3.11)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\exp\big(V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s_{0})\big)}\exp\Bigg(\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})\Bigg)

since
V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(\bot)=0
(the terminal state has no child).
V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s_{0})
is a constant independent of
\tau
.
∎

With our choice of reward function in
Equation

2.3.1
, where the sum of rewards only depends on the energy of the terminating state, the distribution in
Proposition

2.3.1
corresponds
almost
exactly to
P^{\star}
in
Equation

2.1.1
. However, the key difference between
Equation

2.3.6
and our goal of sampling from the Gibbs distribution is that
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(\tau)
is a distribution over complete trajectories, while
P^{\star}
is over
{\mathcal{X}}
only. In other words, we are interested in the terminating state distribution
\pi^{\star\top}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(x)
, a distribution over the
outcomes
of the sequential process, and not a distribution over
how
we got there.

Fortunately when there is a unique complete trajectory
s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x
for any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, then the terminating state distribution matches exactly
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(\tau)
(
i.e.
, there is only a single term in the sum appearing in
Definition

2.2.6
). For instance, this is the case in our factor graph inference example of
Section

2.2.5
(see
Figure

2.4
), and this is true more generally whenever
{\mathcal{G}}
has a tree structure. Therefore in those cases, if we know the policy
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}
maximizing
Equation

2.3.3
, then we can sample from
P^{\star}
by simply applying
Algorithm

2
(
i.e.
, sampling a complete trajectory using this policy, and returning the state right before reaching
\bot
). Furthermore, unlike MCMC which produces autocorrelated samples, we can easily obtain independent samples from
P^{\star}
by running this procedure multiple times.

Algorithm 3

Soft Q-Learning algorithm

1:

A MDP
{\mathcal{M}}=({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax r)
, a step size
\beta>0
, a behavior policy
\pi_{b}(\cdot\mid s)

2:

A stochastic policy
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}
maximizing
Equation

2.3.3

3:

Initialize
Q(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
for all
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
, such that
Q(x\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=0
for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
.

4:

loop

5:

Start a trajectory at the initial state
s_{0}

6:

repeat

7:

Sample the next state:
s_{t+1}\sim\mathrm{Categorical}\big(\pi_{b}(\cdot\mid s_{t})\big)

8:

Q(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})\leftarrow Q(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})+\beta\big[r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})+\mathrm{LogSumExp}_{s^{\prime}}Q(s_{t+1}\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-Q(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})\big]

9:

until

s_{t+1}=\bot

10:

return

\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\propto\exp\big(Q(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\big)

Finding the optimal policy

To find
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}
, we can then use any algorithm from the MaxEnt RL literature
(
Nachum et al. 2017
;
Haarnoja et al. 2018
)
. Probably the simplest is the
Soft Q-Learning

algorithm
(
Haarnoja et al. 2017
)
, which is the direct counterpart of the well-known Q-Learning algorithm in standard reinforcement learning
(
Watkins 1989
)
, whose objective is to find a state-action value function
Q(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
that satisfies the soft Bellman optimality equations; see
Algorithm

3
for reference. Soft Q-Learning is an
off-policy
algorithm, meaning that we can use any arbitrary behavior policy
\pi_{b}
in order to transition to the next state
s_{t+1}
on line 5 (as opposed to being
on-policy
, where we would be forced to use the policy derived from the current
Q(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
). In their application to inference in factor graphs described in
Section

2.2.5
,
Buesing et al. 2020
leveraged the tree structure of
{\mathcal{G}}
and used an algorithm based on
Monte Carlo tree search
(MCTS;
Coulom 2006
) in order to find
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}
.

2.3.3.
Multi-path environments & biased sampling

Figure 2.5
.
Illustration of the bias of the terminating state distribution associated with
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}
on a soft MDP with a DAG structure. The labels on each transition of the MDP corresponds to the reward function, satisfying
Equation

2.3.1
(
i.e.
, sparse reward setting). The terminating state distribution
\pi^{\star\top}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(x)
is computed by marginalizing
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(\tau)
over trajectories leading to
x
(
e.g.
, two trajectories
s_{0}\rightarrow s_{1}\rightarrow x_{4}
and
s_{0}\rightarrow s_{2}\rightarrow x_{4}
to
x_{4}
).
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(\tau)
is computed based on
Proposition

2.3.1
. The terminating state distribution
\pi^{\star\top}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(x)
should be contrasted with the Gibbs distribution
P^{\star}(x)\propto\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x))
in
Equation

2.1.1
. The normalization constant is
Z^{\prime}=\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x_{3}))+2\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x_{4}))+\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x_{5}))
. This is a simplified version of the molecule generation task described in
Section

2.2.4
.

We saw that when
{\mathcal{G}}
has a tree structure, meaning that there is a unique complete trajectory going to any terminating state
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, simply following
\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}
yields samples from the Gibbs distribution if the reward is defined as
Equation

2.3.1
. But when there are multiple ways of constructing a terminating state, as in
Sections

2.2.1
and
2.2.4
, this does not hold any longer. It is important to recall that while the distribution over
how
the objects are generated (
i.e.
, the complete trajectories) is proportional to
\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x))
by
Proposition

2.3.1
, we are interested in the (marginal) distribution over the
outcomes
(
i.e.
, the corresponding terminating state distribution). In fact, it is easy to show (based on
Definition

2.2.6
and
Proposition

2.3.1
) that in general the terminating state distribution associated with
\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}
is given by

(2.3.12)

\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star\top}(x)=\sum_{\tau:x\rightarrow\bot\in\tau}\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(\tau)\propto n(x)\exp\left(\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})\right)\mathchar 59\relax

where
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{numtrajectories}{{{}}n(x)}=|\{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}\mid x\rightarrow\bot\in\tau\}|
is the number of complete trajectories that terminate at
x
, and where
s_{T}=x
and
s_{T+1}=\bot

(
Bengio et al. 2021
)
. With the reward function
r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
defined as
Equation

2.3.1
, this means that
\pi^{\star\top}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(x)\propto n(x)\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x))
, which does
not
correspond to our target distribution
Equation

2.1.1
. As an illustrative example, we consider a simplified version of the small molecule generation example of
Section

2.2.4
in
Figure

2.5
, where the sample space only contains 3 molecules. If we follow the optimal policy
\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}
, the molecule
x_{4}
will be artificially over-represented since it has 2 complete trajectories leading to it.

Our objective in the first part of this thesis is to build a framework that does not suffer from this bias anymore. Our goal will still be to find a policy whose terminating state distribution matches the Gibbs distribution of interest, but the approach will be
a priori
quite different from the control perspective we adopted in this section. This policy (or forward transition probability distribution
P_{F}
) will be derived from a
flow
in a flow network, as we will see in the following chapters, but we will eventually tie it back to MaxEnt RL in
Chapter

5
.

Chapter 3
Flow Networks

This chapter contains material from the following paper:

•

Yoshua Bengio
∗
, Salem Lahlou
∗
,
Tristan Deleu
∗
, Edward Hu, Mo Tiwari, Emmanuel Bengio (2023).
GFlowNet Foundations
. Journal of Machine Learning Research (JMLR).
\notecite
bengio2023gflownetfoundations

In the previous chapter, we saw that when the objects have some compositional structure, we can naturally sample from the Gibbs distribution in
Equation

2.1.1
as a sequential decision making process through a pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
whose terminating states
{\mathcal{X}}
is the sample space of
P^{\star}
. The main challenge was to find a forward transition probability
P_{F}
such that its corresponding terminating state distribution matched the target Gibbs distribution. We saw in
Section

2.3
that naively applying (maximum entropy) reinforcement learning in order to find this
P_{F}
could lead to a distribution which is biased when
{\mathcal{G}}
is a general DAG, with multiple ways of constructing the objects of interest. To avoid this bias, we will see in the next chapter how to find such a
P_{F}
in a general case for any structure
{\mathcal{G}}
. This solution will be based on a standard tool of graph theory called
flow networks
, of which we introduce the foundations in this chapter.

3.1.
Flow networks & Markovian flows

What made the maximum entropy reinforcement learning approach fail in
Section

2.3.3
was the possibility of having multiple paths leading to the same object in the pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
, where the probabilities of each path were added together. This was effectively treating different paths going to the same object as being completely “independent”, even though they could have some components in common (and they even share the same endpoint). To alleviate this issue, we need a structure that will take into account these dependencies between paths. This will come in the form of a
flow network
, which we will introduce in this section, along with its Markovian counterpart which will play a central role in practice when we will use them for generative modeling in
Chapter

4
.

3.1.1.
Flow networks

In
Section

2.2.2
, we introduced the notion of pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
to encode the structure of a state space
{\mathcal{S}}
. We augment this DAG with a function
F^{\star}
over complete trajectories of
{\mathcal{G}}
called a
flow
.

Definition 3.1.1

(Trajectory flow)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a pointed DAG, and
{\mathcal{T}}
be the set of complete trajectories over
{\mathcal{G}}
. A non-negative function over complete trajectories
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{flow}{{{}}F^{\star}}:{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
is called a
trajectory flow
. The set of all trajectory flows is denoted by
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{flownetwork}{{{}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}({\mathcal{G}})}\triangleq{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{T}}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathbb{R}}^{+})
(
i.e.
, the set of all non-negative functions over
{\mathcal{T}}
). The pair
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
is called a
flow network
.

Although a trajectory flow
F^{\star}
is defined as a function over complete trajectories, it can also naturally induce a measure over the measurable space of complete trajectories
({\mathcal{T}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
, where the
\sigma
-algebra
\Sigma=2^{{\mathcal{T}}}
is the power set of
{\mathcal{T}}
. We make an abuse of notation here and denote by
F^{\star}
both the trajectory flow (a function), and its corresponding measure, where for every subset of complete trajectories
A\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}
, we have

(3.1.1)

F^{\star}(A)=\sum_{\tau\in A}F^{\star}(\tau).

In the special case where the subset
A
is a singleton trajectory
\{\tau\}
, we will simply write its measure as
F^{\star}(\tau)
to identify it with the trajectory flow function. By another abuse of notation, we will use
F^{\star}
to also denote flows going through specific states, transitions, or partial trajectories (see
Section

3.1.4
). For example, the
state flow

F^{\star}(s)
corresponds to the amount of flow going through the state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, and is defined by

(3.1.2)

F^{\star}(s)\triangleq F^{\star}(\{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}\mid s\in\tau\})=\sum_{\tau:s\in\tau}F^{\star}(\tau).

We will always assume that the trajectory flow
F^{\star}
is non-identically zero, and that for any state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, there exists at least a trajectory going through
s
with positive flow, to guarantee that
F^{\star}(s)>0
. Similarly, the
edge flow

F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
corresponds to the amount of flow going through the transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
:

(3.1.3)

F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})\triangleq F^{\star}(\{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}\mid s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau\})=\sum_{\tau:s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau}F^{\star}(\tau).

These state and edge flows will play a crucial role throughout this section, and in particular the edge flows through terminating transitions of the form
F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)
for
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, as we will see in
Chapter

4
. For any flow network, these flows are related via a “flow conservation” rule, which we will expand on in
Section

3.2
.

Proposition 3.1.2
.

Given a flow network
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
, the state and edge flows satisfy

(3.1.4)

\displaystyle\forall s\in{\mathcal{S}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ \qquad F^{\star}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})

(3.1.5)

\displaystyle\forall s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\qquad F^{\star}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})

Proof.

For any
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, the set of complete trajectories going through
s
is the (disjoint) union of the sets of trajectories going through
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
, for all
s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)

(3.1.6)

\{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}\mid s\in\tau\}=\bigcup_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}\mid s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau\}.

In other words, any complete trajectory
\tau
going through
s
has to also go through the transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
, for one of the children
s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)
. Therefore, it follows by definition of the state and edge flows that

(3.1.7)

F^{\star}(s)=\sum_{\tau:s\in\tau}F^{\star}(\tau)=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\sum_{\tau:s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau}F^{\star}(\tau)=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime}).

Similarly, for
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
, the set of complete trajectories going through
s^{\prime}
is the (disjoint) union of the sets of trajectories going through
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
for all
s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})

(3.1.8)

\{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}\mid s^{\prime}\in\tau\}=\bigcup_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}\{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}\mid s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau\}.

Again, any complete trajectory
\tau
going through
s^{\prime}
has necessarily gone through the transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
, for one of the parents
s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})
if
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
is not the initial state. Therefore

(3.1.9)

F^{\star}(s^{\prime})=\sum_{\tau:s^{\prime}\in\tau}F^{\star}(\tau)=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}\sum_{\tau:s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau}F^{\star}(\tau)=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime}).

∎

The condition “
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
” in
Equation

3.1.5
highlights the particular role played by the initial state: it acts as a single source for all the flow going through the flow network. Similarly, the terminal state
\bot
is a single sink state where all the flow ends at. Using the measure perspective of
F^{\star}
, the total amount of flow going through the flow network then corresponds to the flow over the whole space of complete trajectories.

Definition 3.1.3

(Total flow)
.

Let
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
be a flow network. The
total flow

Z^{\star}
of the flow network is the sum of the flows of all the complete trajectories (equivalently, the measure of the whole set
{\mathcal{T}}
)

(3.1.10)

Z^{\star}\triangleq F^{\star}({\mathcal{T}})=\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}F^{\star}(\tau).

Since
F^{\star}
is non-identically zero, the total flow
Z^{\star}>0
itself is clearly guaranteed to be positive. The following proposition makes the intuition above clear that all the flow through the flow network originates from the initial state
s_{0}
, linking the total flow
Z^{\star}
with the flow going through (going out of) the initial state
s_{0}
.

Proposition 3.1.4

(Initial flow)
.

Let
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
be a flow network, and let
Z^{*}
be its total flow. The state flow at the initial state is equal to the total flow:
F^{\star}(s_{0})=Z^{*}
.

Proof.

All the complete trajectories go through the initial state. Therefore

(3.1.11)

F^{\star}(s_{0})=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\in\tau}F^{\star}(\tau)=\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}F^{\star}(\tau)=Z^{\star}.

∎

We will consistently use the “
⋆
” notation to denote any quantity related to a valid trajectory flow (
e.g.
, the total flow, or the corresponding transition probabilities in
Definition

3.1.7
). In further sections, and especially in
Chapter

4
, the functions without the star notation will denote any function that may not necessarily correspond to a proper trajectory flow, and therefore may not satisfy the properties above such as the identities in
Proposition

3.1.2
and the link between the total flow and the state flow at the initial state in
Proposition

3.1.4
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 3.1
.
Illustration of a flow network. (a) A flow of particles traverses the network of pipes, from
s_{0}
to the terminal state
\bot
(illustrated by the gradual change in color). The edge flow
F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
quantifies the “amount of particles” going through
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
. (b) Representation of the underlying pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
, with the nomenclature introduced in
Section

2.2.2
.

A more concrete interpretation of a flow network is as a “fluid” going through conduits, as illustrated in
Figure

3.1(a)
. A flow can be viewed as a stream of particles flowing through these conduits from the initial state
s_{0}
(the source of all the fluid,
e.g.
, a faucet) to the terminal state
\bot
(a sink). The edge flow
F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
is the total amount of flow going through a certain conduit. In general though, since flows are defined at a global level over complete trajectories, these particles have the rather unnatural property (from a physical perspective) that they can remember their history (
i.e.
, which sequence of conduits they took). We will see in
Section

3.1.4
a family of trajectory flows where particles will no longer have memory of their past, making this physical intuition more realistic.

3.1.2.
Backward transition probabilities

In
Section

2.2.3
, we defined the notion of forward transition probability distribution
P_{F}
as being a distribution over the children of a particular state
s
. In what follows, it will be necessary to also consider distributions over the
parents
of a state. We call these
backward transition probabilities
.

Definition 3.1.5

(Consistent backward transition probabilities)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG. A function
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{backwardtransitionprobability}{{{}}P_{B}}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
is called a
backward transition probability
distribution consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
if for any
s\neq s_{0}
,
P_{B}(\cdot\mid s)
is a properly defined distribution over
\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)
,
i.e.
, for all
s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)
,
P_{B}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\geq 0
and

(3.1.12)

\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}P_{B}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=1.

We use
{\mathcal{S}}
as the domain of
P_{B}
in our definition for simplicity, even though the backward transition probability is undefined for the initial state
s_{0}
, since it has no parent (by definition of a pointed DAG in
Definition

2.2.1
). Moreover, while it would be technically possible to define
P_{B}
at the terminal state
\bot
, as a distribution over
{\mathcal{X}}=\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(\bot)
, this is intentionally left out from the definition here since
{\mathcal{X}}
may be much larger than any other
\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)
and defining such a “
P_{B}(\cdot\mid\bot)
” would eventually be as hard as computing the Gibbs distribution in
Equation

2.1.1
, which we want to avoid. Similar to
Definition

2.2.4
, for any state
s\neq s_{0}
, we use the notation “
\Delta(\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
” to denote the space of probability distributions over the parents of
s
in
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Again similar to how we extended the notion of forward transition probabilities to (partial) trajectories, we can naturally do the same for
P_{B}
as well. For some partial trajectory
\tau=(s_{m}\mathchar 59\relax s_{m+1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{n})
,

(3.1.13)

P_{B}(\tau)=\prod_{t=m}^{n-1}P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1}).

We saw in
Lemma

2.2.5
that a forward transition probability distribution consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
induced a distribution over the partial trajectories starting at any arbitrary state. The following lemma shows similarly that a backward transition probability distribution would also induce a distribution over partial trajectories, but this time ending at any state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

Lemma 3.1.6

(Distribution over prefixes)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG, and let
P_{B}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
be a backward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
. For any state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
s\neq s_{0}
,
P_{B}
induces a distribution over partial trajectories
\tau
starting at the initial state
s_{0}
and ending at
s
, i.e.,
P_{B}(\tau)\geq 0
and

(3.1.14)

\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}P_{B}(\tau)=1.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of
Lemma

2.2.5
, and we prove
Equation

3.1.14
by strong induction on the maximum length of the partial trajectories. For any state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, let
d_{s}
denote the maximum length of a partial trajectory from the initial state
s_{0}
to
s
.

Base case:
There exists at least a state
s\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{0})
such that
d_{s}=1
. In that case,
s_{0}
is the only parent of
s
, and there exists a single partial trajectory of the form
s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s
, which is the transition
s_{0}\rightarrow s
. In that case

(3.1.15)

\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}P_{B}(\tau)=P_{B}(s_{0}\mid s)=1.

Induction step:
Suppose that
Equation

3.1.14
is valid for all states
s^{\prime}
such that
d_{s^{\prime}}\leq d
for some
d>0
. Since
{\mathcal{G}}
is a pointed DAG, there exists a state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
d_{s}=d+1
. We can decompose the set of all partial trajectories to
s
into a disjoint union of partial trajectories going through each parent
s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)
as

(3.1.16)

\{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s\}=\bigcup_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\{\tau^{\prime}\oplus(s^{\prime}\rightarrow s)\mid\tau^{\prime}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s^{\prime}\}.

Since all the partial trajectories of the form
s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s^{\prime}
have a maximum depth
d_{s^{\prime}}\leq d
, we can apply the induction step to them. Using the decomposition above, we get

(3.1.17)

\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}P_{B}(\tau)=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}P_{B}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\sum_{\tau^{\prime}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s^{\prime}}P_{B}(\tau^{\prime})=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}P_{B}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=1.

This proves that
Equation

3.1.14
is also valid for a state
s
with maximum trajectory length
d+1
.
∎

A consequence of this lemma is that a backward transition probability distribution induces a distribution over the set of partial trajectories
s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x
leading to some terminating state
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
. This distribution over partial trajectories is denoted
P_{B}(\tau\mid x)

(
Malkin et al. 2022
)
, and it will be useful in order to estimate the terminating state probability
P_{F}^{\top}(x)
of a specific state, using importance sampling
(
Zhang et al. 2022a
)
; see
Section

7.6.1
for details.

3.1.3.
Probability distribution induced by a flow

To bridge the gap between flow networks, which may seem a bit
ad hoc
thus far, and transition probabilities, we can derive forward and backward transition probability distributions from a valid flow
F^{\star}
by simply normalizing it with appropriate quantities.

Definition 3.1.7

(Transition probabilities)
.

Let
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
be a flow network. For any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
and any transition
s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
, the
forward transition probability
distribution
P^{\star}_{F}
and the
backward transition probability
distribution
P^{\star}_{B}
(associated with
F^{\star}
) are defined by

(3.1.18)

\displaystyle P^{\star}_{F}(s^{\prime\prime}\mid s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{F^{\star}(s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}{F^{\star}(s^{\prime})}

\displaystyle P^{\star}_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{F^{\star}(s^{\prime})}.

It is clear from
Proposition

3.1.2
that
P_{F}^{\star}
is a properly defined forward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
(
Definition

2.2.4
), and
P_{B}^{\star}
is a backward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
(
Definition

3.1.5
). When flow networks are interpreted as being a fluid going through pipes, the forward transition probability
P^{\star}_{F}
has a natural interpretation as “following the flow”, in the sense that at each state
s^{\prime}
, we have a higher chance of going to a state
s^{\prime\prime}
as the flow
F^{\star}(s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})
is larger.

Although in general computing the terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\top}(x)
associated with an arbitrary
P_{F}
might be challenging due to the summation over all the trajectories
s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x
(
Definition

2.2.6
), the following proposition shows that the terminating state distribution associated with
P_{F}^{\star}
derived from a flow
F^{\star}
has a notably simple form that only depends on the flow at the terminating edge.

Proposition 3.1.8
.

Let
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
be a flow network, with
P_{F}^{\star}
the forward transition probability associated with the trajectory flow
F^{\star}
, and let
Z^{\star}
be the total flow of
F^{\star}
. The corresponding terminating state probability distribution satisfies for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}

(3.1.19)

P_{F}^{\star\top}(x)=\frac{F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)}{Z^{\star}}.

Proof.

By definition of the terminating state probability of
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
(
Definition

2.2.6
)

(3.1.20)

\displaystyle P_{F}^{\star\top}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}P_{F}^{\star}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}\frac{F^{\star}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{F^{\star}(s_{t})}

(3.1.21)

\displaystyle=\frac{F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)}{F^{\star}(s_{0})}\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\prod_{t=1}^{T_{\tau}}\frac{F^{\star}(s_{t-1}\rightarrow s_{t})}{F^{\star}(s_{t})}

(3.1.22)

\displaystyle=\frac{F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)}{Z^{\star}}\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\prod_{t=1}^{T_{\tau}}P_{B}^{\star}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})

(3.1.23)

\displaystyle=\frac{F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)}{Z^{\star}}\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}P_{B}^{\star}(\tau)=\frac{F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)}{Z^{\star}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used the conventions
s_{T_{\tau}}=x
and
s_{T_{\tau}+1}=\bot
, the definition of
P_{F}^{\star}
in terms of the edge and state flows of
F^{\star}
in
Equation

3.1.20
, the definition of
P_{B}^{\star}
and
Proposition

3.1.4
in
Equation

3.1.22
, and
Lemma

3.1.6
in
Equation

3.1.23
applied to
x
since
P_{B}^{\star}
is a backward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
.
∎

The proposition above is very important. It shows that
if we know the flow
F^{\star}
, then the terminating state distribution associated with
P_{F}^{\star}
(which is derived from the flow) is a distribution known up to a normalization constant, which is reminiscent of a Gibbs distribution in
Equation

2.1.1
. Therefore we can reduce the problem of sampling from the Gibbs distribution to finding a valid flow such that
F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)=\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x))
; all we would have to do to sample from the Gibbs distribution would be to follow
P_{F}^{\star}
and return the terminating state. How to find such a flow that satisfies these “boundary conditions” will be the subject of
Chapter

4
. We will often say that
P_{F}^{\star\top}
is the distribution (over terminating states) induced by the flow
F^{\star}
.

Another consequence of this proposition is that since
P_{F}^{\star\top}
is a properly defined distribution over terminating states, in addition to being equal to the flow at the initial state (
Proposition

3.1.4
), the total flow
Z^{\star}=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)
is equal to the sum of all the flow going through terminating edges. Just like
s_{0}
is the unique source of all the flow in the flow network,
\bot
is the unique sink for all the flow.

3.1.4.
Markovian flows

Defining a trajectory flow requires the specification of
|{\mathcal{T}}|
non-negative values, one for every complete trajectory, which can be exponentially large in the number of edges in
{\mathcal{G}}
in general. In this section, we introduce a special class of trajectory flows satisfying some
Markov property
, which have the remarkable characteristic that they can be defined with much fewer values thanks to a factorization along
{\mathcal{G}}
. Similar to the state and edge flows in
Section

3.1.1
, we first need to define the flow going through a prefix (partial) trajectory of the form
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s)
, ending at some state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
(not necessarily the terminal state), by

(3.1.24)

F^{\star}(\tau\preceq\cdot)\triangleq F^{\star}(\{\tau^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}\mid\tau\preceq\tau^{\prime}\})=\sum_{\tau^{\prime}:\tau\preceq\tau^{\prime}}F^{\star}(\tau^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

where
\tau\preceq\tau^{\prime}
means that
\tau
is a prefix of
\tau^{\prime}
. This partial trajectory
\tau
can be interpreted as the full history before reaching
s
. With this perspective, we will use
F^{\star}(\tau\preceq\cdot)
, alongside the state and edge flows, to define the Markovian nature of a trajectory flow
F^{\star}
.

Definition 3.1.9

(Markovian flow)
.

Let
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
be a flow network, with
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
.
F^{\star}
is called a
Markovian flow
if for any state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, any partial trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s)
leading to
s
, and any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}

(3.1.25)

F^{\star}(s)F^{\star}(\tau^{\prime}\preceq\cdot)=F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})F^{\star}(\tau\preceq\cdot)\mathchar 59\relax

where
\tau^{\prime}=\tau\oplus(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
is the (partial) trajectory where the transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
has been appended to
\tau
. The set of Markovian flows is denoted by
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{markovianflownetwork}{{{}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\mathrm{Markov}}({\mathcal{G}})}\subsetneq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}({\mathcal{G}})
.
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
is called a
Markovian flow network
.

Unlike in
(
Bengio et al. 2023
)
, our definition of a Markovian flow does not involve probabilities over trajectories explicitly, and only relies on properties of the flow itself. However, the standard notion of a Markov process, where the probability of transitioning only depends on the current state and not the whole history, can be recovered as a consequence of
Definition

3.1.9
. For a flow
F^{\star}
, we can define a probability distribution over the space of complete trajectories
({\mathcal{T}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
(where
\Sigma=2^{{\mathcal{T}}}
) by simply normalizing the measure
F^{\star}
by its total flow
Z^{\star}
. In other words, for any set
A\in\Sigma
, we have

(3.1.26)

P^{\star}(A)\triangleq\frac{F^{\star}(A)}{Z^{\star}}=\frac{1}{Z^{\star}}\sum_{\tau\in A}F^{\star}(\tau).

If we assume that
F^{\star}
is Markovian, then by definition of conditional probability, the probability of transitioning to
s^{\prime}
conditioned on the whole history prior to
s
, given by the partial trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s)
, is

(3.1.27)

P^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\mid\tau)=\frac{P^{\star}(\tau^{\prime}\preceq\cdot)}{P^{\star}(\tau\preceq\cdot)}=\frac{F^{\star}(\tau^{\prime}\preceq\cdot)}{F^{\star}(\tau\preceq\cdot)}=\frac{F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{F^{\star}(s)}=P_{F}^{\star}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\mathchar 59\relax

where the forward transition probability of
F^{\star}
only depends on the current state
s
. Note that the Markov property does not hold for all trajectory flows, and there exist non-Markovian flows. In
Figure

3.2
we show multiple examples of Markovian and non-Markovian flows. For example, to see that
F_{1}^{\star}
is not Markovian, we can observe that for the partial trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax s_{2})
,

(3.1.28)

\displaystyle F_{1}^{\star}(s_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{4}F_{1}^{\star}(\tau_{i})=5

\displaystyle F_{1}^{\star}(s_{2}\rightarrow s_{3})

\displaystyle=F_{1}^{\star}(\tau_{3})+F_{1}^{\star}(\tau_{4})=3

(3.1.29)

\displaystyle F_{1}^{\star}(\tau_{4}\prec\cdot)

\displaystyle=F_{1}^{\star}(\tau_{4})=2

\displaystyle F_{1}^{\star}(\tau\prec\cdot)

\displaystyle=F_{1}^{\star}(\tau_{2})+F_{1}^{\star}(\tau_{4})=3\mathchar 59\relax

and therefore
F_{1}^{\star}(s_{2})F_{1}^{\star}(\tau_{4}\prec\cdot)\neq F_{1}^{\star}(s_{2}\rightarrow s_{3})F_{1}^{\star}(\tau\prec\cdot)
. On the other hand, using a similar reasoning, we have that
F_{2}^{\star}(s_{2})F_{2}^{\star}(\tau_{4}\prec\cdot)=F_{2}^{\star}(s_{2}\rightarrow s_{3})F_{2}^{\star}(\tau\prec\cdot)=9
(and similarly for all other states and partial trajectories), making
F_{2}^{\star}
Markovian.

Figure 3.2
.
Examples of Markovian, non-Markovian, and equivalent flows. The flows
F^{\star}_{2}
&
F^{\star}_{4}
are Markovian, whereas
F^{\star}_{1}
&
F^{\star}_{3}
are not. The first two flows are equivalent
F^{\star}_{1}\sim F^{\star}_{2}
. Similarly,
F^{\star}_{3}\sim F^{\star}_{4}
, but they are not equivalent to
F^{\star}_{1}
and
F^{\star}_{2}
. All these trajectory flows have the same edge flow at the terminating edges (
F^{\star}(s_{2}\rightarrow\bot)=2
&
F^{\star}(s_{3}\rightarrow\bot)=3
).

Although Markovian flows will play a key role in what follows, showing that a flow is Markovian via
Definition

3.1.9
can be tedious. The following proposition offers an alternative characterization of a Markovian flow, via a decomposition into its total flow and forward transition probabilities.

Proposition 3.1.10

(Characterization of Markovian flows)
.

Let
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
be a flow network, with
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
.
F^{\star}
is a Markovian flow if and only if there exists a constant
C>0
and a forward transition probability
P_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
such that for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)\in{\mathcal{T}}
,

(3.1.30)

F^{\star}(\tau)=C\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
. Under these conditions,
C
corresponds to the total flow of
F^{\star}
, and
P_{F}
is the forward transition probability associated with
F^{\star}
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
: Let
\tau_{t}=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{t})
denote the (partial) prefix trajectory of
\tau
, with the convention that
\tau_{T+1}=\tau
. If
F^{\star}
is a Markovian flow, and since
\tau_{t+1}=\tau_{t}\oplus(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})
, then by definition we have that

(3.1.31)

F^{\star}(\tau_{t+1}\preceq\cdot)=\frac{F^{\star}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{F^{\star}(s_{t})}F^{\star}(\tau_{t}\preceq\cdot)=P_{F}^{\star}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})F^{\star}(\tau_{t}\preceq\cdot).

An immediate consequence of
Equation

3.1.31
is that

(3.1.32)

F^{\star}(\tau\preceq\cdot)=F^{\star}(\tau_{T+1}\preceq\cdot)=F^{\star}(\tau_{0}\preceq\cdot)\prod_{t=0}^{T}P^{\star}_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t}).

Since
\tau
is a complete trajectory, it is clear from
Equation

3.1.24
that
F^{\star}(\tau\preceq\cdot)=F^{\star}(\tau)
,
\tau
itself being the only complete trajectory whose prefix is
\tau
. Furthermore, since
\tau_{0}=(s_{0})
only consists of the initial state, we also have that
F^{\star}(\tau_{0}\preceq\cdot)=F^{\star}(s_{0})=Z^{\star}
, where the last equality is a consequence of
Proposition
