3.1.4
. Using this in
Equation

3.1.32
, we get the expected decomposition of the Markovian flow
F^{\star}
in terms of its forward transition probabilities
P^{\star}_{F}
and its total flow
Z^{\star}

(3.1.33)

F^{\star}(\tau)=Z^{\star}\prod_{t=0}^{T}P^{\star}_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t}).

\Leftarrow
: To show the sufficiency of
Equation

3.1.30
, we will prove that (1) the trajectory flow
F^{\star}
is Markovian, and (2) that its total flow and associated forward transition probability are
C
and
P_{F}
respectively.

(1)

Let
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
be a state,
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s)
a partial trajectory leading to
s
, and a transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
. We denote by
\tau^{\prime}=\tau\oplus(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
the (partial) trajectory corresponding to
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
being appended to
\tau
. Any complete trajectory
\tau^{\prime\prime}
having
\tau
as a prefix can be decomposed into the partial trajectory
\tau
, followed by a (partial) trajectory
\overrightarrow{\tau}
from
s
to the terminal state
\bot
. By definition of
F^{\star}(\tau\preceq\cdot)
,

(3.1.34)

\displaystyle F^{\star}(\tau\preceq\cdot)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau^{\prime\prime}:\tau\preceq\tau^{\prime\prime}}F^{\star}(\tau^{\prime\prime})=C\sum_{\tau^{\prime\prime}:\tau\preceq\tau^{\prime\prime}}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau^{\prime\prime}}}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

(3.1.35)

\displaystyle=C\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}-1}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\sum_{\overrightarrow{\tau}:s\rightsquigarrow\bot}\prod_{t=T_{\tau}}^{T_{\overrightarrow{\tau}}-1}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

(3.1.36)

\displaystyle=CP_{F}(\tau)\sum_{\overrightarrow{\tau}:s\rightsquigarrow\bot}P_{F}(\overrightarrow{\tau})

(3.1.37)

\displaystyle=CP_{F}(\tau)

where we used
Lemma

2.2.5
in
Equation

3.1.37
. Similarly, it is easy to show that

(3.1.38)

F^{\star}(\tau^{\prime}\preceq\cdot)=CP_{F}(\tau^{\prime})=F^{\star}(\tau\preceq\cdot)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s).

Any complete trajectory
\tau^{\prime\prime}
going through the transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
can also be decomposed as (1) a prefix (partial) trajectory
\overleftarrow{\tau}
from
s_{0}
to
s
, (2) the transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
, and (3) a suffix (partial) trajectory
\overrightarrow{\tau}
from
s^{\prime}
to the terminal state
\bot
. By definition of the edge flow

(3.1.39)

\displaystyle F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau^{\prime\prime}:s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau^{\prime\prime}}F^{\star}(\tau^{\prime\prime})=C\sum_{\tau^{\prime\prime}:s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau^{\prime\prime}}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau^{\prime\prime}}}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

(3.1.40)

\displaystyle=C\Bigg(\sum_{\overleftarrow{\tau}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\overleftarrow{\tau}}-1}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\Bigg)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\Bigg(\sum_{\overrightarrow{\tau}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\bot}\prod_{t=T_{s^{\prime}}}^{T_{\overrightarrow{\tau}}-1}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\Bigg)

(3.1.41)

\displaystyle=C\Bigg(\sum_{\overleftarrow{\tau}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}P_{F}(\overleftarrow{\tau})\Bigg)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\Bigg(\sum_{\overrightarrow{\tau}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\bot}P_{F}(\overrightarrow{\tau})\Bigg)

(3.1.42)

\displaystyle=C\Bigg(\sum_{\overleftarrow{\tau}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}P_{F}(\overleftarrow{\tau})\Bigg)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)

where we again used
Lemma

2.2.5
in
Equation

3.1.42
. By
Proposition

3.1.2
, and using the fact that
P_{F}
is a forward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
, we have

(3.1.43)

F^{\star}(s)=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})=C\Bigg(\sum_{\overleftarrow{\tau}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}P_{F}(\overleftarrow{\tau})\Bigg)\Bigg(\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}P_{F}(s^{\prime\prime}\mid s)\Bigg)=C\sum_{\overleftarrow{\tau}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}P_{F}(\overleftarrow{\tau})

Combining
Equation

3.1.42
and
Equation

3.1.43
, this shows in particular that

(3.1.44)

F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F^{\star}(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s).

And again combining
Equation

3.1.38
and
Equation

3.1.44
, we have
F^{\star}(s)F^{\star}(\tau^{\prime}\preceq\cdot)=F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})F^{\star}(\tau\preceq\cdot)
. Since this is true for any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
and any partial trajectory
\tau
leading to
s
, by
Definition

3.1.9
, this shows that
F^{\star}
is a Markovian flow.

(2)

We have shown already in
Equation

3.1.44
that
P_{F}
is the forward transition probability associated with
F^{\star}
. To show that
C
is the total flow of
F^{\star}
, we can apply
Lemma

2.2.5

(3.1.45)

Z^{\star}=\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}F^{\star}(\tau)=C\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow\bot}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}-1}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=C\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow\bot}P_{F}(\tau)=C.

∎

While there exist other characterizations of Markovian flows, for example based on a backward transition probability
P_{B}

(
Bengio et al. 2023
)
, we only include this result based on
P_{F}
instead since this is sufficient here, in particular for proving the statements related to flow matching conditions in
Section

3.2
.

Flow networks in graph theory

Markovian flows are closer to the standard notion of “flow networks” in graph theory, where flows are specified only along the edges of a graph, and not more globally over complete trajectories; see also
Section

3.2.1
for a characterization of a Markovian flow in terms of its edge flows. The key difference is that standard flow networks typically have a notion of “capacity” associated with each edge of the graph, which is the maximal amount of flow that can go through it. In the case of Markovian flows though, there is no such capacity since the flows may be arbitrarily large (equivalently, each edge has infinite capacity), and their values will only be later grounded using boundary conditions (see
Chapter

4
). Applications of flow networks in operational research include traffic regulations
(
Coclite et al. 2005
)
and identification of decease outbreaks
(
Pinto et al. 2012
)
.

3.1.5.
Equivalence relation between trajectory flows

For a fixed pointed DAG, there might be different flows that essentially behave the same for what matters most to us eventually: using the flow as a sampling mechanism, as described in
Section

3.1.3
. To formalize this, we define a notion of
equivalent flows
if their flows through any edge are equal.

Definition 3.1.11

(Equivalent flows)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a pointed DAG, and
F_{1}^{\star}\mathchar 59\relax F_{2}^{\star}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}({\mathcal{G}})
two trajectory flows.
F_{1}^{\star}
and
F_{2}^{\star}
are said to be
equivalent
, denoted
F_{1}^{\star}\sim F_{2}^{\star}
, if they coincide on the edge flows:

(3.1.46)

F_{1}^{\star}\sim F_{2}^{\star}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\forall s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\quad F_{1}^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F_{2}^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime}).

We can show that this is a properly defined equivalence relation (
i.e.
, reflexive, symmetric, and transitive). For example in
Figure

3.2
, we can show that
F^{\star}_{1}\sim F^{\star}_{2}
(recall that
F^{\star}_{2}
is Markovian while
F^{\star}_{1}
isn’t); moreover
F^{\star}_{3}\sim F^{\star}_{4}
, but they are not equivalent to
F^{\star}_{1}
and
F^{\star}_{2}
. It is clear that two equivalent flows will necessarily induce the same transition probabilities (in the sense of
Definition

3.1.7
). Under this equivalence relation, Markovian flows play a remarkable role in that if two Markovian flows are equivalent, then they are equal.

Proposition 3.1.12
.

Two Markovian flows are equivalent if and only if they are equal.

Proof.

If two Markovian flows are equal, then they are obviously equivalent. Conversely, for a pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
, let
F_{1}^{\star}\mathchar 59\relax F_{2}^{\star}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\mathrm{Markov}}({\mathcal{G}})
be two Markovian flows such that
F_{1}^{\star}\sim F_{2}^{\star}
. Let
Z_{1}^{\star}
and
Z_{2}^{\star}
be their respective total flows. By
Proposition

3.1.2
and equivalence of the Markovian flows, we have

(3.1.47)

Z_{1}^{\star}=F_{1}^{\star}(s_{0})=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{0})}F_{1}^{\star}(s_{0}\rightarrow s^{\prime})=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{0})}F_{2}^{\star}(s_{0}\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F_{2}^{\star}(s_{0})=Z_{2}^{\star}.

By
Proposition

3.1.10
, we can decompose both Markovian flows in terms of their corresponding forward transition probabilities, denoted
P_{F}^{1\star}
and
P_{F}^{2\star}
respectively, and their total flows
Z_{1}^{\star}=Z_{2}^{\star}
. By definition of these forward transition probabilities in term of the edge flows, and using the equivalence of the flows, we have for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)\in{\mathcal{T}}

(3.1.48)

\displaystyle F_{1}^{\star}(\tau)

\displaystyle=Z_{1}^{\star}\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}^{1\star}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=Z_{1}^{\star}\prod_{t=0}^{T}\frac{F_{1}^{\star}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F_{1}^{\star}(s_{t}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}

(3.1.49)

\displaystyle=Z_{2}^{\star}\prod_{t=0}^{T}\frac{F_{2}^{\star}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F_{2}^{\star}(s_{t}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}=Z_{2}^{\star}\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}^{2\star}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=F_{2}^{\star}(\tau).

This shows that these two Markovian flows are equal:
F_{1}^{\star}=F_{2}^{\star}
.
∎

The proposition above shows in particular that a Markovian flow is uniquely defined by its edge flows, which will be expanded on in
Theorem

3.2.1
. But since they represent a priori a smaller family of flows, it is natural to wonder what we are losing when working with Markovian flows as opposed to (general) trajectory flows. It turns out that from the point of view of equivalent flows, we are not losing much in terms of generality, in the sense that for any trajectory flow, there exists an equivalent Markovian flow.

Proposition 3.1.13
.

Let
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
be a flow network. There exists a unique Markovian flow
F^{\prime\star}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\mathrm{Markov}}({\mathcal{G}})
such that
F^{\prime\star}\sim F^{\star}
.

Proof.

This proposition can be proven by applying
Theorem

3.2.1
stated further in the next section. If
F^{\star}
is a trajectory flow, then by
Proposition

3.1.2
we have that the edge flows derived from
F^{\star}
satisfy the flow matching condition
Equation

3.2.1
for all
s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
. Therefore, based on
Equation

3.2.2
, there exists a unique Markovian flow
F^{\prime\star}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\mathrm{Markov}}({\mathcal{G}})
defined, for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)\in{\mathcal{T}}
, by

(3.1.50)

F^{\prime\star}(\tau)=F^{\star}(s_{0}\rightarrow s_{1})\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{F^{\star}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F^{\star}(s_{t}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}\mathchar 59\relax

with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
, such that the edge flows of
F^{\prime\star}
match the edge flows of
F^{\star}
(the edge flows of
F^{\star}
being the function over the edges of
{\mathcal{G}}
that satisfies the flow matching condition in the application of
Theorem

3.2.1
). By definition, this means that
F^{\prime\star}\sim F^{\star}
.
∎

This shows that in each equivalence class stands out a particular flow which is Markovian, a property that no other member of the equivalence class has. In other words, the space of Markovian flows is equivalent to the quotient space of trajectory flows under the relation in
Definition

3.1.11
:
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\mathrm{Markov}}({\mathcal{G}})\equiv\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}({\mathcal{G}})\,/\!\sim
. Based on this observation, we will work exclusively with Markovian flows throughout this thesis from now on.

3.2.
Flow matching conditions

In the previous section we showed multiple properties of flow networks,
provided that we know that
F^{\star}
is a valid (Markovian) flow
. However, apart from the characterization
Proposition

3.1.10
that serves more as a way to verify that a flow is Markovian, we have not given any details on
how
we can construct a valid flow over a given pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
. We will show in this section that this can be done by finding quantities that satisfy some
conservation laws
at a certain level of granularity. These are generally called
flow matching conditions
, and are illustrated in
Figure

3.3
.

(a)

Flow matching

(b)

Detailed balance

(c)

Trajectory balance

(d)

Sub-trajectory balance

(e)

Generalized sub-trajectory balance

Figure 3.3
.
Illustration of the flow matching conditions. (a) The flow matching condition operates at the level of states
s^{\prime}
(
Section

3.2.1
) (b) The detailed balance condition operates at the level of transitions (
Section

3.2.2
). (c) The trajectory balance condition operates at the level of complete trajectories (
Section

3.2.3
). (d-e) The sub-trajectory balance condition operates at the level of partial trajectories (directed, and “forward then backward”;
Section

3.2.4
).

3.2.1.
Flow matching condition

Going back to the intuitive interpretation of a flow network as some fluid going through pipes (
Section

3.1.1
), physics tells us that at any branching point, the total amount of fluid going into it has to be equal to the total amount of fluid going out of it. In other words, apart from the initial and terminal states which play a special role, there is no “new fluid” being created, nor is there any fluid “leaking” out of the pipes. This intuitive observation can be formalized as the following
flow matching condition

(
Bengio et al. 2021
)
, which characterizes the condition a function
F
must satisfy to be a valid edge flow. Note that here we are using the notation “
F
” (a priori an arbitrary function) and not “
F^{\star}
”, which we used to denote a valid flow in the previous section.

Theorem 3.2.1

(Flow matching condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG. A function
F:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
defines the edge flow of a unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
if and only if it satisfies the following
flow matching condition
for all
s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
:

(3.2.1)

\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime}).

Moreover, under these conditions, the unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
whose edge flows match the function
F
is defined, for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)\in{\mathcal{T}}
, by

(3.2.2)

F^{\star}(\tau)=F(s_{0}\rightarrow s_{1})\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{F(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F(s_{t}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}\mathchar 59\relax

with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
.

Figure 3.4
.
Decomposition of a complete trajectory going through the transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
into a prefix
\overleftarrow{\tau}
to a parent
\bar{s}
, a transition
\bar{s}\rightarrow s
, the transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
, and a suffix
\overrightarrow{\tau}
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
: If
F^{\star}
is a Markovian flow, then its edge flow satisfies the flow matching condition
Equation

3.2.1
for all
s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
by
Proposition

3.1.2
. This proves the necessity of the flow matching condition for the edge flows of a Markovian flow.

\Leftarrow
: To prove sufficiency, we will prove that under the flow matching condition (1) the edge flows of the trajectory flow
F^{\star}
match the function
F
, (2) the trajectory flow
F^{\star}
is Markovian, and finally (3) the Markovian flow
F^{\star}
is unique. In what follows, we will assume that the function
F
satisfies
Equation

3.2.1
for all
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
.

(1)

Let
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
be an arbitrary transition; we will assume for now that
s\neq s_{0}
. Any complete trajectory
\tau
going through
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
can be decomposed into (1) a prefix (partial) trajectory
\overleftarrow{\tau}
from
s_{0}
to some parent
\bar{s}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)
, (2) a transition
\bar{s}\rightarrow s
, (3) the transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
, and (4) a suffix (partial) trajectory
\overrightarrow{\tau}
from
s^{\prime}
to the terminal state
\bot
; see
Figure

3.4
for an illustration. By definition of the edge flow
Equation

3.1.3
, we have

(3.2.3)

\displaystyle F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau}F^{\star}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\sum_{\bar{s}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\sum_{\overleftarrow{\tau}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow\bar{s}}F(s_{0}\rightarrow s_{1})\prod_{t=1}^{T_{\overleftarrow{\tau}}}\frac{F(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F(s_{t}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}\Bigg)

(3.2.4)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\frac{F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}\Bigg(\sum_{\overrightarrow{\tau}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\bot}\prod_{t=T_{s^{\prime}}}^{T_{\overrightarrow{\tau}}-1}\frac{F(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F(s_{t}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}\Bigg)

where we used the convention
s_{T_{\overleftarrow{\tau}}+1}=s
in the first term of the decomposition above. We introduce the notation
P_{F}
to denote the distribution defined using the function
F
by

(3.2.5)

P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\triangleq\frac{F(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F(s_{t}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}.

Note that while it is true that
P_{F}
is the forward transition probability associated with
F^{\star}
, we will only use it as a new notation in this proof.
P_{F}
is clearly a forward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
. Using
Lemma

2.2.5
, the last term of
Equation

3.2.4
is equal to
1
:

(3.2.6)

\sum_{\overrightarrow{\tau}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\bot}\prod_{t=T_{s^{\prime}}}^{T_{\overrightarrow{\tau}}-1}\frac{F(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F(s_{t}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}=\sum_{\overrightarrow{\tau}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\bot}\prod_{t=T_{s^{\prime}}}^{T_{\overrightarrow{\tau}}-1}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=\sum_{\overrightarrow{\tau}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\bot}P_{F}(\overrightarrow{\tau})=1

Similarly, we also introduce the notation
P_{B}
to denote the backward transition probability defined using the function
F
by

(3.2.7)

P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})\triangleq\frac{F(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t+1})}F(s^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}.

Again, even though
P_{B}
is the backward transition probability associated with
F^{\star}
, we only use it as a new notation here.
P_{B}
is a backward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
. We can rewrite the first term of
Equation

3.2.4
(for a fixed
\bar{s}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)
) in terms of
P_{B}
:

(3.2.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{\overleftarrow{\tau}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow\bar{s}}F(s_{0}\rightarrow s_{1})

\displaystyle\prod_{t=1}^{T_{\overleftarrow{\tau}}}\frac{F(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F(s_{t}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}

(3.2.9)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\overleftarrow{\tau}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow\bar{s}}F(s_{0}\rightarrow s_{1})\prod_{t=1}^{T_{\overleftarrow{\tau}}}\frac{F(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F(s^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow s_{t})}

(3.2.10)

\displaystyle=F(\bar{s}\rightarrow s)\sum_{\overleftarrow{\tau}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow\bar{s}}\prod_{t=1}^{T_{\overleftarrow{\tau}}}\frac{F(s_{t-1}\rightarrow s_{t})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F(s^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow s_{t})}

(3.2.11)

\displaystyle=F(\bar{s}\rightarrow s)\sum_{\overleftarrow{\tau}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow\bar{s}}\prod_{t=1}^{T_{\overleftarrow{\tau}}}P_{B}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})

(3.2.12)

\displaystyle=F(\bar{s}\rightarrow s)\sum_{\overleftarrow{\tau}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow\bar{s}}P_{B}(\overleftarrow{\tau})=F(\bar{s}\rightarrow s)

where we used the flow matching condition applied to
s_{t}\neq s_{0}
in
Equation

3.2.9
, and
Lemma

3.1.6
in
Equation

3.2.12
. Using both
Equation

3.2.6
and
Equation

3.2.12
in the expression of
F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
in
Equation

3.2.4
, we get

(3.2.13)

F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=\Bigg(\sum_{\bar{s}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}F(\bar{s}\rightarrow s)\Bigg)\frac{F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}=F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

where we again used the flow matching condition, this time applied to
s\neq s_{0}
. Note that this derivation is also valid if
s^{\prime}=\bot
, since in that case the third term in the decomposition
Equation

3.2.4
vanishes (being equal to
Equation

3.2.6
).

If
s=s_{0}
, then we can directly write the edge flow
F^{\star}(s_{0}\rightarrow s^{\prime})
as

(3.2.14)

\displaystyle F^{\star}(s_{0}\rightarrow s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau}F^{\star}(\tau)

(3.2.15)

\displaystyle=F(s_{0}\rightarrow s^{\prime})\sum_{\tau^{\prime}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\bot}\prod_{t=1}^{T_{\tau^{\prime}}-1}\frac{F(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F(s_{t}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}

(3.2.16)

\displaystyle=F(s_{0}\rightarrow s^{\prime})\sum_{\tau^{\prime}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\bot}\prod_{t=1}^{T_{\tau^{\prime}}-1}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

(3.2.17)

\displaystyle=F(s_{0}\rightarrow s^{\prime})\sum_{\tau^{\prime}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\bot}P_{F}(\tau^{\prime})=F(s_{0}\rightarrow s^{\prime})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used
Lemma

2.2.5
to conclude in
Equation

3.2.17
. This proves that for any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
, the edge flow of
F^{\star}
matches the function
F
:
F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
.

(2)

Using our notation
Equation

3.2.5
, the trajectory flow
F^{\star}
can be rewritten as

(3.2.18)

\displaystyle F^{\star}(\tau)

\displaystyle=F(s_{0}\rightarrow s_{1})\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{F(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})}F(s_{t}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}

(3.2.19)

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{0})}F(s_{0}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})\Bigg)\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

Since the sum of flows going out of
s_{0}
is a constant (independent of
\tau
), and
P_{F}
is a forward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
, we can conclude by
Proposition

3.1.10
that
F^{\star}
is a Markovian flow.

(3)

Suppose that there exists another Markovian flow
F^{\prime\star}
such that the function
F
matches its edge flow. By definition of equivalent flows, and since we already know that
F^{\star}
also has its edge flow defined by the function
F
, we have
F^{\prime\star}\sim F^{\star}
. By
Proposition

3.1.12
, this means that
F^{\prime\star}=F^{\star}
, showing the unicity of the Markovian flow
F^{\star}
.

∎

The flow matching condition is a condition that applies locally at the level of states
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
. It is reminiscent of the
global balance
condition in statistical physics
(
Mallick 2009
)
, which finds applications in irreversible Markov chain Monte Carlo algorithms
(
Suwa & Todo 2010
;
Hukushima 2016
)
. But more generally, the connection with fluid dynamics allows us to make a number of analogies with various domains of physics; we will give an example from electrical engineering below. In fluid dynamics, a fluid with density
\rho
and velocity
{\bm{u}}
(
{\bm{u}}
being a vector field) satisfy the following
continuity equation

(3.2.20)

\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}+\mathrm{div}(\rho{\bm{u}})=0\mathchar 59\relax

where “
\mathrm{div}
” is the divergence operator. This equation can be interpreted as a conservation of mass in an infinitesimal volume. Note that the use of the continuity equation in machine learning has gained popularity recently with
flow matching
generative models
(
Lipman et al. 2023
;
Liu et al. 2023
;
Albergo & Vanden-Eijnden 2023
;
Tong et al. 2024
)
. Although they are loosely related to the “flow matching” condition presented here (as we will see below), it is important to distinguish these two concepts even if they share the same name.

In the case where the fluid is incompressible, meaning that its density is constant over time, this continuity equation becomes
\mathrm{div}({\bm{u}})=0
. The divergence being a local measure of how much fluid goes out of an infinitesimal volume, having it equal to zero means that the amount of flow going into that volume (with negative flux) and out of it (with positive flux) cancel each other out. If we interpret the function
F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
as being a discrete version of the vector field
{\bm{u}}
, then this corresponds to the flow matching condition
Equation

3.2.1
.

(a)

Flow network

(b)

Electrical network

Figure 3.5
.
Analogy between the flow matching condition and Kirchhoff’s current law. (a) A Markovian flow network with the edge flow defined at each transition. (b) An equivalent electrical circuit with a power source and 6 resistors. The values of the resistances are chosen in such a way that the current at each branch is equal to the edge flow in the corresponding flow network in (a). This electrical network must satisfy Kirchhoff’s current law.

Kirchhoff’s current law

The flow matching condition is analogous to the first Kirchhoff’s law in electrical networks, also known as
Kirchhoff’s current law
. This is a conservation law stating that at any junction point in an electrical network, the sum of currents flowing into that point is equal to the sum of current flowing out of it. In
Figure

3.5
we illustrate this correspondence between the flow matching condition on a flow network and Kirchhoff’s law on an equivalent electrical network. With this interpretation, the edge flow in
Figure

3.5(a)
is exactly the current between the two corresponding nodes in
Figure

3.5(b)
. One difference between these two points of view is that the electrical circuit is a
closed loop
, whereas the flow network goes from
s_{0}
to the terminal state
\bot
; we will come back to this closed loop perspective of flow networks in
Section

6.1.2
.

3.2.2.
Detailed balance condition

Taking inspiration from the
detailed balance
equations from the literature on Markov chains
(
Grimmett & Stirzaker 2020
)
characterizing the stationary distribution of a reversible Markov chain, we can introduce another characterization of a Markovian flow, this time working at the level of transitions instead of states as in the previous section. Instead of parametrizing a flow with its edge flows directly, we will use a combination of its state flows and transition probabilities. We call this the
detailed balance condition

(
Bengio et al. 2023
)
.

Theorem 3.2.2

(Detailed balance condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG. A function
F:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
defines the state flow,
P_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
the forward transition probabilities, and
P_{B}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
the backward transition probabilities of a unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
if and only if they satisfy the following
detailed balance condition
for all transitions
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
:

(3.2.21)

F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=F(s^{\prime})P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime}).

Moreover, under these conditions, the unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
whose state flow, forward transition probabilities and backward transitions probabilities match
F
,
P_{F}
, and
P_{B}
respectively is defined, for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)\in{\mathcal{T}}
, by

(3.2.22)

F^{\star}(\tau)=F(s_{0})\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\mathchar 59\relax

with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
: If
F^{\star}
is a Markovian flow, let
P^{\star}_{F}
and
P^{\star}_{B}
denote its forward transition probabilities and backward transition probabilities respectively. By
Definition

3.1.7
, we can express both
P^{\star}_{F}
and
P^{\star}_{B}
in terms of the state and edge flows of
F^{\star}
: for any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
, we have

(3.2.23)

\displaystyle P^{\star}_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)

\displaystyle=\frac{F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{F^{\star}(s)}

\displaystyle P^{\star}_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{F^{\star}(s^{\prime})}.

With two ways of writing the edge flow
F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
, this shows that the detailed balance condition
Equation

3.2.21
is satisfied by the state flow, the forward transition probabilities, and the backward transition probabilities of
F^{\star}
. This proves the necessity of the detailed balance condition.

\Leftarrow
: Let
F
,
P_{F}
, and
P_{B}
be functions satisfying the detailed balance condition for all transitions
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
, and let
F^{\star}
be the trajectory flow defined in
Equation

3.2.22
. Since
F(s_{0})
is a constant and
P_{F}
is a forward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
, the flow
F^{\star}
is Markovian by
Proposition

3.1.10
. Moreover, this also shows that
P_{F}
matches the forward transition probabilities of
F^{\star}
(again, by
Proposition

3.1.10
). To show the sufficiency of the detailed balance condition, we then need to prove that (1)
F
matches the state flow of
F^{\star}
, (2)
P_{B}
matches its backward transition probabilities, and that (3) the Markovian flow
F^{\star}
is unique.

(1)

Let
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
be an arbitrary state. From the proof of
Proposition

3.1.10
, and in particular
Equation

3.1.43
, we know that the state flow
F^{\star}(s)
can be written as

(3.2.24)

\displaystyle F^{\star}(s)

\displaystyle=F(s_{0})\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}P_{F}(\tau)=F(s_{0})\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}-1}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

(3.2.25)

\displaystyle=F(s_{0})\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}-1}\frac{F(s_{t+1})}{F(s_{t})}P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})

(3.2.26)

\displaystyle=F(s)\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}\prod_{t=1}^{T_{\tau}}P_{B}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})

(3.2.27)

\displaystyle=F(s)\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}P_{B}(\tau\mid s)=F(s)

where we used the convention
s_{T_{\tau}}=s
, the detailed balance condition in
Equation

3.2.25
(since
s_{t+1}\neq\bot
), and
Lemma

3.1.6
in
Equation

3.2.27
. We implicitly assumed that
F(s)>0
for all states, which is a reasonable assumption since we guaranteed that the state flows
F^{\star}(s)>0
are positive. This shows that the function
F
matches the state flow of
F^{\star}
.

(2)

Let
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
be an arbitrary transition such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
. Again using the proof of
Proposition

3.1.10
, in particular
Equation

3.1.44
, the detailed balance condition applied to
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
, and using
Equation

3.2.27
above to move from
F^{\star}(s)
to
F(s)
and back, we can write the edge flow of
F^{\star}
as

(3.2.28)

F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F^{\star}(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=F(s^{\prime})P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})=F^{\star}(s^{\prime})P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime}).

By definition, this shows that
P_{B}
matches the backward transition probabilities of
F^{\star}
:

(3.2.29)

P^{\star}_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})=\frac{F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{F^{\star}(s^{\prime})}=P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime}).

(3)

Suppose that there exists another Markovian flow
F^{\prime\star}
such that
F
matches its state flow,
P_{F}
matches its forward transition probabilities, and
P_{B}
matches its backward transition probabilities. We denote by
P^{\prime\star}_{F}
the forward transition probabilities associated with
F^{\prime\star}
. By definition, we then have for any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
(where
s^{\prime}
may be equal to the terminal state
\bot
)

(3.2.30)

F^{\prime\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F^{\prime\star}(s)P^{\prime\star}_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=F^{\star}(s)P^{\star}_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the fact that
F^{\star}
and
F^{\prime\star}
have both their matching state flows and forward transition probabilities equal to
F
and
P_{F}
respectively. Since this is valid for all transitions
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
, by definition of equivalent flows we have
F^{\prime\star}\sim F^{\star}
. By
Proposition

3.1.12
, this means that the two flows are equal, showing the unicity of
F^{\star}
.

∎

In their standard form, the detailed balance equations in the Markov chain literature assume that the transition kernel is reversible, meaning that the same kernel appears on both sides of the equation. With our notations here, this would mean having
P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
on the
LHS
of
Equation

3.2.21
, and
P_{F}(s\mid s^{\prime})
on the
RHS
. However, the latter is undefined in our case since we necessarily have
s\notin\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})
(
{\mathcal{G}}
is a pointed DAG). This detailed balance condition is closer to the concept of
skew detailed balance
equations used to define irreversible Markov chain Monte Carlo algorithms
(
Turitsyn et al. 2011
;
Hukushima & Sakai 2013
)
, which use two separate kernels. We will come back to the interpretation of the state flow
F(s)
as the stationary distribution of
P_{F}
in
Section

6.1
.

Since the detailed balance condition operates at the level of individual transitions, it may seem more efficient at first glance than the flow matching condition which requires summing over all incoming and outgoing transitions. However, note that since
P_{F}(\cdot\mid s)
and
P_{B}(\cdot\mid s)
are consistent transition probabilities, they implicitly require summing over the children and the parents of
s
respectively due to the normalization. The detailed balance condition offers an alternative characterization of a Markovian flow, and no condition is a priori better than another. Finally for certain structures of
{\mathcal{G}}
which we will study in
Section

7.2
, it is possible to adapt the detailed balance condition to not depend on a state flow function
F(s)
anymore.

3.2.3.
Trajectory balance condition

While the detailed balance condition works at the level of individual transitions, at the other end of the spectrum the
trajectory balance condition

(
Malkin et al. 2022
)
is a conservation law that operates at the level of complete trajectories in
{\mathcal{T}}
.

Theorem 3.2.3

(Trajectory balance condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG, and
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
the set of terminating states in
{\mathcal{G}}
. A scalar
Z>0
defines the total flow, and functions
F_{\bot}:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
the edge flow of the terminating transitions,
P_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
the forward transition probabilities, and
P_{B}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
the backward transition probabilities of a unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
if and only if they satisfy the following
trajectory balance condition
for all complete trajectories
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)\in{\mathcal{T}}
:

(3.2.31)

Z\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=F_{\bot}(s_{T})\prod_{t=1}^{T}P_{B}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
, and
s_{T}\in{\mathcal{X}}
is a terminating state. Moreover, under these conditions, the unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
whose total flow, edge flow of the terminating transitions, forward transition probabilities and backward transition probabilities match
Z
,
F_{\bot}
,
P_{F}
, and
P_{B}
respectively is defined by

(3.2.32)

F^{\star}(\tau)=Z\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t}).

Proof.

\Rightarrow
: If
F^{\star}
is a Markovian flow, let
P^{\star}_{F}
,
P^{\star}_{B}
, and
Z^{\star}
denote its forward transition probabilities, its backward transition probabilities, and its total flow respectively. By
Proposition

3.1.4
, we know that
Z^{\star}=F^{\star}(s_{0})
. By definition of the forward transition probabilities and the backward transition probabilities in terms of the edge flows, we have for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)

(3.2.33)

\displaystyle Z^{\star}\prod_{t=0}^{T}P^{\star}_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

\displaystyle=F^{\star}(s_{0})\prod_{t=0}^{T}\frac{F^{\star}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{F^{\star}(s_{t})}=\frac{\prod_{t=0}^{T}F^{\star}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\prod_{t=1}^{T}F^{\star}(s_{t})}

(3.2.34)

\displaystyle F^{\star}(s_{T}\rightarrow\bot)\prod_{t=1}^{T}P^{\star}_{B}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})

\displaystyle=F^{\star}(s_{T}\rightarrow\bot)\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{F^{\star}(s_{t-1}\rightarrow s_{t})}{F^{\star}(s_{t})}=\frac{\prod_{t=0}^{T}F^{\star}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})}{\prod_{t=1}^{T}F^{\star}(s_{t})}

with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
. This shows that the trajectory balance condition
Equation

3.2.31
is satisfied by the total flow, the edge flow of the terminating transitions, the forward transition probabilities, and the backward transition probabilities of
F^{\star}
. This proves the necessity of the trajectory balance condition.

\Leftarrow
: Let
Z
be a scalar and
F_{\bot}
,
P_{F}
, and
P_{B}
be functions satisfying the trajectory balance condition for all complete trajectories
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)\in{\mathcal{T}}
. By
Proposition

3.1.10
, the flow
F^{\star}
defined in
Equation

3.2.32
is Markovian. Moreover, this also shows that
Z
is the total flow and
P_{F}
matches the forward transition probabilities of
F^{\star}
(again, by
Proposition

3.1.10
). To show the sufficiency of the trajectory balance condition, we then need to prove that (1)
F_{\bot}
matches the edge flow of the terminating transitions of
F^{\star}
, (2)
P_{B}
matches its backward transition probabilities, and that (3) the Markovian flow
F^{\star}
is unique.

(1)

Let
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
be an arbitrary terminating state. By definition of the flow
F^{\star}
, we can write the edge flow of the terminating transition
F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)
as

(3.2.35)

\displaystyle F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:x\rightarrow\bot\in\tau}F^{\star}(\tau)=Z\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

(3.2.36)

\displaystyle=F_{\bot}(x)\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\prod_{t=1}^{T_{\tau}}P_{B}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})

(3.2.37)

\displaystyle=F_{\bot}(x)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used the conventions
s_{T_{\tau}}=x
, and
s_{T_{\tau}+1}=\bot
, the trajectory balance condition in
Equation

3.2.36
, and
Lemma

3.1.6
applied to
x
in
Equation

3.2.37
. This shows that the function
F_{\bot}
matches the edge flow of the terminating transitions of
F^{\star}
.

(2)

Let
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
be an arbitrary transition such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
. Any complete trajectory going through
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
can be decomposed into (1) a prefix (partial) trajectory
\overleftarrow{\tau}
from
s_{0}
to
s
, (2) the transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
, (3) a suffix (partial) trajectory
\overrightarrow{\tau}
from
s^{\prime}
to some terminating state
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, and finally (4) a transition
x\rightarrow\bot
. We can write the edge flow
F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
as

(3.2.38)

\displaystyle F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau}F^{\star}(\tau)=Z\sum_{\tau:s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

(3.2.39)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in\tau}F_{\bot}(s_{T_{\tau}})\prod_{t=1}^{T_{\tau}}P_{B}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})

(3.2.40)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}F_{\bot}(x)\Bigg(\sum_{\overleftarrow{\tau}:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow s}P_{B}(\overleftarrow{\tau})\Bigg)P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})\Bigg(\sum_{\overrightarrow{\tau}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow x}P_{B}(\overrightarrow{\tau})\Bigg)

(3.2.41)

\displaystyle=P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}F_{\bot}(x)\Bigg(\sum_{\overrightarrow{\tau}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow x}P_{B}(\overrightarrow{\tau})\Bigg)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used the convention
s_{T_{\tau}+1}=\bot
, the trajectory balance condition in
Equation

3.2.39
, the decomposition of complete trajectories going through
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
in
Equation

3.2.40
, and
Lemma

3.1.6
in
Equation

3.2.41
. Using
Proposition

3.1.2
, we can also write the state flow
F^{\star}(s^{\prime})
as

(3.2.42)

F^{\star}(s^{\prime})=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F^{\star}(s^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime})=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}F_{\bot}(x)\Bigg(\sum_{\overrightarrow{\tau}:s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow x}P_{B}(\overrightarrow{\tau})\Bigg)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used the fact that
P_{B}
is a backward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
. Combining
Equation

3.2.41
and
Equation

3.2.42
, we have that
F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})F^{\star}(s^{\prime})
. By definition,

(3.2.43)

P_{B}^{\star}(s\mid s^{\prime})=\frac{F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{F^{\star}(s^{\prime})}=P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

showing that
P_{B}
matches the backward transition probabilities of
F^{\star}
.

(3)

Suppose that there exists another Markovian flow
F^{\prime\star}
such that
Z
matches its total flow,
F_{\bot}
matches its edge flow at terminating transitions,
P_{F}
matches its forward transition probabilities, and
P_{B}
matches its backward transition probabilities. We denote by
P^{\prime\star}_{F}
the forward transition probabilities associated with
F^{\prime\star}
, and
Z^{\prime\star}
its total flow. We first prove that
F^{\prime\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
for all transitions
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
by strong induction on the depth of the state
s
in
{\mathcal{G}}
. We will use
d_{s}
to denote the maximum length of a trajectory from the initial state
s_{0}
to
s
.

Base case:
If
d_{s}=0
, then it means that
s=s_{0}
is the initial state. In that case, using
Proposition

3.1.4
and the definition of the forward transition probabilities associated with
F^{\prime\star}
and
F^{\star}

(3.2.44)

\displaystyle F^{\prime\star}(s_{0}\rightarrow s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=F^{\prime*}(s_{0})P^{\prime\star}_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s_{0})=Z^{\prime\star}P^{\prime\star}_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s_{0})=ZP_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s_{0})

(3.2.45)

\displaystyle=Z^{\star}P_{F}^{\star}(s^{\prime}\mid s_{0})=F^{\star}(s_{0})P^{\star}_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s_{0})=F^{\star}(s_{0}\rightarrow s^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

since
Z
and
P_{F}
match the total flow and forward transition probabilities of both Markovian flows.

Induction step:
If we have
F^{\prime\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
for all transitions such that
d_{s}\leq d
, for some
d>0
, then let
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
be a transition such that
d_{s}=d+1
. Using
Proposition

3.1.2
(since
s\neq s_{0}
), we then have

(3.2.46)

\displaystyle F^{\prime\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=P^{\prime\star}_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)F^{\prime\star}(s)=P^{\prime\star}_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\sum_{\bar{s}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}F^{\prime\star}(\bar{s}\rightarrow s)

(3.2.47)

\displaystyle=P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\sum_{\bar{s}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}F^{\star}(\bar{s}\rightarrow s)=P^{\star}_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)F^{\star}(s)=F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the induction step on
F^{\prime\star}(\bar{s}\rightarrow s)=F^{\star}(\bar{s}\rightarrow s)
since
d_{\bar{s}}\leq d
, and the fact that
P_{F}
matches the forward transition probabilities of both Markovian flows.

This proves that for any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
,
F^{\prime\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
, meaning that
F^{\prime\star}\sim F^{\star}
. By
Proposition

3.1.12
, we can conclude that the two Markovian flows are equal, showing the unicity of
F^{\star}
.

∎

Similar to how the conditions introduced in the previous sections have analogues in the (irreversible) Markov chain literature, the trajectory balance condition is evocative of the
Kolmogorov criterion

(
Kelly 1979
)
. Moreover, we can rewrite
Equation

3.2.31
for a complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
as

(3.2.48)

\displaystyle P_{F}(\tau)=\frac{F_{\bot}(s_{T})}{Z}P_{B}(\tau\mid s_{T})\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the notations
P_{F}(\tau)
and
P_{B}(\tau\mid s_{T})
we introduced in
Sections

2.2.3
and
3.1.2
. If we interpret the factor
F_{\bot}(s_{T})/Z
as a distribution over
{\mathcal{X}}
(which is true when we have a trajectory flow, see
Proposition

3.1.8
), then the RHS of this equation can be seen as a distribution over all the complete trajectories in
{\mathcal{T}}
(recall that
P_{B}(\tau\mid s_{T})
is a distribution over the trajectories terminating at
s_{T}
, by
Lemma

3.1.6
). Similarly, the LHS of
Equation

3.2.48
is also a distribution over all the complete trajectories (
Lemma

2.2.5
). Therefore, another way to interpret the trajectory balance condition is as an equality between two distributions over complete trajectories, one as a forward process and the other as a backward process.

3.2.4.
Sub-trajectory balance condition

As an alternative in between the fully local detailed balance condition at the level of transitions and the more global trajectory balance condition at the level of complete trajectories,
Malkin et al. 2022
also introduced a similar criterion operating at the level of partial trajectories in
{\mathcal{G}}
. This is called the
sub-trajectory balance condition

(
Madan et al. 2023
)
, and uses a similar parametrization as the detailed balance condition in terms of state flow and transition probabilities.

Theorem 3.2.4

(Sub-trajectory balance condition)
.

If
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
be a Markovian flow with
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
, then its state flow
F^{\star}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, its forward transition probabilities
P^{\star}_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
, and its backward transition probabilities
P^{\star}_{B}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
satisfy the following
sub-trajectory balance condition
for all partial trajectories
\tau=(s_{m}\mathchar 59\relax s_{m+1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{n})
(for some
n>m
) such that
s_{n}\neq\bot
:

(3.2.49)

F^{\star}(s_{m})\prod_{t=m}^{n-1}P^{\star}_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=F^{\star}(s_{n})\prod_{t=m}^{n-1}P^{\star}_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1}).

Proof.

By
Theorem

3.2.2
, since
F^{\star}
is a Markovian flow, we know that for any transition
s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1}\in{\mathcal{G}}
,
F^{\star}
,
P^{\star}_{F}
, and
P^{\star}_{B}
satisfy the detailed balance condition

(3.2.50)

F^{\star}(s_{t})P^{\star}_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=F^{\star}(s_{t+1})P^{\star}_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1}).

Applying the equality above for all the transitions in
\tau=(s_{m}\mathchar 59\relax s_{m+1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{n})
and taking their product yields the expected result (factoring out the common term
F^{\star}(s_{m+1})\times\ldots\times F^{\star}(s_{n-1})
).
∎

Unlike the results in the previous sections though, the sub-trajectory balance condition is only a necessary condition for a Markovian flow, but it is not sufficient. If the sub-trajectory balance condition is satisfied for all partial trajectories
of fixed length
, then this may not define a valid Markovian flow. To see this, consider the counter-example in
Figure

3.6
. If we look at all the partial trajectories of length 2, we can write down the sub-trajectory balance condition
Equation

3.2.49
for each of them while simplifying the known terms (
e.g.
,
P_{F}(s_{1}\mid s_{0})=1
because
s_{1}
is the unique child of
s_{0}
, or
P_{B}(s_{3}\mid s_{4})=1
because
s_{3}
is the unique parent of
s_{4}
); for example for
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax s_{2})
:

(3.2.51)

F(s_{0})\underbrace{P_{F}(s_{1}\mid s_{0})}_{=\,1}P_{F}(s_{2}\mid s_{1})=F(s_{2})\underbrace{P_{B}(s_{1}\mid s_{2})}_{=\,1}\underbrace{P_{B}(s_{0}\mid s_{1})}_{=\,1}

The (simplified) equations for the sub-trajectory balance condition of all the partial trajectories of length 2 are given in
Figure

3.6
(right). This gives us a system of 3 equations with 7 unknowns and 1 constraint (for
P_{F}(\cdot\mid s_{1})
). This system is underdetermined and admits an infinite number of solutions; in particular, the following is a solution of this system:

(3.2.52)

\displaystyle P_{F}(s_{2}\mid s_{1})

\displaystyle=P_{F}(s_{3}\mid s_{1})=1/2

\displaystyle F(s_{0})

\displaystyle=4

\displaystyle F(s_{1})

\displaystyle=2

(3.2.53)

\displaystyle F(s_{2})

\displaystyle=2

\displaystyle F(s_{3})

\displaystyle=2

\displaystyle F(s_{4})

\displaystyle=1.

This solution is only one of many we could find. But if we assumed that
F
defines the state flow and
P_{F}
the forward transition probabilities of a valid Markovian flow, then the total flow would necessarily be
Z=F(s_{0})=4
(by
Proposition

3.1.4
). However, this contradicts the conservation of the total flow throughout the network, since the total amount of flow going into
\bot
(which is supposed to be equal to the total flow) is
F(s_{2})+F(s_{4})=3\neq F(s_{0})
. Therefore,
F
and
P_{F}
cannot define the state flow and forward transition probabilities of a valid (Markovian) flow.

Figure 3.6
.
Counter-example of the sufficiency of the sub-trajectory balance condition. We consider all the partial trajectories of length 2 in the pointed DAG (left), and write the corresponding sub-trajectory balance condition for each trajectory (right; simplified). This system of equations admits an infinite amount of solutions, among which many do not correspond to the state flow and transition probabilities of a valid flow (
e.g.
,
Equation

3.2.52
).

The sub-trajectory balance condition is not sufficient to define a valid flow: satisfying
Equation

3.2.49
for all partial trajectories of fixed length does
not
yield a valid Markovian flow. This may appear as a surprise since the detailed balance condition of
Theorem

3.2.2
is effectively a special case of the sub-trajectory balance condition applied to all partial trajectories of fixed length
1
(
i.e.
, transitions), but it
is
sufficient. Intuitively, what
Figure

3.6
shows is that the sub-trajectory balance condition may “jump over” a certain state. The value of
F(s_{1})
is never grounded by
F(s_{0})
: only the third equation in the system involves
F(s_{1})
, which is independent of
F(s_{0})
, and the other partial trajectories of length
2
starting at
s_{0}
all “jump over”
s_{1}
. This is not a situation that we would encounter with detailed balance, as there would be an equation for the transition
s_{0}\rightarrow s_{1}
.

However, if the sub-trajectory balance condition is satisfied for all partial trajectories of
any length

\leq L
(and not of fixed length anymore), then it becomes a sufficient condition for a Markovian flow. This is somewhat trivial because if it is satisfied for any length
\leq L
, it is in particular true for partial trajectories of length
1
(
i.e.
, transitions), in which case it reduces to the detailed balance condition of
Theorem

3.2.2
. Looser conditions are also possible depending on the structure of
{\mathcal{G}}
; we will see an example in
Section

8.3.2
where the sub-trajectory balance condition for some (but not all) partial trajectories of fixed length still defines a valid flow.

We can observe that the sub-trajectory balance condition unifies the detailed balance condition of
Theorem

3.2.2
(applying the sub-trajectory balance condition to partial trajectories of length 1,
i.e.
, transitions), as well as the trajectory balance condition of
Theorem

3.2.3
(applying the condition to complete trajectories). That’s why even if this is not a sufficient characterization of a Markovian flow, it will sometimes be useful to prove statements based on the sub-trajectory balance condition, such as in
Section

5.2.1
, and infer results for the detailed balance and trajectory balance conditions as byproducts.
Malkin et al. 2022
further generalized this sub-trajectory balance condition to undirected paths that go “backward then forward” between two states
s_{m}
and
s_{n}
:

(3.2.54)

F^{\star}(s_{m})\prod_{t=k}^{m-1}P^{\star}_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})\prod_{t=k}^{n-1}P^{\star}_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=F^{\star}(s_{n})\prod_{t=k}^{n-1}P^{\star}_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})\prod_{t=k}^{m-1}P^{\star}_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\mathchar 59\relax

where
s_{k}
is a common ancestor of both
s_{m}
and
s_{n}
; see
Figure

3.3(e)
for an illustration of this generalized condition. This general form has been frequently used on undirected trajectories between 2 terminating states, similar to MCMC
(
Zhang et al. 2022a
;
Deleu et al. 2023
;
Kim et al. 2024b
)
.

3.3.
Conditional flow networks

In
Section

3.1.1
, we defined a flow network as a pointed DAG, augmented with some function
F^{\star}
over the set of complete trajectories
{\mathcal{T}}
. We can extend this notion by
conditioning
each of its components on some additional information
y\in{\mathcal{Y}}
. In general, this conditioning variable can represent any information, either external to the network structure (but still influencing the flows), or internal (
e.g.
,
y
can be a property of complete trajectories over another flow network, such as passing through a particular state). We will define a
conditional flow network
as a collection of flow networks, indexed by
y\in{\mathcal{Y}}
.

Definition 3.3.1

(Conditional flows)
.

Let
{\mathcal{Y}}
be a set of conditioning values. We consider a family of pointed DAGs
{\mathcal{G}}_{y}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{y}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}}_{y})
indexed by
y\in{\mathcal{Y}}
, with their corresponding initial states denoted by
(s_{0}\mid y)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{y}
. A
conditional flow
is a family of trajectory flows
F^{\star}_{y}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}({\mathcal{G}}_{y})
defined on each
{\mathcal{G}}_{y}
. The family
\{({\mathcal{G}}_{y}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star}_{y})\}_{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}
is called a
conditional flow network
.

We will assume that all the pointed DAGs have a common terminal state
\bot
that belongs to each
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{y}
(but similar to unconditional flow networks, is not an element of
{\mathcal{S}}_{y}
). Since conditional flow networks are defined using the same components as their unconditional counterparts, they inherit all the properties from
Section

3.1
. In particular, we can directly extend any of the flow matching conditions in
Section

3.2
by conditioning each component on
y\in{\mathcal{Y}}
. In the following sections, we will elaborate two important examples: flow networks conditioned on external information that modifies the flows
F^{\star}_{y}
, and state-conditional flow networks that depend on previously visited states.

3.3.1.
Parametric flow networks

While
Definition

3.3.1
is fully general and allows us to change the structure of the pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}_{y}
(along with its initial state) with
y\in{\mathcal{Y}}
, in many interesting cases we will have a common structure for the pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
, and only the flows are
parametrized
by some external variable
y
.

Definition 3.3.2

(Parametric flow network)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a pointed DAG, and
{\mathcal{Y}}
a set of conditioning values. A family
\{({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star}_{y})\}_{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}
is called a
parametric flow network
if for all
y\in{\mathcal{Y}}
,
F^{\star}_{y}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}({\mathcal{G}})
is a trajectory flow.

In this context, the conditioning values in
{\mathcal{Y}}
will be called “parameters”; however, it is important to note that these do not correspond to the parameters of a function approximating the flow (
e.g.
, parameters of a neural network, see
Chapter

4
), because for any parameter
y
,
F^{\star}_{y}
is a proper trajectory flow and not an approximation. We will see in
Section

8.2
an application of parametric flow networks.

3.3.2.
State-conditional flow networks

For an (unconditional) flow network
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
, where
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
, we saw in
Proposition

3.1.4
that the state flow at the initial state
s_{0}
corresponds to the total amount of flow going through
{\mathcal{G}}
. By
Proposition

3.1.8
, this means that

(3.3.1)

F^{\star}(s_{0})=Z^{\star}=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot).

This equation is a simple form of “marginalization”, where we sum over all the terminating states accessible from
s_{0}
(all of them). All of the flow coming out of the initial state
s_{0}
eventually ends up at one of the terminating states accessible from
s_{0}
. A natural question then is: does this marginalization also hold for
F^{\star}(s)
the flow through any intermediate state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
? More precisely, we would like to know if
F^{\star}(s)
corresponds to summing the edge flows at the terminating transitions, for all the terminating states that are accessible from
s
:

(3.3.2)

F^{\star}(s)\overset{?}{=}\sum_{x:s\leq x}F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot).

Unfortunately, in general, the answer to this question is
no
as illustrated in
Figure

3.7
. In this example, we have
F^{\star}(s_{2})=4
, whereas the sum of edge flows at the terminating transitions accessible from
s_{2}
is
6
(the terminating states accessible from
s_{2}
being
\{x_{5}\mathchar 59\relax x_{6}\mathchar 59\relax x_{7}\}
). This discrepancy is caused by the flow through
(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax x_{5}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
that contributes to
F^{\star}(x_{5}\rightarrow\bot)
, but is not accounted for in
F^{\star}(s_{2})
since this trajectory does not go through
s_{2}
. To address this, we can introduce a family of flow networks called a
state-conditional flow network
indexed by the states of
{\mathcal{G}}
, where each conditional flow satisfies
Equation

3.3.1
for different subgraphs.

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 3.7
.
Example of a state-conditional flow network. (a) The original Markovian flow
F^{\star}
, with the edge flows indicated at each transition. (b) The subgraph of states reachable from
s_{2}
; there is a flow through
(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax s_{5}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
that contributes to
F^{\star}(s_{5}\rightarrow\bot)
, but is not accounted for in
F^{\star}(s_{2})
. This shows that
F^{\star}(s_{2})
does not marginalize the flows out of its descendants into
\bot
. (c) The state-conditional flow
F_{s_{2}}^{\star}
, which differs from the original flow
F^{\star}
on the subgraph, but satisfies the desired marginalization property. Note that the flows at the terminating edges are left unchanged.

Definition 3.3.3

(State-conditional flow network)
.

Let
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
be a flow network, where
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
, and let
{\mathcal{X}}
be the set of terminating states of
{\mathcal{G}}
. For each state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, let
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
be the subgraph of
{\mathcal{G}}
, rooted in
s
, containing all the states
s^{\prime}
accessible from
s
in
{\mathcal{G}}
(
s^{\prime}\geq s
). A family
\{({\mathcal{G}}_{s}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star}_{s})\}_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}
is called a
state-conditional flow network
if the terminating edge flows of
F^{\star}_{s}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}({\mathcal{G}}_{s})
at the terminating transitions match those of
F^{\star}
: for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,

(3.3.3)

F_{s}^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)=F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot).

Unlike parametric flow networks though, the structure of the pointed DAG in this conditional flow network depends on the anchor state
s
, as well as its initial states
(s_{0}\mid s)=s
. In particular, it is clear that
F^{\star}_{s_{0}}(\tau)=F^{\star}(\tau)
always satisfies the conditions above. Since the definition of a state-conditional flow network depends on an original flow network, we must ensure that this definition is indeed valid,
i.e.
, that such a state-conditional flow network satisfying
Equation

3.3.3
exists.

Proposition 3.3.4

(Existence of state-conditional flows)
.

For any flow network
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
, there exists a state-conditional flow network satisfying
Definition

3.3.3
.

Proof.

Let
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
be a fixed state. Since the structure of the pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
rooted at
s
is clearly well-defined, we just need to show that there exists a trajectory flow
F^{\star}_{s}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}({\mathcal{G}}_{s})
that satisfies
Equation

3.3.3
. Let
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
be a terminating state accessible from
s\leq x
, and let
{\mathcal{T}}_{s}
be the set of complete trajectories in
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
; it is clear that
{\mathcal{T}}_{s}\equiv s\rightsquigarrow\bot
corresponds to the partial trajectories in
{\mathcal{G}}
, starting at
s
and ending at
\bot
. Finally, let
A_{x\mid s}=\{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{s}\mid x\rightarrow\bot\in\tau\}
be the set of complete trajectories (in
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
) terminating in
x
. The condition in
Equation

3.3.3
then reads

(3.3.4)

F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)=F^{\star}_{s}(x\rightarrow\bot)=F^{\star}_{s}(A_{x\mid s})=\sum_{\tau\in A_{x\mid s}}F^{\star}_{s}(\tau).

Note that in
Equation

3.3.4
,
F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)
is a given quantity because the flow
F^{\star}
is known. Since
{\mathcal{T}}_{s}=\bigcup_{x:s\leq x}A_{x\mid s}
forms a partition of all the complete trajectories
{\mathcal{T}}_{s}
,
Equation

3.3.4
is a system of linear equations, with as many equations as there are terminating states accessible from
s
, and whose unknowns are
F^{\star}_{s}(\tau)
for all
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{s}
, where each equation involves separate sets of unknowns. Therefore, there exists at least a solution
F^{\star}_{s}(\tau)
of this linear system.

We can construct such a solution explicitly in the following way. For some
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{s}
, we can start by selecting the complete trajectories
\bar{\tau}\in{\mathcal{T}}
in the original pointed DAG that contain
\tau
:
C_{\tau}\triangleq\{\bar{\tau}\in{\mathcal{T}}\mid\tau\subseteq\bar{\tau}\}
. The key difference between the pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
and the subgraph
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
though is that
{\mathcal{G}}
may contain trajectories that terminate in some
x\geq s
, but do not pass though
s
, and those are therefore not covered by the trajectories of
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
(see
Figure

3.7(b)
for an illustration). Let
U_{x\mid s}
be the set of complete trajectories of
{\mathcal{G}}
handling this case

(3.3.5)

U_{x\mid s}\triangleq\{\bar{\tau}\in{\mathcal{T}}\mid x\in\bar{\tau}\mathchar 59\relax s\notin\bar{\tau}\}.

For all
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{s}
such that
\tau
terminates in some
x\geq s
, we can therefore construct the trajectory flow
F^{\star}_{s}(\tau)
as

(3.3.6)

F^{\star}_{s}(\tau)\triangleq F^{\star}(C_{\tau})+\frac{1}{|A_{x\mid s}|}F^{\star}(U_{x\mid s})\mathchar 59\relax

where all the quantities are well defined, since the sets
C_{\tau}
and
U_{x\mid s}
are subsets of
{\mathcal{T}}
, and therefore have valid flows
F^{\star}
. It is easy to show that
F^{\star}_{s}(\tau)
defined in
Equation

3.3.6
is a solution of
Equation

3.3.4
.
∎

The construction above shows that the state-conditional flow network associated with some flow network
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
is not unique, since we could have redistributed the flow
F^{\star}(U_{x\mid s})
going to
x
but not through
s
in any way among the trajectories in
A_{x\mid s}
. Going back to the motivation of interpreting state flows as marginalizing the edge flows at the terminating transitions, we can now leverage the marginalization property at the initial state of each
({\mathcal{G}}_{s}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star}_{s})
.

Proposition 3.3.5

(Marginalization)
.

Let
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
be a flow network, with
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
, and let
{\mathcal{X}}
be the set of terminating states of
{\mathcal{G}}
. Let
\{({\mathcal{G}}_{s}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star}_{s})\}_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}
be a corresponding state-conditional flow network. The state flow at the initial state satisfies the following marginalization property

(3.3.7)

F_{s}^{\star}(s_{0}\mid s)=F_{s}^{\star}(s)=\sum_{x:s\leq x}F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot).

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Proposition

3.1.4
and
Proposition

3.1.8
,

(3.3.8)

F^{\star}_{s}(s_{0}\mid s)=\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{s}}F^{\star}_{s}(\tau)=\sum_{x:s\leq x}F^{\star}_{s}(x\rightarrow\bot)=\sum_{x:s\leq x}F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)\mathchar 59\relax

where we used
Equation

3.3.3
, and
{\mathcal{T}}_{s}
is the set of complete trajectories in
{\mathcal{G}}_{s}
.
∎

This suggests a way to estimate the marginal
Equation

3.3.7
over the terminating states accessible from
s
: for each
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, find a flow
F^{\star}_{s}
using one of the flow matching conditions in
Section

3.2
, under the constraint
Equation

3.3.3
. For example, if we were to apply the
flow matching condition
of
Theorem

3.2.1
, this amounts to finding a function
F:{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
that satisfies for all
\bar{s}\in{\mathcal{S}}
(anchor state) and for all
s^{\prime}>\bar{s}
:

(3.3.9)

\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(\bar{s}\mathchar 59\relax s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(\bar{s}\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

under the constraint
F(\bar{s}\mathchar 59\relax x\rightarrow\bot)=F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)
for any terminating state
x\geq\bar{s}
accessible from
\bar{s}
. This kind of
boundary condition
on the edge flow of the terminating transitions will play a central role in the next chapter, where flow networks will be used to model distributions over
{\mathcal{X}}
.

Finally, flow networks may also benefit from other marginalization properties, depending on the structure of the pointed DAG itself. For example, if we consider the pointed DAG of
Figure

2.4
(inference over discrete factor graphs), then stopping the generation after one step effectively corresponds to marginalizing over the values of
X_{2}
&
X_{3}
. We will see another example of this in
Section

8.3.3
. This is all the more powerful when the variables of a joint distribution represented by the flow network can be assigned in different orders
(
Bengio et al. 2023
, Section 5.3)
.

Chapter 4
Generative Flow Networks

This chapter contains material from the following papers:

•

Yoshua Bengio
∗
, Salem Lahlou
∗
,
Tristan Deleu
∗
, Edward Hu, Mo Tiwari, Emmanuel Bengio (2023).
GFlowNet Foundations
. Journal of Machine Learning Research (JMLR).
\notecite
bengio2023gflownetfoundations

•

Nikolay Malkin
∗
, Salem Lahlou
∗
,
Tristan Deleu
∗
, Xu Ji, Edward Hu, Katie Everett, Dinghuai Zhang, Yoshua Bengio (2023).
GFlowNets and Variational Inference
. International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
\notecite
malkin2023gfnhvi

In the previous chapter, we saw how flow networks were defined and some of their properties. In particular, we saw in
Section

3.1.3
that a flow network
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
induces a distribution over terminating states by following its corresponding forward transition probability distribution
P_{F}^{\star}
. Going back to our original problem introduced in
Chapter

2
, we are more concerned with the “inverse problem”: if we know the distribution (not the flow) up to an intractable normalization constant, can we sample from it by following the forward transition probability of a certain flow network? In this chapter, we will see how we can find a flow
F^{\star}
whose corresponding terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\star\top}
matches the Gibbs distribution in
Equation

2.1.1
. Unlike
Section

2.3
though, where we viewed this problem as a control problem over a very specific MDP, this strategy will be valid for any arbitrary pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
and not limited to tree structures.

4.1.
Discrete probabilistic modeling with flow networks

Recall that we are interested in the broad problem of sampling from a target probability distribution
P^{\star}
defined over a discrete (and often compositional) sample space
{\mathcal{X}}
, known only up to a normalization constant. This distribution may be defined based on some
reward function

R:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, such that for any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}

(4.1.1)

P^{\star}(x)=\frac{R(x)}{Z}\mathchar 59\relax

where
Z=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}R(x)
is again the partition function. This is a general formulation for distributions defined up to some normalization constant;
e.g.
, in the case of a Gibbs distribution
Equation

2.1.1
, we have
R(x)=\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x))
. We use the term “reward” to denote the function
R
by analogy with reinforcement learning
(
Bengio et al. 2021
)
. Intuitively, the reward function gives a notion of “preference” for some elements over others: objects with higher rewards are sampled more frequently than those with lower reward. We will come back to the connections existing between flow networks and reinforcement learning in the next chapter.

The idea behind using flow networks to represent the distribution in
Equation

4.1.1
is to construct a flow network
({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax F^{\star})
such that (1) the sample space
{\mathcal{X}}
of
P^{\star}
is the set of terminating states of
{\mathcal{G}}
, and (2) the terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\star\top}
associated with the flow
F^{\star}
matches
P^{\star}
. This would provide a natural way to sample from
P^{\star}
, despite the unknown normalization constant
Z
, via
Algorithm

2
: start from the initial state
s_{0}
, sample consecutive states using
P_{F}^{\star}
until we reach the terminal state
\bot
, and return the last state visited right before terminating. In
Proposition

3.1.8
, we saw that the terminating state distribution associated with a flow is proportional to its edge flows at the terminating transitions
F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)
. Thus in order to match
Equation

4.1.1
, this motivates some additional constraints the flow
F^{\star}
must satisfy, beyond the flow matching conditions alone: a function
F:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is said to satisfy the
boundary conditions
with the reward function
R
if for any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, we have

(4.1.2)

F(x\rightarrow\bot)=R(x).

In the context of differential equations, this type of boundary conditions is evocative of
Neumann boundary conditions

(
Cheng & Cheng 2005
)
, where the normal derivative of the solution of a partial differential equation must match a predefined function at the boundary of its domain; here,
F(x\rightarrow\bot)
plays the role of the normal derivative at the boundary
{\mathcal{X}}
of the state space (“going out” of
{\mathcal{X}}
). Using a flow network as a generative model is called a
generative flow network
(
GFlowNet
, or sometimes
GFN
;
Bengio et al. 2021
;
Bengio et al. 2023
).

(a)

Flow network

(b)

Generative flow network

Figure 4.1
.
Difference between a flow network and a generative flow network. (a) A flow network has a flow which is completely unrestricted, which only needs to satisfy some flow matching conditions. (b) A generative flow network (GFlowNet) is a flow network satisfying some boundary conditions
Equation

4.1.2
, where the flow at terminating edges must equal the reward at the terminating state.

4.1.1.
Flow matching condition under boundary constraints

In
Section

3.2
, we presented multiple ways to characterize
an arbitrary
Markovian flow
F^{\star}
by following some conservation law. Based on our discussion above, the only ingredient necessary to eventually sample from
Equation

4.1.1
is to find not just any flow, but a flow that also satisfies the boundary condition
Equation

4.1.2
. The following theorem combines together the flow matching condition of
Theorem

3.2.1
with the boundary condition in order to guarantee that the corresponding terminating state distribution is proportional to the reward function. This is largely considered as
the
fundamental theorem of generative flow networks
(
Bengio et al. 2021
)
.

Theorem 4.1.1

(Flow matching condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG, where
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
is the set of terminating states of
{\mathcal{G}}
, and let
R:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be a reward function defined over the terminating states. If a function
F:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
satisfies the
flow matching condition
for all
s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}

(4.1.3)

\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

and satisfies the boundary condition
F(x\rightarrow\bot)=R(x)
for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, then the terminating state probability distribution associated with the forward transition probability
P_{F}
defined, for
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
, by

(4.1.4)

P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=\frac{F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})}

is proportional to the reward:
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\propto R(x)
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Theorem

3.2.1
and
Proposition

3.1.8
. Since
F
satisfies the flow matching condition, then by
Theorem

3.2.1
there exists a unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
whose edge flow matches
F
. Moreover, this also shows that
P_{F}
defined in
Equation

4.1.4
matches the forward transition probability of
F^{\star}
(by definition of
P_{F}^{\star}
). By
Proposition

3.1.8
, the terminating state probability distribution associated with
P_{F}^{\star}=P_{F}
is proportional to
F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)=F(x\rightarrow\bot)
. Using the boundary condition
F(x\rightarrow\bot)=R(x)
, we can conclude that
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\propto R(x)
.
∎

As an alternative way to write the flow matching condition together with the boundary conditions,
Bengio et al. 2021
in their original formulation incorporated the reward directly into
Equation

4.1.3
as

(4.1.5)

\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})-\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\underline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})=R(s^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

using the convention
R(s^{\prime})=0
for any intermediate state
s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}\backslash{\mathcal{X}}
, and where
\underline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})=\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})\backslash\{\bot\}
are all the children of
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
except the terminal state. Putting
Equation

4.1.5
in words, this means that the total amount of flow going into any state
s^{\prime}
has to be equal to the total amount of flow going out, plus some residual
R(s^{\prime})
.

Solution of a linear system

When the reward function
R
is known, one way to interpret the constraint
Equation

4.1.5
that the (non-negative) function
F
must satisfy is as a system of
p\triangleq|{\mathcal{S}}|-1
linear equations (one for each state
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
). This system has the form
{\bm{A}}F={\bm{b}}
, where
F
can be viewed as a vector of
|{\mathcal{A}}|
unknowns (one for each edge of
{\mathcal{G}}
),
{\bm{A}}
is a sparse matrix with entries in
\{-1\mathchar 59\relax 0\mathchar 59\relax+1\}
, and
{\bm{b}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is a vector that depends on
R
, with exactly
|{\mathcal{X}}|
non-zero entries. Treating
F
as a vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{A}}|}
is called a
tabular representation
of the flow, once again by analogy with the reinforcement learning literature
(
Sutton & Barto 2018
)
. In addition to this linear constraint, the vector
F\geq 0
is also required to be non-negative. Without any assumption on the structure of the problem though (the sparsity pattern of
{\bm{A}}
), there is no guarantee that this underdetermined system admits a solution at all, let alone a non-negative one. Fortunately, since
{\mathcal{G}}
is a pointed DAG, the following proposition shows the existence of a solution to this problem, regardless of the reward function
R
(provided it is non-negative).

Proposition 4.1.2

(Existence of a flow)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG, where
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
is the set of terminating states of
{\mathcal{G}}
, and let
R:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be a reward function defined over the terminating states. There exists a function
F:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
satisfying the flow matching condition and boundary conditions in
Theorem

4.1.1
(or, alternatively
Equation

4.1.5
).

Proof.

We can prove the existence of a flow by constructing it recursively. Let
\sigma
be a topological ordering of the states in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\top}
, the transpose of
{\mathcal{G}}
(
i.e.
, the pointed DAG constructed from
{\mathcal{G}}
