by reversing all of its edges:
s^{\prime}\rightarrow s\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\top}
if and only if
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
); such a topological ordering exists thanks to the acyclicity of
{\mathcal{G}}
. We first initialize the values of the flows at the terminating transitions of the form
x\rightarrow\bot
to satisfy the boundary conditions:
F(x\rightarrow\bot)=R(x)
. Then the values of the flows at the other edges are determined by following the ordering of states
s^{\prime}\in\sigma
: for any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
, we define the flow
F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
as

(4.1.6)

F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})|}\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime}).

This is well defined because all the flows of the form
F(s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})
have already been determined, since any child
s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})
appears before
s^{\prime}
in the ordering
\sigma
. It is clear that the function
F
defined recursively by
Equation

4.1.6
also satisfies the flow matching condition (in addition to the boundary conditions), and is non-negative.
∎

While a solution of
Equation

4.1.5
exists, it is important to note that it is not unique, and the construction in
Equation

4.1.6
was arbitrary (assigning the total amount of outgoing flow at
s^{\prime}
equally among its incoming edges). Nevertheless, any of these solutions will still induce the same terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\propto R(x)
, for
P_{F}
the corresponding forward transition probability defined in
Equation

4.1.4
.

Relation to max-flow problems

We saw in
Section

3.1.4
that Markovian flows have a natural connection with (standard) flow networks in graph theory, where each edge has infinite capacity:
F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})\leq+\infty
. When introducing the boundary conditions of
Equation

4.1.2
, this creates new capacity constraints, where
R(x)
now determines the capacity of the flow through the terminating transitions of the form
x\rightarrow\bot
:
F(x\rightarrow\bot)\leq R(x)
. Using this perspective, we can see that finding a flow satisfying
Equation

4.1.5
is equivalent to finding a maximum flow (in a graph theoretical sense), and that any solution flow necessarily saturates the capacity for all the terminating transitions (
i.e.
, it satisfies the boundary conditions). To obtain a flow satisfying
Theorem

4.1.1
, we can therefore use any algorithm for finding a max-flow, such as the
Ford-Fulkerson algorithm

(
Ford & Fulkerson 1956
)
, or the
push-relabel algorithm

(
Goldberg & Tarjan 1988
)
that can find a solution in time
O(|{\mathcal{S}}|^{3})
. Alternatively, there exists a dynamic programming algorithm capable of finding a solution in
O(|{\mathcal{A}}|)

(
Lahlou 2024
)
. In many cases though, the state space will be so large that these approaches become impractical. We will see in
Section

4.2
how this flow can be found approximately by
learning
it with gradient methods (via amortization): from visiting a subset of possible transitions and terminating states, one can generalize more generally to other states, thanks to the generalization capabilities of neural networks.

4.1.2.
Flows over cyclic graphs

Generative flow networks adopt a purely
constructive
approach, where pieces are only allowed to be added to ensure the acyclicity of the state space, and are never removed. Otherwise, if removal steps were also allowed, this would have the risk of introducing cycles in
{\mathcal{G}}
, where some piece may be removed and added back to reach the same state. To handle both the addition and removal of pieces, similar to MCMC
(
Madigan & York 1995
)
, a natural solution is to augment the states with a “timestamp”
t
that increases at each step of the generation. Concretely, this means that the state space of this “augmented” generative flow network is
{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathbb{N}}
, with transitions
(s\mathchar 59\relax t)\rightarrow(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax t+1)
for all
t\in{\mathbb{N}}
if and only if
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
is a transition in the original state graph
{\mathcal{G}}
(not necessarily acyclic). Although this ensures that this new flow network is acyclic, this is now breaking the assumption that the state space should be finite; we will see in
Section

6.1
that under some conditions GFlowNets can also be extended to infinite state spaces.

Another way of accommodating for cycles in the state graph is to find a
minimal flow
that satisfies the conditions of
Theorem

4.1.1
.

Proposition 4.1.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be an arbitrary directed graph, where
\bot\notin{\mathcal{S}}
is a terminal state that has no child in
{\mathcal{G}}
,
{\mathcal{X}}=\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(\bot)
be the set of terminating states of
{\mathcal{G}}
, and let
R:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be a reward function defined over the terminating states. Let
F:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be a function over the edges of
{\mathcal{G}}
solution of the following constrained optimization problem:

(4.1.7)

\displaystyle\min_{F\geq 0}

\displaystyle\sum_{s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}}F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.t.}

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})-\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\underline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})=R(s^{\prime})\qquad

\displaystyle\forall s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}

using the convention
R(s^{\prime})=0
for any non-terminating state
s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}\backslash{\mathcal{X}}
. Then the terminating state probability distribution associated with the forward transition probability
P_{F}
defined in
Equation

4.1.4
is proportional to the reward:
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\propto R(x)
.

Proof.

Although we can’t directly apply
Proposition

4.1.2
since
{\mathcal{G}}
is not necessarily acyclic anymore, by adopting the “max-flow” perspective mentioned in
Section

4.1.1
, the problem
Equation

4.1.7
admits a solution (the constraint set is not empty), possibly infinite. We will show that if
F
is a solution of the problem, then it has no “positive cycle”,
i.e.
, a cycle where the flow is positive on each of its edges.

Suppose that
F
is a solution of
Equation

4.1.7
, and that there exists a cycle
\gamma=(s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax s_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1})
such that
F(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})>0
(with the convention
s_{T+1}=s_{1}
). We can build a new function
F^{\prime}
from
F
as

(4.1.8)

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})

\displaystyle=F(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})-\varepsilon

if
s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1}\in\gamma

(4.1.9)

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\textrm{otherwise}\mathchar 59\relax

where
\varepsilon>0
is chosen such that
F^{\prime}
stays non-negative. It is easy to see that
F^{\prime}
satisfies the flow matching condition (constraint of
Equation

4.1.7
): for any state along
\gamma
, we removed the same amount
\varepsilon
from its incoming and outgoing flow
F
that was already satisfying the flow matching condition. However, the sum of flows of
F^{\prime}
is strictly smaller than the one of
F

(4.1.10)

\sum_{s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}}F^{\prime}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=\sum_{s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}}F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})-T\varepsilon<\sum_{s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}}F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

which contradicts the fact that
F
was a minimizer of
Equation

4.1.7
. Since a solution
F
of this problem has no positive cycle, we can construct a pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
from the directed graph
{\mathcal{G}}
by only extracting the edges that have positive flow:
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
iff
F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})>0
. In particular, each terminating transition of the form
x\rightarrow\bot
is kept in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
, provided its corresponding reward is positive (this is not limiting, since otherwise it wouldn’t contribute to the terminating state probability anyway). Therefore, we have a function
F
that satisfies the flow matching condition, as well as the boundary condition for the pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
, since it satisfies the constraints in
Equation

4.1.7
. By
Theorem

4.1.1
, this shows that the terminating state probability distribution associated with
P_{F}
defined by
Equation

4.1.4
is proportional to the reward:
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\propto R(x)
.
∎

These considerations are only necessary when we have little control over the way the state graph
{\mathcal{G}}
is defined (
e.g.
, black-box simulations). However in many practical cases, we are free to choose how the state space is structured (similar to how one can choose a transition kernel in MCMC), and therefore it is recommended to incorporate the acyclicity of
{\mathcal{G}}
in our design choices directly to avoid any additional complexity. All the GFlowNets introduced in this thesis will be guaranteed to have a DAG structure.

4.1.3.
Alternative conditions

The flow matching condition of
Theorem

3.2.1
is only one of many characterizations of a Markovian flow, as we saw throughout
Section

3.2
. We can obtain similar results, based on any of the flow matching conditions we presented in the previous chapter. For example, we can combine the detailed balance condition of
Theorem

3.2.2
with the boundary condition. Note that since the detailed balance condition does not depend directly on the edge flow function
F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
but on a state flow function
F(s)
and transition probabilities, the boundary condition needs to be slightly adapted to only depend on
F(x)
and
P_{F}(\bot\mid x)
(instead of
F(x\rightarrow\bot)
directly).

Theorem 4.1.4

(Detailed balance condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG, where
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
is the set of terminating states of
{\mathcal{G}}
, and let
R:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be a reward function defined over the terminating states. If the functions
F:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
P_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
, and
P_{B}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
satisfy the
detailed balance condition
for all transitions
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot

(4.1.11)

F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=F(s^{\prime})P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

and satisfies the boundary conditions
F(x)P_{F}(\bot\mid x)=R(x)
for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, then the terminating state distribution associated with
P_{F}
is proportional to the reward:
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\propto R(x)
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Theorem

3.2.2
and
Proposition

3.1.8
. Since
F
,
P_{F}
and
P_{B}
satisfy the detailed balance condition, then by
Theorem

3.2.2
there exists a unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
whose state flow matches
F
, and whose forward transition probability
P^{\star}_{F}
matches
P_{F}
. By definition of
P^{\star}_{F}
in
Definition

3.1.7
, we know that for any terminating state
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)=F^{\star}(x)P^{\star}_{F}(\bot\mid x)=F(x)P_{F}(\bot\mid x)
. By
Proposition

3.1.8
, the terminating state probability distribution associated with
P^{\star}_{F}=P_{F}
is proportional to
F^{\star}(x\rightarrow\bot)=F(x)P_{F}(\bot\mid x)
. Using the boundary condition
F(x)P_{F}(\bot\mid x)=R(x)
, we can conclude that
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\propto R(x)
.
∎

We can do the same exercise for the trajectory balance condition of
Theorem

3.2.3
. One advantage of the trajectory balance condition is that the flow at terminating edges (which appears in the boundary condition) is already part of its parametrization. We can therefore plug the reward function directly into the condition without the need for an additional explicit boundary condition, unlike for detailed balance and flow matching (although we saw in
Equation

4.1.5
that the reward function can be integrated inside the flow matching condition). As a consequence, the trajectory balance condition is considered the most popular in the GFlowNet literature.

Theorem 4.1.5

(Trajectory balance condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG, where
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
is the set of terminating states of
{\mathcal{G}}
, and let
R:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be a reward function defined over the terminating states. If
Z>0
, and the functions
P_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
, and
P_{B}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
satisfy the
trajectory balance condition
for all complete trajectories
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)\in{\mathcal{T}}

(4.1.12)

Z\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=R(s_{T})\prod_{t=1}^{T}P_{B}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})\mathchar 59\relax

with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
, then the terminating state distribution associated with
P_{F}
is proportional to the reward:
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\propto R(x)
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Theorem

3.2.3
and
Proposition

3.1.8
. Since
Z
,
P_{F}
, and
P_{B}
(and
F_{\bot}\triangleq R
) satisfy the flow matching condition, then by
Theorem

3.2.3
there exists a unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
whose edge flow at the terminating transitions matches
R
, and whose forward transition probability
P^{\star}_{F}
matches
P_{F}
. By
Proposition

3.1.8
, the terminating state probability distribution associated with
P_{F}^{\star}=P_{F}
is proportional to the reward:
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\propto R(x)
.
∎

We do not include a similar theorem based on the sub-trajectory balance condition of
Theorem

3.2.4
though, because we saw that in general this was not a sufficient condition for a Markovian flow.

4.1.4.
Case study: Bayesian inference over decision trees

In this section, we will see an example of application of GFlowNets applied to Bayesian inference. Consider a dataset
{\mathcal{D}}=\{({\bm{x}}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax y_{1})\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax({\bm{x}}_{N}\mathchar 59\relax y_{N})\}
of inputs/outputs, where each
{\bm{x}}\in\{0\mathchar 59\relax 1\}^{p}
is a vector of
p
binary features, and
y\in\{0\mathchar 59\relax 1\}
is also a binary label. A natural choice for tabular data like this is to use
decision trees
in order to model the underlying system of interest. Instead of returning a single (weak) classifier though, most popular approaches use an ensemble of trees like a random forest
(
Breiman 2001
)
, in order to increase the expressive power of the model. Alternatively, taking a Bayesian perspective from
Section

1.3.1
, we could consider
all
the possible decision trees one could build over
{\mathcal{D}}
(
e.g.
, up to a certain maximum depth
d
), and reweight them using the posterior distribution over trees
P(T\mid{\mathcal{D}})
when making our predictions.

Because the number of such decision trees is combinatorially large (in both
p
and
d
), characterizing the whole posterior distribution can be very challenging, due to the intractable normalization constant (evidence)

(4.1.13)

P(T\mid{\mathcal{D}})=\frac{P({\mathcal{D}}\mid T)P(T)}{P({\mathcal{D}})}=\frac{P({\mathcal{D}}\mid T)P(T)}{\sum_{T^{\prime}}P({\mathcal{D}}\mid T^{\prime})P(T^{\prime})}.

However, since decision trees (or at least their structure) are discrete and compositional objects, and the distribution is defined up to a normalization constant, we could instead model it using a generative flow network. Each state of this flow network would correspond to the structure of a decision tree with maximum depth
d
. The states are organized into a pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
, where the initial state
T_{0}
corresponds to a “tree” with a single node (leaf) containing all the data in
{\mathcal{D}}
, and where a transition
T\rightarrow T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
corresponds to
expanding
a leaf of
T
in order to obtain
T^{\prime}
.

Figure 4.2
.
Bayesian inference of decision tree structures with a generative flow network. This is an illustration of the pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
, each state being the structure of a decision tree. The shading at the leaves indicates the label proportions. A transition
T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
corresponds to selecting a leaf and a variable and expanding the tree. The state in red is invalid since the same decision variable is used twice on the same path to a leaf node; it will not be included in the structure of
{\mathcal{G}}

Concretely, transitioning to a new graph requires selecting which leaf to expand, and which variable
\{x_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax x_{p}\}
to use for the new decision node. But these transitions are also subject to additional constraints: (1) a leaf which is already at the maximum length cannot be further expanded, (2) the variable for the new decision node must be distinct from its ancestors in the tree (
i.e.
, we can’t split on the same variable multiple times), and (3) we can’t have “trivial” splits, where no example of
{\mathcal{D}}
ends up in one of the leaves, meaning that there is no unnecessary decision node. All of these rules can be easily verified, and they shape the structure of
{\mathcal{G}}
; see
Figure

4.2
for an illustration. Interestingly, all the states here are terminating (
{\mathcal{X}}\equiv{\mathcal{S}}
), since they all correspond to valid samples of the distribution
Equation

4.1.13
(valid decision trees); we will see in
Section

7.2.4
that this has some consequences in terms of the flow matching conditions that can be applied. Unlike in
Section

2.3
, we are making full use of the DAG assumption for
{\mathcal{G}}
here, since there may be multiple ways of constructing the same decision tree, depending on the order in which decision nodes are added.

Given
Equation

4.1.13
and the fact that generative flow networks are used to model distributions up to normalization, we can clearly choose
R(T)=P({\mathcal{D}}\mid T)P(T)
as our reward function, which we assume can be computed analytically for any decision tree
T
. On the one hand, the prior
P(T)
can be used for example to penalize more complex decision trees;
e.g.
, we can set
P(T)\propto\exp(-\beta|T|)
, where
|T|
is the size of
T
, to mimic the
Akaike information criterion
(AIC) frequently used in model selection
(
Akaike 1974
)
. The marginal likelihood
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid T)
, on the other hand, can be decomposed into pieces following the partition of
{\mathcal{D}}
obtained with the decision tree

\displaystyle\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid T)

\displaystyle=\log\left[\int_{\theta}P({\mathcal{D}}\mid T\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P(\theta\mid T)d\theta\right]=\log\left[\int_{\theta}\left(\prod_{n=1}^{N}P(y_{n}\mid{\bm{x}}_{n}\mathchar 59\relax T\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\right)P(\theta\mid T)d\theta\right]

(4.1.14)

\displaystyle=\log\Bigg[\int_{\theta}\prod_{l\in T}\Bigg(P(\theta_{l})\prod_{n:{\bm{x}}_{n}\in l}P(y_{n}\mid\theta_{l})\Bigg)d\theta\Bigg]

(4.1.15)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in T}\log\Bigg[\int_{\theta_{l}}\Bigg(\prod_{n:{\bm{x}}_{n}\in l}P(y_{n}\mid\theta_{l})\Bigg)P(\theta_{l})d\theta_{l}\Bigg]\mathchar 59\relax

where the outer sum is over the leaves
l
of the decision tree, the decisions at each leaf depending on some parameters
\theta_{l}
that need to be integrated over, based on the subset of
{\mathcal{D}}
consistent with the leaf
l
. These integrals can be computed in closed form if we assume that the parameters
\theta_{l}
have a Beta prior (
i.e.
, a Beta-Binomial model).

Since the normalization constant
P({\mathcal{D}})
is rarely tractable, GFlowNets are particularly appropriate for Bayesian inference in general, and especially in Bayesian model averaging
(
Madigan & Raftery 1994
)
since these models are typically discrete objects. This formulation based on a pointed DAG over states allows us to have maximum flexibility, the extreme case being where
{\mathcal{G}}
is a rooted tree of depth 1, where the initial state (empty state) is connected to all possible outcomes of the posterior distribution (
e.g.
, the different models). However, flow networks become exponentially more effective when these objects exhibit some compositional structure, just like with decision trees, trading off the generation of an object in multiple steps for a much smaller branching factor (the components to add) in
{\mathcal{G}}
. We will see in
Chapter

7
and onward how GFlowNets can be used to model the posterior distribution over the DAG structure of a Bayesian network.

4.2.
Learning a generative flow network

Up to this point, we assumed that the flow
F
, or any quantity parametrizing the generative flow network, were simply
functions
that satisfied some conditions. Taking the example of the flow matching condition in
Section

4.1.1
, having a “tabular representation” of this flow function would then require specifying the value of
F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
for each possible transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
, which can be prohibitively large in practice. Instead, we will often consider a parametric family of functions
F_{\phi}
to
amortize
the cost of finding a flow (as in
Section

1.2.2
), and find parameters
\phi
that approximately satisfy the conditions of
Theorem

4.1.1
. This is a
variational approach
, where inference over the unknown target distribution
P^{\star}
is viewed as an optimization problem; we will come back to the links between GFlowNets & variational inference in details in
Section

4.3
.

4.2.1.
Flow matching losses

In order to find these optimal parameters that approximately satisfy the conditions given in
Section

4.1
, we can convert any of those flow matching conditions into a (non-linear) least-square objective of the form

(4.2.1)

{\mathcal{L}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\big[\Delta^{2}(\cdot;\phi)\big]\mathchar 59\relax

where
\pi_{b}
is an arbitrary distribution over appropriate quantities (
i.e.
, states, transitions, or complete trajectories) with full support, meaning that it assigns a non-zero probability to all elements, and
\Delta(\cdot;\phi)
are
residuals
that quantify the “mismatch” in the flow matching (and boundary) conditions. By analogy with flow matching conditions, we will call this a
flow matching loss
(associated with
\Delta(\cdot;\phi)
). For example, taking inspiration from the formulation in
Equation

4.1.5
, the residual associated with the flow matching condition of
Theorem

4.1.1
can be written as

(4.2.2)

\Delta_{\mathrm{FM}}(s^{\prime};\phi)=\log\frac{\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F_{\phi}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\underline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F_{\phi}(s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})+R(s^{\prime})}.

In that case,
\pi_{b}
is a distribution over states
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
. The choice of working in logarithmic space is intuitive: since
F_{\phi}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
would ideally represent a flow, flows closer to the initial state will naturally have a significantly larger magnitude than those closer to the terminal state, since they would have to accumulate downstream flows (because of the flow matching conditions). Even for upstream and downstream flows at the level of a state
s^{\prime}
, these changes in orders of magnitudes are better controlled numerically in logarithmic space. It can easily be shown that if the flow matching loss associated with
\Delta_{\mathrm{FM}}(s^{\prime};\phi)
is minimized, then the corresponding flow satisfies the conditions of
Theorem

4.1.1
, thanks to the full support of
\pi_{b}
, provided that the flow satisfying these conditions can be represented by
F_{\phi}
(
i.e.
, the minimum flow matching loss is zero).

Proposition 4.2.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG, and let a family of functions
F_{\phi}:{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
parametrized by
\phi
. Let
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{FM}}(\phi)
be the objective in
Equation

4.2.1
using the flow matching residual
\Delta_{\mathrm{FM}}(s^{\prime};\phi)
defined in
Equation

4.2.2
. There exists
\phi^{\star}
such that
F_{\phi^{\star}}
satisfies the flow matching & boundary conditions of
Theorem

4.1.1
if and only if
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{FM}}(\phi^{\star})=0
.

Proof.

Clearly if
F_{\phi^{\star}}
satisfies the conditions of
Theorem

4.1.1
, then the loss will be zero since all the residuals themselves will be zeros. Conversely, if
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{FM}}(\phi^{\star})=0
, we can write it more explicitly as

(4.2.3)

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{FM}}(\phi^{\star})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}}\pi_{b}(s^{\prime})\Delta_{\mathrm{FM}}^{2}(s^{\prime};\phi^{\star})=0.

Since we assumed that
\pi_{b}
has full support, we know that for all
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
,
\pi_{b}(s^{\prime})>0
. Moreover since
\Delta_{\mathrm{FM}}^{2}(s^{\prime};\phi^{\star})\geq 0
, this implies that the flow matching residuals are all zeros, and therefore
Equation

4.1.5
is satisfied for all
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
.
∎

The key property we used was that
\pi_{b}
had full support. Indeed if there was a state for which
\pi_{b}(s)=0
, then
\Delta_{\mathrm{FM}}(s;\phi)
could be completely arbitrary while still minimizing
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{FM}}
. We can also define flow matching losses based on other conditions, such as the detailed balance loss based on
Theorem

4.1.4
, with the following residual defined over transitions
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
:

(4.2.4)

\Delta_{\mathrm{DB}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)=\log\frac{F_{\phi}(s)P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)}{F_{\phi}(s^{\prime})P_{B}^{\phi}(s\mid s^{\prime})}.

Unlike the flow matching loss above, this residual relies on a family of state flows
F_{\phi}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, forward transitions probabilities
P_{F}^{\phi}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
, and backward transition probabilities
P_{B}^{\phi}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
; we assume for clarity that
\phi
encapsulates the parameters of all these functions. Another major difference with the flow matching residual in
Equation

4.2.2
is that this covers the detailed balance condition, but not the boundary conditions which depend on the reward function. To address this, we need to define an additional residual for the boundary conditions at terminating transitions
x\rightarrow\bot
, with
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
:

(4.2.5)

\Delta_{\mathrm{DB}}(x\rightarrow\bot;\phi)=\log\frac{F_{\phi}(x)P_{F}^{\phi}(\bot\mid x)}{R(x)}.

This type of residual is also called a
reward matching loss

(
Bengio et al. 2023
)
. For the detailed balance loss,
\pi_{b}
is a distribution with full support over all the transitions of
{\mathcal{G}}
, including the terminating transitions. Similarly, we also have the trajectory balance loss based on
Theorem

4.1.5
, with the following residual defined over complete trajectories
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
:

(4.2.6)

\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)=\log\frac{Z_{\phi}\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})}{R(s_{T})\prod_{t=1}^{T}P_{B}^{\phi}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})}.

In addition to a parametrization of the forward and backward transition probabilities, like in the detailed balance loss, the trajectory balance loss also uses an additional learned scalar
Z_{\phi}>0
to represent the total flow. For the trajectory balance loss,
\pi_{b}
is a distribution with full support over complete trajectories. We present a simplified training loop to learn a GFlowNet based on the trajectory balance loss in
Algorithm

4
. Note that even if working in logarithmic space for the detailed balance and the trajectory balance losses was inherited from the original flow matching loss
Equation

4.2.2
, we will see in
Section

5.2
that it finds a natural interpretation when seeing these from the perspective of maximum entropy RL. Similar to
Proposition

4.2.1
, it can also be shown that minimizing the detailed balance loss satisfies the conditions of
Theorem

4.1.4
, and minimizing the trajectory balance loss satisfies the conditions of
Theorem

4.1.5
, provided their respective quantities can be represented by the family parametrized by
\phi
(
i.e.
, the minimum loss is zero).

Other losses that are not based on a least-square objective have also been introduced in the GFlowNet literature. For example,
Zhang et al. 2023
introduced a
log-partition variance loss

(
Richter et al. 2020
)
that minimizes the variance of an estimate of the log-partition function induced by the trajectory balance condition.

Algorithm 4

Training a generative flow network with the trajectory balance loss
Equation

4.2.6
.

1:

A pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
, a reward function
R:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, a batch size
K
.

2:

Initialize the parameters
\phi
of
P_{F}^{\phi}
,
P_{B}^{\phi}
&
Z_{\phi}

3:

loop

4:

\triangleright

Sample
K
trajectories & get the reward of their terminating states

\triangleleft

5:

{\mathcal{B}}\leftarrow\emptyset

6:

for

k=1\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax K

do

7:

Initialize the state:
s_{0}

8:

repeat

9:

Sample the next state:
s_{t+1}\sim\pi_{b}(\cdot\mid s_{t})

10:

until

s_{T+1}=\bot

11:

{\mathcal{B}}\leftarrow{\mathcal{B}}\cup\{(\tau\mathchar 59\relax R(s_{T}))\}

\triangleright

\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)

12:

Update

13:

\triangleright

Update the parameters with stochastic gradient descent

\triangleleft

14:

Estimate the loss
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{TB}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\big[\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}^{2}(\tau;\phi)\big]
based on
{\mathcal{B}}
(Monte Carlo)

\triangleright

Equation

4.2.6

15:

Update the parameters:
\phi\leftarrow\phi-\beta\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{TB}}(\phi)

16:

return
the forward transition probability
P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)

4.2.2.
Off-policy training

The least-square objective in
Equation

4.2.1
is an expected loss that depends on a distribution
\pi_{b}
with full support. Although we used the same notation to denote distributions over various quantities depending on the type of flow matching loss (
e.g.
,
\pi_{b}
is a distribution over states
s^{\prime}
for the flow matching loss
Equation

4.2.2
, and it is a distribution over complete trajectories
\tau
for the trajectory balance loss
Equation

4.2.6
), they will all typically be derived from a distribution of the form
\pi_{b}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})
consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
; for example to build a distribution
\pi_{b}(\tau)
over complete trajectories thanks to
Lemma

2.2.5
. This is called a
behavior policy
in the reinforcement learning literature
(
Sutton & Barto 2018
)
. The simplest choice for such a behavior policy is to use the current forward transition probability
\pi_{b}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})
(possibly derived from
F_{\phi}
in the case of the flow matching loss, by normalizing the outgoing flows
Equation

4.1.4
); this strategy is called
on-policy
.

The behavior policy can be a priori completely arbitrary though, as long as it has full support. When
\pi_{b}
is different from the target forward transition probability, this is called
off-policy
learning. This approach is usually effective to encourage exploration, which is often essential to discover regions of the state space leading to yet unseen modes of the target distribution. A naive way to incorporate exploration is via the
\varepsilon
-sampling
strategy, where the next state is sampled from
P_{F}^{\phi}
with probability
1-\varepsilon
, and uniformly at random with probability
\varepsilon\in(0\mathchar 59\relax 1]
. The behavior policy can therefore be written as the following mixture model:

(4.2.7)

\pi_{b}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=(1-\varepsilon)P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})+\frac{\varepsilon}{|\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s_{t})|}.

An alternative strategy is to use a
temperature
parameter
\alpha>0
to control how “diffused” the probabilities of reaching the next states are, based on the current
P_{F}^{\phi}
:

(4.2.8)

\pi_{b}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\propto\big[P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\big]^{1/\alpha}.

Although either
\varepsilon
or
\alpha
can be fixed hyperparameters, in practice it is customary to use a schedule to slowly transition from the uniform policy (
\varepsilon=1
, or
\alpha\rightarrow+\infty
) to the forward transition probability
P_{F}^{\phi}
(
\varepsilon\approx 0
, or
\alpha\approx 1
, in order to still keep some exploratory behavior) over the course of training. This is inherited once again from the reinforcement learning literature
(
Mnih et al. 2015
)
.

There also exists more advanced strategies to define the behavior policy.
Rector-Brooks et al. 2023
introduced a strategy based on Thompson sampling
(
Thompson 1933
)
to improve the exploration in GFlowNets by maintaining an approximate posterior over policies.
Morozov et al. 2024
proposed to use Monte Carlo tree search
(
Xiao et al. 2019
)
to improve planning at both training and test time. QGFN
(
Lau et al. 2024
)
used a policy based on Q-values learned by standard reinforcement learning in order to encourage exploration in regions with high reward. Finally, an increasingly popular way to train generative flow networks off-policy is to use reweighted experience stored in a
replay buffer
over the course of training, possibly acquired using an exploration-enhanced behavior policy, in order to compute the loss
(
Deleu et al. 2022
;
Vemgal et al. 2023
)
, as with
prioritized experience replay

(
Schaul et al. 2016
)
..

Differentiation through the behavior policy

We will use gradient based methods in order to optimize the flow matching losses we presented in the previous section (see line 14 of
Algorithm

4
). We can show that the gradient of the objective in
Equation

4.2.1
can also be written as an expectation over the behavior policy
(
Schulman et al. 2015
)

(4.2.9)

\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\Delta^{2}(\cdot;\phi)+\nabla_{\phi}\log\pi_{b}(\cdot)\Delta^{2}(\cdot;\phi)\big]\mathchar 59\relax

where again the quantities the expectation is taken over depend on the specific flow matching loss. When the behavior policy
\pi_{b}
is completely independent of
\phi
, then the second term of this expectation vanishes. However, if
\pi_{b}
does depend on
\phi
, then this term should still appear; this is most obviously the case when we are on-policy, and the behavior policy
\pi_{b}\equiv P_{F}^{\phi}
is the current forward transition probability. But in a subtle way, this would also be the case off-policy when we consider either
\varepsilon
-sampling in
Equation

4.2.7
, or tempering in
Equation

4.2.8
, as they both depend on
\phi
as well. In practice though, we will always ignore the second term of
Equation

4.2.9
, and estimate the gradient
as if
\pi_{b}
was completely independent of
\phi
:
\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}(\phi)\approx\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}[\nabla_{\phi}\Delta^{2}(\cdot;\phi)]
. This does not hinder convergence, and it can be estimated with Monte Carlo based on samples from
\pi_{b}
.

4.2.3.
Convergence guarantees

The approximation of the flow matching conditions (and boundary conditions) inevitably leads to errors, the source of which is typically two-fold: (1) due to the choice of the variational family to parametrize the quantities of interest (
e.g.
, flows, transition probabilities), and (2) due to the finite nature of optimization. Unlike standard methods in variational inference that minimize some divergence between a variational approximation and the target distribution (typically the reverse KL-divergence;
Jordan et al. 1999
), GFlowNets instead minimize some losses that quantify the “mismatch” in the flow matching conditions which, upon being satisfied, ensure that the terminating state probability corresponds to the target distribution. By way of getting this mismatch as small as possible over the course of optimization, we hope that the model will indirectly get closer to the target in some way. However, does minimizing any of the flow matching losses presented in
Section

4.2.1
offer any guarantee that the corresponding terminating state probability distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
approaches the target distribution
P^{\star}
?

Estimation of the partition function

Although convergence guarantees probably exist for other flow matching conditions, throughout this section we will consider the trajectory balance loss
Equation

4.2.6
as a representative example, since it is a popular objective to train generative flow networks. One advantage of this loss is that it provides an approximation of the partition function with the learned scalar
Z_{\phi}
, in addition to an approximate sampler for
P^{\star}
. However, it has been shown empirically that
Z_{\phi}
alone may yield an unreliable estimation of the partition function, which may be detrimental if it is used for model comparison via the Bayes factor
(
Morey et al. 2016
)
. The following proposition offers a more precise relation between the true partition function
Z
, its learned counterpart
Z_{\phi}
, and the residuals
\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)
.

Proposition 4.2.2

(Estimation of the partition function)
.

Let
\phi
be the parameters of the forward transition probabilities
P_{F}^{\phi}
, the backward transition probabilities
P_{B}^{\phi}
, and the total flow
Z_{\phi}
in the trajectory balance loss
Equation

4.2.6
. The log-partition function
\log Z
of the target distribution
Equation

4.1.1
is related to
\log Z_{\phi}
via

(4.2.10)

\log Z=\log Z_{\phi}+\log\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\exp(-\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))\big]\Big).

Proof.

Based on
Equation

4.2.6
, for some complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
, we can find an expression for the product of the reward at the terminating state
s_{T}
and the backward probability of
\tau
as a function of the residual
\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)

(4.2.11)

R(s_{T})P_{B}^{\phi}(\tau\mid s_{T})=R(s_{T})\prod_{t=1}^{T}P_{B}^{\phi}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})=Z_{\phi}\exp(-\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the notation
P_{B}^{\phi}(\tau\mid s_{T})=\prod_{t=1}^{T}P_{B}^{\phi}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})
introduced in
Section

3.1.2
. Since
P_{B}^{\phi}(\cdot\mid x)
is a distribution over the complete trajectories terminating at
x
, the reward
R(x)
is given by

(4.2.12)

R(x)=\sum_{\tau:x\rightarrow\bot\in\tau}R(x)P_{B}^{\phi}(\tau\mid x)=Z_{\phi}\sum_{\tau:x\rightarrow\bot\in\tau}P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\exp(-\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))

By definition of the partition function
Z
, and using the fact that
P_{F}^{\phi}
induces a probability distribution over all the complete trajectories by
Lemma

2.2.5

(4.2.13)

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}R(x)

(4.2.14)

\displaystyle=Z_{\phi}\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}\sum_{\tau:x\rightarrow\bot\in\tau}P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\exp(-\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))

(4.2.15)

\displaystyle=Z_{\phi}\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\exp(-\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))

(4.2.16)

\displaystyle=Z_{\phi}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\exp(-\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))\big].

We can conclude by taking the
\log
of the equality above.
∎

This result is reminiscent of the estimation of the log-marginal likelihood via a variational lower-bound in PhyloGFN
(
Zhou et al. 2024
)
, obtained by applying Jensen’s inequality to
Equation

4.2.10
. Using
Lemma

A.1.1
, this gives us an upper-bound this time on the error between the learned
\log Z_{\phi}
and the true log-partition function
\log Z
that is controlled by the maximal residual over all possible complete trajectories

(4.2.17)

|\log Z_{\phi}-\log Z|\leq\max_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}|\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)|.

Convergence of the terminating state distribution

While
Equation

4.2.17
gives us a guarantee on the convergence of
\log Z_{\phi}
towards the target log-partition function as the trajectory balance loss gets minimized, we would like to also have stronger guarantees on the convergence of the terminating state probability
P_{F}^{\phi\top}(x)
itself towards
P^{\star}(x)
. Using a similar argument, the following proposition shows that the difference between the log-terminating state probability and the target log-probability is directly controlled by the residual
\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)
being minimized. This ensures that as this residual gets smaller, the approximation becomes more accurate at a particular
x
.

Proposition 4.2.3
.

Let
\phi
be the parameters of the forward transition probabilities
P_{F}^{\phi}
, the backward transition probabilities
P_{B}^{\phi}
, and the total flow
Z_{\phi}
in the trajectory balance loss
Equation

4.2.6
. For any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, the error between the log-terminating state probability associated with
P_{F}^{\phi}
and the target log-probability is bounded by

(4.2.18)

|\log P_{F}^{\phi\top}(x)-\log P^{\star}(x)|\leq\max_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}|\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)|+\max_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}|\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)|.

Proof.

We can use
Equation

4.2.6
to find an expression for
P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)
as a function of the residual
\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)
:

(4.2.19)

\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})=\exp(\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))\frac{R(s_{T})}{Z_{\phi}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}P_{B}^{\phi}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t}).

For any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, using the notation
P_{B}^{\phi}(\tau\mid x)=\prod_{t=1}^{T}P_{B}^{\phi}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})
introduced in
Section

3.1.2
(with
s_{T}=x
), which is a properly defined distribution over the complete trajectories terminating in
x
, and by
Definition

2.2.6
of the terminating state probability distribution associated with
P_{F}^{\phi}
, we get

(4.2.20)

\displaystyle P_{F}^{\phi\top}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

(4.2.21)

\displaystyle=\frac{R(x)}{Z_{\phi}}\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\exp(\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))P_{B}^{\phi}(\tau\mid x)

(4.2.22)

\displaystyle=\frac{R(x)}{Z}\frac{Z}{Z_{\phi}}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{B}^{\phi}(\cdot\mid x)}\big[\exp(\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))\big]

(4.2.23)

\displaystyle=P^{\star}(x)\frac{Z}{Z_{\phi}}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{B}^{\phi}(\cdot\mid x)}\big[\exp(\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))\big]

Although the normalization constant
Z
of the target distribution is still unknown, we can fortunately write it as a function of the residual as well thanks to
Proposition

4.2.2
. Combining it with
Equation

4.2.23
, we get an expression of the difference in log-probabilities as a function of
\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)
only, where the expectations over complete trajectories are respectively taken wrt.
P_{F}^{\phi}
and
P_{B}^{\phi}(\cdot\mid x)

(4.2.24)

\log P_{F}^{\phi\top}(x)-\log P^{\star}(x)=\log\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\exp(-\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)\big]\Big)+\log\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{B}^{\phi}(\cdot\mid x)}\big[\exp(\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))\big]\Big).

Using the triangle inequality, we can conclude by applying
Lemma

A.1.1
to both terms of the RHS

(4.2.25)

\displaystyle\left|\log\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\exp(-\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))\big]\Big)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}|\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)|

(4.2.26)

\displaystyle\left|\log\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{B}^{\phi}(\cdot\mid x)}\big[\exp(\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))\big]\Big)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}|\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)|.

∎

As an immediate consequence of this proposition, we can get a simpler (albeit looser, because it becomes independent of the terminating state
x
) bound of the form

(4.2.27)

|\log P_{F}^{\phi\top}(x)-\log P^{\star}(x)|\leq 2\max_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}|\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)|.

The term
\max_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}|\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)|
in the bound of
Proposition

4.2.4
, independent of
x
, has an intuitive interpretation. We could allocate all the training capacity to learn parameters
\phi
so that the trajectory balance conditions match almost perfectly for all the trajectories leading to a certain terminating state
x
only (
e.g.
, using a behavior policy that focuses almost exclusively on those trajectories). In that case, we would have
\max_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}|\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)|\approx 0
. However, that does not mean that we perfectly recovered
\log P^{\star}(x)
, because we have to take into account all the other terminating states as well in the computation of its normalization constant; this is precisely what the term
\max_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}|\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)|
controls for.

Instead of considering what happens at the level of a single
x
, we can also get more global guarantees on the divergence between these two distributions, similar to what typical variational methods would minimize. The following proposition gives a bound on their KL-divergence that still depends exclusively on
\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)
.

Proposition 4.2.4
.

Let
\phi
be the parameters of the forward transition probabilities
P_{F}^{\phi}
, the backward transition probabilities
P_{B}^{\phi}
, and the total flow
Z_{\phi}
in the trajectory balance loss
Equation

4.2.6
. The KL-divergence between the terminating state probability distribution associated with
P_{F}^{\phi}
and the target distribution
P^{\star}
is bounded by

(4.2.28)

\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi\top}(x)\,\|\,P^{\star}(x)\big)\leq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{F}^{\phi\top}}\Big[\max_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)\Big]-\min_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi).

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Equation

4.2.24
in the proof of
Proposition

4.2.3
above:

(4.2.29)

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}

\displaystyle\big(P_{F}^{\phi\top}(x)\,\|\,P^{\star}(x)\big)=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{F}^{\phi\top}}\big[\log P_{F}^{\phi\top}(x)-\log P^{\star}(x)\big]

(4.2.30)

\displaystyle=\log\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\exp(-\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))\big]\Big)+\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{F}^{\phi\top}}\Big[\log\Big(\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{B}^{\phi}(\cdot\mid x)}\big[\exp(\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi))\big]\Big)\Big]

(4.2.31)

\displaystyle\leq\Big[\max_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}-\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)\Big]+\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{F}^{\phi\top}}\Big[\max_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)\Big]

(4.2.32)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim P_{F}^{\phi\top}}\Big[\max_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)\Big]-\min_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)\mathchar 59\relax

where the inequality in
Equation

4.2.31
is a consequence of
Equation

A.1.10
in the proof of
Lemma

A.1.1
.
∎

We will see an alternative bound on this KL-divergence in
Proposition

4.3.1
. Because the bounds of
Propositions

4.2.3
and
4.2.4
both involve the maximum error being incurred for any complete trajectory, they are often impractical for precisely quantifying how close the model learned with the GFlowNet is to the target. Nevertheless, the objective of these results is to confirm the intuition that the approximation found by the GFlowNet gets better as the trajectory balance loss is minimized.

4.3.
GFlowNets & variational inference

We saw in the previous section that generative flow networks are closely related to variational inference, as they treat the problem of inference (via sampling) as an optimization problem (via the minimization of a flow matching loss in
Section

4.2.1
). We will show in this section that this connection is more than superficial, and we can actually represent a variational model as a GFlowNet and conversely
(
Malkin et al. 2023
)
. Due to the sequential nature of generation in a GFlowNet, the type of variational model will be slightly different from the ones we presented in
Section

1.2
, and we will have to consider a
hierarchy
of latent variables (corresponding to the intermediate states of a GFlowNet).

4.3.1.
Hierarchical variational inference

A
hierarchical variational model
is a generalization of variational inference introduced in
Section

1.2
that extends to a hierarchy of
T
latent variables
z_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax z_{T}
. In particular in the Markovian case, the generative process is given by the following Markov chain

(4.3.1)

P_{\psi}(z_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax z_{T})=P^{\star}(z_{T})\prod_{t=1}^{T-1}P_{\psi}(z_{t}\mid z_{t+1})

This generative model depends on some parameters
\psi
, that may be learned, and its marginal
P^{\star}(z_{T})
is a Gibbs distribution. Unlike a standard VAE though, where the objective is to find an approximation of the posterior
(
Kingma & Welling 2014
)
, here the goal is to find an
inference model

Q_{\phi}(z_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax z_{T})
whose marginal approximates the (equally intractable) marginal
P_{\psi}(z_{T})=P^{\star}(z_{T})
. The form of inference model we consider here is also a Markov chain, operating forward in time
(
Bachman & Precup 2015
)

(4.3.2)

Q_{\phi}(z_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax z_{T})=Q_{\phi}(z_{1})\prod_{t=1}^{T-1}Q_{\phi}(z_{t+1}\mid z_{t}).

Since our objective is to approximate the marginal distribution
P_{\psi}(z_{T})
with
Q_{\phi}(z_{T})
, we can use the data processing inequality in
Proposition

A.1.2
in order to bound the KL divergence between these marginals by the KL divergence between their respective joint distributions over all latent variables

(4.3.3)

\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\phi}(z_{T})\,\|\,P^{\star}(z_{T})\big)=\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\phi}(z_{T})\,\|\,P_{\psi}(z_{T})\big)\leq\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\phi}(z_{1:T})\,\|\,P_{\psi}(z_{1:T})\big).

A natural choice of objective function is therefore to use the RHS of the inequality above, in order to find parameters
(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)
such that the marginal
Q_{\phi}(z_{T})
is an accurate approximation of
P^{\star}(z_{T})

(4.3.4)

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)=\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\phi}(z_{1:T})\,\|\,P_{\psi}(z_{1:T})\big).

Interestingly, while this objective depends on the target distribution
P^{\star}(z_{T})
via the generative model in
Equation

4.3.1
which is unknown, its intractable partition function
Z
disappears when computing the gradients
\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)
. Thus, it is possible to minimize this objective using gradient methods, based on samples from the inference model
Q_{\phi}(z_{1:T})
, despite knowing
P^{\star}(z_{T})
only up to a normalization constant. This is reminiscent of the intractable evidence a priori appearing in
Equation

1.2.4
.

4.3.2.
Construction of a GFlowNet from a hierarchical variational model

Sampling a sequence
(z_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax z_{T})
from a hierarchical model is closely related to how complete trajectories are sampled in a generative flow network. To see this, consider a pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
whose states are organized in “layers”: the states at a distance
t
of the initial state
s_{0}
correspond to all the possible values the latent variable
z_{t}
may take in the hierarchical model, and all the states at distance
t
are connected to those at distance
t+1
through an edge in
{\mathcal{A}}
. All the states corresponding to the values the final latent
z_{T}
may take are then connected to the terminal state
\bot
(
i.e.
, they are terminating states).

On top of this pointed DAG, we can also define forward and backward transition probabilities based on the generative and inference models in
Equation

4.3.1
&
Equation

4.3.2
. On the one hand, the backward transition probabilities are given by the generative model

(4.3.5)

P_{B}^{\psi}(z_{t}\mid z_{t+1})=P_{\psi}(z_{t}\mid z_{t+1})\mathchar 59\relax

where the values
z_{t}
&
z_{t+1}
must be interpreted as their corresponding states in
{\mathcal{G}}
. Moreover, based on the structure of the pointed DAG, it is clear that
P_{B}^{\psi}(s_{0}\mid z_{1})=1
, no matter the state
z_{1}
in the first layer. On the other hand, the forward transition probabilities are given by the inference model

(4.3.6)

\displaystyle P_{F}^{\phi}(z_{t+1}\mid z_{t})

\displaystyle=Q_{\phi}(z_{t+1}\mid z_{t})

and

\displaystyle P_{F}^{\phi}(z_{1}\mid s_{0})=Q_{\phi}(z_{1})\mathchar 59\relax

where again it is clear that
P_{F}^{\phi}(\bot\mid z_{T})=1
based on the structure of
{\mathcal{G}}
. Rewriting the hierarchical model in terms of the forward and backward transition probabilities of this GFlowNet, we have for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)

(4.3.7)

\displaystyle P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

\displaystyle P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)

\displaystyle=P^{\star}(x_{\tau})P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau\mid x_{\tau})=P^{\star}(s_{T})\prod_{t=1}^{T}P_{B}^{\psi}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})\mathchar 59\relax

where
s_{T+1}=\bot
. We use the notation
x_{\tau}\equiv s_{T}
to denote the terminating state reached by
\tau
. Similar to how we transformed any hierarchical model into a GFlowNet, we showed that any GFlowNet can conversely be transformed into an equivalent hierarchical model as well
(
Malkin et al. 2023
)
. The following proposition is the equivalent of
Equation

4.3.3
, this time working exclusively with quantities specific to GFlowNets and generalized to any
f
-divergence.

Proposition 4.3.1
.

Let
f
be a convex function. Let
P_{F}^{\phi}
be a forward transition probability distribution parametrized by
\phi
, and
P_{B}^{\psi}
be a backward transition probability distribution parametrized by
\psi
. The following inequality holds:

(4.3.8)

D_{f}\big(P^{\star}(x)\,\|\,P_{F}^{\phi\top}(x)\big)\leq D_{f}\big(P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)\,\|\,P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\big)\mathchar 59\relax

where the RHS involves distributions over complete trajectories defined in
Equation

4.3.7
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the data processing inequality in
Proposition

A.1.2
, bounding the
f
-divergence between marginal distributions (here, marginalized over trajectories leading to a terminating state) by the
f
-divergence between the joint distributions (here, the distributions over complete trajectories in
Equation

4.3.7
). Indeed, by
Definition

2.2.6
,
P_{F}^{\phi\top}(x)
is the marginal of
P_{F}^{\phi}
(as a distribution over complete trajectories) over the trajectories leading to
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
. Similarly, the marginal of
P_{B}^{\psi}
is

(4.3.9)

\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)=P^{\star}(x)\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau\mid x)=P^{\star}(x)\mathchar 59\relax

where we used
Lemma

3.1.6
to conclude
∎

It is important to reiterate that the distributions involved in the
f
-divergence on the RHS of
Equation

4.3.8
are distributions over complete trajectories, whereas those on the LHS are distributions over terminating states only. A particular case of this is using the convex function
f(x)=-\log x
, where we get a bound on the KL divergence between the terminating state distribution and the target
P^{\star}

(4.3.10)

\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi\top}(x)\,\|\,P^{\star}(x)\big)\leq\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\,\|\,P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)\big).

One way to interpret this inequality is that in order to approximate the target distribution, we must find a pair of distribution
(P_{F}^{\phi}\mathchar 59\relax P_{B}^{\psi})
that are as close as possible to one another (as distributions over complete trajectories). This notion of consistency between distributions over complete trajectories, either defined in terms of forward probabilities or backward probabilities, reminds us of the discussion in
Section

3.2.3
about the trajectory balance condition imposing an equality between those distributions.

4.3.3.
Analysis of the gradients

Recall that the trajectory balance loss is defined as
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{TB}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\big[\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}^{2}(\tau;\psi\mathchar 59\relax\psi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)\big]
, where for a complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
,

(4.3.11)

\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)=\log\frac{Z_{\omega}P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)}{R(s_{T})P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau\mid s_{T})}=\log\frac{Z_{\omega}\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})}{R(s_{T})\prod_{t=1}^{T}P_{B}^{\psi}(s_{t-1}\mid s_{t})}.

Here we explicitly separate the parameters of the forward transition probabilities
\phi
, those of the backward transition probabilities
\psi
, and those of the approximation of the partition function
\omega
to highlight the updates of each individual parameter. We saw in
Proposition

4.2.4
that this loss bounds the KL divergence between the corresponding terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\phi\top}
and the unknown target distribution
P^{\star}
. This is similar to the role played by the hierarchical variational inference objective
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)=\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\,\|\,P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)\big)
introduced in the previous section.

Is this connection between
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{TB}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)
only superficial? It turns out that the hierarchical variational inference objective can be viewed as a surrogate loss for the trajectory balance loss. In other words, instead of updating the parameters of
P_{F}^{\phi}
with gradient descent based on the trajectory balance loss, we could use the gradient of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}
(wrt.
\phi
).

Proposition 4.3.2
.

Let
P_{F}^{\phi}
be a forward transition probability distribution parametrized by
\phi
, and
P_{B}^{\psi}
be a backward transition probability distribution parametrized by
\psi
. The gradient of the hierarchical variational inference objective is related to the gradient of the trajectory balance loss via

(4.3.12)

\nabla_{\phi}\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\,\|\,P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)\big)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}^{2}(\tau;\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)\big].

Proof.

First, recall that
P_{B}^{\psi}
as a distribution over complete trajectories is defined for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
by

(4.3.13)

P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)=P^{\star}(s_{T})P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau\mid s_{T})=\frac{R(s_{T})}{Z}P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau\mid s_{T}).

Therefore, we can rewrite the difference in log-probabilities between
P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)
and
P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)
in terms of the residual of the trajectory balance condition
Equation

4.3.11

(4.3.14)

\log\frac{P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)}{P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)}=\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)+\log Z-\log Z_{\omega}.

To prove
Equation

4.3.12
, we can write the KL-divergence as a function of the residual as well:

(4.3.15)

\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\,\|\,P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)\big)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\left[\log\frac{P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)}{P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)\big]+\log Z-\log Z_{\omega}\mathchar 59\relax

where we compacted the notation for
\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)
, implicitly depending on
(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)
. Taking the gradient of this equation wrt.
\phi
, we get

(4.3.16)

\nabla_{\phi}\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\,\|\,P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)\big)=\nabla_{\phi}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)+\nabla_{\phi}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)\big].

Moreover, the second term of
Equation

4.3.16
vanishes, since
P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)
is a properly defined distribution over complete trajectories, and

(4.3.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\big]

(4.3.18)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\nabla_{\phi}\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)=\nabla_{\phi}\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}}P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)=0.

Based on the fact that
\nabla_{\phi}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)=\nabla_{\phi}\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)
, we can conclude

(4.3.19)

\nabla_{\phi}\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\,\|\,P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)\big)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)\big]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}^{2}(\tau)\big].

∎

It is important to observe that this equality holds only when the trajectory balance loss is
on-policy
, meaning that we are using the same policy
\pi_{b}\equiv P_{F}^{\phi}
for sampling trajectories and evaluating
\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)
. However since this is on-policy, the RHS of
Equation

4.3.16
does not correspond exactly to
\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{TB}}
. Indeed, there is an additional term appearing from differentiating through the sampling process

(4.3.20)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{TB}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\phi}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}^{2}(\tau;\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)\big]\right]

(4.3.21)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}^{2}(\tau)+\nabla_{\phi}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}^{2}(\tau)\big]

(4.3.22)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}^{2}(\tau)\big].

In practice, when considering the trajectory balance loss on-policy (or any other flow matching loss for that matter), it is standard to completely ignore the second term of
Equation

4.3.22
as we saw in
Section

4.2.2
, effectively treating
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{TB}}
as if it were off-policy; see
Section

8.4.1
for example.

We similarly showed that the gradient of the trajectory balance loss, this time wrt. the parameters
\psi
of the backward transition probabilities, is related to the gradient of the
forward KL
objective
(
Malkin et al. 2023
)

(4.3.23)

\nabla_{\psi}\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{B}^{\psi}\,\|\,P_{F}^{\phi}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{B}^{\psi}}\big[\nabla_{\psi}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}^{2}(\tau;\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)\big].

This result, along with
Proposition

4.3.2
may be extended to sub-trajectories
(
Malkin et al. 2023
)
. Unlike the gradient wrt.
\phi
though, the expectations appearing on both sides of this equation are taken over
P_{B}^{\psi}
itself, which is intractable since it depends on
P^{\star}
(see
Equation

4.3.7
). Although it is common practice to resort to importance sampling in those cases to get an unbiased estimator off-policy (
e.g.
, based on trajectories sampled with
P_{F}^{\phi}
; off-policy here wrt.
P_{B}^{\psi}
), this is impractical since
P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)
cannot even be evaluated (a necessary condition to apply importance sampling). The following proposition relates the gradient of the trajectory balance loss with the gradient of a combination of the reverse KL and a pseudo
f
-divergence.

Proposition 4.3.3
.

Let
P_{F}^{\phi}
be a forward transition probability distribution parametrized by
\phi
, and
P_{B}^{\psi}
be a backward transition probability distribution parametrized by
\psi
. The gradient of the (on-policy) trajectory balance loss wrt.
\psi
is equal to

(4.3.24)

\nabla_{\psi}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}^{2}(\tau;\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)\big]\right]=\nabla_{\psi}\left[D_{0.5\log^{2}}\big(P_{B}^{\psi}\,\|\,P_{F}^{\phi}\big)+\log\frac{Z_{\omega}}{Z}\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi}\,\|\,P_{B}^{\psi}\big)\right]\mathchar 59\relax

where
D_{0.5\log^{2}}
is the pseudo
f
-divergence, with the function
f(x)=0.5\log^{2}(x)
.

Proof.

We will use the notation
c(\tau)=\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)-\log P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)
, which is implicitly a function of
\phi
and
\psi
. Similarly, we use the notation
\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)
to denote the residual of the trajectory balance condition, hiding its dependency on the parameters for clarity. It is clear that for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
, we have
\nabla_{\psi}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)=-\nabla_{\psi}\log P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau\mid s_{T})=-\nabla_{\psi}\log P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)=\nabla_{\psi}c(\tau)
.

(4.3.25)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\psi}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}^{2}(\tau)\big]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\nabla_{\psi}\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\left[\nabla_{\psi}c(\tau)\log\frac{P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)Z_{\omega}}{P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)Z}\right]

(4.3.26)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\psi}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\left[\frac{1}{2}c^{2}(\tau)+\log\frac{Z_{\omega}}{Z}c(\tau)\right]\right]

(4.3.27)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\psi}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\left[\left(\log\frac{P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)}{P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)}\right)^{2}\right]+\log\frac{Z_{\omega}}{Z}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\left[\log\frac{P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)}{P_{B}^{\psi}(\tau)}\right]\right]

∎

This provides another surrogate loss for the (on-policy) trajectory balance loss, this time to update the parameters of
P_{B}^{\psi}
. However, this is equally impractical since the surrogate loss in question depends on the unknown partition function
Z
.
Table

4.1
highlights the similarities between the surrogate losses considered for trajectory balance and optimizing the reverse KL objective directly, as is typically the case in the literature on variational inference.
Zhang et al. 2024a
also used a similar connection between reverse KL and the detailed balance objective this time (
Theorem

4.1.4
) to devise an on-policy algorithm inspired by
PPO

(
Schulman et al. 2017
)
.

Table 4.1
.
Summary of the surrogate losses for variational inference algorithms & GFlowNets. This highlights that reverse KL & (on-policy) trajectory balance optimize the same objective when updating the parameters
\phi
of the inference model/forward transition probabilities.

Surrogate loss

Algorithm

\phi\leftarrow\phi-\eta\nabla_{\phi}[-]

\psi\leftarrow\psi-\eta\nabla_{\psi}[-]

Reverse KL

\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi}\,\|\,P_{B}^{\psi}\big)

\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi}\,\|\,P_{B}^{\psi}\big)

Wake-sleep

\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{B}^{\psi}\,\|\,P_{F}^{\phi}\big)

\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi}\,\|\,P_{B}^{\psi}\big)

(On-policy) Trajectory balance

\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi}\,\|\,P_{B}^{\psi}\big)

Proposition

4.3.3

Proposition

4.3.2

4.3.4.
Variance reduction of the gradient estimate

To circumvent the complexity of studying the update of
P_{B}
via the surrogate loss in
Proposition

4.3.3
, we will consider it fixed in this section and focus on learning only
P_{F}^{\phi}
(along with
Z_{\omega}
in the case of trajectory balance). We saw that both the reverse KL and (on-policy) trajectory balance objectives correspond to the same surrogate loss when considering the update of
\phi
(
Table

4.1
). Recall that

(4.3.28)

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}(\phi)=\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\,\|\,P_{B}(\tau)\big)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\left[\log\frac{ZP_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)}{R(x_{\tau})P_{B}(\tau\mid x_{\tau})}\right].

We will typically use gradient methods to update the parameters
\phi
of the forward transition probabilities. In order to estimate the gradient of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}
, we can take the
score function estimator

(
Mohamed et al. 2020
)
, also known as the REINFORCE estimator in the context of reinforcement learning
(
Williams 1992
)
. This estimator is based on the following observation:

(4.3.29)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)c(\tau;\phi)\big]

where

\displaystyle c(\tau;\phi)

\displaystyle=\log\frac{P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)}{R(x_{\tau})P_{B}(\tau\mid x_{\tau})}.

The term
\mathbb{E}\big[\nabla_{\phi}c(\tau;\phi)\big]=0
that would have appeared in the expression of the gradient vanishes since
\mathbb{E}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\big]=0
(see also
Equation

4.3.18
). For similar reasons, it is standard to add a
control variate

b
(also called a
baseline
in reinforcement learning) independent of the trajectories
\tau
, in order to reduce the variance of the future estimator (although this is not guaranteed;
Weaver & Tao 2001
)

(4.3.30)

\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}(\phi)=\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)\big(c(\tau;\phi)-b\big)\big].

While
b
might be completely arbitrary, a typical choice is to use
b=\mathbb{E}\big[c(\tau;\phi)\big]
. We can estimate the gradient based on trajectories
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
sampled iid. from the distribution
P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau)

(4.3.31)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\nabla_{\phi}\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\tau_{k})\big(c(\tau_{k};\phi)-b_{\mathrm{local}}\big)

where

\displaystyle b_{\mathrm{local}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}c(\tau_{k};\phi)\mathchar 59\relax

and where the control variate is also estimated based on sample trajectories. The “local” aspect of this estimate is due to using only information from the current batch of trajectories
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
.

A more accurate approximation of
\mathbb{E}\big[c(\tau;\phi)\big]
can be obtained by maintaining a running average of the values
c(\tau;\phi)
across multiple batches, leading to a “global” baseline whose update is given by

(4.3.32)

b_{\mathrm{global}}\leftarrow(1-\eta)b_{\mathrm{global}}+\eta b_{\mathrm{local}}.

Going back to the trajectory balance loss (on-policy), while we focused on the update of the parameters
\phi
of the forward transition probabilities thus far, we can also study the gradient update of the parameters of
Z_{\omega}
. Suppose that we use the parametrization
Z_{\omega}=\exp(-\omega)
(which is a standard practice;
Malkin et al. 2022
). The gradient of the trajectory balance objective is then given by

(4.3.33)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\omega}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{TB}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\omega}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\Big[\big(\log Z_{\omega}+c(\tau;\phi)\big)^{2}\Big]\right]

(4.3.34)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\omega}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\Big[\big(-\omega+c(\tau;\phi)\big)^{2}\Big]=\omega-\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{F}^{\phi}}\big[c(\tau;\phi)\big]

Using the notation
b_{\mathrm{local}}
introduced in
Equation

4.3.31
, it is easy to see that the gradient wrt. the parameters
\omega
can be approximated based on a batch of trajectories with
\nabla_{\omega}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{TB}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\omega)\approx\omega-b_{\mathrm{local}}
. With this approximation of the gradient, we can write the update rule of the parameters
\omega
after one step of gradient descent with step size
\eta
as

(4.3.35)

\omega_{t+1}=\omega_{t}-\eta\nabla_{\omega}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{TB}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\omega_{t})=\omega_{t}-\eta\big(\omega_{t}-b_{\mathrm{local}}\big)=(1-\eta)\omega_{t}+\eta b_{\mathrm{local}}\mathchar 59\relax

which corresponds exactly to the update rule of the global baseline in
Equation

4.3.32
. In other words, minimizing the trajectory balance loss (on-policy) coincides with minimizing the reverse KL loss with a control variate estimated with a moving average
Equation

4.3.32
. Since the gradient of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}(\phi)
is typically approximated using a local baseline
Equation

4.3.31

(
Weber et al. 2015
)
, on-policy trajectory balance and reverse KL only differ in the way the control variate is being updated. We show this similarity between both methods empirically in
Section

7.5.3
on the problem of Bayesian structure learning, and more exhaustively in
Malkin et al. 2023
.

It is important to remember that this correspondence holds when we consider the trajectory balance loss
on-policy
. One of the major strengths of the flow matching losses for GFlowNets is that they are also valid off-policy without any modification (see
Section

4.2.2
). This has the advantage of accelerating training since we can encourage exploration with a behavior policy
\pi_{b}
decoupled from
P_{F}^{\phi}
being learned. On the other hand, the reverse KL objective is inherently on-policy, and using trajectories sampled from another policy requires importance sampling in order to obtain an unbiased estimate of
\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}(\phi)

(4.3.36)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{HVI}}(\phi)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}w_{k}\nabla_{\phi}\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\widetilde{\tau}_{k})\big(c(\widetilde{\tau}_{k};\phi)-b_{\mathrm{local}}\big)

where

\displaystyle w_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{F}^{\phi}(\widetilde{\tau}_{k})}{\pi_{b}(\widetilde{\tau}_{k})}\mathchar 59\relax

where
\{\widetilde{\tau}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
are trajectories sampled iid. from the behavior policy
\pi_{b}
. The variance of this estimator is usually high, despite the control variate, making the reverse KL less effective when training off-policy. The key observation here is that the trajectory balance objective (and its gradient) do
not
require importance sampling, and therefore do not suffer from this high variance.

Chapter 5
GFlowNets & Maximum Entropy

Reinforcement Learning

This chapter contains material from the following paper:

•

Tristan Deleu
, Padideh Nouri, Nikolay Malkin, Doina Precup, Yoshua Bengio (2024).
Discrete Probabilistic Inference as Control in Multi-path Environments
. Conference on Uncertainty in Artificial Intelligence (UAI).
\notecite
deleu2024gfnmaxentrl

We saw in
Section

2.3
that while being an appealing solution at first glance, maximum entropy reinforcement learning (MaxEnt RL;
Ziebart 2010
;
Fox et al. 2016
) alone was not sufficient to sample from the Gibbs distribution
Equation

2.1.1
without introducing some bias towards terminating states with multiple complete trajectories leading to them. This bias motivated the introduction of GFlowNets as a general solution for sampling as a sequential decision making problem, regardless of the structure of the pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
. Recall that given an energy function
{\mathcal{E}}(x)
, our objective is to sample from the following Gibbs distribution

(5.0.1)

P^{\star}(x)\propto\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x)/\alpha)\mathchar 59\relax

where this distribution over the (discrete and compositional) sample space
{\mathcal{X}}
is slightly more general than
Equation

2.1.1
by introducing a
temperature
parameter
\alpha>0
; we can get back to our original formulation by simply taking
\alpha=1
. In this chapter, we will show how we can incorporate a correction to MaxEnt RL in order to remove the bias, this time regardless of the structure of
{\mathcal{G}}
.

5.1.
Unbiased sampling with maximum entropy RL

Once again, to be consistent with the reinforcement learning literature and similar to
Section

2.3
, we will use the term “policies” and denote them by “
\pi
” when talking about quantities related to reinforcement learning, and keep “forward transition probabilities” and “
P_{F}
” for quantities related to GFlowNets. Recall that the objective of MaxEnt RL is to find an optimal stochastic policy
\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}
that maximizes the expected return, augmented with the entropy of the policy
{\mathcal{H}}(\pi(\cdot\mid s))=-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\pi(s^{\prime}\mid s)\log\pi(s^{\prime}\mid s)
along the trajectory:

(5.1.1)

\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}=\argmax_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi}\Bigg[\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})+\alpha{\mathcal{H}}(\pi(\cdot\mid s_{t}))\Bigg]\mathchar 59\relax

with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
. With this newly introduced temperature parameter
\alpha
controlling the relative weight of the entropy regularization compared to the return,
Haarnoja et al. 2017
showed that the optimal policy maximizing
Equation

5.1.1
can be written for any
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
as

(5.1.2)

\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\big(Q^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s)\big)\right)\mathchar 59\relax

where
Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
is a state-action value function and
V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s)
is a state value function that satisfy the following soft Bellman optimality equations (adapted to our setting with a deterministic & undiscounted MDP):

(5.1.3)

\displaystyle Q^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})+V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s^{\prime})

(5.1.4)

\displaystyle V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s)

\displaystyle=\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right).

Note that these equations slightly differ from
Equation

2.3.4
&
Equation

2.3.5
because of the parameter
\alpha
.

5.1.1.
Bellman optimality & flow matching on trees

In this section only, we will assume that
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
has a tree structure, meaning that every state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
has a unique parent state (except of course the initial state
s_{0}
). Note that we omit the case
s=\bot
, since by
Definition

2.2.2

\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(\bot)={\mathcal{X}}
. If the reward function is defined such that
\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})
(with
s_{T+1}=\bot
), then we saw in
Section

2.3.2
that the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}
induces a distribution over complete trajectories
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
such that

(5.1.5)
