\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}(\tau)\propto\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})/\alpha)\mathchar 59\relax

once again slightly differing from
Proposition

2.3.1
due to the parameter
\alpha
(the proof of this proposition can be immediately adapted). In the case where
{\mathcal{G}}
is a tree, there is a unique complete trajectory leading to each terminating state
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, and therefore we can sample from the Gibbs distribution by following the optimal policy
\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}
. Let’s consider the case of a sparse reward received only at the end of the trajectory, meaning that
r(s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})
, and is zero otherwise. The following proposition shows that the flow matching condition we established in
Theorem

4.1.1
is exactly equivalent to the soft Bellman optimality equations.

Proposition 5.1.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}=({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax r)
be a MDP such that
{\mathcal{G}}
is a tree over
{\mathcal{S}}
, and the reward function is sparse, meaning that for any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
r(x\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=-{\mathcal{E}}(x)
, and is zero otherwise (i.e., for
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
,
r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})=0
). The flow matching condition & boundary conditions of
Theorem

4.1.1
are equivalent (in log-space) to the soft Bellman optimality equations
Equation

5.1.3
&
Equation

5.1.4
, with the following correspondence for all
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
:

(5.1.6)

Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})=\alpha\log F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

where
F
is the edge flow function satisfying the flow matching & boundary conditions of
Theorem

4.1.1
.

Proof.

First, note that the boundary condition of
Theorem

4.1.1
reads as
F(x\rightarrow\bot)=\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x)/\alpha)
, when the reward function (in the sense of GFlowNets, which must not be confused with the reward
r
of
{\mathcal{M}}
) is
R(x)=\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x)/\alpha)
. For some transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
, we consider two cases:

•

If
s^{\prime}=\bot
, then we know that
r(s\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=-{\mathcal{E}}(s)
and that
\bot
has no children in
{\mathcal{G}}
, meaning that
V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(\bot)=0
. Therefore, using the correspondence in
Equation

5.1.6
, we can write
Equation

5.1.3
as

(5.1.7)

\alpha\log F(s\rightarrow\bot)=Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=r(s\mathchar 59\relax\bot)+V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(\bot)=-{\mathcal{E}}(s)\mathchar 59\relax

which corresponds to the boundary condition in log-space.

•

If
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
, then we know that
r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})=0
(sparse reward). Using the correspondence in
Equation

5.1.6
, we can write the soft Bellman optimality equations as

(5.1.8)

\displaystyle\alpha\log F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})=r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})+\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)

(5.1.9)

\displaystyle=\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)=\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime}).

Moreover, since
{\mathcal{G}}
has a tree structure,
s^{\prime}
has only a single parent which is
s
. Thus

(5.1.10)

\log\sum_{\tilde{s}\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(\tilde{s}\rightarrow s^{\prime})=\log F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

where we used
Equation

5.1.9
in the second equality. This corresponds to the flow matching condition in log-space.

∎

An interesting point about this result is that the edge (log-)flow in a GFlowNet finds a natural interpretation as a state-value function in reinforcement learning; this intuitive correspondence will be confirmed throughout
Section

5.2
. In the general case though, recall that when
{\mathcal{G}}
does
not
have a tree structure anymore, the terminating state distribution associated with the optimal policy
\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}
is biased towards states having multiple paths leading to them:

(5.1.11)

\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star\top}(x)\propto n(x)\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x)/\alpha)\mathchar 59\relax

where
n(x)
counts the number of paths going to a certain terminating state
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
.

5.1.2.
Reward correction

To correct the bias in
Equation

5.1.11
caused by multiple complete trajectories leading to the same terminating state, we could treat the terminating state distribution
\pi^{\star\top}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(x)
in
Definition

2.2.6
not as a sum, but as an
expectation
over trajectories leading to
x
, by reweighting
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(\tau)
(which is constant for any complete trajectory terminating at
x

Equation

5.1.5
) with a probability distribution over these trajectories. We saw in
Section

3.1.2
that a backward transition probability distribution induces a probability distribution
P_{B}(\tau\mid x)
over complete trajectories terminating at
x
; this would be a natural choice for such a reweighting. The following theorem shows that if the reward function is defined using the energy function, as in
Equation

2.3.1
, but this time corrected by a fixed backward transition probability, then the terminating state distribution associated with the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}
matches the Gibbs distribution of interest.

Theorem 5.1.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}=({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax r)
be a MDP with a pointed DAG structure
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
, and let
P_{B}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
be an arbitrary backward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
. For any
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}
, let
r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
be the reward function of the MDP corrected with
P_{B}
, satisfying for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
:

(5.1.12)

\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})+\alpha\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\log P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})\mathchar 59\relax

with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
. Then the terminating state distribution associated with the optimal policy
\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}
solution of
Equation

5.1.1
satisfies
\pi^{\star\top}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(x)\propto\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x)/\alpha)
for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
.

Proof.

The proof of this theorem uses similar techniques as the proof of
Proposition

2.3.1
. First, note that based on
Equation

5.1.2
&
Equation

5.1.3
we can write the optimal policy for any
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
as

(5.1.13)

\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\big(r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})+V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s^{\prime})-V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s)\big)\right).

By
Definition

2.2.6
, we can write the terminating state probability distribution associated with
\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}
for any terminating state
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
as

(5.1.14)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star\top}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}(\tau)=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

(5.1.15)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\sum_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}\big(r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})+V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s_{t+1})-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s_{t})\big)\right)

(5.1.16)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\exp\Bigg(\frac{1}{\alpha}\Bigg(\sum_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})+\underbrace{V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(\bot)}_{=\,0}-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s_{0})\Bigg)\Bigg)

(5.1.17)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(-{\mathcal{E}}(x)+\alpha\sum_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}-1}\log P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s_{0})\right)\right)

(5.1.18)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}(-{\mathcal{E}}(x)-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s_{0}))\right)\underbrace{\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}-1}P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})}_{=\,1}

(5.1.19)

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x)/\alpha)}{\exp(V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}(s_{0})/\alpha)}\propto\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x)/\alpha)\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the telescoping of
V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\star}
in
Equation

5.1.16
, and the fact that
P_{B}
induces a probability distribution over the complete trajectories leading to
x
in
Equation

5.1.18
(see
Lemma

3.1.6
). Note that we used the convention
s_{T_{\tau}}=x
and
s_{T_{\tau}+1}=\bot
throughout our derivation, where
T_{\tau}
indicates the length of the complete trajectory
\tau
(which may be different, even if they all terminate at the same
x
).
∎

In other words,
Theorem

5.1.2
shows that by appropriately correcting the reward function of the MDP, we can emulate the behavior of a GFlowNet (
i.e.
, sampling from
Equation

5.0.1
) with MaxEnt RL. This theorem generalizes the result of
Tiapkin et al. 2024
, who considered a special case of reward correction which we will go back to in the next section. Unlike the reward function we considered in
Section

2.3
where
\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})
, the return now depends on the complete trajectory leading to
s_{T}
via the second term in
Equation

5.1.12
, and not just the state
s_{T}
it reaches at the end. Interestingly, the temperature parameter
\alpha
introduced in the MaxEnt RL literature
(
Haarnoja et al. 2017
)
used to balance the effect of the entropy regularization in
Equation

5.1.1
finds a natural interpretation as the temperature of the Gibbs distribution
Equation

5.0.1
. Note that the correction in
Equation

5.1.12
only involves the backward probability
P_{B}(\tau\mid x)
of the whole complete trajectory
\tau
, making it applicable even with non-Markovian backward transition probabilities
(
Shen et al. 2023
;
Bengio et al. 2023
)
(
i.e.
, a generalization of
Section

3.1.2
).

This correction of the reward is fully compatible with our observation in
Section

2.3
that sampling terminating states
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}
yields samples from
Equation

5.0.1
when the soft MDP is a tree with the (uncorrected) reward in
Equation

2.3.1
, since in that case any state
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
has a unique parent
s
, and thus we necessarily have
P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})=1
, as also observed by
Tiapkin et al. 2024
. Since
P_{B}
can be arbitrary, a simple option is for example to set
P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})
as the uniform distribution over the parents of
s
. Another option is to set
P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})=n(s)/n(s^{\prime})
, where
n(s)
is the number of partial trajectories from the initial state
s_{0}
to
s
.
Mohammadpour et al. 2024
proved that this backward transition probability has the remarkable property of maximizing the flow entropy (
i.e.
, the entropy of
\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}(\tau)
as a distribution over complete trajectories;
Zhang et al. 2022
;
Shen et al. 2023
); we will come back to this property in
Section

7.2.4
.

5.2.
Equivalence between MaxEnt RL & GFlowNet objectives

Theorem

5.1.2
suggests that solving the MaxEnt RL problem in
Equation

5.1.1
with the corrected reward is comparable to finding a solution of a GFlowNet, as they both lead to a policy/forward transition probability whose terminating state distribution is
Equation

5.0.1
. It turns out that there exist strong equivalences between specific algorithms solving these two problems, which we will detail in this section. Taking inspiration from
Section

4.2.1
, all the objectives we will consider here, including those for MaxEnt RL, will have the form
{\mathcal{L}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\big[\Delta^{2}(\cdot;\phi)\big]
where
\pi_{b}
is an arbitrary distribution over appropriate quantities (behavior policy), and
\Delta(\cdot;\phi)
are residuals that are algorithm-specific. A full summary of the connections between different MaxEnt RL and GFlowNet objectives is available in
Figure

5.1
, with further details in
Figure

5.3
. Throughout this section, we will assume that the backward transition probability
P_{B}
used in
Equation

5.1.12
is fixed.

(
Nachum et al. 2017
)

(
Madan et al. 2023
)

(
Malkin et al. 2022
)

(

)

(

)

(

)

)

(

)

(

.
Equivalence between objectives in MaxEnt RL, with corrected rewards, and the objectives in GFlowNets. The objectives are classified based on whether they operate at the level of (complete) trajectories (left), transitions (middle), or if all the states are terminating (right). Further details about the form of the different residuals and the correspondences to transfer from one objective to another are available in
Figure

5.3
.

Is it the end for GFlowNets?

If GFlowNets and MaxEnt RL are two faces of the same coin, as we will see in this section, is all the research in GFlowNets going to be superseded by MaxEnt RL? After all, the latter is a well-established field, with a large community and applications well beyond the relatively narrow (in comparison) domain of probabilistic inference. Fortunately, the answer to this question is
no
. The main reason being that this equivalence between these two fields holds
under the assumption that
P_{B}
is fixed
, even if it could be learned in GFlowNets
(
Malkin et al. 2022
)
. This is necessary because the backward transition probability appears in the correction added to the reward
r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
, which in the standard formulation of reinforcement learning is required to be a known quantity (and not learned). Despite these theoretical considerations though, from a practical perspective it would be as easy to also learn
P_{B}
within the framework of MaxEnt RL in this situation, without having to resort to inverse RL.

However, these connections invite some bridges to be made between the two fields, where a lot of existing tools from the reinforcement learning literature can be readily transferred to GFlowNets. We saw in
Section

4.2.2
some of them already, with the use of Thompson sampling to improve exploration
(
Rector-Brooks et al. 2023
)
, advanced planning methods with Monte Carlo tree search
(
Morozov et al. 2024
)
, or replay buffers
(
Deleu et al. 2022
)
. We will also see in
Chapter

7
that it may be advantageous to also use a
target network
with objectives inspired by Q-Learning (
Section

5.2.4
), as popularized by the seminal work on Deep Q-Networks
(
Mnih et al. 2015
)
.
Zhang et al. 2023b
introduced a distributional version of GFlowNets, taking inspiration from distributional RL
(
Bellemare et al. 2023
)
. This intersection is an exciting direction of research.

5.2.1.
Path consistency learning & (sub-)trajectory balance

Similar to how
Equation

2.3.1
covered the particular case of a sparse reward that is only received at the end of the trajectory (
i.e.
,
r(s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})
, and zero everywhere else), in this section and
Section

5.2.2
we will consider a reward function satisfying
Equation

5.1.12
where the energy function only appears at the end of the trajectory

(5.2.1)

\displaystyle r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})

\displaystyle=\alpha\log P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})

\displaystyle r(s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})\mathchar 59\relax

as introduced in
Tiapkin et al. 2024
; we can see that this reward function satisfies the condition
Equation

5.1.12
of
Theorem

5.1.2
. With this particular choice of reward function, we will establish an equivalence between the
Path Consistency Learning
algorithm (
PCL
;
Nachum et al. 2017
) in MaxEnt RL and the
sub-trajectory balance
loss (SubTB;
Malkin et al. 2022
;
Madan et al. 2023
) in the GFlowNet literature. On the one hand, the PCL objective encourages the consistency at the level of partial trajectories
\tau=(s_{m}\mathchar 59\relax s_{m+1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{n})
between a policy
\pi_{\phi}
parametrized by
\phi
and a state value function
V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}
parametrized by
\psi
, whose residual can be written as

(5.2.2)

\Delta_{\mathrm{PCL}}(\tau;\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)=-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(s_{m})+V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(s_{n})+\sum_{t=m}^{n-1}\big(r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})-\alpha\log\pi_{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\big)\mathchar 59\relax

where we enforce that
V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(\bot)=0
. On the other hand, the SubTB objective also encourages some form of consistency at the level of partial trajectories, but this time between a forward transition probability
P_{F}^{\phi}
parametrized by
\phi
, and a state flow function
F_{\psi}
parametrized by
\psi
, where the residual is defined by

(5.2.3)

\Delta_{\mathrm{SubTB}}(\tau;\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)=\log\frac{F_{\psi}(s_{n})\prod_{t=m}^{n-1}P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})}{F_{\psi}(s_{m})\prod_{t=m}^{n-1}P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})}\mathchar 59\relax

if
s_{n}\neq\bot
. It is important to recall that satisfying the sub-trajectory balance conditions for all partial trajectories of fixed length does not necessarily guarantee that the terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\phi\top}
matches
Equation

5.0.1
(see
Section

3.2.4
; although it does if we consider all partial trajectories of length
\leq L
for any
L
). Nevertheless, this loss is interesting since it generalizes existing GFlowNet losses we introduced in
Section

4.2.1
and makes the presentation clearer. Similar to how the detailed balance loss in
Equation

4.2.4
required an additional reward matching loss to account for the boundary conditions, if the end of the partial trajectory
s_{n}=\bot
is the terminal state, then the residual is

(5.2.4)

\Delta_{\mathrm{SubTB}}(\tau;\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)=\log\frac{\prod_{t=m}^{n-2}P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})}{F_{\psi}(s_{m})\prod_{t=m}^{n-1}P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})}-\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(s_{n-1})}{\alpha}.

The following proposition establishes the equivalence between the two objectives induced by these residuals, up to a constant that only depends on the temperature
\alpha
, and provides a way to move from the policy/value function parametrization in MaxEnt RL to the forward transition probability/flow function parametrization in GFlowNets.

Proposition 5.2.1
.

The sub-trajectory balance objective (GFlowNet;
Madan et al. 2023
) is proportional to the Path Consistency Learning objective (MaxEnt RL;
Nachum et al. 2017
) on the MDP with the reward function defined in
Equation

5.2.1
, in the sense that
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{PCL}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)=\alpha^{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{SubTB}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)
, with the following correspondence

(5.2.5)

\displaystyle\pi_{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)

\displaystyle=P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(s)

\displaystyle=\alpha\log F_{\psi}(s).

Proof.

In order to show the equivalence between
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{PCL}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{SubTB}}
, it is sufficient to only show the equivalence between their corresponding residuals on a partial trajectory
\tau=(s_{m}\mathchar 59\relax s_{m+1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{n})
, by replacing the reward by
Equation

5.2.1
. We will use the correspondence in
Equation

5.2.5
in order to move between the parameterizations of MaxEnt RL and GFlowNets. We consider two cases

•

If
s_{n}\neq\bot
is not the terminal state, then the residual for SubTB is given by
Equation

5.2.3
. Substituting
Equation

5.2.1
&
Equation

5.2.5
into the residual
\Delta_{\mathrm{PCL}}
:

(5.2.6)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{PCL}}

\displaystyle(\tau;\phi\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\psi)=-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(s_{m})+V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(s_{n})+\sum_{t=m}^{n-1}\big(r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})-\alpha\log\pi_{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\big)

(5.2.7)

\displaystyle=-\alpha\log F_{\psi}(s_{m})+\alpha\log F_{\psi}(s_{n})+\alpha\sum_{t=m}^{n-1}\big(\log P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})-\log P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\big)

(5.2.8)

\displaystyle=\alpha\log\frac{F_{\psi}(s_{n})\prod_{t=m}^{n-1}P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})}{F_{\psi}(s_{m})\prod_{t=m}^{n-1}P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})}=\alpha\Delta_{\mathrm{SubTB}}(\tau;\phi\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\psi).

•

If
s_{n}=\bot
, then the residual for SubTB is given by
Equation

5.2.4
. Once again, substituting
Equation

5.2.1
&
Equation

5.2.5
into the residual
\Delta_{\mathrm{PCL}}
, and using the fact that
V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(\bot)=0
, we have

(5.2.9)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{PCL}}(\tau;\phi\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\psi)

\displaystyle=-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(s_{m})+V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(\bot)+\sum_{t=m}^{n-1}\big(r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})-\alpha\log\pi_{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\big)

\displaystyle=-\alpha\log F_{\psi}(s_{m})-\big({\mathcal{E}}(s_{n-1})+\alpha\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\bot\mid s_{n-1})\big)

(5.2.10)

\displaystyle\qquad+\alpha\sum_{t=m}^{n-2}\big(\log P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})-\log P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\big)

(5.2.11)

\displaystyle=\alpha\left[\log\frac{\prod_{t=m}^{n-2}P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})}{F_{\psi}(s_{m})\prod_{t=m}^{n-1}P_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})}-\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(s_{n-1})}{\alpha}\right]=\alpha\Delta_{\mathrm{SubTB}}(\tau;\phi\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\psi).

This concludes the proof, showing that
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{PCL}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)=\alpha^{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{SubTB}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)
.
∎

Similarities between PCL and SubTB have also been mentioned in prior work
(
Malkin et al. 2022
;
Jiralerspong et al. 2024
;
Hu et al. 2024
;
Mohammadpour et al. 2024
)
, although without showing this strong equivalence. The equivalence between the value function in MaxEnt RL and the state flow function in GFlowNets was also found in
Tiapkin et al. 2024
. When applied to complete trajectories, this proposition also shows an equivalence between PCL and the
trajectory balance
loss (TB;
Malkin et al. 2022
). Indeed, the trajectory balance loss in
Equation

4.2.6
corresponds exactly to
Equation

5.2.4
when
s_{m}=s_{0}
is the initial state (using a scalar parametrization
Z
instead of
F(s_{0})
).

5.2.2.
Soft Q-Learning & detailed balance

If we consider consistency over complete trajectories in PCL/TB as one end of a spectrum, on the other end of the spectrum we can consider objectives measuring the consistency at the level of transitions
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
. We saw in
Section

2.3.2
that one of the simplest method for finding the optimal policy
\pi_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}^{\star}
is the Soft Q-Learning algorithm (SQL;
Haarnoja et al. 2017
), that operates on transitions via a state-action value function
Q(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
. In the tabular case, we can interpret the procedure detailed in
Algorithm

3
as performing stochastic (semi-)gradient descent on a certain objective with the residual

(5.2.12)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{SQL}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)

\displaystyle=Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-\big(r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})+V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s^{\prime})\big)

(5.2.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{where}\quad V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\triangleq\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right).

quantifying the mismatch in the soft Bellman optimality equations in
Equation

5.1.3
&
Equation

5.1.4
. In the case where
s^{\prime}=\bot
is the terminal state, then we have
V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s^{\prime})=0
. This residual depends only on a state-action value function
Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
parametrized by
\phi
.

Since SQL works at the level of transitions, it would be natural to study its equivalence with the
detailed balance
loss (DB;
Bengio et al. 2023
) in GFlowNets. The DB loss introduced in
Section

4.2.1
, whose residual (including for the reward matching loss, with the energy function here) is given by

(5.2.14)

for
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{DB}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)

\displaystyle=\log\frac{F_{\phi}(s)P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)}{F_{\phi}(s^{\prime})P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})}

(5.2.15)

for
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{DB}}(x\rightarrow\bot;\phi)

\displaystyle=\log\big(F_{\phi}(x)P_{F}^{\phi}(\bot\mid x)\big)+\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(x)}{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax

which corresponds exactly to the residuals of SubTB in
Equation

5.2.3
&
Equation

5.2.4
(up to a change of sign) when the partial trajectory is of length 1 (
i.e.
, a transition). With this in mind, would it be possible to use the result of
Proposition

5.2.1
in order to establish a connection between SQL and DB?

There is a discrepancy between
Equation

5.2.12
and
Equation

5.2.14
though: while SQL uses a single state-action value function
Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
, DB depends on a state flow
F_{\psi}(s)
and a forward transition probability
P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
which are in general parametrized by two separate functions. This separation also existed in the residual of PCL. Fortunately,
Nachum et al. 2017
showed that SQL can be interpreted as a special case of PCL under what they called the
Unified PCL
perspective, where the state value function
V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s)
and the policy
\pi_{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
appearing in
Equation

5.2.2
can be parametrized by a single state-action value function
Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
via

(5.2.16)

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s)

\displaystyle=\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\pi_{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\right).

As a corollary of
Proposition

5.2.1
under this Unified PCL perspective, the following proposition shows that there exists a strong equivalence between DB and SQL, once again under our choice of reward function detailed in the previous section.

Proposition 5.2.2
.

The detailed balance objective (GFlowNet;
Bengio et al. 2023
) is proportional to the Soft Q-Learning objective (MaxEnt RL;
Haarnoja et al. 2017
) on the MDP with the reward function defined in
Equation

5.2.1
, in the sense that
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{SQL}}(\phi)=\alpha^{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{DB}}(\phi)
, with the following correspondence

(5.2.17)

\displaystyle F_{\phi}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)

\displaystyle P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\right).

Proof.

While this proposition can be seen as a corollary of
Proposition

5.2.1
, we prove it here as a standalone result for completeness. Let
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
be a transition. We consider two cases:

•

If
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
is not the terminal state, then we know that
r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})=\alpha\log P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})
. Substituting it into the residual
\Delta_{\mathrm{SQL}}
, and using the correspondence in
Equation

5.2.17
, we have:

(5.2.18)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{SQL}}

\displaystyle(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)=Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)

(5.2.19)

\displaystyle=Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-\alpha\log P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})-\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha\log\left(\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\right)\right)-\alpha\log P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})-\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)

(5.2.20)

\displaystyle\qquad-\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)+\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)

(5.2.21)

\displaystyle=\alpha\left[\log P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)+\log F_{\phi}(s)-\log P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})-\log F_{\phi}(s^{\prime})\right]=\alpha\Delta_{\mathrm{DB}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)

•

If
s^{\prime}=\bot
is the terminal state, then the reward is
r(s\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=-{\mathcal{E}}(s)
. Substituting it again into the residual
\Delta_{\mathrm{SQL}}
, and using the correspondence in
Equation

5.2.17
, we have:

(5.2.22)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{SQL}}(s\rightarrow\bot;\phi)

\displaystyle=Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax\bot)-r(s\mathchar 59\relax\bot)

(5.2.23)

\displaystyle=\alpha\log\left(\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax\bot)\right)\right)+{\mathcal{E}}(s)

\displaystyle\qquad-\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)+\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)

(5.2.24)

\displaystyle=\alpha\left[\log P_{F}^{\phi}(\bot\mid s)+\log F_{\phi}(s)+\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(s)}{\alpha}\right]=\alpha\Delta_{\mathrm{DB}}(s\rightarrow\bot;\phi)

This concludes the proof, showing that
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{SQL}}(\phi)=\alpha^{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{DB}}(\phi)
.
∎

Note that
Tiapkin et al. 2024
established a similar connection between SQL and DB through a dueling architecture perspective
(
Wang et al. 2016
)
, where the state-action value function
Q_{\mathrm{soft}}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
is decomposed as (adapted to incorporate the parameter
\alpha
):

(5.2.25)

\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})=V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(s)+A_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\big(A_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\big)\mathchar 59\relax

where
A_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
is an advantage function quantifying the added value of taking the transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
compared to the “baseline” value
V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(s)
at state
s
. The additional log-sum-exp term in the decomposition above is to account for the identifiability between the advantage and the state-action value functions. The connection
Tiapkin et al. 2024
established is then between the state value function
V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(s)
and the state flow function
F_{\psi}(s)
on the one hand, and the advantage function
A_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
and the forward transition probability
P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
on the other hand, via

(5.2.26)

\displaystyle\log F_{\psi}(s)

\displaystyle=V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi}(s)=\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\psi\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)

(5.2.27)

\displaystyle\log P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)

\displaystyle=A_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\big(A_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\big).

This differs from our result above in that
Proposition

5.2.2
does not explicitly require a separate advantage function. However, we remark that both results are effectively equivalent to one another when the
V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s)
and
A_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
share the same parametrization, since
Equation

5.2.26
corresponds exactly to
Equation

5.2.17
, and

(5.2.28)

\log P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s)=\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)\mathchar 59\relax

where we plugged
Equation

5.2.25
into
Equation

5.2.27
to also match the correspondence in
Proposition

5.2.2
. This connection through a dueling architecture is closer to DB when the forward transition probability and the state flow function are parametrized separately. Empirically, we found that on some environments this strategy where the state value and the advantage functions (or equivalently the flow function and the forward transition probability in GFlowNets) are parametrized by two separate functions was more performant than having a single function parametrizing both
(
Deleu et al. 2024
)
. However, a more extensive study is necessary in order to conclude on the effectiveness of both methods.

5.2.3.
Soft Q-Learning & forward-looking detailed balance

Up until now, we have considered cases where the energy function was only received at the end of the complete trajectory. This was due to the fact that in general the energy function
{\mathcal{E}}(x)
is only defined for terminating states
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, and nowhere else (
i.e.
, it is undefined on intermediate states in
{\mathcal{S}}\backslash{\mathcal{X}}
). However there are situations where the energy function itself has a natural decomposition that would allow us to get intermediate signals. We illustrate this by revisiting the example detailed in
Section

2.2.5
, where we use MaxEnt RL (or GFlowNets) for inference in a discrete factor graph. To make this example more concrete, we consider the following energy function

(5.2.29)

{\mathcal{E}}(x_{1}\mathchar 59\relax x_{2}\mathchar 59\relax x_{3})=\psi_{1}(x_{1}\mathchar 59\relax x_{2}\mathchar 59\relax x_{3})+\psi_{2}(x_{2}\mathchar 59\relax x_{3})+\psi_{3}(x_{1})\mathchar 59\relax

where the functions (factors)
\psi_{m}
are known and only depend on a subset of values, and each
x_{i}
is a value in
\{0\mathchar 59\relax 1\}
(see
Figure

5.2
). The standard approach to evaluate this energy function would be to wait until all the random variables have been assigned a value in order to evaluate
Equation

5.2.29
all at once, as we did in
Section

2.2.5
.

Figure 5.2
.
Revisiting sampling in a discrete factor graph of
Section

2.2.5
, with intermediate rewards. Instead of computing the energy of the whole terminating state
{\mathcal{E}}(x_{1}\mathchar 59\relax x_{2}\mathchar 59\relax x_{3})
upon termination, there are some intermediate rewards along the way as soon as all the information necessary to evaluate the factors is available. The sum of rewards along a complete trajectory (in red) still equals
{\mathcal{E}}(1\mathchar 59\relax 0\mathchar 59\relax 1)
.

Alternatively, we could evaluate the terms in
Equation

5.2.29

as soon as all the necessary information is available
, meaning as soon as all the variables appearing in a factor have been assigned a value (recall that the values are assigned one random variable at a time, following a fixed order;
Buesing et al. 2020
). This is illustrated in
Figure

5.2
. For example, at the first step of generation the value of
x_{1}
is assigned a value in
\{0\mathchar 59\relax 1\}
, meaning that we can readily evaluate the term
\psi_{3}(x_{1})
. Then at the second step of generation when the value of
x_{2}
is assigned, no new term can be evaluated since all the factors in which
x_{2}
appears also depend on the value of
x_{3}
which has not been assigned yet. And finally once the value of
x_{3}
is known at the final step of generation, we can evaluate the remaining two terms
\psi_{1}(x_{1}\mathchar 59\relax x_{2}\mathchar 59\relax x_{3})+\psi_{2}(x_{2}\mathchar 59\relax x_{3})
. This provides intermediate information along the way.

While in this example
{\mathcal{G}}
is a tree (defeating the purpose of the correction in
Section

5.1.2
), it is important to note that there is nothing preventing us from applying the same idea to an arbitrary pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
, as long as for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
, the energy function at the terminating state can be decomposed as

(5.2.30)

{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}{\mathcal{E}}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})\mathchar 59\relax

where we overloaded the notation
{\mathcal{E}}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})
to denote the intermediate energy received along
\tau
. In our example above, we have for instance
{\mathcal{E}}\big((\cdot\mathchar 59\relax\cdot\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)\rightarrow(x_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\cdot\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)\big)=\psi_{3}(x_{1})
. It is not always possible to have such a decomposition though (
e.g.
, in molecule generation in
Section

2.2.4
). However when it is available,
Pan et al. 2023
introduced a class of losses for GFlowNets called
forward-looking
losses that takes advantage of this decomposition. In particular, the
forward-looking detailed balance
loss (
FL-DB
) can be written with the following residual, for any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
:

(5.2.31)

\Delta_{\mathrm{FL}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DB}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)=\log\frac{\widetilde{F}_{\phi}(s)P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)}{\widetilde{F}_{\phi}(s^{\prime})P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})}+\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax

where
P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
is a forward transition probability, and
\widetilde{F}_{\phi}(s)
is an
offset
state flow function. This represents an offset to the (true) state flow function
F(s)
in the sense that we make full use of the intermediate energy signal
{\mathcal{E}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
that we don’t have to learn. The difference in state flows is related to
\widetilde{F}_{\phi}
via

(5.2.32)

\log F(s^{\prime})-\log F(s)=\big(\log\widetilde{F}_{\phi}(s^{\prime})-\log\widetilde{F}_{\phi}(s)\big)-\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{\alpha}.

Unlike the standard detailed balance loss, the forward looking detailed balance loss does not require an additional reward matching loss for the terminating transition, since it already leverages intermediate information with
{\mathcal{E}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
.
Jang et al. 2024
showed that this it is also possible to learn this decomposition of the energy function.

Under the decomposition of the energy function in
Equation

5.2.30
, we can define the corrected reward as follows on a complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
, in order to satisfy the conditions of
Theorem

5.1.2
:

(5.2.33)

\displaystyle r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})+\alpha\log P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})

\displaystyle r(s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)

\displaystyle=0.

The same way we can consider the reward function
Equation

5.2.1
we used in the previous two sections to be analogous to a “sparse” reward (where the energy is only obtained at the end, only the correction is added along the way), we reshaped the rewards in order to obtain “dense” rewards
(
Ng et al. 1999
)
, while still satisfying the correction in
Equation

5.1.12
. The following proposition, similar to
Proposition

5.2.2
, establishes an equivalence between FL-DB and SQL under this new choice of reward function.

Proposition 5.2.3
.

The forward-looking detailed balance objective (GFlowNet;
Pan et al. 2023
) is proportional to the Soft Q-Learning objective (MaxEnt RL;
Haarnoja et al. 2017
) on the MDP with the reward function defined in
Equation

5.2.33
, in the sense that
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{SQL}}(\phi)=\alpha^{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{FL}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DB}}(\phi)
, with the following correspondence

(5.2.34)

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{\phi}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)

\displaystyle P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\right).

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of
Proposition

5.2.2
. Let
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
be a transition such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
. We our choice of reward function in
Equation

5.2.33
we know that
r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})=-{\mathcal{E}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})+\alpha\log P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})
. Substituting it into the residual of SQL in
Equation

5.2.12
, we get

(5.2.35)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{SQL}}

\displaystyle(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)=Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-\big(r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})+V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)+{\mathcal{E}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})-\alpha\log P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})

(5.2.36)

\displaystyle\qquad+\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)-\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime\prime})\right)

(5.2.37)

\displaystyle=\alpha\left[\log P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)-\log P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})+\log\widetilde{F}_{\phi}(s)-\log\widetilde{F}_{\phi}(s^{\prime})+\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}{\alpha}\right]

(5.2.38)

\displaystyle=\alpha\Delta_{\mathrm{FL}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DB}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)

This concludes the proof, showing that
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{SQL}}(\phi)=\alpha^{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{FL}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DB}}(\phi)
.
∎

Interestingly, the correspondence in
Equation

5.2.34
between the (offset) state flow function and the forward transition probability on the one hand (GFlowNet) and the state-action value function on the other hand (MaxEnt RL) is exactly the same as the one in
Proposition

5.2.2
.

5.2.4.
Policy parametrized SQL & modified detailed balance

As a special case of the decomposition of the energy function we considered in the previous section, suppose that all the states of the states space are terminating
{\mathcal{S}}\equiv{\mathcal{X}}
. We saw an example of this situation in
Section

4.1.4
, and we will come back to this setting in
Chapter

7
in the context of GFlowNets over DAGs. In that case, it is clear that the energy function is defined for all the states
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, and it can trivially be decomposed along a trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
as

(5.2.39)

{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\big({\mathcal{E}}(s_{t+1})-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{t})\big)\mathchar 59\relax

provided that
{\mathcal{E}}(s_{0})=0
; this is not a limiting assumption, since we can always subtract a constant offset
{\mathcal{E}}(s_{0})
to the energy at all states without changing the underlying Gibbs distribution. We will see in
Section

7.4.1
that in some cases the difference in energies between two consecutive states can be computed efficiently. This difference of energies also appears in the acceptance probability of Metropolis-Hastings in
Section

2.1.2
. Using this intermediate signal
{\mathcal{E}}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})={\mathcal{E}}(s_{t+1})-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{t})
in the reward function
Equation

5.2.33
, we can rewrite it as

(5.2.40)

\displaystyle r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}(s_{t})-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{t+1})+\alpha\log P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})

\displaystyle r(s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)

\displaystyle=0.

With this particular choice of rewards, and especially because no reward is received upon termination, we can express the objective of Soft Q-Learning as a function of a policy
\pi_{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
parametrized by
\phi
, instead of a state-value function
Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
; we call this method
\pi
-SQL
.

Proposition 5.2.4

(
\pi
-SQL)
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}=({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax r)
be a MDP such that all the states of
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
are terminating (i.e.,
{\mathcal{S}}\equiv{\mathcal{X}}
), and the reward function satisfies
r(s\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=0
for all states
s
. Then the residual appearing in the objective of Soft Q-Learning (
5.2.12
;
Haarnoja et al. 2017
) can be written as a function of a policy
\pi_{\phi}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
: for any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot

(5.2.41)

\Delta_{\pi\textrm{-}\mathrm{SQL}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)=\alpha\big[\log\pi_{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)-\log\pi_{\phi}(\bot\mid s)+\log\pi_{\phi}(\bot\mid s^{\prime})\big]-r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime}).

Proof.

Since we assume that
r(s\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=0
, we can enforce the fact that
Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=0
in our parametrization of the state-action value function used in the residual of SQL; this grounding at the terminal transitions allows us to identify the state-action value function with a policy. If we define a policy
\pi_{\phi}
as

(5.2.42)

\pi_{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\big(Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s)\big)\right)\mathchar 59\relax

then we have in particular
\pi_{\phi}(\bot\mid s)=\exp(-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s)/\alpha)
based on our observation above. Moreover, we can write the different value functions appearing in the residual of SQL
Equation

5.2.12
as a function of
\pi_{\phi}
alone

(5.2.43)

\displaystyle Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=Q_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s)+V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s)-V_{\mathrm{soft}}^{\phi}(s^{\prime})

(5.2.44)

\displaystyle=\alpha\big[\log\pi_{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)-\log\pi_{\phi}(\bot\mid s)+\log\pi_{\phi}(\bot\mid s^{\prime})\big]

which concludes the proof, by substituting this in the residual of SQL.
∎

We will see in
Section

7.2.4
that when all the states are terminating, it is also possible to modify the detailed balance loss to only depend on a forward transition probability
P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
, without any state flow function
(
Deleu et al. 2022
)
. With an energy function, the corresponding residual can be written, for
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
, as

(5.2.45)

\Delta_{\mathrm{M}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DB}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)=\log\frac{P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)P_{F}^{\phi}(\bot\mid s^{\prime})}{P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})P_{F}^{\phi}(\bot\mid s)}+\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(s^{\prime})-{\mathcal{E}}(s)}{\alpha}.

It can be shown that this modified detailed balance objective is a special case of the forward-looking detailed balance objective introduced in
Section

5.2.3

(
Pan et al. 2023
)
. The following proposition establishes an equivalence between the modified detailed balance loss and
\pi
-SQL with the reward function
Equation

5.2.40
.

Proposition 5.2.5
.

The modified detailed balance objective (GFlowNet;
Deleu et al. 2022
) is proportional to the Soft Q-Learning objective with policy parametrization (MaxEnt RL;
Proposition

5.2.4
) on the MDP with the reward function defined in
Equation

5.2.40
, in the sense that
{\mathcal{L}}_{\pi\textrm{-}\mathrm{SQL}}(\phi)=\alpha^{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DB}}(\phi)
, with the correspondence
\pi_{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of
Proposition

5.2.3
. Let
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
be a transition such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
. With our choice of reward function in
Equation

5.2.40
we know that
r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})={\mathcal{E}}(s)-{\mathcal{E}}(s^{\prime})+\alpha\log P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})
. Substituting it into the residual of
\pi
-SQL in
Equation

5.2.41
, we get

(5.2.46)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\pi\textrm{-}\mathrm{SQL}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)=\alpha\big[\log\pi_{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)-\log\pi_{\phi}(\bot\mid s)+\log\pi_{\phi}(\bot\mid s^{\prime})\big]-r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})

(5.2.47)

\displaystyle\qquad=\alpha\big[\log\pi_{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)-\log\pi_{\phi}(\bot\mid s)+\log\pi_{\phi}(\bot\mid s^{\prime})\big]-\big[{\mathcal{E}}(s)-{\mathcal{E}}(s^{\prime})+\alpha\log P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})]

(5.2.48)

\displaystyle\qquad=\alpha\left[\log\pi_{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)+\log\pi_{\phi}(\bot\mid s^{\prime})-\log P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})-\log\pi_{\phi}(\bot\mid s)+\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(s^{\prime})-{\mathcal{E}}(s)}{\alpha}\right]

(5.2.49)

\displaystyle\qquad=\alpha\left[\log\frac{P_{F}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mid s)P_{F}^{\phi}(\bot\mid s^{\prime})}{P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})P_{F}^{\phi}(\bot\mid s)}+\frac{{\mathcal{E}}(s^{\prime})-{\mathcal{E}}(s)}{\alpha}\right]=\alpha\Delta_{\mathrm{M}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DB}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)

which concludes the proof, showing that
{\mathcal{L}}_{\pi\textrm{-}\mathrm{SQL}}(\phi)=\alpha^{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DB}}(\phi)
.
∎

Figure 5.3
.
Summary of the equivalences between the residuals in MaxEnt RL (top, in each box) and GFlowNet (bottom, in each box) objectives, using the classification of
Figure

5.1
. All objectives can be written as
{\mathcal{L}}(\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}[\Delta^{2}(\cdot)]
, where
\pi_{b}
is a distribution over appropriate quantities (
i.e.
, trajectories, or transitions). The
terminal reward
setting corresponds to
r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})=\alpha\log P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})
&
r(s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})
(
Section

5.2.1
), whereas the
intermediate rewards
setting corresponds to
r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1})+\alpha\log P_{B}(s_{t}\mid s_{t+1})
&
r(s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)=0
(
Section

5.2.3
).

5.3.
Relative entropy regularization

Generalizing the notion of entropy-regularized reinforcement learning considered so far in this chapter, we can consider the problem of reinforcement learning regularized by the
relative entropy
with an “anchor” policy
\mu
. The optimization problem then becomes

(5.3.1)

\pi_{\mathrm{RelEnt}}^{\star}=\argmax_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})-\alpha\mathrm{KL}\big(\pi(\cdot\mid s_{t})\,\|\,\mu(\cdot\mid s_{t})\big)\right].

This type of relative entropy regularization has gained popularity recently with
reinforcement learning from human feedback
(
RLHF
;
Ziegler et al. 2019
;
Stiennon et al. 2020
;
Bai et al. 2022
;
Rafailov et al. 2023
), a framework for aligning AI models. Similar to how the optimal policy in MaxEnt RL could be expressed in terms of soft value functions, it can be shown that the optimal policy maximizing
Equation

5.3.1
can be written, for any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
, as

(5.3.2)

\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{RelEnt}}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=\mu(s^{\prime}\mid s)\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\big(Q^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})-V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s)\big)\right)\mathchar 59\relax

where
Q^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})
and
V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s)
satisfy some generalization of the soft Bellman optimality equations given in
Equation

5.1.3
&
Equation

5.1.4
, taking the following form:

(5.3.3)

\displaystyle Q^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})+V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s^{\prime})

(5.3.4)

\displaystyle V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s)

\displaystyle=\alpha\log\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}\mu(s^{\prime}\mid s)\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}Q^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\right).

Contrary to
Section

5.1.2
, we will consider in this section the case where the reward function of the MDP is
uncorrected
, meaning that
\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})
. Note that the case where
\mu
is the uniform policy does not recover exactly the MaxEnt RL objective in
Equation

5.1.1
, despite the relative entropy to the uniform distribution being tightly related to the entropy; the reason is that
\mu(s^{\prime}\mid s)=1/|\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)|
is not independent of the state
s
, and it would introduce an extra term depending on
\pi
in
Equation

5.3.1
.

5.3.1.
Terminating state distribution associated with the optimal policy

The following proposition shows that the terminating state distribution associated with the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{RelEnt}}
is related to the terminating state distribution associated with the anchor policy
\mu
.

Proposition 5.3.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}=({\mathcal{G}}\mathchar 59\relax r)
be a MDP with a pointed DAG structure
{\mathcal{G}}
, where the reward function satisfies, for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
,
\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})=-{\mathcal{E}}(s_{T})
(with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
). Then the terminating state distribution associated with the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{RelEnt}}
solution of
Equation

5.3.1
satisfies for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}

(5.3.5)

\pi^{\star\top}_{\mathrm{RelEnt}}(x)\propto\mu^{\top}(x)\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x)/\alpha).

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of
Theorem

5.1.2
. We can again write the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{RelEnt}}
in terms of the state value function
V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}
alone, using
Equation

5.3.3
:

(5.3.6)

\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{RelEnt}}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=\mu(s^{\prime}\mid s)\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\big(r(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})+V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s^{\prime})-V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s)\big)\right).

By
Definition

2.2.6
, we can write the terminating state distribution associated with
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{RelEnt}}
for any terminating state
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
as

(5.3.7)

\displaystyle\pi^{\star\top}_{\mathrm{RelEnt}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{RelEnt}}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})

(5.3.8)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\underbrace{\left[\prod_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}\mu(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\right]}_{=\,\mu(\tau)}\exp\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\sum_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}\big(r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})+V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s_{t+1})-V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s_{t})\big)\right)

(5.3.9)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\mu(\tau)\exp\Bigg(\frac{1}{\alpha}\underbrace{\sum_{t=0}^{T_{\tau}}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})}_{=\,-\mathcal{E}(x)}+\underbrace{\vphantom{\sum_{t=0}}V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(\bot)}_{=\,0}-V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s_{0})\Bigg)

(5.3.10)

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(-\mathcal{E}(x)/\alpha)}{\exp(V^{\star}_{\mathrm{soft}}(s_{0})/\alpha)}\underbrace{\sum_{\tau:s_{0}\rightsquigarrow x}\mu(\tau)}_{=\,\mu^{\top}(x)}\propto\mu^{\top}(x)\exp(-{\mathcal{E}}(x)/\alpha).

∎

A similar result can also been found in various contexts in the literature on language model alignment in the case of autoregressive generation
(
Korbak et al. 2022
;
Mudgal et al. 2024
;
Rafailov et al. 2024
)
. Following our remark above noting that the case where the anchor policy
\mu
is uniform does not correspond exactly to MaxEnt RL, it is also important to note that this proposition does not relate to our previous observation from
Section

2.3.3
that the terminating state distribution associated with
\pi^{\star}_{\mathrm{MaxEnt}}
is biased (towards terminating states having multiple trajectories leading to them) in the case where the reward function is uncorrected. Indeed, in general
\mu^{\top}(x)\not\propto n(x)
if
\mu
is the uniform policy, where
n(x)
is the number of complete trajectories leading to
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
.

5.3.2.
Consequences for Bayesian inference

One potential application of this result with relative-entropy regularization is in Bayesian inference
(
Korbak et al. 2022
)
. Recall that by Bayes rule, we know that for some dataset of observations
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
and a new observation
x_{n+1}
, we can write the posterior distribution of a model
\theta
wrt. the new dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{n+1}={\mathcal{D}}_{n}\cup\{x_{n+1}\}
(with the convention
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}=\emptyset
) in terms of the posterior wrt.
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}

(5.3.11)

P(\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}}_{n+1})\propto P(\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}}_{n})P(x_{n+1}\mid\theta)\mathchar 59\relax

provided the observations are mutually independent given
\theta
. If we model the posterior
P(\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
using a GFlowNet (or MaxEnt RL, using the techniques described in
Section

5.1
&
5.2
), then this amounts to finding a policy
\pi_{n}
such that its terminating state distribution matches the posterior:
\pi_{n}^{\top}(\theta)=P(\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
. If we know the policy
\pi_{n}
, then
Proposition

5.3.1
gives us a strategy to find a policy
\pi_{n+1}
such that its terminating state distribution matches the posterior wrt. the updated dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{n+1}
: we simply have to find the optimal policy maximizing the following objective:

(5.3.12)

\pi_{n+1}=\argmax_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim\pi}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})-\mathrm{KL}\big(\pi(\cdot\mid s_{t})\,\|\,\pi_{n}(\cdot\mid s_{t})\big)\right]\mathchar 59\relax

where we set
\alpha=1
, with
\pi_{n}
serving as the anchor policy, and where the reward function is defined such that the corresponding return is equal to the log-likelihood of the new datapoint

(5.3.13)

\sum_{t=0}^{T}r(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})=\log P(x_{n+1}\mid\theta)\mathchar 59\relax

where
s_{T}=\theta
is the terminating state, and
s_{T+1}=\bot
. This guarantees that
\pi_{n+1}^{\top}(\theta)=P(\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}}_{n+1})
. In order to find the solution of
Equation

5.3.12
, we can use any algorithm similar to the ones presented in the previous section specifically adapted to the relative entropy regularization; for example, the recently introduced
relative trajectory balance
objective
(
Venkatraman et al. 2024
)
can be interpreted as Path Consistency Learning applied to the problem of KL-regularized reinforcement learning.

To find the initial policy
\pi_{0}
whose terminating state distribution should match the prior
P(\theta)
, we can use any GFlowNet method described in
Chapter

4
, or their MaxEnt RL counterparts. In particular, if the prior distribution is uniform, we can simply train a GFlowNet with a constant reward
R(\theta)=1
for all models (again,
R
here being a reward in the sense of GFlowNets
Equation

4.1.1
, not the reward
r
in the reinforcement learning counterpart). This will be typically easier than training a GFlowNet to approximate the posterior
P(\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
directly (with the reward
R(\theta)=P({\mathcal{D}}\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
), since the prior is completely data-independent. We will come back to the practical challenges of training a GFlowNet for Bayesian inference in
Section

7.4.3
.

Chapter 6
GFlowNets over General State Spaces

This chapter contains material from the following papers:

•

Salem Lahlou,
Tristan Deleu
, Pablo Lemos, Dinghuai Zhang, Alexandra Volokhova, Alex Hernández-García, Léna Néhale Ezzine, Yoshua Bengio, Nikolay Malkin (2023).
A Theory of Continuous Generative Flow Networks
. International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML).
\notecite
lahlou2023continuousgfn

•

Tristan Deleu
, Yoshua Bengio (2023).
Generative Flow Networks: a Markov Chain Perspective
.
\notecite
deleu2023gfnmarkovchain

So far, we have assumed that the elements of the sample space
{\mathcal{X}}
were discrete and had some compositional structure. This was mainly due to the fact that this case is relatively less explored in the context of variational inference, which has found more applications in continuous settings thanks to the reparametrization trick, as we discussed in
Section

1.2.3
. To expand the scope of applications of generative flow networks, we extend in this chapter the results we have seen previously to more general state spaces; this includes, notably, continuous spaces but also mixtures of discrete (with some compositional aspect) and continuous spaces. The key takeaway of this chapter is that under some conditions, the flow matching losses that were introduced for discrete GFlowNets will still hold in the general case, possibly with minimal modifications. This will play a critical role in
Chapter

8
, where we will be able to perform Bayesian inference over the structure and parameters of Bayesian networks under the unique framework of GFlowNets.

6.1.
Generative flow networks over discrete spaces

Before going deeper into the extensions of generative flow networks to more general spaces, we will briefly revisit what we have seen so far about discrete GFlowNets from a different point of view. In this section, we will show that everything we have achieved with flow networks and pointed DAGs can be cast from the perspective of
Markov chains
.

6.1.1.
Revisiting the flow matching condition

We saw in
Definition

3.1.7
that the forward transition probability is defined in terms of a flow function by normalizing the outgoing edge flow. Conversely, the edge flow
F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})
can be viewed as the product of the state flow and the forward transition probability:
F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F^{\star}(s)P^{\star}_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
; this is an observation we have already made for the boundary condition of detailed balance in
Section

4.1.3
. To characterize a valid Markovian flow, we can rewrite the flow matching condition of
Theorem

3.2.1
not in terms of a conservation law between the incoming and outgoing edge flows, but using the decomposition above in conjunction with
Proposition

3.1.2
. The following proposition provides an alternative perspective on the flow matching condition, in terms of state flows and forward transition probabilities.

Proposition 6.1.1

(Flow matching condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG. A function
F:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
defines the state flow, and
P_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
the forward transition probabilities of a unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
if and only if they satisfy the following condition for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
:

(6.1.1)

F(s^{\prime})=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s).

Moreover, under these conditions, the unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
whose state flow and forward transition probabilities match
F
and
P_{F}
respectively is defined, for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
, by

(6.1.2)

F^{\star}(\tau)=F(s_{0})\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\mathchar 59\relax

with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
.

The proof of this proposition is almost identical to the proof of
Theorem

3.2.1
, and is deferred to
Section

A.1.1
for completeness. Up until now, we considered that the codomain of a forward transition probability
\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
was dependent on the current state
s
(the children of
s
). Alternatively, we could simply write the flow matching condition in
Equation

6.1.1
as

(6.1.3)

F(s^{\prime})=\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\mathchar 59\relax

for all
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
, where we use the convention
P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=0
if
s\notin\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})
. With the exception of the condition on
s^{\prime}
not being the initial state, the equation above looks oddly similar to the definition of
F
being
invariant
under the forward transition probability
P_{F}
. Recall that a vector
{\bm{F}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
is said to be invariant under a stochastic matrix
{\bm{P}}_{F}
called the
transition matrix
whose rows are
P_{F}(\cdot\mid s)
if and only if
{\bm{F}}=[{\bm{P}}_{F}]^{\top}{\bm{F}}
; note that we use the notation
[{\bm{P}}_{F}]^{\top}
to denote the transpose of
{\bm{P}}_{F}
to avoid any confusion with the terminating state distribution associated with
P_{F}
. In order to prepare for the generalization of GFlowNets to arbitrary state spaces, we will therefore make a detour first and view a (discrete) GFlowNet
as if

F
was indeed the invariant measure of a certain transition matrix.

6.1.2.
The cyclic nature of flow networks

The way we sample from a Markovian flow network is to follow its forward transition probability until a terminating transition is taken (
Algorithm

2
), and then restart again from the initial state in order to get a new (independent) sample. Instead of treating independent samples as being completely separate trajectories in a pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
, we can view this process as being a
single
Markov chain that regenerates every time it reaches a special state. We will denote by
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
the canonical (homogeneous) Markov chain with transition probability
P_{F}
and starting at
s_{0}
:

(6.1.4)

{\mathbb{P}}_{s_{0}}(X_{n+1}=s^{\prime}\mid X_{0}=s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax X_{1}=s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax X_{n}=s)={\mathbb{P}}_{s_{0}}(X_{n+1}=s^{\prime}\mid X_{n}=s)=P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\mathchar 59\relax

by the Markov property, and where
{\mathbb{P}}_{s_{0}}(X_{0}=s)=\delta_{s_{0}}(s)
is the Dirac measure at
s_{0}
. We use the notation “
{\mathbb{P}}_{s_{0}}
” to denote the joint distribution of the chain (and later “
\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}
” to denote expectations under this distribution). In the context of Markov chains,
P_{F}
is also called a
Markov kernel
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 6.1
.
Discrete GFlowNet as a recurrent Markov chain. (a) The pointed DAG structure of a GFlowNet, with the flow going from the initial state
s_{0}
to the terminal state
\bot
. (b) The “wrapped-around” version of the same GFlowNet, where the initial state has been merged with the terminal state
s_{0}\equiv\bot
. We can place a recurrent Markov chain over this state space that regenerates every time it hits
s_{0}
.

A flow network represented as a pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
with a forward transition probability
P_{F}
can be transformed into such a Markov chain by simply “wrapping around” the graph and merging the terminal state
\bot
with the initial state
s_{0}
, as depicted in
Figure

6.1
. It is clear that whenever this Markov chain goes through
s_{0}\equiv\bot
, this would be effectively equivalent to starting a new complete trajectory in our standard formulation. Furthermore,
{\mathcal{G}}
was originally a DAG where all the complete trajectories are guaranteed to eventually end at the terminal state. This ensures that
s_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}
is a
recurrent state
, meaning that the Markov chain eventually comes back to
s_{0}
with probability 1:

(6.1.5)

{\mathbb{P}}_{s_{0}}(\exists n\geq 1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\,X_{n}=s_{0})=1.

More than being simply recurrent, the Markov chain is also
positive recurrent
, meaning the expected time to return to
s_{0}
is finite. Moreover, since we assumed in
Section

2.2.2
that
{\mathcal{G}}
was structured in such a way that all the states of
{\mathcal{S}}
are accessible from the initial state (in the sense that there exists a partial trajectory), and that the terminal state is also itself accessible from all the states, this Markov chain is also clearly
irreducible
, meaning that all the states are accessible from any other state (possibly via
s_{0}\equiv\bot
).

6.1.3.
Generative flow network as a Markov chain

In the previous section, we showed how we could transform a flow network in order to create an equivalent irreducible and positive recurrent Markov chain. In this section, we will do the opposite: starting with a Markov chain with those properties, we will show that under appropriate boundary conditions we can sample objects proportionally to some reward function
R(x)
, just like we did with GFlowNets. We will assume here that the state space
{\mathcal{S}}
is discrete, but not necessarily finite anymore, and that we have a Markov chain with transition probability
P_{F}
that is
irreducible
and
positive recurrent
. Unlike the case where
{\mathcal{S}}
is finite though, positive recurrence is not an immediate property when the chain is merely recurrent and
{\mathcal{S}}
is infinite
(
Deleu & Bengio 2023
)
.

The fact that the Markov chain is recurrent guarantees that it will eventually return back to its initial state
s_{0}
an infinite amount of times. We can define the
return time

\sigma_{s_{0}}
as being the (random) time the chain first goes back to the initial state:

(6.1.6)

\sigma_{s_{0}}\triangleq\inf\{n\geq 1\mid X_{n}=s_{0}\}.

Excursions of the Markov chain between two consecutive returns to
s_{0}
are independent by the strong Markov property, since
\sigma_{s_{0}}
is a
stopping time
(
i.e.
, the decision whether the chain returns to
s_{0}
for the first time does not depend on future information). In other words, the independence for two different trajectories through a flow network is preserved even with a single recurrent Markov chain running indefinitely. The return time
\sigma_{s_{0}}
is guaranteed to be finite in expectation thanks to the positive recurrence of the chain (in particular, it is finite with probability
1
). Moreover, the irreducibility and positive recurrence of the chain ensure the existence of a measure
\lambda
which is
invariant
for
P_{F}
:
\lambda
is a measure over
{\mathcal{S}}
that satisfies
\lambda P_{F}=\lambda
, in the sense that

(6.1.7)

\lambda(s^{\prime})=\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\lambda(s)\mathchar 59\relax

for all
s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
. Note that
\lambda
is not necessarily normalized. The following theorem shows the existence of a such an invariant measure, unique up to a multiplicative constant.

Theorem 6.1.2

(Existence of an invariant measure;
Douc et al. 2018
, Theorem 7.2.1)
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be an irreducible and positive recurrent Markov chain over a discrete state space
{\mathcal{S}}
, starting at
s_{0}
and with transition probability
P_{F}
. There exists a non-trivial invariant measure
\lambda
for
P_{F}
(i.e.,
\lambda P_{F}=\lambda
), unique up to a multiplicative positive constant, defined for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
by

(6.1.8)

\lambda(s)=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\left[\sum_{n=0}^{\sigma_{s_{0}}-1}\mathds{1}(X_{n}=s)\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n<\sigma_{s_{0}})\mathds{1}(X_{n}=s)\big].

In particular,
\lambda
is the unique invariant measure for
P_{F}
such that
\lambda(s_{0})=1
.

Intuitively, the way
\lambda(s)
is constructed is by measuring, on average, how often the chain hits
s
before going back to its initial state. This quantity is related to the
occupancy measure
in the context of reinforcement learning
(
Szepesvári 2020
)
. It may seem surprising that we could hit a state
s
multiple times before returning to
s_{0}
when we were working exclusively with pointed DAGs for GFlowNets (
i.e.
, we couldn’t visit the same state multiple times since it would violate acyclicity). This will not invalidate our results further, since the most important property is the
positive recurrence
of the chain, to guarantee that we can return to
s_{0}
in finite time almost surely.

The same way we defined the terminating state distribution in
Definition

2.2.6
as being the marginal over trajectories terminating at a certain state, we can define it more generally for any positive recurrent Markov chain as being the distribution of states reached by the chain right before going back to
s_{0}
.

Definition 6.1.3

(Terminating state distribution)
.

Given an irreducible and positive recurrent Markov chain over
{\mathcal{S}}
, with an initial state
s_{0}
and a transition probability
P_{F}
, the
terminating state probability distribution
is defined as the marginal distribution of the chain right before returning to its initial state: for all
x\in{\mathcal{S}}

(6.1.9)

P_{F}^{\top}(x)\triangleq{\mathbb{P}}_{s_{0}}(X_{\sigma_{s_{0}}-1}=x)=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(X_{\sigma_{s_{0}}-1}=x)\big].

We can show that the terminating state distribution is again a properly defined probability distribution (
i.e.
, non-negative, and sums to 1); see
Proposition

A.2.1
for a result analogous to
Proposition

2.2.7
for flow networks, but this time working exclusively with Markov chains. The support of
P_{F}^{\top}
is a subset
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
corresponding to the states having non-zero probability of transitioning to
s_{0}
(
i.e.
, the “parents” of
s_{0}
):
{\mathcal{X}}=\{x\in{\mathcal{S}}\mid P_{F}(s_{0}\mid x)>0\}
. The terminating state distribution is actually related to invariant measures of
P_{F}
in an interesting way.

Proposition 6.1.4
.

The terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
is related to the invariant measure
\lambda
defined in
Theorem

6.1.2
by,
\forall x\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
P_{F}^{\top}(x)=\lambda(x)P_{F}(s_{0}\mid x)
.

Proof.

For any
n\geq 0
and any
x\in{\mathcal{S}}
, we have

(6.1.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n<\sigma_{s_{0}})\mathds{1}(X_{n}=x)\big]

\displaystyle P_{F}(s_{0}\mid x)=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n<\sigma_{s_{0}})\mathds{1}(X_{n}=x)P_{F}(s_{0}\mid X_{n})\big]

(6.1.11)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n<\sigma_{s_{0}})\mathds{1}(X_{n}=x)\mathbb{E}_{X_{n}}[\mathds{1}(X_{1}=s_{0})]\big]

(6.1.12)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n<\sigma_{s_{0}})\mathds{1}(X_{n}=x)\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}[\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}=s_{0})\mid X_{0:n}]\big]

(6.1.13)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n<\sigma_{s_{0}})\mathds{1}(X_{n}=x)\mathds{1}(X_{n+1}=s_{0})\big]

(6.1.14)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(\sigma_{s_{0}}=n+1)\mathds{1}(X_{n}=x)\big].

In details, we used an equivalent definition of
P_{F}(s_{0}\mid X_{n})
in
Equation

6.1.11
as the expectation of the chain moving to
s_{0}
after one step (
\mathds{1}(X_{1}=s_{0})
) starting at
X_{n}
, the Markov property in
Equation

6.1.12
, the law of total expectation in
Equation

6.1.13
, and finally the definition of the return time
\sigma_{s_{0}}

Equation

6.1.6
in
Equation

6.1.14
. Using the definition of the invariant measure
\lambda
, we then obtain the expected result:

(6.1.15)

\displaystyle\lambda(x)P_{F}(s_{0}\mid x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n<\sigma_{s_{0}})\mathds{1}(X_{n}=x)\big]P_{F}(s_{0}\mid x)

(6.1.16)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(\sigma_{s_{0}}=n+1)\mathds{1}(X_{n}=x)\big]

(6.1.17)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(\sigma_{s_{0}}=n)\mathds{1}(X_{n-1}=x)\big]

(6.1.18)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(X_{\sigma_{s_{0}}-1}=x)\big]=P_{F}^{\top}(x).

∎

It is important to observe that while it is related to an invariant measure,
P_{F}^{\top}(x)
itself is
not
invariant for
P_{F}
, despite being equal to
\lambda(x)
up to a multiplicative factor (but dependent on
x
). This is the key difference with most of the literature on MCMC which constructs a Markov chain whose invariant distribution is the distribution of interest (see also
Section

6.1.4
).

Boundary condition

Similar to the flow network perspective, our objective is not to find
P_{F}^{\top}
given the Markov kernel
P_{F}
, but instead the inverse problem of finding a
P_{F}
such that its corresponding terminating state distribution matches the target distribution (
i.e.
, the Gibbs distribution in
Equation

2.1.1
). Just like we did in
Chapter

4
, we have to introduce
boundary conditions
that the Markov kernel
P_{F}
and one of its invariant measures
F
must satisfy. In preparation for the more general case that we will see in
Section

6.2
, we will say that they satisfy the boundary condition for a finite measure
R
over
{\mathcal{X}}
if for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}

(6.1.19)

F(x)P_{F}(s_{0}\mid x)=R(x).

Following the intuition that we “wrapped-around” the pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
like in
Figure

6.1
with
s_{0}\equiv\bot
, this condition above is identical to the one we saw initially for generative flow networks in
Equation

4.1.2
. If
P_{F}
admits an invariant measure
F
that satisfies the boundary condition above, then we obtain the counterpart of the fundamental theorem of GFlowNet in
Theorem

4.1.1
, but this time working exclusively with Markov chains.

Theorem 6.1.5
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be an irreducible and positive recurrent Markov chain over
{\mathcal{S}}
, with initial state
s_{0}
and transition probability
P_{F}
, that admits an invariant measure
F
such that
\forall x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
F(x)P_{F}(s_{0}\mid x)=R(x)
, where
R
is a finite measure on
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
. Then the terminating state probability distribution satisfies
\forall x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\propto R(x)
.

Proof.

Since
F
is an invariant measure of
P_{F}
, by unicity of the invariant measure of
P_{F}
up to a multiplicative constant (
Theorem

6.1.2
), there exists a constant
\gamma>0
such that
F=\gamma\lambda
, where
\lambda
is the invariant measure defined in
Equation

6.1.8
. Using
Proposition

6.1.4
and the boundary condition
F(x)P_{F}(s_{0}\mid x)=R(x)
, we get for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}

(6.1.20)

P_{F}^{\top}(x)=\lambda(x)P_{F}(s_{0}\mid x)=\frac{1}{\gamma}F(x)P_{F}(s_{0}\mid x)=\frac{R(x)}{\gamma}.

In fact, since
P_{F}^{\top}
is a probability distribution (
Proposition

A.2.1
), the multiplicative constant happens to be
\gamma=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}}R(x)
(
i.e.
, the partition function).
∎

In practice though, showing that a measure
F
is invariant proves to be at least as computationally expensive as our original problem of sampling from the Gibbs distribution. Indeed, verifying invariance involves in particular to check that
Equation

6.1.7
is satisfied for
s^{\prime}=s_{0}
, where we would have to sum over all the elements of
{\mathcal{X}}
