; this is precisely what we wanted to avoid with the intractable partition function
Z
of the Gibbs distribution in
Equation

2.1.1
. In standard GFlowNets, it is common practice to only check this for any
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
(
Proposition

6.1.1
). We can show that these two perspectives are in fact equivalent in the discrete case, and checking
Equation

6.1.7
for all states except
s_{0}
still implies invariance over the whole state space, including the initial state; see
Proposition

A.2.2
.

6.1.4.
Comparison with Markov chain Monte Carlo methods

As the name implies, Markov chains play a central role in
Markov chain Monte Carlo
methods (MCMC; see also
Section

2.1.2
). GFlowNets and MCMC methods have both been introduced to solve the same problem: sampling from a probability distribution that is defined up to a normalization constant, such as the Gibbs distribution. One of the main advantages of viewing GFlowNets from the perspective of Markov chains is that it places them under the same theoretical framework as MCMC, highlighting the similarities and differences between both methods. These differences are summarized in
Table

6.1
.

Table 6.1
.
Comparison between MCMC methods and GFlowNets, from the perspective of Markov chains, defined on a discrete state space
{\mathcal{S}}
with a positive recurrent Markov kernel
P_{F}
& invariant measure/distribution
F
. In both cases, the objective is to approximate a target distribution
\propto R(x)
. The row “GFlowNet” corresponds to the Markov chain perspective introduced in
Section

6.1.3
.

Sample

Conditions

Target distribution

Sampling

space

(+ positive recurrence)

\propto R(x)

MCMC

{\mathcal{S}}

Aperiodicity

=F(x)

Asymptotic

(convergence)

(invariant dist.)

(correlated)

GFlowNet

{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}

Boundary condition

=P_{F}^{\top}(x)

Finite time

(marginal)

(terminating state dist.)

(independent)

Recall that the goal of MCMC methods is to construct a Markov chain whose invariant distribution matches the target distribution defined up to a normalization constant. Samples from the distribution are then obtained by running the Markov chain until convergence to the invariant distribution, which typically requires to run the Markov chain for a long (burn-in) period. The convergence of iterates
\{P^{n}(\cdot\mid s_{0})\}_{n\geq 0}
to the invariant distribution is guaranteed by the
ergodicity
of the chain (
i.e.
, positive recurrent,
and
aperiodic). In contrast, the Markov chain related to a GFlowNet as described in previous section is only required to be positive recurrent in order to guarantee the existence of an invariant measure
F
, but
no guarantee on the convergence to
F
is necessary
. Moreover, while the Markov kernel
P_{F}
of MCMC methods needs to be carefully built to ensure that the invariant distribution matches the target distribution, the invariant measure of the Markov kernel in a GFlowNet may be arbitrary, as long as it matches the boundary condition
Equation

6.1.19
; there may be multiple Markov kernels with different invariant distributions yielding the same terminating state distribution, as mentioned in
Section

4.1.1
. This could explain why the Markov kernels in MCMC methods are typically handcrafted (
e.g.
, Metropolis-Hastings in
Algorithm

1
), as opposed to GFlowNets where
P_{F}
is learned (
e.g.
, with neural networks). For completeness, we note that some components of the kernel in MCMC may also be learned, but this would often be limited to the proposal distribution of Metropolis-Hastings for example
(
Wang et al. 2018
)
.

Probably the main difference between GFlowNets and MCMC is the relation between the invariant measure/distribution of the Markov chain and the target distribution. While MCMC requires the invariant distribution to match the target distribution, GFlowNets only require the marginal distribution of the chain (
i.e.
, the terminating state probability distribution;
Definition

6.1.3
) to match the target distribution. The state space of the chain in MCMC therefore corresponds to the sample space of the target distribution; we saw in
Section

2.1.1
that the chain moves exclusively between valid elements of the sample space. As a consequence, these chains are known to poorly mix, leading to slow convergence to the invariant distribution, especially in the presence of multiple modes
(
Robert et al. 2018
;
Roy 2020
)
. On the other hand, the Markov chain associated with a GFlowNet is constructed on an
augmented
state space
{\mathcal{S}}
, broader than the sample space
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
, allowing the chain to use these intermediate steps to move between modes more easily. It is worth noting that there exist some approaches such as Hamiltonian Monte Carlo methods (HMC;
Neal 1993
;
MacKay 2003
), where the target distribution is the marginal of the invariant distribution over an augmented space.

Finally, since MCMC methods rely on the convergence to the invariant distribution, samples of the target distribution are only guaranteed asymptotically. Moreover, consecutive samples are correlated by the Markov kernel
P_{F}
, and additional post-processing techniques are required to reduce the effect of the cross-correlation between samples. Conversely, samples of a GFlowNet are obtained in finite time thanks to the positive recurrence of the Markov chain, and are guaranteed to be independent from one another because of the strong Markov property.

6.2.
Generative flow networks over general state spaces

One of the major obstacles to define a GFlowNet over continuous states spaces is that the notion of pointed DAG, which was essential throughout
Chapters

3
and
4
, would not make sense anymore in a continuous state space. We will see in this section that approaching this from the angle of Markov chains, as we did in the previous section, will allow us to more naturally transition to more general spaces without relying so heavily on DAGs anymore. Before going into the details of this extension though, we need to first establish what we mean by “general state space”. This is done via the concept of
measurable space
from measure theory.

Definition 6.2.1

(Measurable space)
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a state space. The pair
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
is called a
measurable space
if
\Sigma
is a
\sigma
-algebra associated to
{\mathcal{S}}
,
i.e.
,
\Sigma
is a set of sets of
{\mathcal{S}}
satisfying

(1)

{\mathcal{S}}\in\Sigma
;

(2)

\Sigma
is closed under complementation:
\forall B\in\Sigma
,
{\mathcal{S}}\backslash B\in\Sigma
;

(3)

\Sigma
is closed under countable unions: if
\forall n\mathchar 59\relax B_{n}\in\Sigma
, then
\cup_{n\geq 0}B_{n}\in\Sigma
.

An example of measurable space is
{\mathbb{R}}
along with the
Borel
\sigma
-algebra
, which is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the open sets of
{\mathbb{R}}
. Measurable spaces are critical in order to define notions of
measures
over general state spaces, as well as the notion of
measurable functions
, where the preimage of any measurable set (element of the
\sigma
-algebra) remains a measurable set in the domain of that function. In this thesis, we will always assume that the measures
\mu
we consider are positive and
\sigma
-finite, meaning that
{\mathcal{S}}
is the countable union of measurable sets
(B_{n})_{n\geq 0}
with
\mu(B_{n})<\infty
.

In the discrete case, having a pointed DAG was convenient to structure how we could move through the state space. But what is more important is how
P_{F}
was defined, which in itself captures this structure locally: if we are in a certain state, it encodes how can we move to the next state and with what probability. This can be naturally extended to measurable state spaces through the notion of
Markov kernel
, a name we already used in
Section

6.1.2
. A Markov kernel gives us a probability distribution that we can transition from while being in a state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

Definition 6.2.2

(Markov kernel)
.

Let
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
be a measurable state space. A function
\kappa:{\mathcal{S}}\times\Sigma\rightarrow[0\mathchar 59\relax\infty)
is called a positive
\sigma
-finite
transition kernel
if

(1)

For any
B\in\Sigma
, the mapping
s\mapsto\kappa(s\mathchar 59\relax B)
is measurable, where the space
[0\mathchar 59\relax\infty)
is associated with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{B}}([0\mathchar 59\relax\infty))
;

(2)

For any
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, the mapping
B\mapsto\kappa(s\mathchar 59\relax B)
is a positive
\sigma
-finite measure on
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
.

Furthermore, if the mappings
\kappa(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)
are probability distributions (
i.e.
,
\kappa(s\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{S}})=1
), the transition kernel is called a
Markov kernel
.

Similar to the discrete case, we will denote by
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
the canonical Markov chain
(
Petritis 2015
)
following the Markov kernel
P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)
. We use the notation “
P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)
” for a Markov kernel (instead of
\kappa(s\mathchar 59\relax B)
) by analogy with the forward transition probabilities
P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
in the discrete case; this new notation with “
s
” (the state we are transitioning from) as the first argument highlights that we are no longer working exclusively in discrete space. We will use once again the notation
{\mathbb{P}}_{s}
to denote the joint distribution of the Markov chain starting at some state
s
. We leave the question of
where
this Markov chain starts (the “initial state”) for the next section.

Although we defined transition kernels over a single space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
(
i.e.
, we are transitioning into the same space), it is easy to extend this definition to transition kernels from one space to a different one
(
Petritis 2015
)
; see
Section

A.2.3
for details. This allows us to also define in
Section

A.2.3
different operations over transition kernels, such as the
product kernel

\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}
, or the
composition kernel

\kappa_{1}\cdot\kappa_{2}
, which will be useful in what follows.

Finally, a property on which we have not focused our attention too much in discrete spaces is the notion of
irreducibility
(
i.e.
, all the states are accessible from any other state); this was also a necessary property when we talked about pointed DAGs, as we discussed in
Section

6.1.1
. On a measurable state space, irreducibility encodes a notion of accessibility that can be formalized for sets that are not “degenerate”, in the sense of a measure
\phi
. This is called
\phi
-irreducibility
.

Definition 6.2.3

(
\phi
-irreducibility)
.

Let
\phi
be a measure over a measurable space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
. A Markov chain
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is said to be
\phi
-irreducible
if any set
B\in\Sigma
such that
\phi(B)>0
is accessible, in the sense that for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}

(6.2.1)

{\mathbb{P}}_{s}(\eta_{B}<\infty)>0\mathchar 59\relax

where
\eta_{B}=\inf\{n\geq 0\mid X_{n}\in B\}
is the hitting time of the Markov chain to
B
.

In what follows, instead of being the Gibbs distribution
Equation

2.1.1
, or some distribution defined by a reward function up to a normalization constant
Equation

4.1.1
, our objective will be to sample from a distribution
P^{\star}
defined from a positive and finite measure
R
over the measurable space
({\mathcal{X}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}})
by

(6.2.2)

P^{\star}(B)\propto R(B)\mathchar 59\relax

for all
B\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}
, where
\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}
is the trace
\sigma
-algebra of the subsets of
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
. This distribution is well-defined since we assumed that
R
was finite. This section will follow loosely the structure of
Section

6.1.3
: (1) we will first ensure that there exists a notion of terminating state distribution, by taking inspiration from the “wrapped-around” construction of
Section

6.1.2
; (2) then we will introduce an extension of positive recurrence for Markov chains on measurable state spaces to guarantee the existence of an invariant measure; and (3) finally, we will combine invariance with a boundary condition to find a Markov kernel whose corresponding terminating state distribution matches the target distribution
P^{\star}
.

6.2.1.
Creation of an atom via the splitting technique

The key property preserved by wrapping around the state space at
s_{0}\equiv\bot
in
Section

6.1.2
was that the Markov chain was effectively “regenerating” every time it was returning to the initial state
s_{0}
, thanks to the (strong) Markov property. In this context,
\{s_{0}\}
is called an
atom
of the Markov chain
(
Meyn & Tweedie 1993
)
, which informally corresponds to the chain “forgetting” about the past every time it goes through any state in the atom. We can generalize this notion to Markov chains over general state spaces.

Definition 6.2.4

(Atom)
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be a Markov chain over a measurable space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
, with Markov kernel
P_{F}
. A set
A\in\Sigma
is called an
atom
if there exists a probability measure
\nu
over
{\mathcal{S}}
such that
\forall s\in A
, and
\forall B\in\Sigma
,

(6.2.3)

P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\nu(B).

The notion of atom is evident for singletons in discrete spaces, but in general Markov chains may not contain any accessible atom. Although it was not interpreted this way, we can view the augmentation of the state space in
Lahlou et al. 2023
with a terminal state
\bot\notin{\mathcal{S}}
as a way to create an accessible artificial atom at
\bot
, if we were to also (informally) wrap around the state space at
s_{0}\equiv\bot
as in
Section

6.1.2
. We will come back to this construction of an explicit atom in
Section

6.3
.

Besides introducing an artificial atom though, which requires changes to the definition of the state space, we will show in this section how the
split chain
construction
(
Nummelin 1978
)
can be used as an alternative way to create a pseudo-atom for a large class of Markov chains, without ever having to change the state space. Instead of introducing a new state, we will use a set
{\mathcal{X}}\in\Sigma
that satisfies a
minorization condition
. The choice of the notation
{\mathcal{X}}
is not coincidental because this set will eventually be the sample space of the corresponding terminating state distribution that we will define in the next section (as well as the sample space of
Equation

6.2.2
).

Definition 6.2.5

(Minorization condition;
Nummelin 1978
)
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be a
\phi
-irreducible Markov chain over a measurable space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
, with Markov kernel
P_{F}
. A set
{\mathcal{X}}\in\Sigma
such that
\phi({\mathcal{X}})>0
is said to satisfy the
minorization condition
if there exists a non-negative measurable function
\varepsilon
such that
\varepsilon^{-1}((0\mathchar 59\relax+\infty))={\mathcal{X}}
(
i.e.
,
{\mathcal{X}}
is the set on which
\varepsilon
is positive), and a probability measure
\nu
such that,
\forall s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
\forall B\in\Sigma
,

(6.2.4)

P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)\geq\varepsilon(s)\nu(B).

Taking
B={\mathcal{S}}
in the inequality above, we can see that
\varepsilon
is necessarily bounded, with
\varepsilon(s)\in[0\mathchar 59\relax 1]
; as such, we can interpret
\varepsilon(s)
as a probability. This minorization condition is not particularly interesting when
s\notin{\mathcal{X}}
, as it simply implies that
P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)\geq 0
. When
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
though, this allows us to interpret a fixed Markov kernel
P_{F}
satisfying the minorization condition as a mixture of two Markov kernels, whose mixture weights depend on
\varepsilon(x)
:

(6.2.5)

P_{F}(x\mathchar 59\relax B)=\big(1-\varepsilon(x)\big)R_{\nu}(x\mathchar 59\relax B)+\varepsilon(x)\nu(B)\mathchar 59\relax

and where
R_{\nu}(x\mathchar 59\relax B)
is a “remainder” Markov kernel that can be defined as

(6.2.6)

R_{\nu}(x\mathchar 59\relax B)=\mathds{1}(\varepsilon(x)<1)\frac{P_{F}(x\mathchar 59\relax B)-\varepsilon(x)\nu(B)}{1-\varepsilon(x)}+\mathds{1}(\varepsilon(x)=1)\nu(B).

The exact form of the remainder kernel is not as important as the fact that it is a properly defined Markov kernel whose existence is guaranteed when
P_{F}
satisfies the minorization condition. The important property to note is that the second kernel in the mixture
Equation

6.2.5
,
\nu(B)
, is completely independent of
x
—only the mixture weight
\varepsilon(x)
depends on it. We can interpret
Equation

6.2.5
as follows: (1) we first select which of the two kernels to apply, with probability
\varepsilon(x)
, and upon the selection of the second kernel (2) we “reset” the Markov chain with
\nu(B)
. Using the terminology of GFlowNets used in previous chapters, and connecting with the Markov kernel
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
of
Lahlou et al. 2023
detailed in
Section

6.3
(we use a different notation to distinguish it from the Markov kernel
P_{F}
considered here, to avoid confusion), we can view
\varepsilon(x)\approx\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(x\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})
the probability of terminating at
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
.

This suggests the construction of a
split chain

(Z_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, where each element can be broken down into
Z_{n}=(X_{n}\mathchar 59\relax Y_{n})
, with
X_{n}
being a state in
{\mathcal{S}}
, and
Y_{n}
being a binary variable indicating which of the two kernels in the mixture
Equation

6.2.5
to select at the next step. This is a Markov chain over the product space
({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma^{\prime})=\big({\mathcal{S}}\times\{0\mathchar 59\relax 1\}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma\otimes\sigma(\{0\mathchar 59\relax 1\})\big)
, with Markov kernel
P_{F}^{\mathrm{split}}=Q_{\nu}\otimes b_{\varepsilon}
, where

(6.2.7)

\displaystyle Q_{\nu}((x\mathchar 59\relax y)\mathchar 59\relax B)

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}(y=0)R_{\nu}(x\mathchar 59\relax B)+\mathds{1}(y=1)\nu(B)

\displaystyle B\in\Sigma

(6.2.8)

\displaystyle b_{\varepsilon}(x\mathchar 59\relax C)

\displaystyle=\big(1-\varepsilon(x)\big)\delta_{0}(C)+\varepsilon(x)\delta_{1}(C)

\displaystyle C\in\sigma(\{0\mathchar 59\relax 1\})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where
\delta_{y}
is the Dirac measure at
y
, and
\sigma(\{0\mathchar 59\relax 1\})
is the discrete
\sigma
-algebra on
\{0\mathchar 59\relax 1\}
. The notions of product
\sigma
-algebra
\Sigma\otimes\sigma(\{0\mathchar 59\relax 1\})
and product kernel
Q_{\nu}\otimes b_{\varepsilon}
are defined in
Section

A.2.3
. This construction, illustrated in
Figure

6.2
, is similar to splitting the terminating states
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
in discrete GFlowNets into transitions
x\rightarrow x^{\top}
, were
x^{\top}
has no children except the terminal state
\bot

(
Malkin et al. 2022
)
. It is easy to show that the set
{\mathcal{X}}\times\{1\}\in\Sigma^{\prime}
is an atom of the split chain
(
Nummelin 1978
)
. We will call this atom
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}\triangleq{\mathcal{X}}\times\{1\}
, by analogy with the initial state in discrete flow networks, which plays a similar role (as an atom, although there exist subtle differences between
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
and
\{s_{0}\}
; see the proof of
Theorem

6.3.7
for details).

(a)

(b)

Figure 6.2
.
Construction of an atom via the splitting technique. (a) Illustration of the atom
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}={\mathcal{X}}\times\{1\}
created from
{\mathcal{X}}
. Once the Markov chain reaches the atom
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
, it transitions to its next state with the kernel
\nu(\cot)
, independent of
x
. (b) The split chain
Z_{n}=(X_{n}\mathchar 59\relax Y_{n})
, where
X_{n}
are elements of
{\mathcal{S}}
, and
Y_{n}
is a binary value indicating which kernel to choose next: if
Y_{n}=0
, the remainder kernel
R_{\nu}(X_{n}\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)
is picked, otherwise it is
\nu(\cdot)
representing the “end” of a trajectory with a corresponding sample from the terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
(
Definition

6.2.8
).

6.2.2.
Harris recurrence and invariant measures

Similar to the discrete case, we will also assume
some form of recurrence for the Markov kernel in addition to (
\phi
-)irreducibility, in order to guarantee the existence of an invariant measure for
P_{F}
. Although other notions of recurrence exist for measurable state spaces, we will use a stronger notion called
Harris recurrence
, which can be seen as a counterpart of positive recurrence in discrete space.

Definition 6.2.6

(Harris recurrence)
.

A
\phi
-irreducible Markov chain
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
over a measurable state space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
is said to be
Harris recurrent
if for all set
B\in\Sigma
such that
\phi(B)>0
, any sequence starting in
B
eventually returns back to
B
in finite time with probability 1:

(6.2.9)

\forall s\in B\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\qquad{\mathbb{P}}_{s}(\sigma_{B}<\infty)=1\mathchar 59\relax

where
\sigma_{B}=\inf\{n\geq 1\mid X_{n}\in B\}
is the return time of the Markov chain to
B
(similar to
Equation

6.1.6
).

The condition that the Markov chain returns to any accessible set with probability
1
is reminiscent
of the “finitely absorbing” condition of
Lahlou et al. 2023
that requires all trajectories to be of bounded length as we will see in
Section

6.3.1
. A measure
\lambda
over
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
is said to be
invariant
for
P_{F}
if for any bounded measurable function
f:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
we have

(6.2.10)

\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}f(s^{\prime})\lambda(ds^{\prime})=\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{S}}}f(s^{\prime})\lambda(ds)P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime}).

This equation transposes the notion of invariance in the same way
Equation

6.1.7
did in the discrete case as an equality between two measures; take for example
f(s)=\mathds{1}_{B}(s)
. We will often use the response of an arbitrary bounded measurable function
f
to prove equality between measures, especially in
Section

6.3
. The notation
\int f(s)\lambda(ds)
must be understood as the integral of
f
wrt. the measure
\lambda
, and can often be interpreted as
\int f(s)g(s)ds
with some appropriate function
g
(called the
density
of the measure
\lambda
, or its
Radon-Nikodym derivative
); we will come back to densities in
Section

6.3.5
. The following theorem shows the existence of such an invariant measure for a Harris recurrent Markov chain, and is the counterpart of
Theorem

6.1.2
for general state spaces.

Theorem 6.2.7

(Existence of an invariant measure)
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be a Harris recurrent Markov chain over a measurable state space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
, with Markov kernel
P_{F}
, such that
{\mathcal{X}}
satisfies the minorization condition in
Definition

6.2.5
. Then there exists a non-trivial invariant measure
\lambda
for
P_{F}

Equation

6.2.10
, unique up to a positive multiplicative constant, defined for all bounded measurable functions
f:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
by

(6.2.11)

\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}f(s)\lambda(ds)=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\Bigg[\sum_{n=1}^{\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}}f(X_{n})\Bigg]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n\leq\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})f(X_{n})\big].

Moreover,
\lambda
is the unique invariant measure such that
\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\varepsilon(s)\lambda(ds)=1
, where
\varepsilon
is the measurable function in the minorization condition.

Proof.

The existence and the form of the invariant measure
\lambda
are given in
(
Nummelin 1978
, Theorem 3)
. It is therefore sufficient to prove that
\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\varepsilon(s)\lambda(ds)=1
. We first show that for any
n\geq 1
, we have

(6.2.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n\leq\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})\varepsilon(X_{n})\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n\leq\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})\mathbb{E}_{Z_{n-1}}[\mathds{1}(Y_{1}=1)\mid X_{1}]\big]

(6.2.13)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n\leq\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}[\mathds{1}(Y_{n}=1)\mid Z_{0:n-1}\mathchar 59\relax X_{n}]\big]

(6.2.14)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n\leq\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})\mathds{1}(Y_{n}=1)\big]

(6.2.15)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}=n)\big]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used an equivalent definition of
\varepsilon(X_{n})
in
Equation

6.2.12
as being the probability of setting the indicator variable
Y
of the split chain to
1
at the next step (
\mathds{1}(Y_{1}=1)
) based on
Equation

6.2.8
, the Markov property of the chain
(Z_{n})_{n\geq 0}
in
Equation

6.2.13
, the law of total expectation in
Equation

6.2.14
, and finally the definition of
\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}
in
Equation

6.2.15
as being the return time to
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
, where we necessarily have
Y=1
. Therefore, using the form of the invariant measure
\lambda
, and since
\varepsilon
is a bounded measurable function (guaranteeing the existence of the integral), we have

(6.2.16)

\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\varepsilon(s)\lambda(ds)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n\leq\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})\varepsilon(X_{n})\big]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}=n)\big]=1\mathchar 59\relax

where we were able to conclude since the split chain
(Z_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is also Harris recurrent, meaning that it must return to
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
with probability 1 (
Definition

6.2.6
).
∎

Since
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
is an atom of the split chain, the kernel
\nu
in the mixture
Equation

6.2.5
is guaranteed to be selected to transition from any state of
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
; this justifies our notation
\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}[\cdot]
to denote
\mathbb{E}_{z}[\cdot]
for any
z\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
(
i.e.
, the exact initial state
z
has no impact on the expectation as long as that we start from a state of
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
).

6.2.3.
GFlowNets as recurrent Markov chains

Similar to
Section

6.1.2
, we will define a GFlowNet in terms of a (Harris) recurrent Markov chain, this time over a general measurable state space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
. Instead of wrapping around the GFlowNet to construct an atom
\{s_{0}\}
though, we now use the atom
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
constructed in
Section

6.2.1
via the splitting technique. Just like in
Definition

6.1.3
, we can also define the
terminating state distribution
over
{\mathcal{S}}
as the marginal distribution of the split chain returning to the atom
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
. This is guaranteed to exist for a Harris recurrent chain since it eventually returns to
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
with probability
1
.

Definition 6.2.8

(Generalized terminating state distribution)
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be a Harris recurrent Markov chain over a measurable space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
, with Markov kernel
P_{F}
, such that
{\mathcal{X}}
satisfies the minorization condition in
Definition

6.2.5
. The
terminating state distribution
is defined as the marginal distribution of the split chain
(Z_{n})_{n\geq 0}
defined in
Section

6.2.1
returning to the atom
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}={\mathcal{X}}\times\{1\}
. For all
B\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}
:

(6.2.17)

P_{F}^{\top}(B)\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}_{B}(X_{\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}_{B\times\{1\}}(Z_{\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}})\big]\mathchar 59\relax

where
\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}
is the trace
\sigma
-algebra induced by
\Sigma
on
{\mathcal{X}}
(
i.e.
,
\sigma
-algebra of subsets of
{\mathcal{X}}
).

It is interesting to see that while the terminating state distribution in discrete spaces involved the state of the Markov chain at time
\sigma_{s_{0}}-1
(
Definition

6.1.3
), the general case above does not have this offset by one. The reason is that in the split chain, we can treat
X_{n}\rightarrow Y_{n}
as being a “sub-transition”, which may be enough to make up for the extra step required in the discrete case. We can again show that the terminating state distribution is related to the invariant measure
\lambda
. The following proposition is the counterpart of
Proposition

6.1.4
for general state spaces.

Proposition 6.2.9
.

The terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
is related to the invariant measure
\lambda
defined in
Theorem

6.2.7
by,
\forall B\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}

(6.2.18)

P_{F}^{\top}(B)=\int_{B}\varepsilon(x)\lambda(dx)\mathchar 59\relax

where
\varepsilon
is the measurable function in
Definition

6.2.5
.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition follows the same pattern as the proof of
Proposition

6.1.4
. Following the same steps as
Equation

6.2.12
–
Equation

6.2.15
in the proof of
Theorem

6.2.7
, we can show that for any
n\geq 1
, we have

(6.2.19)

\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n\leq\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})\mathds{1}_{B}(X_{n})\varepsilon(X_{n})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}=n)\mathds{1}_{B}(X_{n})\big].

Using the definition of the invariant measure
\lambda
in
Theorem

6.2.7
in addition to the equation above, and given that for any
B\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}
the measurable function
\mathds{1}_{B}\varepsilon
is bounded (guaranteeing the existence of the integral),

(6.2.20)

\displaystyle\int_{B}\varepsilon(x)\lambda(dx)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n\leq\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})\mathds{1}_{B}(X_{n})\varepsilon(X_{n})\big]

(6.2.21)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}=n)\mathds{1}_{B}(X_{n})\big]

(6.2.22)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}_{B}(X_{\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}})\big]=P_{F}^{\top}(B).

∎

The proposition above shows in particular that
P_{F}^{\top}
is a properly defined distribution over
({\mathcal{X}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}})
, in the sense that
P_{F}^{\top}({\mathcal{X}})=1
, since
\lambda
is the unique invariant measure of
P_{F}
such that
\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\varepsilon(x)\lambda(dx)=1
, and
\varepsilon
is positive only on
{\mathcal{X}}
.

Under the minorization condition of
Definition

6.2.5
, we saw that the Markov kernel
P_{F}
can be interpreted as being a mixture of two separate kernels, whose mixture components depend on the function
\varepsilon
. This suggests a natural strategy to sample (continuously) from
P_{F}^{\top}
: (1) sample which mixture component to apply next based on the current state, and (2) sample from the corresponding Markov kernel, returning the state anytime the Markov chain is reset with
\nu
. This is illustrated in
Figure

6.2(b)
, and detailed in
Algorithm

5
.

Algorithm 5

Sampling from the terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
(measurable space).

1:

A Markov kernel
P_{F}
satisfying the conditions of
Definition

6.2.8
, the measure
\nu
and the function
\varepsilon
in the minorization condition
Definition

6.2.5
.

2:

Samples
x\sim P_{F}^{\top}(\cdot)
of the terminating state distribution.

3:

Initialization:
s_{0}\sim\nu(\cdot)
,
y_{0}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\varepsilon(s_{0}))

4:

loop

5:

if

y_{t}=0

then

6:

Sample the next state:
s_{t+1}\sim R_{\nu}(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)

\triangleright

Equation

6.2.6

7:

else

8:

yield

x\equiv s_{t}

9:

Sample the next state:
s_{t+1}\sim\nu(\cdot)

10:

Sample the next mixture component:
y_{t+1}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\varepsilon(s_{t+1}))

Boundary condition

In addition to the existence of some invariant measure
F
thanks to Harris recurrence, and similar to discrete spaces in
Section

6.1.3
, we will require
F
to also satisfy some boundary condition in order to obtain a terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
that matches
P^{\star}
in
Equation

6.2.2
. For some positive and finite measure
R
over
({\mathcal{X}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}})
, where
{\mathcal{X}}
is the set that we defined in the minorization condition in
Definition

6.2.5
, we say that
F
satisfies the boundary condition if for any bounded measurable function
f:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

(6.2.23)

\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f(x)R(dx)=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f(x)\varepsilon(x)F(dx)\mathchar 59\relax

where
\varepsilon
is the function defined in
Definition

6.2.5
; we saw that this function could be loosely interpreted as
\varepsilon(x)\approx\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(x\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})
, making this boundary condition analogous to
Equation

6.1.19
in the discrete case. We are finally ready to state the fundamental theorem of generative flow networks, extended to measurable state spaces.

Theorem 6.2.10
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be a Harris recurrent Markov chain over a measure state space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
, with Markov kernel
P_{F}
, such that
{\mathcal{X}}\in\Sigma
satisfies the minorization condition in
Definition

6.2.5
. Moreover, assume that
P_{F}
admits an invariant measure
F
such that for any bounded measurable function
f:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
,

(6.2.24)

\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f(x)R(dx)=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f(x)\varepsilon(x)F(dx)\mathchar 59\relax

where
R
is a positive and finite measure on
{\mathcal{X}}
, and
\varepsilon
is the measurable function in
Definition

6.2.5
. Then the terminating state distribution is proportional to the measure
R
:
\forall B\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}
,
P_{F}^{\top}(B)\propto R(B)
.

Proof.

The proof relies once again on the unicity of the invariant measure of a Harris recurrent Markov chain, up to a normalization constant. By
Theorem

6.2.7
, there exists a constant
\gamma>0
such that
F=\gamma\lambda
. Using
Proposition

6.2.9
, we get for all
B\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}

(6.2.25)

P_{F}^{\top}(B)=\int_{B}\varepsilon(x)\lambda(dx)=\frac{1}{\gamma}\int_{B}\varepsilon(x)F(dx)=\frac{R(B)}{\gamma}\propto R(B)\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the boundary condition in
Equation

6.2.24
with
f(x)=\mathds{1}_{B}(x)
.
∎

6.3.
Practical implementation of generalized GFlowNets

Although Markov chains provide an elegant framework to describe generative flow networks on general state spaces, in practice we need to find a way to
learn
the Markov kernel
P_{F}
and its corresponding invariant measure
F
satisfying the boundary condition. Following how discrete GFlowNets are trained, we could transform these conditions (invariance & boundary condition) into loss functions that can eventually be minimized. In this section, we will take even further inspiration from discrete GFlowNets and provide a construction of generalized GFlowNets as a special case of the framework described in
Section

6.2
, which offers some advantages for a practical implementation. Throughout this section, we will assume that
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
is a measurable state space, and we identify a fixed initial state
s_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}
; moreover, we will also assume that
\{s_{0}\}\in\Sigma
.

Prior attempts to define continuous GFlowNets

Even if this section will culminate to the observation that the flow matching losses we defined in the discrete case in
Section

4.2.1
can be transferred almost directly with minimal modifications, the path to get there is not necessarily evident. Notably
Li et al. 2023a
introduced
CFlowNets
, which was meant as an initial attempt to extend discrete GFlowNets to continuous spaces. They did so by simply writing
a
flow matching condition (distinct from
Theorem

3.2.1
) with integrals instead of sums. This was too closely inspired by the original formulation of
Bengio et al. 2021
, who worked with
actions
instead of transitions (which is valid in discrete space), leading to the inaccurate “continuous flow matching condition”

(6.3.1)

\int_{s^{\prime}:s\rightarrow s^{\prime}}F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}=\int_{a}F(s\rightarrow T(s\mathchar 59\relax a))da\mathchar 59\relax

where the second integral is taken over actions and
T(s\mathchar 59\relax a)
represents the state by taking the action
a
in state
s
(all quantities being loosely defined). This change of variable from transitions to actions is invalid in general: the integrand on the RHS is missing a Jacobian term
dT(s\mathchar 59\relax a)/da
which is not necessarily equal to
1
.

6.3.1.
Separating structure from transition probabilities

Apart from the nature of the state space, the key difference between discrete and generalized GFlowNets as described in the previous section is that, in the discrete case, the structure of the state space
{\mathcal{G}}
(as a pointed DAG) was distinct from the forward transition probabilities
P_{F}
placed onto it. Conversely, generalized GFlowNets directly work at the level of the kernel
P_{F}
without calling for an explicit underlying structure. This was motivated by the fact that it is impossible to define a notion of “graph” over a continuous state space for example, let alone ensuring that it is acyclic. Here, we will instead use another construction called
measurable pointed graph
, whose structure will be defined in terms of transition kernels (
Definition

6.2.2
).

Definition 6.3.1

(Measurable pointed graph)
.

Let
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})
be a measureable space, where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}={\mathcal{S}}\cup\{\bot\}
is a state space augmented with a distinct state
\bot\neq{\mathcal{S}}
(with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\sigma(\Sigma\cup\{\bot\})
being the
\sigma
-algebra induced by augmenting
\Sigma
with
\bot
), and let
s_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}
be a fixed initial state. Let
\kappa_{F}
&
\kappa_{B}
be two transition kernels and
\nu
be a positive
\sigma
-finite measure on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
. The tuple
{\mathcal{G}}=((\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{B}\mathchar 59\relax\nu)
is called a
measurable pointed graph
if it satisfies the following properties:

(1)

\forall B\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, s.t.
\nu(B)>0
,
\exists n\geq 0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ \kappa_{F}^{n}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax B)>0

(2)

\forall B\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
,
\kappa_{F}(\bot\mathchar 59\relax B)=\mathds{1}(\bot\in B)

(3)

\forall B\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\otimes\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, s.t.
(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{0})\notin B
and
(\bot\mathchar 59\relax\bot)\notin B
,
\nu\otimes\kappa_{F}(B)=\nu\otimes\kappa_{B}(B)

(4)

\forall B\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ \kappa_{B}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax B)=0

The kernels
\kappa_{F}
and
\kappa_{B}
, and the measure
\nu
, are called the forward reference kernel, the backward reference kernel, and the reference measure of
{\mathcal{G}}
respectively.

The reference kernels provide a sense of “structure” for the state space, where
\kappa_{F}
encodes how to move forward in the state space, and
\kappa_{B}
how to move backward.
\nu\otimes\kappa
denotes the measure defined as the product of a measure
\nu
with a transition kernel
\kappa
; see
Equation

A.2.12
for the definition. In practice, if the structure of the measurable pointed graph is only defined by the forward reference kernel
\kappa_{F}
, then under mild assumptions we can find a measure
\nu
and a kernel
\kappa_{B}
satisfying the conditions of
Definition

6.3.1

(
Cappé et al. 2005
, Definition 2)
. To draw a closer parallel with the notion of pointed DAGs in the discrete case, a measurable pointed graph is said to be
finitely absorbing
if
\exists N>0
,
\forall s\in{\mathcal{S}}

(6.3.2)

\mathrm{supp}\big(\kappa_{F}^{N}(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)\big)=\{\bot\}\mathchar 59\relax

where
\mathrm{supp}(\cdot)
represents the support of the measure, and where
\kappa_{F}^{N}
is the composition kernel defined recursively in
Equation

A.2.14
by applying the same kernel
\kappa_{F}

N
times consecutively. This condition in
Equation

6.3.2
means that any trajectory following
\kappa_{F}
is guaranteed to eventually end up at
\bot
almost surely, in which case the minimal such integer
N
is called the maximal trajectory length of
{\mathcal{G}}
. Drawing further inspiration from the discrete case, each of the four properties in the definition above can be interpreted as follows: (1) encodes the fact that all the states are accessible from the initial state
s_{0}
, (2) represents the fact that
\bot
is a terminal state with no “children” other than itself, (3) encodes the fact that the kernels
\kappa_{F}
and
\kappa_{B}
are inverse of one another, relative to some measure
\nu
, and finally (4) represents the fact that the initial state
s_{0}
has no “parent” (note that this does not adopt the “wrapped-arround” construction anymore).

Example 6.3.2

(Pointed DAG as a measurable pointed graph)
.

Finite state spaces with a pointed DAG structure are special cases of finitely absorbing measurable pointed graphs. Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG, where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}={\mathcal{S}}\cup\{\bot\}
is a finite state space and
{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
the set of directed edges, and let
s_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}
be the initial state. The finite state space can be associated with the canonical
\sigma
-algebra
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
corresponding to subsets of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
(
i.e.
,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\equiv 2^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}
is the power set of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
). For any state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and any subset
B\subseteq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, the forward reference kernel
\kappa_{F}
can be defined by the structure of
{\mathcal{G}}
as

(6.3.3)

\kappa_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in B}\mathds{1}(s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s))+\mathds{1}(s=\bot)\mathds{1}(\bot\in B).

Similarly, for any state
s^{\prime}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
the backward reference kernel
\kappa_{B}
can also be defined as

(6.3.4)

\kappa_{B}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\sum_{s\in B}\mathds{1}(s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime}))\mathchar 59\relax

and the reference measure
\nu
is simply the counting measure on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
(
i.e.
, the measure counting the number of elements in a subset of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
). It can be shown that the kernels and measure defined this way satisfy all the properties of
Definition

6.3.1
. Moreover,
Equation

6.3.2
is also satisfied thanks to the acyclicity of
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Similar to how we defined forward and backward transition probability distributions over a pointed DAG in
Sections

2.2.3
and
3.1.2
, we can also define a similar notion of Markov kernels
compatible
with a measurable pointed graph
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Definition 6.3.3

(Compatible Markov kernels)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=((\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{B}\mathchar 59\relax\nu)
be a measurable pointed graph. A Markov kernel
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
over
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})
is called a
forward Markov kernel
compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
if for all
s\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, the distribution
P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)
is absolutely continuous wrt. the measure
\kappa_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)
. Similarly, a Markov kernel
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}
is a
backward Markov kernel
compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
if for all
s\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, the distribution
P_{B}(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)
is absolutely continuous wrt. the measure
\kappa_{B}(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)
.

(6.3.5)

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)

\displaystyle\ll\kappa_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)

\displaystyle\ll\kappa_{B}(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)

We use the notation “
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}
” to highlight the fact that these kernels are defined over
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})
(unlike some of the kernels below). Although it was not initially interpreted as such by
Lahlou et al. 2023
, the new state
\bot
is introduced as an
explicit
atom of a Markov chain that would be “wrapped-around” like we did in
Section

6.1.2
. The following proposition defines the kernel of this Markov chain (over
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
this time) precisely in terms of a forward Markov kernel compatible with a measurable pointed graph
{\mathcal{G}}
. This new Markov kernel has the notable properties of being
\nu
-irreducible (
Definition

6.2.3
) and Harris recurrent (
Definition

6.2.6
).

Proposition 6.3.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=((\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{B}\mathchar 59\relax\nu)
be a finitely absorbing measurable pointed graph, and let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
be a forward Markov kernel compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
. The Markov kernel
P_{F}
defined for any
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and any
B\in\Sigma
by

(6.3.6)

P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)+\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})\delta_{s_{0}}(B)\mathchar 59\relax

is
\nu
-irreducible and Harris recurrent over the measurable space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
.

Proof.

P_{F}
is a well defined Markov kernel over
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
. Indeed, it is clearly a transition kernel since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
itself is a Markov kernel, and

(6.3.7)

P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{S}})=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{S}})+\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})=1\mathchar 59\relax

since
s_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}
and therefore
\delta_{s_{0}}({\mathcal{S}})=1
. Before showing Harris recurrence, we first show that
P_{F}
is
\nu
-irreducible. Recall that by definition of finitely absorbing in
Equation

6.3.2
, there exists a maximal trajectory length
N>0
of
{\mathcal{G}}
such that for any
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
\mathrm{supp}\big(\kappa^{N}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)\big)=\{\bot\}
. Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
is compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
(therefore
P_{F}\ll\kappa_{F}
), then we necessarily have that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{N}(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)=\delta_{\bot}(\cdot)
as well. Moreover, we can show by induction that for any
m>0
and any set
A\in\Sigma

(6.3.8)

P_{F}^{m}(s\mathchar 59\relax A)\geq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{m}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})\delta_{s_{0}}(A).

In particular, we have
P_{F}^{N}(s\mathchar 59\relax A)\geq\delta_{s_{0}}(A)
from what we saw above. Let
B\in\Sigma
be a set such that
\nu(B)>0
. By definition of a measurable pointed graph, in particular property (1) of
Definition

6.3.1
, there exists
n\geq 0
such that
\kappa_{F}^{n}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax B)>0
. Again, since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
is compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
, we also have
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}}^{n}_{\!F}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax B)>0
. By the Chapman-Kolmogorov equation

(6.3.9)

P_{F}^{N+n}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}P_{F}^{N}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})P_{F}^{n}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax B)\geq\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\delta_{s_{0}}(ds^{\prime})P_{F}^{n}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax B)=P_{F}^{n}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax B)\geq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{n}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax B)>0.

Since this is true for any state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, and any set
B\in\Sigma
such that
\nu(B)>0
, we can conclude that
P_{F}
is
\nu
-irreducible.

Finally to show that
P_{F}
is Harris recurrent, we can follow the same argument as above, but this time for any set
B\in\Sigma
such that
\nu(B)>0
, and any
s\in B
. The only additional requirement is to ensure that
N+n>0
(since the definition of Harris recurrence involves the
return time
to
B
), which is clear since
Equation

6.3.9
can be interpreted as hitting
\bot\notin{\mathcal{S}}
, and restarting at
s_{0}
(therefore, there is at least a transition to
\bot
to account for).
∎

A consequence of this proposition is that the Markov kernel
P_{F}
also admits an invariant measure, by
Theorem

6.2.7
,
regardless of the forward Markov kernel
compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
. It will play an important role in what follows. Interestingly, it is clear that
P_{F}
also satisfies the minorization condition, since
P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)\geq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})\delta_{s_{0}}(B)
; in that context, we have
\nu(B)=\delta_{s_{0}}(B)
and
\varepsilon(s)=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})
in
Definition

6.2.5
.

6.3.2.
Generalization of the flow matching condition

We saw in the discrete case that the only difference between the flow matching condition and invariance was the inclusion of the initial state
s_{0}
when we check those conditions. In measurable state space, we will have the same separation of the initial state. This
generalized flow matching condition
will be similar to invariance in
Equation

6.2.10
, except that we will not measure the response against any function
f
, but only those such that
f(s_{0})=0
, effectively ignoring the initial state.

Definition 6.3.5

(Generalized flow matching condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=((\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{B}\mathchar 59\relax\nu)
be a finitely absorbing measurable pointed graph. Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
be a forward Markov kernel compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
, and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\ll\nu
be a
\sigma
-finite measure over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
. The pair
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F})
is said to satisfy the
flow matching condition
if for any bounded measurable function
f:\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{S}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that
f(s_{0})=0
, it satisfies

(6.3.10)

\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds^{\prime})=\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime}).

Instead of being a function, the flow
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
is now a measure over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
. We saw in the previous section that we could construct a Markov kernel
P_{F}
, from
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
, that admits an invariant measure. The following proposition shows that this invariant measure is related to the flow when it satisfies the generalized flow matching condition; it happens to simply be the restriction of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
to the state space
{\mathcal{S}}
(
i.e.
, ignoring the terminal state
\bot
).

Proposition 6.3.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=((\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{B}\mathchar 59\relax\nu)
be a finitely absorbing measurable pointed graph. If a pair
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F})
satisfies the flow matching condition in
Definition

6.3.5
, then the restriction of the measure
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
over
{\mathcal{S}}
is invariant for the Markov kernel defined over the measurable space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
by

(6.3.11)

P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)+\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})\delta_{s_{0}}(B).

Proof.

We use the notation
F
to denote the restriction of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
(defined as a measure over
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})
) over
{\mathcal{S}}
. For any bounded measurable function
g:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, there exists a unique bounded measurable function
f:\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that
f(s_{0})=0
that can be defined by

(6.3.12)

\displaystyle f(s_{0})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle f(\bot)

\displaystyle=g(s_{0})

\displaystyle\textrm{and $\forall s\neq s_{0}$,}\ \ f(s)

\displaystyle=g(s).

This transformation is a bijection, meaning that we can go back and forth in a unique way between
g
and
f
. Using such a function
g
, we have

\displaystyle\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{S}}}

\displaystyle g(s^{\prime})F(ds)P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})

(6.3.13)

\displaystyle=\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{S}}}g(s^{\prime})\mathds{1}(s^{\prime}\neq s_{0})F(ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})+\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\underbrace{\left[\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}g(s^{\prime})\delta_{s_{0}}(ds^{\prime})\right]}_{=\,g(s_{0})}F(ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})

(6.3.14)

\displaystyle=\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{S}}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})+\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}f(\bot)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})

(6.3.15)

\displaystyle=\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used the definition of
P_{F}
and the fact that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{s_{0}\})=0
since
{\mathcal{G}}
is finitely absorbing in
Equation

6.3.13
, the bijection between
g
&
f
and the fact that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
&
F
match on
{\mathcal{S}}
in
Equation

6.3.14
. Continuing the derivation, we can apply the flow matching condition since
f
is such that
f(s_{0})=0
:

(6.3.16)

\displaystyle=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds^{\prime})

(6.3.17)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds^{\prime})+f(\bot)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(\{\bot\})

(6.3.18)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}g(s^{\prime})\mathds{1}(s^{\prime}\neq s_{0})F(ds^{\prime})+g(s_{0})F(\{s_{0}\})=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}g(s^{\prime})F(ds^{\prime})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used the bijection between
f
and
g
, the fact that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
and
F
match on
{\mathcal{S}}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(\{\bot\})=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(\{s_{0}\})=F(\{s_{0}\})
, and
f(s_{0})=0
in
Equation

6.3.18
. Since this holds for any bounded measurable function
g
, this shows that
F
is invariant for
P_{F}
.
∎

6.3.3.
Terminating state distribution

Now that we have separated the structure of the state space through the finitely absorbing measurable pointed graph
{\mathcal{G}}
from the Markov kernel
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
, we can one last time define a notion of terminating state distribution. The domain
{\mathcal{X}}
of this distribution will be the “parents” of the terminal state
\bot
(
i.e.
, states from which
\bot
is directly accessible), just like in
Definition

2.2.2
for pointed DAGs, as given by the forward transition kernel
\kappa_{F}

(6.3.19)

{\mathcal{X}}=\{x\in{\mathcal{S}}\mid\kappa_{F}(x\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})>0\}.

This is fully compatible with the space
{\mathcal{X}}
in the minorization condition, which was the domain of distribution defined in
Definition

6.2.8
, in the sense that
\varepsilon(x)=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(x\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})
is zero if
\kappa_{F}(x\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})=0
as well (by absolute continuity, because
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
is compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
). We will use
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
to denote the canonical Markov chain with kernel
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
starting at
s_{0}
. Note that since
{\mathcal{G}}
is finitely absorbing
Equation

6.3.2
, we necessarily have for all
n\geq N
,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{n}=\bot
. Based on this chain, we can define the terminating state distribution associated with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
similarly to
Definition

6.1.3
, as being for any
B\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}

(6.3.20)

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{\top}(B)=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}_{B}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{\bar{\eta}_{\bot}-1})\big]\mathchar 59\relax

where
\bar{\eta}_{\bot}=\inf\{n\geq 0\mid\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{n}=\bot\}
is the
hitting time
of the Markov chain at
\bot
. Note that although the hitting time is typically defined as only being non-negative (as opposed to the
return time

Equation

6.1.6
being
\geq 1
), we necessarily have
\bar{\eta}_{\bot}\geq 1
since
s_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}
cannot be equal to
\bot\notin{\mathcal{S}}
, making the terminating state distribution well defined. Once again, we can show that this is a properly defined probability distribution over
{\mathcal{X}}
. To sample from
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{\top}
, the strategy is exactly the same as in
Algorithm

2
for pointed DAGs: starting at the initial state
s_{0}
, sample sequentially using the Markov kernel
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
until we reach
\bot
, and return the state obtained right before termination.

Boundary condition

Going back to our original problem of finding a Markov kernel whose terminating state distribution matches the target
P^{\star}
in
Equation

6.2.2
, we still have to define a boundary condition, depending on the positive and finite measure
R
. Moreover, we will assume that
R\ll\nu
. Adapted to our setting here with a flow measure
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
and a forward Markov kernel
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
defined on top of a measurable pointed graph, the boundary condition in
Equation

6.2.23
becomes

(6.3.21)

\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f(x)R(dx)=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f(x)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(dx)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(x\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})\mathchar 59\relax

for any bounded measurable function
f:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
. Once again, this condition is reminiscent to
Equation

4.1.2
in the discrete case. Combining it with the flow matching condition, and after an excursion through the Markov chain perspective detailed in
Section

6.2
, we are now ready to state the direct extension of the fundamental theorem of GFlowNets (
Theorem

4.1.1
), this time for measurable spaces.

Theorem 6.3.7

(Generalized flow matching condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=((\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{B}\mathchar 59\relax\nu)
be a finitely absorbing measurable pointed graph. Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
be a forward Markov kernel compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\ll\nu
be a
\sigma
-finite measure over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, and
R
be a finite measure on
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
such that
R\ll\nu
. If
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F})
satisfies the flow matching condition of
Definition

6.3.5
and the following boundary condition for any bounded measurable function
f:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}

(6.3.22)

\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f(x)R(dx)=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f(x)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(dx)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(x\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})\mathchar 59\relax

then the terminating state distribution associated with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
is proportional to the measure
R
:
\forall B\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}
,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{\top}(B)\propto R(B)
.

Figure 6.3
.
Relation between the atom
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
for the split chain
Z_{n}=(X_{n}\mathchar 59\relax Y_{n})
and the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
. The value
\ast
indicates that the value can be arbitrary. Returning to
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
at random time
\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}
happens exactly one step before hitting the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
(corresponding to hitting the terminating state for the Markov chain
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{n}
).

Proof.

This theorem is a direct consequence on
Theorem

6.2.10
. If
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F})
satisfies the flow matching condition and, and since
{\mathcal{G}}
is a finitely absorbing measurable pointed graph, then by
Propositions

6.3.4
and
6.3.6
the Markov kernel over
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
defined by

(6.3.23)

P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)+\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})\delta_{s_{0}}(B)

is
\nu
-irreducible, Harris recurrent, and admits
F
, the restriction of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
on
{\mathcal{S}}
, as an invariant measure. The set
{\mathcal{X}}
clearly satisfies the minorization condition of
Definition

6.2.5
, with the corresponding function
\varepsilon(s)=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})
: by
Equation

6.3.23
,
P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)\geq\varepsilon(s)\delta_{s_{0}}(B)
. Since
F
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
are equal on
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
, and with the form of
\varepsilon(s)
, the boundary condition
Equation

6.3.22
is exactly equivalent to the one in
Equation

6.2.24
. Therefore, by
Theorem

6.2.10
, we get that for all
B\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}
,
P_{F}^{\top}(B)\propto R(B)
.

To conclude, we only need to prove that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{\top}(B)=P_{F}^{\top}(B)
. First, note that in addition to the function
\varepsilon
, we can also identify the measure
\nu(B)=\delta_{s_{0}}(B)
in the minorization condition. We introduce the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}=\{s_{0}\}\times\{0\mathchar 59\relax 1\}
, by analogy with
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}={\mathcal{X}}\times\{1\}
appearing in the definition of
P_{F}^{\top}

Equation

6.2.17
; we chose the index “1” because transitioning from any element of
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
is guaranteed to yield the state
s_{0}
as its first component (because
\nu=\delta_{s_{0}}
), and the second component of the split chain (called “
Y
” in
Section

6.2.1
) may be arbitrary.

(6.3.24)

\displaystyle P_{F}^{\top}(B)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}_{B}(X_{\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}})\big]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n=\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})\mathds{1}_{B}(X_{n})\big]

(6.3.25)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n\leq\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})\mathds{1}_{B}(X_{n})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(X_{n}\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})\big]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used
Equation

6.2.19
and the definition of
\varepsilon(s)=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})
in
Equation

6.3.25
. Since we are guaranteed to transition to an element of
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
from
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
we get, using Markov property

(6.3.26)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{1}}\big[\mathds{1}(n-1\leq\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})\mathds{1}_{B}(X_{n-1})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(X_{n-1}\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})\big]

(6.3.27)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{1}}\big[\mathds{1}(n\leq\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})\mathds{1}_{B}(X_{n})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(X_{n}\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})\big]

(6.3.28)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n\leq\bar{\eta}_{\bot}-1)\mathds{1}_{B}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{n})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{n}\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})\big]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used the link between
\sigma_{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}
and
\bar{\eta}_{\bot}
illustrated in
Figure

6.3
, and the fact that the Markov chain
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{n}
behaves exactly as
X_{n}
before hitting the terminal state to get
Equation

6.3.28
. Using an argument similar to
Equation

6.2.19
used earlier in this proof, we can conclude that

(6.3.29)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n=\bar{\eta}_{\bot}-1)\mathds{1}_{B}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{n})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}_{B}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}_{\bar{\eta}-1})\big]=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{\top}(B).

This concludes the proof.
∎

The same way we introduced flow matching losses in order to search for quantities that were satisfying the conditions of
Theorem

4.1.1
(or any flow matching condition for that matter), we will also introduce similar losses in
Section

6.3.5
to find a flow and Markov kernel
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F})
satisfying the conditions of this theorem.

6.3.4.
Generalized detailed balance & trajectory balance conditions

So far in this chapter, we have focused exclusively on the flow matching condition, and its relation to invariant measures, first from the perspective of Markov chains, then measurable pointed graphs (analogous to pointed DAGs). In this section, we will derive similar extensions of the other flow matching conditions we introduced in
Section

3.2
. Starting with the
detailed balance
condition, the following proposition shows in particular that the generalization of the detailed balance condition actually implies the flow matching condition of
Definition

6.3.5
.

Proposition 6.3.8

(Generalized detailed balance condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=((\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{B}\mathchar 59\relax\nu)
be a finitely absorbing measurable pointed graph. Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
be a forward Markov kernel and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}
be a backward Markov kernel compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
, and let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\ll\nu
be a
\sigma
-finite measure over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
. The tuple
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B})
is said to satisfy the
detailed balance condition
if for any bounded measurable function
f:{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that
f(s\mathchar 59\relax s_{0})=0
for any
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, it satisfies:

(6.3.30)

\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})=\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax ds).

Moreover if
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B})
satisfy the detailed balance condition above, then
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F})
satisfy the flow matching condition of
Definition

6.3.5
.

Proof.

Following the properties of
Definition

6.3.1
, especially (2) & (3), we have
\kappa_{B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})=0
for any
s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
. Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}
is compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
, it is clear that we also have
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})=0
. For any bounded measurable function
f:\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that
f(s_{0})=0
, we can define a function
g:{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that for all
(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
,
g(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})=f(s^{\prime})
. Note that
g
satisfies
g(s\mathchar 59\relax s_{0})=0
for any
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
. We can show that
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F})
satisfies the flow matching condition:

(6.3.31)

\displaystyle\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}g(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})

(6.3.32)

\displaystyle=\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}g(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax ds)

(6.3.33)

\displaystyle=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds^{\prime})\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax ds)=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds^{\prime})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used the detailed balance condition in
Equation

6.3.32
, and the fact that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}
is a Markov kernel and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})=0
in
Equation

6.3.33
.
∎

Since we reduced the generalized detailed balance condition to the generalized flow matching condition, it is clear by
Theorem

6.3.7
that satisfying it along with the boundary condition
Equation

6.3.22
implies that the corresponding terminating state distribution will also be proportional to the measure
R
. This result matches
Theorem

4.1.4
in the case of discrete GFlowNets.

Corollary 6.3.9
.

If
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B})
satisfy the generalized detailed balance condition of
Proposition

6.3.8
, along with the boundary condition of
Equation

6.3.22
, then the terminating state distribution associated with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
is proportional to the measure
R
:
\forall B\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}
,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{\top}(B)\propto R(B)
.

Finally, we can do the same exercise with the
trajectory balance condition
. Since we saw that this is a conservation law at the level of complete trajectories, we have to extend the notion of (one step) Markov kernel
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
(and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}
) to distributions over trajectories
(
Petritis 2015
)
; see
Section

A.2.3
for details. The following theorem generalizes
Theorem

4.1.5
to measurable spaces; this condition is more involved than those we have seen thus far.

Theorem 6.3.10

(Generalized trajectory balance condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=((\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{S}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{B}\mathchar 59\relax\nu)
be a finitely absorbing measurable pointed graph. Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
be a forward Markov kernel and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}
be a backward Markov kernel compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
, and let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Z}>0
be a positive scalar. The tuple
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Z}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B})
is said to satisfy the
trajectory balance condition
wrt. a positive and finite measure
R
on
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}
(s.t.
R\ll\nu
) if for any
T\geq 0
and any bounded measurable function
f:{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{S}}^{T+2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, it satisfies:

(6.3.34)

\displaystyle\int_{{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}^{T+1}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Z}f(s_{0}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax s_{T+1})\mathds{1}(s_{T+1}=\bot)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{\otimes T+1}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax ds_{1:T+1})

\displaystyle=\int_{{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}^{T+1}}R(ds_{T})f(s\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)\mathds{1}(s=s_{0})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}^{\otimes T}(s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax ds_{T-1:1}ds)\mathchar 59\relax

where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{\otimes T+1}
&
