\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}^{\otimes T}
are the product kernels defined recursively by
Equation

A.2.11
. Moreover, if
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Z}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B})
satisfy the trajectory balance condition above, then the forward kernel
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
and the measure
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
defined for all
B\in\Sigma
by

(6.3.35)

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(B)=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Z}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{n}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax B)\mathchar 59\relax

where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{n}
is the composition kernel defined recursively by
Equation

A.2.14
, satisfy the flow matching condition of
Definition

6.3.5
as well as the boundary condition
Equation

6.3.22
. Therefore, by
Theorem

6.3.7
, the terminating state distribution associated with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
is proportional to
R
:
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{\top}(B)\propto R(B)
for any
B\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}
.

Proof.

Let
f:\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a bounded measurable function such that
f(s_{0})=0
. We can first show that
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F})
with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
defined by
Equation

6.3.35
satisfy the flow matching condition.

(6.3.36)

\displaystyle\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})F(ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})Z\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{n}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})

(6.3.37)

\displaystyle=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Z}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{n+1}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})

(6.3.38)

\displaystyle=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Z}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{n}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds^{\prime})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used the Chapman–Kolmogorov equation in
Equation

6.3.37
, and the fact that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{0}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)=\delta_{s_{0}}(\cdot)
and
f(s_{0})=0
in
Equation

6.3.38
. To show that they also satisfy the boundary condition, we can first observe that for any state
s\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}

(6.3.39)

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}^{n}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{s_{0}\})=1.

This is the generalization of
Lemma

3.1.6
establishing that a backward kernel induces a distribution over trajectories back to
s_{0}
; it follows from the finitely absorbing nature of
{\mathcal{G}}
and the properties of
Definition

6.3.1
. See
(
Lahlou et al. 2023
, Proposition 5)
.

(6.3.40)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f(s)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})

\displaystyle=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\mathds{1}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(s)f(s)Z\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{n}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax ds)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{\bot\})

(6.3.41)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}^{n+1}}\mathds{1}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(s_{n})f(s_{n})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Z}\mathds{1}(s_{n+1}=\bot)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{\otimes n+1}(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax ds_{1:n+1})

(6.3.42)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}^{n+1}}R(ds_{n})\mathds{1}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(s_{n})f(s_{n})\mathds{1}(s^{\prime}=s_{0})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}^{\otimes n}(s_{n}\mathchar 59\relax ds_{n-1:1}ds^{\prime})

(6.3.43)

\displaystyle=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\mathds{1}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(s)f(s)R(ds)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}^{n}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}^{\otimes n}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds_{n-1:1}ds^{\prime})\mathds{1}(s^{\prime}=s_{0})

(6.3.44)

\displaystyle=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\mathds{1}_{{\mathcal{X}}}(s)f(s)R(ds)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}^{n}(s\mathchar 59\relax\{s_{0}\})=\int_{{\mathcal{X}}}f(s)R(ds)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used the marginalization of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{\otimes n+1}
in
Equation

6.3.41
(see
Proposition

A.2.6
), the trajectory balance condition in
Equation

6.3.42
, and
Equation

6.3.39
in
Equation

6.3.44
. This shows that
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F})
also satisfy the boundary condition. Finally, we can conclude that the terminating state distribution
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}^{\top}
is proportional to the measure
R
by
Theorem

6.3.7
.
∎

Interestingly, just like in the discrete case, the theorem above directly integrates the reward measure in the condition
Equation

6.3.34
. The functions
\mathds{1}(s_{T+1}=\bot)
and
\mathds{1}(s=s_{0})
ensure that we only take into account “complete trajectories”. Similar to the generalized detailed balance condition, we proved this theorem by reducing the trajectory balance condition to the flow matching condition (and boundary condition).

6.3.5.
Generalized flow matching losses

Finally in this section, we will see that the flow matching losses we presented in
Section

4.2.1
transfer with minimal modifications to the general case. In particular, all of these losses will still take the form of a non-linear least square objective of the form

(6.3.45)

{\mathcal{L}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\big[\Delta^{2}(\cdot;\phi)\big]\mathchar 59\relax

where
\Delta(\cdot;\phi)
is a residual which depends on the condition being applied, and
\pi_{b}
is a behavior policy with full support. These losses are still
off-policy
, and the considerations of
Section

4.2.2
still hold (we will see some other considerations more specifically for continuous spaces later in this section).

All of the conditions we have seen in
Sections

6.3.3
and
6.3.4
involve to some extent measures
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
&
R
and/or Markov kernels compatible with a measurable pointed graph
{\mathcal{G}}
. Since we assumed absolute continuity of all these quantities wrt. the components of
{\mathcal{G}}
, we can define them in terms of their
density
(or Radon-Nikodym derivatives). For example, since we assumed that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\ll\nu
(
Definition

6.3.5
) and
R\ll\nu
, there exist two functions
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}:\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
and
r:{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
such that for all
B\in\Sigma
and
B^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}}
,

(6.3.46)

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(B)

\displaystyle=\int_{B}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}(s)\nu(ds)

and

\displaystyle R(B^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{B^{\prime}}r(x)\nu(dx).

Similarly, since the Markov kernels
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
&
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}
are compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
(
Definition

6.3.3
), then there exist two functions
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{B}:{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
such that for all
B\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}

(6.3.47)

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(B)

\displaystyle=\int_{B}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\kappa_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})

and

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{B}(B)

\displaystyle=\int_{B}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax s)\kappa_{B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax ds).

With the exception of
r
, which we will assumed to be fixed depending on our problem of interest (recall that we want to sample from a distribution proportional to
R
), all these densities may be parametrized more easily with some
\phi
; for example in a continuous space
{\mathcal{S}}={\mathbb{R}}
, the density of the forward Markov kernel can be parametrized as the density of a Normal distribution with fixed variance and centered around a function of the previous state that would depend on
\phi

(6.3.48)

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{F}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(s^{\prime}-\mu_{\phi}(s))^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

Taking inspiration from the flow matching condition in
Definition

6.3.5
and
Theorem

6.3.7
, we can define for any
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
the residual for the
flow matching loss

(6.3.49)

\Delta_{\mathrm{FM}}(s^{\prime};\phi)=\log\frac{\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}_{\phi}(s){\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}}^{\phi}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\kappa_{B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax ds)}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}_{\phi}(s^{\prime})}.

At first glance, this residual may seem quite different from the flow matching condition, because of the inclusion of
\kappa_{B}
. It is at least not as straightforward as the one we derived in
Equation

4.2.2
based on
Equation

4.1.5
in the discrete case. The following proposition shows that this formulation is actually correct, in the sense that if this residual is zero almost everywhere, then the generalized flow matching condition is satisfied.

Proposition 6.3.11
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=((\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{F}\mathchar 59\relax\kappa_{B}\mathchar 59\relax\nu)
be a finitely absorbing measurable pointed graph, and let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}:\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
be the density of a flow measure
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
the density of a forward Markov kernel
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
compatible with
{\mathcal{G}}
. For
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
, if

(6.3.50)

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}(s^{\prime})=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}(s)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\kappa_{B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax ds)

\nu
-almost surely, then
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F})
satisfy the flow matching condition of
Definition

6.3.5
.

Proof.

We can go back and forth between
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}
&
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
and their corresponding densities. For any bounded measurable function
f:\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
such that
f(s_{0})=0
, we have

(6.3.51)

\displaystyle\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(ds^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}(s^{\prime})\nu(ds^{\prime})

(6.3.52)

\displaystyle=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}(s)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\kappa_{B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax ds)\nu(ds^{\prime})

(6.3.53)

\displaystyle=\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}(s)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})\kappa_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})\nu(ds)

(6.3.54)

\displaystyle=\iint_{{\mathcal{S}}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}f(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}(s)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the condition
Equation

6.3.50
in
Equation

6.3.52
, and property (3) of the definition of a measurable pointed graph (
Definition

6.3.1
) in
Equation

6.3.53
. We implicitly used the fact that
f(s_{0})=0
because the condition
Equation

6.3.50
is only valid
\nu
-almost surely for any
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

It is noteworthy that property (3) of
Definition

6.3.1
has never been used until this very point. Indeed, this condition was included in this definition specifically for working with densities; in fact, this is a property that would be necessary also to prove similar statements for other flow matching conditions. Regarding the flow matching condition, we also need to incorporate a residual for the boundary condition
Equation

6.3.22
; for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}

(6.3.55)

\Delta_{\mathrm{FM}}(x;\phi)=\log\frac{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}_{\phi}(x)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{F}^{\phi}(x\mathchar 59\relax\bot)}{r(x)}.

This is also called a
reward matching loss

(
Bengio et al. 2023
;
Lahlou et al. 2023
)
. In the case of these two residuals, the behavior policy
\pi_{b}
must be a distribution over states. Similarly, the residual for the detailed balance condition in
Proposition

6.3.8
, leading to the
generalized detailed balance loss
, is given for any
s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
by

(6.3.56)

\Delta_{\mathrm{DB}}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime};\phi)=\log\frac{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}_{\phi}(s)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{F}^{\phi}(s\mathchar 59\relax s^{\prime})}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}_{\phi}(s^{\prime})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{B}^{\phi}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax s)}.

Just like the flow matching loss, this detailed balance loss must go along with a reward matching loss of the form
Equation

6.3.55
, with
\Delta_{\mathrm{DB}}(x\rightarrow\bot;\phi)=\Delta_{\mathrm{FM}}(x;\phi)
. The behavior policy
\pi_{b}
is a distribution over transitions in
{\mathcal{G}}
. Finally, the residual used in the
generalized trajectory balance loss
is given for any
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
by

(6.3.57)

\Delta_{\mathrm{TB}}(\tau;\phi)=\log\frac{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Z}_{\phi}\prod_{t=0}^{T}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{F}^{\phi}(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t+1})}{r(s_{T})\prod_{t=1}^{T}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{p}_{B}^{\phi}(s_{t}\mathchar 59\relax s_{t-1})}

where we can also parametrize the scalar
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{Z}_{\phi}
. The behavior policy
\pi_{b}
in that case is a distribution over “complete trajectories” (
i.e.
, trajectories that start at
s_{0}
and end in
\bot
). It is worth noting that with the exception of the flow matching loss in
Equation

6.3.49
, all the residuals presented here match almost identically their discrete counterparts in
Section

4.2.1
. The only major modification is to replace any probability mass functions by densities in the general case.

Off-policy training

We saw in
Section

4.2.2
some options such as
\varepsilon
-sampling in order to define a behavior policy
\pi_{b}
that encourages exploration. In the case of a continuous state space though, this strategy often does not make sense any longer when
\bar{p}_{F}^{\phi}
does not have a finite support (
e.g.
, when it is a Normal distribution). For example if the forward transition probability is a Normal distribution, an option to encourage exploration is to add an extra variance term (
i.e.
, replacing
\sigma^{2}
by
\sigma^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}
in
Equation

6.3.48
;
Lahlou et al. 2023
).

Part II
Bayesian Structure Learning

Chapter 7
Bayesian Structure Learning

with Generative Flow Networks

This chapter contains material from the following paper:

•

Tristan Deleu
, António Góis, Chris Emezue, Mansi Rankawat, Simon Lacoste-Julien, Stefan Bauer, Yoshua Bengio (2022).
Bayesian Structure Learning with Generative Flow Networks
. Conference on Uncertainty in Artificial Intelligence (UAI).
\notecite
deleu2022daggflownet

In the first part of this thesis, we have presented a comprehensive view of the theory underlying generative flow networks as a framework to describe distributions over discrete and compositional objects. In the second part of this thesis, we will use these tools for the problem of
structure learning
that we introduced in
Section

1.4
, but this time from a
Bayesian
perspective. GFlowNets appear as an ideal fit for this problem, since the DAG structure of a Bayesian network over which we will define a posterior distribution is a discrete object and has a natural decomposition as a collection of directed edges (
Figure

2.1
).

7.1.
Bayesian structure learning

We saw in
Section

1.4
that finding
the best
DAG structure of a Bayesian network from data alone was particularly challenging for a number of reasons that we will briefly recall here. From a practical point of view, we saw in
Section

1.4.2
that treating structure learning as an optimization problem meant that we had to search for a single best scoring DAG in a space that was not only discrete (ruling out gradient based methods for optimization), but was most importantly combinatorially large (the number of DAGs with
d
nodes being
2^{\Theta(d^{2})}
). Moreover, these methods also rely implicitly on the fact that we can “trust” the score being maximized, that is we have enough data to draw any conclusion. If the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
is small though, we may be in situations where either (1) the DAG with the highest score may be an artifact of the finiteness of
{\mathcal{D}}
, akin to “overfitting” in machine learning, or (2) many other (not necessarily Markov equivalent) DAGs might be slightly suboptimal due to the lack of evidence and wouldn’t be considered if all we search for is a single graph.

Another major challenge of structure learning is the problem of
identifiability
: even on the other end of the spectrum where we have a large amount of data, there are still inherent ambiguities remaining due to Markov equivalence (
Section

1.1.2
). This means that any method finding a single best DAG has the risk of returning only an arbitrary element of the Markov equivalence class— unless we work with the CPDAG representation
(
Spirtes et al. 2000
;
Chickering 2002
)
, and even in that case making predictions would still require either (1) the selection of an arbitrary element of the Markov equivalence class (MEC), or (2) an aggregation of the predictions made for a possibly exponential number of elements in the MEC. This may not be problematic as long as we are fully aware that Bayesian networks encode purely statistical dependencies, but this becomes an issue as soon as we want to apply structure learning for causal purposes (
i.e.
,
causal discovery
). Despite largely using the same tools as those of
Section

1.4
, returning an arbitrary DAG from the Markov equivalence class now has the risk of leading to unsafe, or even confidently wrong, causal predictions.

We could instead consider
all
the structures that are compatible with the data when making our predictions (
Madigan & Raftery 1994
; see also
Section

1.3.1
). We take a Bayesian perspective of structure learning
(
Madigan et al. 1994
)
, where our objective is no longer to find a single DAG, but to characterize the whole posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
given a dataset of observations
{\mathcal{D}}
. Recall from Bayes’ rule that the posterior is given by

(7.1.1)

P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})=\frac{P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)P(G)}{P({\mathcal{D}})}\mathchar 59\relax

where
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)
is the marginal likelihood and
P(G)
is the prior over graphs. We are using the
marginal
likelihood here since the parameters
\theta
of the Bayesian network have been marginalized over. Although computing
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)
is in general intractable and may necessitate some approximations
(
Cheeseman & Stutz 1996
;
Chickering & Heckerman 1997
)
, we will assume in this chapter that we can compute the marginal likelihood analytically and efficiently
(
Cooper & Herskovits 1992
)
. For the prior
P(G)
, some options have been proposed in the literature that would encourage sparsity
(
Eggeling et al. 2019
)
, or are edge-dependent
(
Rittel & Tschiatschek 2023
)
.

Finding this posterior is at least as difficult as the original problem of structure learning. Indeed, now we don’t simply have to search for one element in the vast space of DAGs, but we have to specify one value (probability) for each individual DAG, and all of this while maintaining the normalization of the distribution; this makes the constant
P({\mathcal{D}})
in
Equation

7.1.1
intractable in general. That being said, having access to the posterior distribution gives us a more complete view of the problem: for example, maximizing the Bayesian score in
Equation

1.4.4
reduces to
maximum a posteriori
(MAP) estimation on
Equation

7.1.1
.

In most cases though, sampling DAGs from this posterior distribution will be sufficient, rather than evaluating the posterior probability of some graph. This means that we have a distribution over discrete and compositional objects (directed acyclic graphs), known up to an intractable normalization constant (
P({\mathcal{D}})
), that we want to sample from; this is a perfect case of application for GFlowNets, as we will see in this chapter. But before this, we will review some existing methods in the following sections based either on MCMC or variational inference.

Exact sampling

Even if the task seems daunting, sampling exactly from
Equation

7.1.1
is sometimes possible in very specific cases using
dynamic programming
. If the marginal likelihood has a certain structure, we can compute the marginal posterior probabilities of certain edges being present in time
O(d2^{d})

(
Koivisto & Sood 2004
;
Koivisto 2006
)
, a significant improvement over the super-exponential number of DAGs, albeit still being exponential. Relaxing the structure to any modular marginal likelihood,
Tian & He 2009
showed that these edge marginals can be computed in
O(d3^{d})
. This in turns allows for exact sampling from the posterior distribution at relatively minimal cost
(
He et al. 2016
;
Talvitie et al. 2020
)
. Recently,
Harviainen & Koivisto 2024
showed that the cost of exactly sampling from the posterior can be brought down to
O(2.829^{d})
by bypassing completely the initial computation of the marginals. Dynamic programming can also be used in conjunction with MCMC to provide an informative proposal distribution allowing for larger moves in the space of DAGs
(
Eaton & Murphy 2007
)
.

7.1.1.
Structure Markov chain Monte Carlo

Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) methods are in general natural choices for Bayesian inference, and it is no different for structure learning. The
Structure MCMC
algorithm (MC
3
;
Madigan & York 1995
) was one of the very first MCMC methods (and one of the first Bayesian structure learning methods in general) designed over the space of DAGs. It operates with a Metropolis-Hastings scheme (
Section

2.1.2
), where the proposal distribution applies mutations to the current graph by either adding or removing a single directed edge. While deleting an edge is always a legal move (in the sense that it also yields a valid DAG), adding an edge on the other hand requires a careful verification to ensure that it wouldn’t introduce a cycle (
Giudici & Castelo 2003
; see also
Section

7.2.2
). This simple set of moves alone leads to very slow mixing though, consequently slowing down the convergence of the Markov chain to the posterior distribution. This set of moves was later enhanced with the reversal of a single edge
(
Grzegorczyk & Husmeier 2008
)
, to encourage mixing between Markov equivalence classes
(
Giudici & Castelo 2003
)
.
Figure

7.1
illustrates the legal moves allowed by these MCMC methods.

Figure 7.1
.
Illustration of the moves made by a proposal distribution in structure Markov chain Monte Carlo, where starting from a certain DAG, we can either add, subtract
(
Madigan & York 1995
)
, or reverse
(
Grzegorczyk & Husmeier 2008
)
a single edge.

Instead of working in the space of DAGs directly,
Friedman & Koller 2003
introduced
Order MCMC
where the Markov chain moves in the space of node orders, further improving the mixing time but at the cost of introducing some bias
(
Ellis & Wong 2008
)
. This prompted a number of derivatives operating on spaces different from the space of DAGs, such as the space of partial orders
(
Niinimäki et al. 2016
)
or the space of node partitions
(
Kuipers & Moffa 2017
)
. An alternative to improve convergence is to change the strategy to accept new moves, switching the standard Metropolis-Hastings update for Gibbs sampling
(
Mansinghka et al. 2006
)
or a birth-death process
(
Jennings & Corcoran 2018
)
, or to change the set of legal moves
(
Castelo & Kocka 2003
;
Kuipers et al. 2021
)
. Recently,
Viinikka et al. 2020
incorporated many of these advances into an efficient MCMC algorithm operating over partial orders called
Gadget
.

7.1.2.
Variational inference

Approximating the posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
with variational inference as we presented it in
Section

1.2
faces a number of challenges. The first one is that variational inference is better adapted to problems with continuous variables, where we can leverage the reparametrization trick of
Section

1.1.4
; nevertheless, some works have successfully applied it to discrete problems
(
Maddison et al. 2017
;
Jang et al. 2017
)
. The second and major challenge though is that the acyclicity constraint is extremely difficult to encode in a variational framework. In other words, it is relatively easy to generate directed graphs with a variational distribution, but far more complex to guarantee that the support of this distribution is limited to directed
acyclic
graphs. In this section, we will present two models representative of how the community has been approaching the problem of Bayesian structure learning from the perspective of variational inference. We will provide a more exhaustive overview in
Section

8.1.2
.

Continuous relaxation of the prior distribution

Recall that the objective of variational inference is to find a distribution
Q_{\phi}(G)
that best approximates the intractable distribution (here
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
) from a family of tractable distributions.
Lorch et al. 2021
chose to model this as a latent variable model, with a latent variable
Z=(U\mathchar 59\relax V)
, where
U\mathchar 59\relax V\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times k}
. The inference model is

(7.1.2)

\displaystyle Q(G\mid Z)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\neq j}Q(G_{ij}\mid{\bm{u}}_{i}\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{v}}_{j})

where

\displaystyle Q(G_{ij}=1\mid{\bm{u}}_{i}\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{v}}_{j})=\sigma\big({\bm{u}}_{i}^{\top}{\bm{v}}_{j}\big)\mathchar 59\relax

and where
\sigma
is the sigmoid function. This is a distribution over
directed
graphs, since a priori
\sigma\big({\bm{u}}_{i}^{\top}{\bm{v}}_{j}\big)\neq\sigma\big({\bm{u}}_{j}^{\top}{\bm{v}}_{i}\big)
, but not over
acyclic
graphs yet. To encode acyclicity,
Lorch et al. 2021
took inspiration from the continuous relaxations mentioned in
Section

1.4.3
. More specifically, recall that there exist functions
h
over directed graphs such that
h(G)=0
iff.
G
is a DAG; an example of such a function is
h(G)=\Tr\big(\exp(G)\big)-d

(
Zheng et al. 2018
)
. The acyclicity is then encouraged thanks to a prior over the latent variables define by the Gibbs distribution

(7.1.3)

Q(Z)\propto\exp\big(\!-\!\lambda\mathbb{E}_{Q(G\mid Z)}\big[h(G)\big]\big)\mathchar 59\relax

with
\lambda
being a hyperparameter controlling the “strength” of this prior. In particular, as
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
, the support of this prior becomes restricted to DAGs, and in turn this makes the support of the overall distribution
Q(G)=\int_{Z}Q(G\mid Z)Q(Z)dZ
also limited to DAGs. But for finite values of
\lambda
, there is unfortunately no guarantee that this distribution will be over DAGs only. Note that while it may seem like
Q(G)
is parameter-free, the posterior distribution is actually approximated with an empirical distribution based on particles
\{Z^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^{M}
, which effectively constitute the variational parameters. This particular model is called
DiBS

(
Lorch et al. 2021
)
, but the idea of having a continuous relaxation of acyclicity in the prior has been used more broadly
(
Annadani et al. 2021
)
.

Variable ordering

A key observation is that under a certain ordering of the variables (a topological ordering), the adjacency matrix of a DAG is guaranteed to be strictly upper-triangular. In other words, any (weighted) adjacency matrix of a DAG can be written as
G=P^{\top}UP
, where
P
is a permutation matrix and
U
is strictly upper triangular. We can approximate
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
with a mean field approximation of the form
Q_{\phi}(G)=Q_{\phi}(P)Q_{\phi}(U)
, with two appropriate distributions. On the one hand,
Q_{\phi}(U)
is a distribution over weighted upper triangular matrices, which is easy to model (
e.g.
, with a collection of Normal distributions). On the other hand, we can make use of continuous relaxations such as the Gumbel-Sinkhorn distribution
(
Mena et al. 2018
)
in order to model the distribution
Q_{\phi}(P)
over permutation matrices. This particular method is called
BCD Nets

(
Cundy et al. 2021
)
, and has been applied specifically to linear-Gaussian models where
G
is a weighted adjacency matrix. This idea of decomposition with a permutation matrix has also been applied to a broader class of Bayesian networks
(
Charpentier et al. 2022
;
Annadani et al. 2023
)
.

7.2.
GFlowNet over directed acyclic graphs

As an alternative to MCMC and variational inference, we saw in
Chapter

4
that generative flow networks were particularly adapted (1) when the target distribution was known only up to an intractable normalization constant, and (2) when the objects to be generated were discrete and had a compositional structure, which is the case of DAGs. In this section we will present the foundations of how to model any distribution over DAGs with a GFlowNet, and defer the discussion about the specific application to Bayesian structure learning (
i.e.
, the choice of reward function) to
Section

7.4
.

7.2.1.
Structure of the GFlowNet

We consider a GFlowNet
{\mathcal{G}}
where the states themselves are DAGs over
d
(labeled) nodes; without loss of generality, we will assume that the vertices of all the DAGs are labeled with
\{1\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax d\}
. Since the states of the GFlowNet are graphs, we will use the notation
G\in{\mathcal{S}}
to denote a state, in favor of
s
as in
Chapter

4
;
{\mathcal{S}}
represents the space of all possible DAGs over
d
nodes. A transition
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
in this GFlowNet corresponds to adding a single edge to
G
in order to obtain the graph
G^{\prime}
. In other words, the graphs are constructed one edge at a time, starting from the initial state
G_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}
which is the fully disconnected graph over
d
nodes. Since all the states of the GFlowNet are valid DAGs, they are all terminating states (
i.e.
, connected to the terminal state
\bot
) with a corresponding reward
R(G)
; we will see in
Section

7.2.4
that having
{\mathcal{X}}\equiv{\mathcal{S}}
(to use the notations of
Section

2.2.2
) has consequences on the conditions one can apply here.
Figure

7.2
shows an illustration of the structure of such a GFlowNet, where the states are DAGs over
d=3
nodes; see also
Figure

7.9
for the full structure of the GFlowNet over DAGs with
d=3
nodes, with
|{\mathcal{S}}|=25
states and
48
transitions.

Figure 7.2
.
Structure of a GFlowNet over DAGs. The states of the GFlowNet correspond to DAGs, with the initial state
G_{0}
being the completely disconnected graph. Each state
G
is terminating (
i.e.
, connected to the terminal state
\bot
, represented by blue arrows for brevity) and associated with a reward
R(G)
. Transitioning from one state to another corresponds to adding an edge to the graph. The state in red is invalid since the graph includes a cycle.

As mentioned in
Section

4.1.2
, we take a
constructive
approach with a GFlowNet, in the sense that we are only allowed to add edges, unlike the MCMC methods of
Section

7.1.1
where the deletion and reversal of edges were also allowed. Only adding edges to the graphs guarantees that the structure of
{\mathcal{G}}
is itself a pointed DAG, since the number of edges in each state strictly increases as we transition. This application of GFlowNets to graphs also highlights the importance of the pointed DAG structure of the GFlowNet
{\mathcal{G}}
itself, as opposed to a tree structure as in
Section

2.3.2
, since there are multiple paths leading to the same state: for any graph
G
with
K
edges, there are
K!
possible paths from the initial state
G_{0}
leading to
G
, because the edges of
G
may have been added in any order. This combinatorially large number of trajectories will constitute a challenge when
evaluating
the terminating state probability of a certain state (
Definition

2.2.6
; not sampling from it though, which can always be done with
Algorithm

2
); we will come back to this in
Section

7.6.1
.

7.2.2.
Efficient verification of valid actions

To guarantee the integrity of the GFlowNet, we have to ensure that adding a new edge to some state
G
also results in a valid DAG. This means that if we have a candidate edge we want to add, this edge (1) must not be already present in
G
, and (2) must not introduce a cycle. While the first condition is generally easy to verify, the second condition requires checking if the resulting graph contains any cycle; this can be done, for example, by finding a topological order of the vertices (if none can be found, then the action is invalid). Although each operation can be relatively inexpensive (typically linear in the size of
G
), the fact that this verification must be done for all
d(d-1)
edges one may want to add makes this solution impractical to obtain all the valid actions from
G
, especially since we would need to do this verification frequently (
i.e.
, every time we transition in the GFlowNet).

Alternatively, it is also possible to filter out invalid actions using some
binary mask

{\bm{M}}\in\{0\mathchar 59\relax 1\}^{d\times d}
that depends on the graph
G
, which can be updated efficiently since the graphs in the GFlowNet are constructed one edge at a time.
Giudici & Castelo 2003
used a similar concept to efficiently obtain the legal moves their MCMC sampler may take. This mask depends not only on
G
itself, but also on the transpose of its transitive closure.

Definition 7.2.1

(Transpose of the transitive closure)
.

The
transpose of the transitive closure
of a DAG
G
, denoted by
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}^{\top}
, is the directed graph defined by

(7.2.1)

i\rightarrow j\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}^{\top}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad j\rightsquigarrow i\in G.

In other words, there is an edge
i\rightarrow j
in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}^{\top}
if and only if there exists a path
j\rightsquigarrow i
from
j
to
i
in
G
. We use the convention that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}^{\top}[i\mathchar 59\relax i]=1
for any node
i\in G
.

In graph theory, the transitive closure of a DAG
G
encodes in its edges the accessibility of any node from any other node through a path following the edges of
G
. Taking its transpose allows us to consider “reverse paths”, which will be important in what follows; note that taking the transpose of a graph is exactly equivalent to transposing its adjacency matrix. It is easy to verify that, apart from the self-loops existing due the convention
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}^{\top}[i\mathchar 59\relax i]=1
, the transpose of the transitive closure of a DAG
G
is also itself a DAG (
i.e.
,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}^{\top}
does not contain any cycle of length greater than 1). The following proposition shows how we can use both
G
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}^{\top}
in order to construct the binary mask
{\bm{M}}
to identify in constant time all the valid actions that can be taken from
G
.

Proposition 7.2.2

(Mask)
.

Let
G_{t}
be a DAG and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}
the transpose of its transitive closure. Let
{\bm{M}}_{t}
be a
d\times d

binary mask
associated with
G_{t}
, defined for any pair of nodes
(i\mathchar 59\relax j)
in
G_{t}
as

(7.2.2)

{\bm{M}}_{t}[i\mathchar 59\relax j]\triangleq 1-\big(G_{t}[i\mathchar 59\relax j]\vee\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}[i\mathchar 59\relax j]\big).

Then adding an edge
u\rightarrow v
to
G_{t}
is a valid action for the GFlowNet over DAGs defined in
Section

7.2.1
(in the sense that it is not already present in
G_{t}
, and it doesn’t introduce a cycle) if and only if
{\bm{M}}_{t}[u\mathchar 59\relax v]=1
.

Proof.

Adding an edge
u\rightarrow v
to
G_{t}
is a valid action in the GFlowNet over DAGs if and only if

(1)

the edge
u\rightarrow v
is not already present in
G_{t}
, meaning that
G_{t}[u\mathchar 59\relax v]=0
;

(2)

and the edge
u\rightarrow v
does not introduce a cycle, meaning that there is no path
v\rightsquigarrow u
from
v
to
u
in
G_{t}
(otherwise
u\rightarrow v
would close the cycle). In terms of the transpose of the transitive closure of
G_{t}
, this means that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}[u\mathchar 59\relax v]=0
.

Using
Equation

7.2.2
, both conditions above can be summarized as
{\bm{M}}_{t}[u\mathchar 59\relax v]=1
.
∎

Although this allows the verification of all
d(d-1)
possible edges at once in constant time, constructing the transpose of the transitive closure
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}^{\top}
still remains an operation equally as expensive as the naive solution described above (
e.g.
, the transitive closure can be constructed in
O(d^{3})
using the Floyd-Warshall algorithm). Fortunately, we can leverage the fact that graphs in the GFlowNet of
Section

7.2.1
are constructed one edge at a time to efficiently update the transpose of the transitive closure every time a new edge is added.

Proposition 7.2.3

(Update of the mask components)
.

Let
G_{t}
be a DAG and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}
the transpose of its transitive closure. Suppose that
G_{t+1}
is the result of adding the edge
u\rightarrow v
to
G_{t}
. Then for any pair of nodes
(i\mathchar 59\relax j)
in the graph, we have

(7.2.3)

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t+1}^{\top}[i\mathchar 59\relax j]=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}[i\mathchar 59\relax j]\vee\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}[u\mathchar 59\relax j]\wedge\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}[i\mathchar 59\relax v]\big).

Moreover, we also have
G_{t+1}[i\mathchar 59\relax j]=G_{t}[i\mathchar 59\relax j]\vee\big(\mathds{1}(i=u)\mathds{1}(j=v)\big)
.

Proof.

The update of
G_{t+1}
is immediate. By definition of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t+1}^{\top}
, for some pair of nodes
(i\mathchar 59\relax j)
, we have that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t+1}^{\top}[i\mathchar 59\relax j]=1
if and only if there exists a path
j\rightsquigarrow i
from
j
to
i
in
G_{t+1}
. Since
G_{t+1}
is the result of adding a single edge
u\rightarrow v
to
G_{t}
, there are only two ways a path
j\rightsquigarrow i
could exist in
G_{t+1}
:

(1)

either this path already existed in
G_{t}
, in which case we have
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}[i\mathchar 59\relax j]=1
;

(2)

or this path goes through
u\rightarrow v
, meaning there exist two paths
j\rightsquigarrow u
and
v\rightsquigarrow i
in
G_{t}
. In that case, we have
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}[u\mathchar 59\relax j]=1
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}[i\mathchar 59\relax v]=1
respectively. This also covers the cases where either
j\equiv u
or
i\equiv v
, since by convention
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}[u\mathchar 59\relax u]=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}[v\mathchar 59\relax v]=1
.

This concludes the proof, since
Equation

7.2.3
corresponds to rewriting the conditions above in logical form.
∎

Figure 7.3
.
Online update of the mask
{\bm{M}}
. The mask
{\bm{M}}_{t}
associated with
G_{t}
represents (in black) the edges that can be added to
G_{t}
to obtain a valid DAG.
{\bm{M}}_{t}
is decomposed in two parts (
Proposition

7.2.2
): (1) the adjacency matrix of
G_{t}
(top) and (2) the transpose of its transitive closure
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}}^{\top}_{t}
(bottom). To update the mask and obtain
{\bm{M}}_{t+1}
associated with
G_{t+1}
, the result of adding the edge
C\rightarrow A
to
G_{t}
, each component must be updated separately (
Proposition

7.2.3
), and then recombined.

The decomposition of the mask
{\bm{M}}
as well as the update of its two components are illustrated in
Figure

7.3
. The second term in
Equation

7.2.3
can be viewed in matrix form as a (binary) outer product
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}[:\mathchar 59\relax v]\otimes\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}[u\mathchar 59\relax:]
. Overall, the update of the mask is dominated by the update of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}^{\top}
, which is done in
O(d^{2})
; however, since these are all binary operations, this can be implemented very efficiently in practice.
Proposition

7.2.3
suggests that we can keep track of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t}^{\top}
along with the graph
G_{t}
itself, and update both every time we make a transition in the GFlowNet; the mask
{\bm{M}}_{t}
is then built “lazily” from both components any time it is necessary (
e.g.
, to filter out invalid actions in the forward transition probability distribution, see
Section

7.3
). The transpose of the transitive closure is initialized as
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{0}^{\top}={\bm{I}}_{d}
, due to the convention adopted in
Definition

7.2.1
. The pseudo-code for sampling from the GFlowNet over DAGs, and its interplay with the mask, is available in
Algorithm

6
.

Algorithm 6

Sampling a DAG from the GFlowNet defined in
Section

7.2
.

1:

A forward transition probability
P_{F}(\cdot\mid G;{\bm{M}})
depending on a mask
{\bm{M}}
; see
Section

7.3.4
.

2:

A sample DAG
G\sim P_{F}^{\top}(G)
from the terminating state distribution.

3:

Initialization of the state:
s_{0}=(G_{0}\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{I}}_{d})
, where
G_{0}
is the empty DAG over
d
nodes

4:

repeat

5:

Construct the mask
{\bm{M}}_{t}
based on
s_{t}

\triangleright

Proposition

7.2.2

6:

Sample the next graph:
G_{t+1}\sim P_{F}(\cdot\mid G_{t};{\bm{M}}_{t})

7:

if

G_{t+1}\neq\bot

then

8:

Update the state
s_{t+1}=(G_{t+1}\mathchar 59\relax\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{G}_{t+1}^{\top})

\triangleright

Proposition

7.2.3

9:

until

G_{T+1}=\bot

10:

return

G_{T}

7.2.3.
Additional structural constraints

In addition to the acyclicity constraint, necessary for the structure of a Bayesian network, we can also encode various structural constraints about the graphs to be generated by the GFlowNet. For example, we may want to generate sparse structures in order to enable efficient inference in those Bayesian networks. This could be done by biasing the reward function
R(G)
towards graphs that have the desired properties, by introducing a prior term that encourages some properties in a
soft
way. Alternatively, we can also encode these constraints in the structure of
{\mathcal{G}}
itself, to have explicit guarantees about the support of the distribution over DAGs. For example, sparsity may be enforced by restricting the number of parents each node may have to be at most
d_{\max}
, which is a common assumption in structure learning
(
Koller & Friedman 2009
)
. In that case, we can encode this by limiting the support of the distribution to be DAGs whose nodes have a maximum of
d_{\max}
parents; this can be achieved by removing from
{\mathcal{G}}
any transition to a graph that would violate this condition. In practice, we can reuse the binary mask of
Proposition

7.2.2
for that purpose, and filter out entire columns that have already exhausted their maximum number of parents:

(7.2.4)

\widetilde{{\bm{M}}}_{t}[i\mathchar 59\relax j]=\mathds{1}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d}G_{t}[k\mathchar 59\relax j]<d_{\max}\right){\bm{M}}_{t}[i\mathchar 59\relax j].

In the context of learning regulatory networks, further properties may also be enforced on the structure of the Bayesian network based on expert knowledge. For example,
Pe’er et al. 2006
used prior biological studies to motivate having a limited number of
regulator genes
,
i.e.
, nodes in the graph with an out-degree greater than zero, from a set of candidate regulators
{\mathcal{C}}
. Similar to
Equation

7.2.4
for limiting the number of parents of each node, these properties can be easily incorporated in the generation process of the GFlowNet by adapting the binary mask
{\bm{M}}
based on these structural constraints.

7.2.4.
Modified detailed balance condition

Although we could use any flow-matching condition from
Sections

4.1.1
and
4.1.3
to guarantee that the GFlowNet over DAGs induces a terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
proportional to the reward, we can exploit the special structure of the GFlowNet to derive more adapted conditions. In particular, since all the states of the GFlowNet are terminating, we can modify the detailed balance condition of
Theorem

4.1.4
to only depend on the transitions probabilities
P_{F}
and
P_{B}
, and not explicitly on a state flow function anymore.

Proposition 7.2.4

(Modified detailed balance)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be a GFlowNet over a state space
{\mathcal{S}}
, with a reward function
R
, a forward transition probability
P_{F}
, and a backward transition probability
P_{B}
. If all the states of the GFlowNet are terminating (i.e.,
{\mathcal{X}}\equiv{\mathcal{S}}
), then for any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
with
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
, the detailed balance condition in
Theorem

4.1.4
(in conjunction with the boundary condition) can be written as

(7.2.5)

R(s^{\prime})P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})P_{F}(\bot\mid s)=R(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)P_{F}(\bot\mid s^{\prime}).

Proof.

Recall from
Theorem

4.1.4
that for any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
, the detailed balance condition is given by

(7.2.6)

F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=F(s^{\prime})P_{B}(s\mid s^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

where
F(s)
is a state flow function, in addition to the boundary condition
F(s\rightarrow\bot)=R(s)
. If all the states are terminating, then we can write the state flow
F(s)
in terms of the reward function and the forward transition probability as

(7.2.7)

P_{F}(\bot\mid s)=\frac{F(s\rightarrow\bot)}{\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})}=\frac{R(s)}{F(s)}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad F(s)=\frac{R(s)}{P_{F}(\bot\mid s)}.

Replacing the state flow function
F
in
Equation

7.2.6
, we get the expected condition
Equation

7.2.5
.
∎

Note that
Proposition

7.2.4
is expressed using the general notation “
s
” for states, since this result can be generally applied to any GFlowNet where
{\mathcal{X}}\equiv{\mathcal{S}}
(the GFlowNet defined in
Section

7.2.1
being one example). This modified version of the detailed balance condition offers a better credit assignment, while remaining local at the level of individual transitions
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
, by directly making use of the reward signal coming from both
R(s)
and
R(s^{\prime})
. It can be interpreted as an instance of forward-looking GFlowNet (FL-GFN;
Pan et al. 2023
), which is a class of GFlowNets we mentioned in
Section

5.2.3
when establishing equivalences with Soft Q-Learning, where local credit is obtained via a decomposition of the reward function; we will come back to this decomposition in the context of structure learning of Bayesian networks in
Section

7.4.1
. Moreover, because this is only a reformulation of the detailed balance condition, we can rely on
Theorem

4.1.4
and guarantee that if
Equation

7.2.5
is satisfied for all transitions in the GFlowNet, then the terminating state probability distribution associated with
P_{F}
is proportional to
R(s)
.

Corollary 7.2.5
.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{X}}\equiv{\mathcal{S}}
. If the modified detailed balance condition of
Proposition

7.2.4
is satisfied for all the transitions
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
s.t.
s^{\prime}\neq\bot
, then the terminating state distribution associated with the corresponding
P_{F}
is proportional to
R
:
P_{F}^{\top}(s)\propto R(s)
.

7.2.5.
Fixed backward transition probability

Because the system of equations in
Equation

7.2.5
may admit many solutions (
i.e.
, forward and backward transition probabilities), we can set the backward transition probability
P_{B}
to some fixed and arbitrary distribution. Throughout this chapter, we will set
P_{B}
as the uniform distribution over the parent states: for a transition
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
in the GFlowNet defined in
Section

7.2.1
, where
G^{\prime}
is a DAG with
K
edges, we have

(7.2.8)

P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})=\frac{1}{K}\mathchar 59\relax

since
G^{\prime}
admits exactly
K
parents, each one being the result of removing a single edge from
G^{\prime}
. Therefore, the only unknown quantity in
Equation

7.2.5
becomes the forward transition probability distribution
P_{F}
, whose parametrization will depend on the binary mask
{\bm{M}}
introduced in
Proposition

7.2.2
; see
Section

7.3
for details. The following proposition shows that once the backward transition probability is fixed, then the modified detailed balance condition admits a unique solution
P_{F}
.

Proposition 7.2.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be the GFlowNet over DAGs defined in
Section

7.2.1
with a reward function
R
. If the backward transition probability
P_{B}
is a fixed distribution over parent states (e.g., as in
Equation

7.2.8
), then there exists a unique (positive) forward transition probability distribution
P_{F}
that satisfies the modified detailed balance condition in
Proposition

7.2.4
for all
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
G^{\prime}\neq\bot
.

Proof.

The proof is based on a reparametrization of the (non-linear) system of equations in
P_{F}
in
Equation

7.2.5
with linear constraints (coming from the fact that
\sum_{G^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(G)}P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)=1
) into an unconstrained system of linear equations;
Equation

7.2.5
is non-linear in
P_{F}
due to the product
P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)P_{F}(\bot\mid s^{\prime})
appearing on the RHS. For any transition
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
such that
G^{\prime}\neq\bot
, we define

(7.2.9)

x(G^{\prime}\mid G)\triangleq\frac{P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)}{P_{F}(\bot\mid G)}.

This is well defined since we are looking for a positive solution, and therefore
P_{F}(\bot\mid G)>0
. We can note that since
P_{F}
is a properly normalized probability distribution, we have

(7.2.10)

\sum_{G^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(G)}x(G^{\prime}\mid G)=\frac{1}{P_{F}(\bot\mid G)}\sum_{G^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(G)}P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)=\frac{1-P_{F}(\bot\mid G)}{P_{F}(\bot\mid G)}=\frac{1}{P_{F}(\bot\mid G)}-1\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the notation
\underline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(G)=\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(G)\backslash\{\bot\}
to denote the children of
G
, except the terminal state
\bot
(recall that all the states of the GFlowNet are terminating, and therefore
\bot
is a child of any state
G
). Using
x(G^{\prime}\mid G)
and
Equation

7.2.10
, we can rewrite the modified detailed balance equation in
Equation

7.2.5
for any transition
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
such that
G^{\prime}\neq\bot
as

(7.2.11)

\displaystyle\frac{R(G^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})}{P_{F}(\bot\mid G^{\prime})}=R(G)\frac{P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)}{P_{F}(\bot\mid G)}

(7.2.12)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle R(G)x(G^{\prime}\mid G)-R(G^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})\sum_{G^{\prime\prime}\in\underline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(G^{\prime})}x(G^{\prime\prime}\mid G^{\prime})=R(G^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime}).

This is a system of linear equations in
x(G^{\prime}\mid G)
, with
p\triangleq|{\mathcal{A}}|-|{\mathcal{S}}|
equations (one for each transition
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
such that
G^{\prime}\neq\bot
), and as many unknowns. If we organize the unknowns into a vector
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
according to a topological order of the DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
(in the sense that
x(G_{1}^{\prime}\mid G_{1})\prec x(G_{2}^{\prime}\mid G_{2})
in
{\bm{x}}
if and only if
G_{1}
is an ancestor of
G_{2}
in
{\mathcal{G}}
), we can rewrite
Equation

7.2.12
as
{\bm{A}}{\bm{x}}={\bm{b}}
(with
{\bm{x}}>0
), where
{\bm{A}}
is an appropriately defined
p\times p
matrix. In fact, due to the topological order used in
{\bm{x}}
, the matrix
{\bm{A}}
is upper-triangular, its diagonal elements are the rewards
R(G)>0
and its off-diagonal elements are
-R(G^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})
. Therefore the matrix
{\bm{A}}
is invertible, and the system
{\bm{A}}{\bm{x}}={\bm{b}}
admits a unique solution. Using variable elimination starting at the “leaves” of
{\mathcal{G}}
(
i.e.
, states with no children other than
\bot
) and moving all the way to the initial state
G_{0}
in reverse topological order, we can see that the solution
{\bm{x}}
is positive—the vector
{\bm{b}}
itself being positive as well.

Since there is a one-to-one mapping between
x(G^{\prime}\mid G)
and the forward transition probability via the transformation

(7.2.13)

\displaystyle P_{F}(\bot\mid G)

\displaystyle=\left[\sum_{G^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(G)}x(G^{\prime}\mid G)+1\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)=P_{F}(\bot\mid G)x(G^{\prime}\mid G)\mathchar 59\relax

based on
Equation

7.2.10
, we can conclude that the modified detailed balance condition admits a unique positive forward transition probability
P_{F}
when
P_{B}
is a fixed distribution over parent states.
∎

The proof of
Proposition

7.2.6
does not rely on the exact form of the backward transition probability (only on the fact that it is fixed), and we could choose any distribution over the parent states for
P_{B}
. In some cases, it has even been shown empirically that learning
P_{B}
along with
P_{F}
, thus leading to an over-parametrization of the problem, may be advantageous
(
Malkin et al. 2022
)
. Our choice of
Equation

7.2.8
is motivated by the fact that we don’t want to encourage specific trajectories
\tau
while generating a DAG
G
one edge at a time. This can be interpreted as finding the corresponding
P_{F}
with maximum conditional entropy.

Proposition 7.2.7
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be the GFlowNet over DAGs defined in
Section

7.2.1
with a reward function
R
. The backward transition probability distribution
P_{B}
defined in
Equation

7.2.8
(i.e., uniform over the parent states) maximizes the conditional entropy over trajectories

(7.2.14)

{\mathcal{H}}(\tau\mid G)=-\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sum_{\tau:G_{0}\rightsquigarrow G}P_{F}(\tau)\log\frac{ZP_{F}(\tau)}{R(G)}\mathchar 59\relax

where
Z=\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{S}}}R(G)
is the partition function, and
P_{F}
is the (unique) forward transition probability distribution associated with
P_{B}
satisfying the modified detailed balance condition of
Proposition

7.2.4
.

Proof.

Let
\tau_{G}
be a trajectory
G_{0}\rightsquigarrow G
from the initial state
G_{0}
to some DAG
G
with
K
edges; we use the subscript “
G
” to emphasize the dependency of the trajectory on the end state. Let us first recall the definition of the conditional entropy over trajectories

(7.2.15)

{\mathcal{H}}(\tau\mid G)\triangleq-\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sum_{\tau_{G}:G_{0}\rightsquigarrow G}P_{F}(\tau_{G})\log\frac{P_{F}(\tau_{G})}{P_{F}^{\top}(G)}\mathchar 59\relax

where
P_{F}^{\top}(G)
is the terminating state probability distribution, which is the marginal of
P_{F}(\tau)=\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}(G_{t+1}\mid G_{t})
for
\tau=(G_{0}\mathchar 59\relax G_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax G\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
with the conventions
G_{T}=G
and
G_{T+1}=\bot
(see also
Section

2.2.3
). Note that
\tau
and
G
in the notation “
{\mathcal{H}}(\tau\mid G)
” represent random variables, and not specific assignments. Since we consider
P_{F}
and
P_{B}
such that they satisfy the modified detailed balance condition of
Proposition

7.2.4
, we know that
P_{F}^{\top}(G)=R(G)/Z
and that the following trajectory balance condition is satisfied:
ZP_{F}(\tau_{G})=R(G)P_{B}(\tau_{G}\mid G)
(
Theorem

4.1.5
). Therefore,

(7.2.16)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(\tau\mid G)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sum_{\tau_{G}:G_{0}\rightsquigarrow G}P_{F}(\tau_{G})\log\frac{ZP_{F}(\tau_{G})}{R(G)}

(7.2.17)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{G\in{\mathcal{S}}}\frac{R(G)}{Z}\sum_{\tau_{G}:G_{0}\rightsquigarrow G}P_{B}(\tau_{G}\mid G)\log P_{B}(\tau_{G}\mid G)

As a function of
P_{B}(\tau_{G}\mid G)
, it is well-known that the distribution maximizing
Equation

7.2.17
is the uniform distribution (over the trajectories leading to
G
):

(7.2.18)

P_{B}(\tau_{G}\mid G)=\frac{1}{K!}\mathchar 59\relax

where
K!
is the total number of trajectories leading to
G
(see
Section

7.2.1
). Let
G^{\prime}
be the result of adding one edge to
G
, and
\tau_{G^{\prime}}
being a trajectory from
G_{0}
to
G^{\prime}
that can be decomposed into a trajectory
\tau_{G}
from
G_{0}
to
G
, followed by the transition
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
. By definition of the backward transition probability of a trajectory and using
Equation

7.2.18
, we get

(7.2.19)

P_{B}(\tau_{G^{\prime}}\mid G^{\prime})=P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{B}(\tau_{G}\mid G)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})=\frac{1}{K+1}\mathchar 59\relax

which corresponds to
P_{B}
being the uniform distribution over parent states
Equation

7.2.8
, since
G^{\prime}
has
K+1
edges.
∎

Zhang et al. 2022a
also showed that, in certain cases further identified by
Mohammadpour et al. 2024
, the uniform backward transition probability
P_{B}
maximizes the expected total entropy of the unique corresponding forward transition probability distribution along complete trajectories, which they defined as a measure of entropy of the overall Markovian flow.

7.3.
Parametrization of the forward transition probabilities

In practice, the forward transition probability distribution will be parametrized by a function, whose parameters will be learned through interactions with the GFlowNet. In this section, we will denote by
\phi
the parameters of this function (
e.g.
, they may be parameters of a neural network), and use the notation
P_{\phi}
to denote the learned components of the forward transition probabilities, instead of
P_{F}^{\phi}
, for clarity and to emphasize their parametrization in
\phi
. Recall from the previous section that the backward transition probabilities
P_{B}
are set to the uniform distribution over parent states
Equation

7.2.8
, and only
P_{\phi}
is learned.

7.3.1.
Learning objective

Similar to how any flow matching condition could be transformed into a learning objective in
Section

4.2.1
, we can also turn the modified detailed balance condition of
Proposition

7.2.4
into a loss function that can be minimized in order to fit the parameters of the neural networks
\phi
parametrizing the forward transition probabilities, by treating it as a non-linear least square problem of the form
{\mathcal{L}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}[\Delta^{2}(G\rightarrow G^{\prime};\phi)]
, where the residual is defined as

(7.3.1)

\Delta(G\rightarrow G^{\prime};\phi)=\log\frac{R(G^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)}{R(G)P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G^{\prime})}\mathchar 59\relax

where
\pi_{b}
is an arbitrary distribution over transitions
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
(such that
G^{\prime}\neq\bot
) with full support. This objective was also introduced in
Section

5.2.4
, where we showed its equivalence with a policy-parametrized version of the Soft Q-Learning algorithm
(
Haarnoja et al. 2017
)
. In practice, we often use the Huber loss
(
Huber 1992
)
instead of the squared loss in
{\mathcal{L}}(\phi)
for stability, following the recommendations from the deep reinforcement learning literature
(
Mnih et al. 2015
)
.

7.3.2.
Improved exploration with off-policy learning

As we showed in
Section

4.2.2
, training a GFlowNet may be done completely
off-policy
, meaning that the (behavior) policy
\pi_{b}
appearing in the loss may be arbitrary as long as it has full-support (
i.e.
, there is no transition s.t.
\pi_{b}(G\rightarrow G^{\prime})=0
). This is essential to improve exploration in the vast space of DAGs. We use the simple
\varepsilon
-sampling
strategy described in
Section

4.2.2
to collect trajectories through interactions with
{\mathcal{G}}
, which consists in using the current policy
P_{\phi}
most of the time and sampling uniformly at random with probability
\varepsilon
. At the beginning of training, we set
\varepsilon=1
in order to enable a fully exploratory behavior, and then we gradually decrease it over the course of training to a baseline value
\varepsilon_{\min}>0
to use more often the current
P_{\phi}
that would become more reliable (while still leaving an option to explore). This is a strategy inspired from the literature in reinforcement learning, and in particular the pioneering work on Deep Q-Networks (
DQN
;
Mnih et al. 2015
); this is not surprising since we saw in
Chapter

5
that GFlowNets share close ties with (maximum entropy) reinforcement learning.

These collected transitions
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
are not directly used to evaluate the loss though, as is standard for the trajectory balance loss in
Algorithm

4
, but they are fed into a
replay buffer

(
Lin 1992
;
Mnih et al. 2015
;
Schaul et al. 2016
)
. This type of buffer stores (up to a certain capacity) all the experience acquired through the interaction with
{\mathcal{G}}
throughout training, which is then “replayed” in the sense that a batch of transitions is sampled from it in order to compute a Monte Carlo estimate of the modified detailed balance loss. This adds yet another layer of “off-policiness” to our behavior policy
\pi_{b}
, which is effectively the combination of
\varepsilon
-sampling (to acquire data) & sampling from the replay buffer (to create a batch to update
\phi
). Our work applying GFlowNet to Bayesian structure learning spearheaded the use of replay buffers in the GFlowNet literature
(
Deleu et al. 2022
)
, which has now gained popularity in this community
(
Vemgal et al. 2023
;
Zhang et al. 2023a
)
.

7.3.3.
Hierarchical representation of the transition probabilities

At every step of the generation of a DAG with the GFlowNet described in
Section

7.2
, we have to make a decision: either (1) we continue adding an edge to the current graph
G
and transition to a new graph
G^{\prime}
with probability
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)
, or (2) we decide to stop adding edges with probability
P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)
and return
G
as a sample of our distribution. Although we could treat each decision equally by defining a
flat
distribution
P_{\phi}(\cdot\mid G)
, the description above suggests a
hierarchical
definition of the forward transition probabilities, where we first decide whether we want to continue adding an edge or not with probability
P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)
, and upon choosing to continue, we can select which edge to add with probability
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})
.

Definition 7.3.1

(Hierarchical representation)
.

Let
G\in{\mathcal{G}}
be a state of the GFlowNet. The forward transition probability distribution
P_{\phi}
has a
hierarchical representation
if

(7.3.2)

\displaystyle P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)

\displaystyle=P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)

(7.3.3)

\displaystyle P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)

\displaystyle=\big(1-P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)\big)P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where
P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)
parametrizes the probability to stop adding new edges to
G
, and
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})
parametrizes a distribution over the next non-terminal states
G^{\prime}
.

Because the GFlowNet is structured in such a way that a transition is the result of adding a single edge to
G
, we can model
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})
not as being a distribution over the next states, but as a probability distribution over the
d(d-1)
possible
edges
one could add to
G
. We can use the binary mask
{\bm{M}}
from
Proposition

7.2.2
in order to filter out actions (edges) that would not lead to a valid DAG
G^{\prime}
(
e.g.
, if this edge were to introduce a cycle), and set
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})=0
for any invalid action, as well as normalize it accordingly.

Moreover, if there is no valid action from
G
other than terminating (
e.g.
, if
G
already contains
d(d-1)/2
edges, which is the maximum number of edges a DAG over
d
nodes can have), we can force termination by setting
P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)=1
. This last constraint is enforced as a post-processing step on top of the (raw) values returned by the neural networks parametrizing
P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)
, based on whether the mask
{\bm{M}}\equiv{\bm{0}}
is identically zero.

7.3.4.
Neural network parametrization

Given that we want to represent the forward transition probability distribution in a hierarchical way, we need to parametrize both
P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)
and
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})
. We choose to parametrize these two components using neural networks taking the current state
G
as an input, to benefit from their capacity to generalize to states that were not encountered during training. In practice, instead of defining two distinct neural networks, we use a single neural network with a common backbone encoding
G
and two separate heads to take advantage of parameter sharing. We experimented with two families of neural network architectures, depending on how the graph
G
is represented: (linear) Transformers
(
Katharopoulos et al. 2020
)
, and Graph Neural Networks
(
Battaglia et al. 2018
)
. There will be no comparative analysis of both architectures in
Section

7.5
and onwards though, since they correspond to two different generations of network architectures in our experiments, the latter having superseded the former for its simplicity and (unreported) equal performance on a non-exhaustive set of cases, and will be used in
Chapter

8
.

Linear Transformer

In
(
Deleu et al. 2022
;
Nishikawa-Toomey et al. 2023
)
, we represented a graph as a collection of
d^{2}
edges, each associated with a binary flag indicating whether the edge was present in
G
(see
Figure

7.4
). Our choice of neural network architecture was then motivated by two factors: we wanted an architecture (1) that is
permutation equivariant
, meaning that any permutation of the edges of
G
in the input would be reflected in the output of the neural network for
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})
(we saw in
Section

7.3.3
that this can be interpreted as a distribution over edges to add), and (2) whose parameters
\phi
do not scale
too much
with the number of nodes
d
. A natural option fitting these requirements would be a Transformer architecture
(
Vaswani et al. 2017
)
; however, because the input of the neural network is of size
d^{2}
already (
i.e.
, one entry for each possible pair of nodes), the self-attention layers of a Transformer would scale in
O(d^{4})
, severely limiting our ability to use a GFlowNet to model a distribution over larger DAGs.

Figure 7.4
.
Linear Transformer architecture for the forward transition probabilities
P_{\phi}(\cdot\mid G)
. The input graph
G
is encoded as a set of
d^{2}
possible edges (including self-loops); each directed edge is embedded using the embeddings of its source and target, with an additional vector indicating whether the edge is present in
G
. The embeddings are fed into a Linear Transformer
(
Katharopoulos et al. 2020
)
, with two separate output heads. The first head (above) gives the probability to add an edge
P_{\phi}(i\rightarrow j\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})
, using the mask
{\bm{M}}
to filter out invalid actions; here, the only valid actions are either adding
B\rightarrow C
or
C\rightarrow B
. The second head (below) gives the probability to terminate the trajectory
P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)
.

We opted for a Linear Transformer
(
Katharopoulos et al. 2020
)
instead, which relies on a linearized attention mechanism, and has the advantage to not suffer from this quadratic scaling in the input size.
Figure

7.4
shows an illustration of the neural network architecture used in
(
Deleu et al. 2022
)
. On the one hand, the distribution
P_{\phi}(i\rightarrow j\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})
over the next edges we can add to
G
(more precisely,
P_{\phi}(G\cup\{i\rightarrow j\}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})
) is

(7.3.4)

P_{\phi}(i\rightarrow j\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})\propto{\bm{M}}[i\mathchar 59\relax j]\exp({\bm{x}}_{ij})\mathchar 59\relax

where
{\bm{x}}
are the logits returned by one head of the Linear Transformer, and where
{\bm{M}}
is the mask associated with
G
; as mentioned in
Section

7.3.3
, this degenerates to all probabilities being equal to
0
if the GFlowNet does not admit any valid action from
G
(
{\bm{M}}\equiv{\bm{0}}
). On the other hand, the probability of terminating
P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)=f_{\phi}({\bm{g}})
is simply a neural network taking as input an embedding
{\bm{g}}
of
G
(
e.g.
, pooling the values returned by the second head of the Linear Transformer), with a sigmoid output.

Graph Neural Network

In subsequent works, starting with
Malkin et al. 2023
, we chose to replace the linear Transformer with an architecture based on Graph Neural Networks (GNN). This family of neural networks fits all the requirements mentioned above, but with the advantage of being more adapted to graph inputs, and overall simpler to implement
(
Godwin et al. 2020
;
Fey & Lenssen 2019
)
. We use a combination of graph networks
(
Battaglia et al. 2018
)
and self-attention blocks
(
Vaswani et al. 2017
)
to encode information about the graph
G
, which
appears in the conditioning of all the quantities of interest. This common backbone returns a graph
embedding
{\bm{g}}
of
G
, as well as 2 embeddings
{\bm{u}}_{i}
&
{\bm{v}}_{i}
for each node
X_{i}
in
G

(7.3.5)

{\bm{g}}\mathchar 59\relax\{{\bm{u}}_{i}\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{v}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}=\mathrm{SelfAttention}_{\phi}\big(\mathrm{GraphNet}_{\phi}(G)\big).

Same as above, we can parametrize the probability of selecting the “
\mathrm{stop}
” action using
{\bm{g}}
with
P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)=f_{\phi}({\bm{g}})
,
where
f_{\phi}
is a neural network with a sigmoid output. Inspired by
Lorch et al. 2021
, we chose to parametrize the remaining distribution of continuing to add an edge as

(7.3.6)

P_{\phi}(i\rightarrow j\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})\propto{\bm{M}}[i\mathchar 59\relax j]\exp\big({\bm{u}}_{i}^{\top}{\bm{v}}_{j}\big).

We will see in subsequent chapters that this neural network architecture is flexible in that it allows us to parametrize additional quantities, necessary for example to sample the parameters of the Bayesian network along with its structure
G
(
Section

8.3.3
).

7.4.
DAG-GFlowNet: Bayesian structure learning with GFlowNets

So far, the description in
Section

7.2
covered the design of a GFlowNet over DAGs, and was completely independent of the application and the choice of reward function
R(G)
. In this section, we will use such a GFlowNet in order to model the posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
in
Equation

7.1.1
over Bayesian networks, thus providing a novel method for Bayesian structure learning (we saw in
Section

7.1
alternative algorithms). Since a GFlowNet that satisfies the modified detailed balance condition of
Proposition

7.2.4
induces a distribution
P_{F}^{\top}(G)\propto R(G)
(
Corollary

7.2.5
), we will naturally define its reward function as the joint probability between a DAG
G
and the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
:

(7.4.1)

R(G)\triangleq P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)P(G)\mathchar 59\relax

where
P(G)
is a prior over DAGs
(
Eggeling et al. 2019
)
, and
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)
is the marginal likelihood; even though the marginal likelihood is in general intractable to compute, we will consider in this chapter only cases where we can compute it analytically in an efficient way. Note that the reward depends implicitly on the fixed dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
. By Bayes’ rule
Equation

1.3.1
, a GFlowNet over DAGs with this choice of reward function will indeed model the posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})\propto R(G)
. We call our method
DAG-GFlowNet

(
Deleu et al. 2022
)
.

7.4.1.
Modularity & computational efficiency

Following prior works on Bayesian structure learning, we assume that both the prior over parameters
P(\theta\mid G)
of the Bayesian network (required to compute the marginal likelihood) and the prior over structures
P(G)
are
modular

(
Heckerman et al. 1995
;
Chickering et al. 1995
)
, meaning that they can be written as a product of terms involving each
X_{j}
and its parents only. As a consequence, the reward
R(G)
is also itself modular, and its logarithm can be written as a sum of local scores that only depend on individual variables and their parents in
G
:

(7.4.2)

\log R(G)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{LocalScore}\big(X_{j}\mid\mathrm{Pa}_{G}(X_{j})\big).

With our choice of reward,
\log R(G)
corresponds to the Bayesian score we introduced in
Section

1.4.2
. Examples of modular scores for the log-marginal likelihood
\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)
include the BDe score
(
Heckerman et al. 1995
)
and the BGe score
(
Geiger & Heckerman 1994
;
Kuipers et al. 2014
)
; see also
Section

B.1.1
for details about these scores. We can observe that the objective in
Equation

7.3.1
used to fit the parameters
\phi
of the neural network for
P_{\phi}
only involves the difference in log-rewards
\log R(G^{\prime})-\log R(G)
between two consecutive states, where
G^{\prime}
is the result of adding some edge
X_{i}\rightarrow X_{j}
to the DAG
G
. Using the assumption of modularity, we can compute this difference efficiently, as the terms in
Equation

7.4.2
remain unchanged for any
j^{\prime}\neq j
:

(7.4.3)

\displaystyle\delta(G\mathchar 59\relax G^{\prime})

\displaystyle\triangleq\log R(G^{\prime})-\log R(G)

(7.4.4)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{LocalScore}\big(X_{j}\mid\mathrm{Pa}_{G}(X_{j})\cup\{X_{i}\}\big)-\mathrm{LocalScore}\big(X_{j}\mid\mathrm{Pa}_{G}(X_{j})\big).

This difference in local scores is sometimes called the
delta score
, or the
incremental value

(
Friedman & Koller 2003
)
, and it has been employed in the literature to improve the efficiency of search algorithms
(
Chickering 2002
;
Koller & Friedman 2009
)
.

7.4.2.
Target network
