Continuing with our inspiration from reinforcement learning, we can make use of other tools that have proven to be effective in that field. Since the modified detailed balance loss we use here is equivalent to (a policy-parametrized version of) the Soft Q-Learning algorithm
(
Haarnoja et al. 2017
)
, a “soft” version of the classic Q-Learning algorithm, it is all the more natural to again borrow tools from Deep Q-Learning
(
Van et al. 2018
)
. In particular, we used a
target network
in our evaluation of
P_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}(\bot\mid G^{\prime})
in
Equation

7.3.1
, where the parameters
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
are only updated periodically at a lower frequency. The residual then becomes

(7.4.5)

\Delta(G\rightarrow G^{\prime};\phi)=\log\frac{R(G^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)}{R(G)P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}(\bot\mid G^{\prime})}.

The use of a target network has been popularized in reinforcement learning
(
Mnih et al. 2015
;
Mnih et al. 2016
;
Lillicrap et al. 2016
)
, and later in self-supervised learning
(
Grill et al. 2020
;
He et al. 2020
;
Caron et al. 2021
)
, to stabilize training.

But beyond the simple analogy with reinforcement learning, we found that having a target network was actually necessary to avoid degeneracy during training. This would happen because of the seemingly lack of “grounding” of the condition in
Proposition

7.2.4
: everything seem to evolve completely freely. To see this better, recall that the modified detailed balance condition is

(7.4.6)

R(G^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)=R(G)P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

for a transition
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
, where the only learnable quantity is
P_{\phi}
. At first glance, a simple way to satisfy this equation regardless of the transition or the reward function would be to set
P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)\approx 0
for all the DAGs
G
, since the probability of terminating appears on both sides of
Equation

7.4.6
, making this equation trivial. Of course since
P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)
is typically parametrized by a neural network with a sigmoid output (
Section

7.3.4
), it would never be truly zero; but it could be so small that it would be effectively as if it were. This would yield a degenerate forward transition probability
P_{\phi}
that would learn to always add edges to the graph, and to never stop. It would be forced to stop only when it reaches the end of the state space, where the graph is “complete” (
i.e.
, it has the maximum number of edges
d(d-1)/2
), there is no edge that can be added without introducing a cycle, and termination is the only option. This would be regardless of the reward function, and clearly not a good way to sample approximately from the posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
.

This last point is in fact critical: we
can’t
set
P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)\approx 0
everywhere, since at least for all the complete graphs
G
, we are forced to terminate and we must have
P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)=1
. That’s why this observation does not contradict our
Corollary

7.2.5
, because it’s not entirely true: there exist some graphs that “ground” the value of
P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)
. But in practice, transitions
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
where
G^{\prime}
is complete are few and far between in our data collection to see the real effect of grounding. Thus, without a target network, what we observe in practice is
P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)
&
P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G^{\prime})
both going towards
0
together. This kind of degeneracy is also a known issue in reinforcement learning. Having a target network for
P_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}(\bot\mid G^{\prime})
allows us to artificially ground that value for some iterations (this one can’t go to
0
along with
P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)
) and mitigates this behavior.

7.4.3.
Limitations of GFlowNets for Bayesian inference

Bayesian inference is particularly appealing when the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
is small to moderately large. Indeed, these are the cases that would benefit the most from an estimation of the epistemic uncertainty over the hypotheses of interest (here, the structure of a Bayesian network). When the dataset becomes very large though, the posterior distribution becomes more and more peaky, and in the limit approaches a distribution whose support is the Markov equivalence class of the “true” structure
G^{\star}
(following the result of
Theorem

1.4.3
, provided that the support of the prior covers it). As a consequence, the delta-score in
Equation

7.4.3
, which is required to compute the modified detailed balance loss, can take a wide range of values: adding an edge to a graph can drastically increase or decrease its score (
i.e.
, the log-reward
\log R(G)
). In turn, the neural network parametrizing
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)
needs to compensate for these large fluctuations, making it harder to train.

Unfortunately, some of the standard techniques used in machine learning to tackle this issue, such as the normalization of the inputs, cannot be applied here. Normalizing the delta-scores would be equivalent to modifying the rewards
R(G)
and
R(G^{\prime})
themselves, and it would inevitably change the distribution being approximated. Indeed, if were were to normalize the delta-score by a constant
\alpha
in the residual in
Equation

7.4.5
, we would get

(7.4.7)

\frac{\log R(G^{\prime})-\log R(G)}{\alpha}+\log\frac{P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)}{P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}(\bot\mid G^{\prime})}=\log\frac{R(G^{\prime})^{1/\alpha}P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)}{R(G)^{1/\alpha}P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}(\bot\mid G^{\prime})}.

In other words, instead of approximating the posterior
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
, we would approximate a distribution
\propto P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})^{1/\alpha}
. Solutions may include:

(1)

Using a scheduled temperature, slowly decreasing the temperature from a large value of
\alpha
(simpler to model with a GFlowNet) to
\alpha=1
over the course of training, inspired by
simulated annealing

(
Kirkpatrick et al. 1983
)
. We could even have the GFlowNet being conditioned on the temperature (
Kim et al. 2024a
; see also
Section

3.3
);

(2)

Reparametrizing the forward transition probability distribution
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)
to better handle large fluctuations of delta-scores. For example in a different context,
Metz et al. 2019
have a network output 2 quantities
o_{1}
and
o_{2}
that are then combined into “
\exp(o_{1})o_{2}
”. This is inspired by the exponent/mantissa decomposition of the floating point representation;

(3)

Rescaling the outputs of the neural network (logits) with the size of the dataset
|{\mathcal{D}}|
. We have found that this simple solution works well in some cases, but at the cost of worsening the stability of optimization.

Note that none of this is specific to DAG-GFlowNet alone, and these considerations hold for modeling posterior distributions with a GFlowNet in general when the dataset is large. Another promising direction is to take an incremental approach as described in
Section

5.3.2
. This would allow us to have a good enough starting point every time new data gets incorporated, to warm start training and refine the parameters
\phi
of the GFlowNet more and more.

7.4.4.
Limitations of DAG-GFlowNet

In addition to the general limitations of applying GFlowNets to the problem of Bayesian inference mentioned in the previous section, there are also limitation specific to DAG-GFlowNet itself. In particular, we have made the strong assumption in this chapter that the marginal likelihood
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)
(necessary to compute the reward, or the delta-score) could be computed in an efficient way. This limits the scope of application of DAG-GFlowNet to cases where the following marginalization

(7.4.8)

P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)=\int_{\theta}P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P(\theta\mid G)d\theta\mathchar 59\relax

can be done analytically, which is only possible for a handful of models and carefully chosen priors
P(\theta\mid G)
. In this chapter, we will only consider linear-Gaussian models as introduced in
Example

1.1.1
with a Normal-Wishart prior over parameters leading to the
Bayesian Gaussian equivalent
score (BGe;
Geiger & Heckerman 1994
;
Kuipers et al. 2014
), and Bayesian networks with categorical random variables with a Dirichlet prior over parameters leading to the
Bayesian Dirichlet equivalent
score (BDe;
Heckerman et al. 1995
). The “equivalent” in both names means that two Markov equivalent structures will have the same marginal likelihood under these scores. We note that marginalization is also possible analytically in cases where the conditional probability distributions are parametrized by Gaussian Processes
(
Kügelgen et al. 2019
)
, which may encode non-linear relations. Instead, we will see in the next chapter how to extend the ideas of DAG-GFlowNet to more general models, including non-linear ones, where the marginalization above will not be necessary anymore.

Finally, another limitation is that we assume that the model has no hidden variables, and we fully observe the whole system. This is a standard assumption in most of the literature on structure learning, with some notable exceptions
(
Friedman 1998
;
Boyen et al. 1999
;
Murphy 2002
)
.

7.5.
Empirical comparison with the exact posterior

Even if generative flow networks were designed specifically to sample from distributions where the partition function was too expensive to compute, it can sometimes be useful to apply them to smaller problems where the target distribution is fully known, in order to test the accuracy of the approximation returned by the GFlowNet. In the context of Bayesian structure learning, this means testing it on scenarios where the number of random variables is small enough, so that the number of possible DAGs is not prohibitively large. In those settings, we can compute the posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
with an exhaustive enumeration of all the DAGs
G
. This is feasible for
d=5
variables, where we have a total of
29\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}281
DAGs.

Experimental setup

We generated a synthetic dataset of observations
{\mathcal{D}}
using a randomly constructed linear-Gaussian Bayesian network, as we described in
Example

1.1.1
. We follow the experimental setup of
Lorch et al. 2021
, which we describe below.

(1)

We first sampled the DAG structure
G^{\star}
of the Bayesian network over
d=5
random variables using an Erdős-Rényi model
(
Erdős & Rényi 1960
)
. The probability of creating an edge between two nodes was scaled in such a way that there is on average
d
edges; this is a setting commonly referred to as “ER1” in the structure learning literature
(
Lachapelle et al. 2020
)
;

(2)

Once the structure is known, we sample the parameters
\theta^{\star}_{ji}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0\mathchar 59\relax 1)
of the linear-Gaussian model
Equation

1.1.3
randomly from a unit Normal distribution. The noise variables are given by
\varepsilon_{i}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0\mathchar 59\relax 0.01)
. This specifies completely the Bayesian network;

(3)

Once the full Bayesian network
(G^{\star}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\star})
is known, we generate
N=100
observations from it using ancestral sampling, as described in
Section

1.1.3
, to form the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
.

After this process, the Bayesian network
(G^{\star}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\star})
used to generate data is completely discarded, and we only keep the resulting dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
, required to compute the reward
R(G)
. In this section, we will compute the exact posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
by computing the reward of all the possible DAGs over
d=5
nodes, and normalizing their values to sum to
1
. We used the
BGe score

(
Geiger & Heckerman 1994
)
in order to compute the reward, with the default hyperparameters suggested by
Kuipers et al. 2014
. We also used a uniform prior
P(G)
over DAGs.

7.5.1.
Evaluation of the terminating state probability with dynamic programming

We introduced generative flow networks only as sampling machines. While we saw in
Section

2.1.1
that this is sufficient for most applications, it might be sometimes necessary to also compute the probability
P_{F}^{\top}(x)
for some terminating state
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
, once we have learned
P_{F}
. This is the case for example if we want to compare the approximation
P_{F}^{\top}(x)
with the target distribution
\propto R(x)
(in cases where we can compute it). In this section, we place ourselves in the general case, where the we have a GFlowNet over a pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
(where all the states are not necessarily all terminating, unlike the application in this chapter), with a forward transition probability
P_{F}
; this
P_{F}
may have been learned with any flow matching loss, and not necessarily the modified detailed balance loss we consider in this chapter.

Algorithm 7

Evaluation of
P_{F}^{\top}(x)
with dynamic programming.

1:

A pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
, a forward transition probability
P_{F}
.

2:

The probability
P_{F}^{\top}(x)
for all terminating states
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
.

3:

Find a topological ordering
\sigma
of
{\mathcal{G}}

\triangleright
Excluding
s_{0}
&
\bot

4:

Initialize
F(s_{0})\leftarrow 1

5:

for all

s^{\prime}\in\sigma

do

6:

F(s^{\prime})\leftarrow\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s)}F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)

7:

return

P_{F}^{\top}(x)\equiv F(x)P_{F}(\bot\mid x)

In general, computing the terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\top}(x)
requires summing over all the trajectories leading to
x
(
Definition

2.2.6
), which may be prohibitively large and involves redundant components across different terminating states. Here, we will instead look for a solution that can compute the whole distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
for all the terminating states at once, based on
dynamic programming
. The key observation is to use
Proposition

3.1.8
: if we could construct a flow whose forward transition probability is
P_{F}
, then we could simply read
P_{F}^{\top}
from the flow at the terminating edges. To build the flow, the idea is to “push” a single unit of flow at the initial state
s_{0}
down
{\mathcal{G}}
, following
P_{F}
. The algorithm is given in
Algorithm

7
, and we can show that it is correct.

Proposition 7.5.1
.

Algorithm

7
is correct, and returns the probability
P_{F}^{\top}(x)
for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
.

Proof.

The flow defined by
Algorithm

7
is a Markovian flow, defined uniquely by the edge flow
F(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
(it follows the flow matching condition of
Proposition

6.1.1
). From this, it is clear that
P_{F}
is the forward transition probability associated with this flow (
Definition

3.1.7
). Moreover, its total flow is
Z=F(s_{0})=1
(
Proposition

3.1.4
), thanks to the initialization of the algorithm. We can conclude with
Proposition

3.1.8
that
P_{F}^{\top}(x)=F(x\rightarrow\bot)=F(x)P_{F}(\bot\mid x)
.
∎

This algorithm allows us to evaluate the whole terminating state distribution in
O(|{\mathcal{A}}|)
, making it applicable only on small scale problems, such as the GFlowNet presented in this chapter, for a small enough number of variables
d
.

7.5.2.
Comparison with the exact posterior

To evaluate the quality of the posterior approximation returned by DAG-GFlowNet, we test it on various structural
features
. A feature is a marginal probability on quantities of interest for Bayesian networks. For example, an
edge feature

(
Buntine 1991a
;
Cooper & Herskovits 1992
)
corresponds to the marginal probability of an edge
X_{i}\rightarrow X_{j}
belonging to the graph:

(7.5.1)

P(X_{i}\rightarrow X_{j})=\mathbb{E}_{G\sim P}\big[\mathds{1}(X_{i}\rightarrow X_{j}\in G)\big]\mathchar 59\relax

where the distribution
P
over DAGs may be either the true posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
(target probability), or the terminating state distribution of the GFlowNet
P_{\phi}^{\top}(G)
(estimate). We can also compare these two distributions in terms of their
path feature
, the marginal probability of a directed path
X_{i}\rightsquigarrow X_{j}
existing from
X_{i}
to
X_{j}
, or their
Markov feature
, the marginal probability of
X_{i}
being in the Markov blanket of
X_{j}

(
Friedman & Koller 2003
)
.

(a)

Edge features

(b)

Path features

(c)

Markov blanket features

Figure 7.5
.
Comparison between the exact posterior distribution and the approximation from DAG-GFlowNet, for different structural features: (a) edge features
X_{i}\rightarrow X_{j}
, (b) path features
X_{i}\rightsquigarrow X_{j}
, and (c) Markov blanket features
X_{i}\sim_{M}X_{j}
. Each point corresponds to a feature (
i.e.
, marginal probability) computed for a specific pair
(X_{i}\mathchar 59\relax X_{j})
in a graph over
d=5
nodes, either based on the exact posterior
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
(x-axis), or the approximation found with DAG-GFlowNet (y-axis; see
Section

7.5.1
). Points on the diagonal line
y=x
correspond to an exact agreement between the exact posterior and the approximation
on that pair of variables
. The Pearson correlation coefficient
r
is included at the bottom-right corner of each plot.

In
Figure

7.5
, we compare the probabilities of these features for both the exact posterior and the distribution induced by DAG-GFlowNet, where we repeated the experiment described above with 20 different datasets coming from different Bayesian networks
(G^{\star}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\star})
. We observe that the probabilities of all structural features estimated by the GFlowNet are strongly correlated with the exact marginal probabilities. This shows that DAG-GFlowNet is capable of learning a very accurate approximation of the posterior distribution over graphs
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
.

7.5.3.
Hierarchical variational inference & the advantage of off-policy learning

In
Section

4.3
, we saw that GFlowNets are closely related to hierarchical variational models, especially when they are trained on-policy. In turn, this highlighted a major advantage of GFlowNets over these other variational methods: they can directly be trained off-policy, without having to resort to importance sampling. In this section, we will show this advantage empirically on DAG-GFlowNet. In addition to training the GFlowNet for Bayesian structure learning with the modified detailed balance loss, we also trained it using the trajectory balance loss in
Equation

4.2.6
, as well as with the reverse KL objective introduced in
Equation

4.3.28
, for comparison. For the latter two losses, we considered two settings: either on-policy, using the current
P_{F}
to acquire data, and off-policy using the behavior policy presented in
Section

4.2.2
; note that the reverse KL objective is trained with the estimate presented in
Section

4.3.4
with a local control variate (called “
b_{\mathrm{local}}
” in that section), and it necessitates importance sampling to be trained off-policy.

We compare the performance of all these methods based on the
Jensen Shannon divergence
(
JSD
) between the distribution induced by the GFlowNet
P_{\phi}^{\top}(G)
and the true posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
. Recall that the JSD is given by

(7.5.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{JSD}\big(P_{\phi}^{\top}(G)\mathchar 59\relax P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathrm{KL}\big(P_{\phi}^{\top}(G)\,\|\,M(G)\big)+\mathrm{KL}\big(P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})\,\|\,M(G)\big)\right]

(7.5.3)

\displaystyle\textrm{where}\quad M(G)

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{\phi}^{\top}(G)+P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})}{2}.

We show in
Table

7.1
the performance of all these methods on Bayesian networks with
d\leq 5
random variables, to ensure that the true posterior
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
can still be computed analytically. We observe that on the easiest setting (graphs over
d=3
nodes), all methods accurately approximate the posterior distribution. But as we increase the complexity of the problem, we observe that the accuracy of the approximation with off-policy reverse KL degrades significantly, while the ones found with the off-policy GFlowNet objectives (modified detailed balance & trajectory balance) remain very accurate. We also note that the performance of the on-policy algorithms degrades too, but not as rapidly; furthermore they achieve the same JSD, confirming our observation in
Section

4.3.3
that trajectory balance and reverse KL are closely connected when trained on-policy.

); lower is better. Each method is run on

different datasets.

This trend gets confirmed when we look at structural features, as we did in the previous section. In
Figure

, we show the root mean-square error (
RMSE
) of the edge features (in other words, this is the RMSE of the linear fit in
Figure

7.5(a)
on the diagonal line). This highlights even more the similar behavior of the trajectory balance and the reverse KL losses on-policy.

7.6.
Evaluation on simulated data

In this section, we will consider a larger setting to fully leverage the advantage of GFlowNets to approximate distributions whose partition function is intractable. We take a similar experimental setup as the one described in the previous section (linear-Gaussian model, where data is obtained from randomly generated Bayesian networks), except that the Bayesian networks will now be over
d=20
random variables. To go along with this increase in the number of nodes, we will still generate DAGs
G^{\star}
based on an Erdős-Rényi model, but this time with
2d
edges on average (ER2), to match the experimental settings studied in prior work
(
Lorch et al. 2021
;
Cundy et al. 2021
)
. The reward is still computed using the BGe score, and any experiment is conducted on
20
different randomly generated datasets
{\mathcal{D}}
from
20
different Bayesian networks
(G^{\star}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\star})
.

7.6.1.
Estimation of the terminating state probability

While we saw a method to compute the terminating state probability
P_{F}^{\top}(x)
using dynamic programming in
Section

7.5.1
, this algorithm is impractical once the pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
of the GFlowNet is too large. This is the case for example in DAG-GFlowNet as soon as
d\geq 7
. For lack of a more efficient way to compute
P_{F}^{\top}(x)
for a single terminating state
x
, we will show in this section how to approximate it. We will take the example of DAG-GFlowNet for concreteness here. Recall that the terminating state probability
P_{F}^{\top}(G)
is given by

(7.6.1)

P_{F}^{\top}(G)=\sum_{\tau:G_{0}\rightsquigarrow G}P_{F}(\tau).

If
G
has
K
edges, then this means that there are
K!
trajectories from
G_{0}
to
G
(
i.e.
, adding the edges in any order), making this sum intractable in general. We can leverage the fact that the backward transition probability
P_{B}
induces a distribution
P_{B}(\tau\mid G)
over the trajectories leading to
G
(
Lemma

3.1.6
) to write it as

(7.6.2)

P_{F}^{\top}(G)=\sum_{\tau:G_{0}\rightsquigarrow G}P_{F}(\tau)=\sum_{\tau:G_{0}\rightsquigarrow G}\frac{P_{B}(\tau\mid G)}{P_{B}(\tau\mid G)}P_{F}(\tau)=K!\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tau\sim P_{B}(\tau\mid G)}\big[P_{F}(\tau)\big]\mathchar 59\relax

where
P_{B}(\tau\mid G)=1/K!
regardless of the trajectory
\tau
, since we assumed that the backward transition probability is fixed to the uniform distribution here (
Section

7.2.5
). This suggests a way to get an unbiased estimate of the terminating state probability: we can build a Monte Carlo estimate of this expectation based on sample trajectories
\{\tau^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^{M}
from
P_{B}(\tau\mid G)

(7.6.3)

P_{F}^{\top}(G)\approx\frac{K!}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}P_{F}\big(\tau^{(m)}\big).

We can get those trajectories by removing one edge at time uniformly at random, starting at
G
.

While
Equation

7.6.3
provides an unbiased estimate of
P_{F}^{\top}(G)
, in practice the variance of this estimate will be large due to the combinatorially large space of trajectories leading to
G
, and therefore due to the wide range of values
P_{F}(\tau)
may take. In order to reduce the variance, we can first identify some trajectories that would contribute the most to the sum in
Equation

7.6.1
and complement them with some randomly sampled trajectories as in
Equation

7.6.3
. In other words, if we had access to a subset
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{top}}
of
B
trajectories
\tau:G_{0}\rightsquigarrow G
that have a large
P_{F}(\tau)
, then

(7.6.4)

P_{F}^{\top}(G)=\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{top}}}P_{F}(\tau)+\sum_{\tau\notin{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{top}}}P_{F}(\tau)\approx\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{top}}}P_{F}(\tau)+\frac{K!-B}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}P_{F}\big(\tau^{(m)}\big)\mathchar 59\relax

where the sample trajectories
\{\tau^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^{M}
can be obtained using rejection sampling, with a uniform proposal as above. The estimate in
Equation

7.6.4
is still unbiased, but with a lower variance. We can use
beam-search
to find the “top-scoring” trajectories in
{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{top}}
, with a beam-size
B
, as described in
Algorithm

10
. More precisely, we need to run beam-search, starting at
G_{0}
, in such a way that the trajectories are guaranteed to end at
G
. We can achieve this by constraining the set of actions one can take at each step of expansion to move from a graph
G_{t}
to
G_{t+1}=G_{t}\cup\{e\}
(using this notation to denote that
G_{t+1}
is the result of adding
the edge
e
to
G_{t}
), with the following score:

(7.6.5)

\widetilde{P}_{F}(G_{t+1}\mid G_{t})=\mathds{1}(e\in G)P_{F}(G_{t+1}\mid G_{t}).

In other words, we only keep transitions corresponding to adding edges that we know are in
G
. Note that even though
\widetilde{P}_{F}
is not a properly defined probability distribution (it does not sum to
1
), we can still use this as a scoring function to run beam-search. Since beam-search is only a heuristic procedure, we are not guaranteed to get the top-
B
trajectories with highest
P_{F}(\tau)
necessarily; in practice though, it is an effective method to obtain trajectories with “high enough” scores.

Although we presented these estimates of the terminating state probabilities in the case of DAG-GFlowNet, note that this is a valid strategy whenever the objects to be generated are collections of parts that can be added in any order (
e.g.
, graphs as a collection of edges). This is not true for any arbitrary GFlowNet though. A notable example that does
not
satisfy this requirement is the molecule generation environment described in
Section

2.2.4
.

7.6.2.
Medium-scale linear-Gaussian models

In this section, we will compare the performance of DAG-GFlowNet against representative methods for Bayesian structure learning. This includes two methods based on MCMC (
Section

7.1.1
), MC
3

(
Madigan & York 1995
)
and Gadget
(
Viinikka et al. 2020
)
, and two methods based on variational inference (
Section

7.1.2
), DiBS
(
Lorch et al. 2021
)
and BCD Nets
(
Cundy et al. 2021
)
. In addition to these 4 methods, we also consider two methods based on bootstrapping the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
(
i.e.
, resampling
N
observations with replacement;
Friedman et al. 1999
), each set being used to learn a single DAG with either the constraint-based method PC
(
Spirtes et al. 2000
)
or the score-based method GES
(
Chickering 2002
)
.

Since we know the ground-truth graph
G^{\star}
that was used to generate
{\mathcal{D}}
, we evaluate the performance of each algorithm based on structural metrics comparing to
G^{\star}
. Following the literature in Bayesian structure learning
(
Lorch et al. 2021
;
Cundy et al. 2021
)
, we consider the
expected structural hamming distance
(
\mathbb{E}
-SHD
) between samples of a posterior approximation
\widehat{P}(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
and
G^{\star}

(7.6.6)

\mathbb{E}\textrm{-}\mathrm{SHD}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\widehat{P}(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})}\big[\mathrm{SHD}(G\mathchar 59\relax G^{\star})\big]\mathchar 59\relax

where
\mathrm{SHD}(G\mathchar 59\relax G^{\star})
is the structural hamming distance measuring the edit distance (
i.e.
, adding, or removing edges) between the two DAGs. We also compute the
area under the receiver operating characteristic curve
(
AUROC
) of the edges of
G^{\star}
against the predictions made from the edge marginals (of the posterior approximation) under varying thresholds
(
Ellis & Wong 2008
)
. We observe in
Figures

7.7(a)
and
7.7(b)
that DAG-GFlowNet is competitive against all other methods, achieving a good trade-off between both metrics, behaving closest to Bootstrap PC.

(a)

Expected SHD

(b)

AUROC

(c)

Linear-Gaussian

Figure 7.7
.
Bayesian structure learning of linear-Gaussian Bayesian networks with
d=20
nodes. Results for (a)
\mathbb{E}
-SHD (lower is better) & (b) AUROC (higher is better) are aggregated over 25 randomly generated datasets
{\mathcal{D}}
. Labels: B-PC =
Bootstrap-PC, B-GES = Bootstrap-GES, BCD = BCD Nets, GFN = DAG-GFlowNet. (c) Comparison between the terminating state log-probability
\log P_{\phi}^{\top}(G)
(estimated, see
Section

7.6.1
) and the log-reward
\log R(G)=\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mathchar 59\relax G)
. Each of the
1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}000
points represents a graph sampled with DAG-GFlowNet. The color of each point indicates the number of edges in the corresponding graph. The slope
\Delta
of the linear fit obtained with RANSAC
(
Fischler & Bolles 1981
)
is shown at the bottom-right corner.

But going past a comparison with other Bayesian structure learning methods, we would like to assess that DAG-GFlowNet is
intrinsically
an accurate approximation of
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
. Since the state space is now too large (
\approx 10^{72}
DAGs over
d=20
nodes), we can’t perform the same evaluation as in
Section

7.5.2
anymore. Instead, we can compare the terminating state log-probability of DAG-GFlowNet
\log P_{\phi}^{\top}(G)
with the log reward
\log R(G)
; indeed, since the terminating state probability of the GFlowNet approximated the posterior, we should ideally get by Bayes’ rule

(7.6.7)

\log P_{\phi}^{\top}(G)\approx\log P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})=\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mathchar 59\relax G)-\log P({\mathcal{D}})=\log R(G)-\log P({\mathcal{D}}).

This means that if
\log P_{\phi}^{\top}(G)
is an accurate approximation of
\log P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
, it should correlate with
\log R(G)
with a slope of
1

(
Bengio et al. 2021
)
,
\log P({\mathcal{D}})
being simply a constant offset. Even though the terminating state probability can’t be computed either, we saw in in the previous section how it can be estimated with beam-search & Monte Carlo. We show in
Figure

7.7(c)
that there is indeed a strong linear correlation between
\log P_{\phi}^{\top}(G)
&
\log R(G)
computed on
1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}000
samples from DAG-GFlowNet, with a slope
\Delta
relatively close to
1
. This suggests that it is again an accurate approximation of the posterior on this medium-size problem,
at least around the modes it captures
.

7.6.3.
Criticism of the evaluation metrics

In (Bayesian) structure learning, the evaluation of the performance is often made relative to the “ground-truth” structure
G^{\star}
used to generate the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
. This explains largely why this community has focused a lot of its attention on evaluation with synthetic data, to have a fully controlled setting. However in real-world applications, and especially in scientific discovery where we hope that these tools will be used to find new scientific theories, there is no such thing as a
known
ground truth structure, precisely because that is what we want to infer from data. Thus, any metric relying on such a ground-truth, which the
SHD
and the AUROC we used in the previous section are examples of, are not applicable
by design
beyond synthetic (or pseudo-real) data.

Another drawback of a structural metric like the SHD that measures differences with
G^{\star}
is that it tends to favor sparse graphs with very few edges
(
Lorch et al. 2022
)
. In the limit case, a trivial algorithm that would always predict the empty graph would fare reasonably well since its SHD would be exactly the number of edges in
G^{\star}
(edits from the empty graph are just edge additions). For example in the experiment of
Figure

7.7(a)
, this would mean having a
\mathbb{E}
-SHD of about
40
, since the ground-truth graphs have on average
2d
edges. This would artificially make some methods look better than others, without being of any use in practice.

But beyond these limitations, which are true for the evaluation in structure learning in general, there is a fundamental issue with using a metric like the (expected) SHD for Bayesian structure learning. The reason being that the objective of the Bayesian perspective is
not
to recover
G^{\star}
, but to provide a faithful approximation of the posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
. Suppose that we have an algorithm that would return a Dirac
\delta(G=G^{\star})
; we would always sample
G^{\star}
. Then it would achieve a remarkable
\mathbb{E}
-SHD of
0
. But this would be a terrible approximation of the posterior, first because it has no quantification of epistemic uncertainty, second because at best we can only recover the Markov equivalence class of
G^{\star}
based on our discussion in
Section

1.4.1
, and third because the posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
that we wish to approximate is a fundamentally “finite-sample” quantity. This last point means in particular that
G^{\star}
may not even be a mode of
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
, due to lack of data. This is true for the AUROC as well.

Probably the most reliable evaluation is on a downstream task. For example, if we have access to a separate set of test data
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
representative of the system being modeled
(
Eaton & Murphy 2007
)
, then we can test the predictive performance of the algorithm based on Bayesian model averaging as described in
Section

1.3.1
. Some caution needs to be taken when we consider the problem of (marginal) Bayesian structure learning though: while new data is independent of
{\mathcal{D}}
given the full specification of the Bayesian network (
i.e.
,
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta
), it is not the case anymore when the parameters have been marginalized over:
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\not\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathcal{D}}\mid G
, meaning that
P({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})\neq P({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\mid G)
, which was necessary for Bayesian model averaging (see also
Figure

8.3
).

Finally, even if it is a more robust measure of the accuracy of the posterior approximation, comparing
\log P_{\phi}^{\top}(G)
to
\log R(G)
(as we did in
Figure

7.7(c)
) suffers from being biased as we are only comparing those two quantities
on a mode of
P_{\phi}^{\top}
. Indeed, we use graphs sampled with the GFlowNet for evaluation, and those will tend to be in the mode(s) of the terminating state distribution. This does not necessarily capture the whole complexity of the true posterior
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
, which may not be reflected in the distribution induced by the GFlowNet. Moreover, since
P_{\phi}^{\top}(G)
can only be estimated (
Section

7.6.1
), this estimate becomes less reliable as the size of the problem increases (
i.e.
, the number of random variables
d
increases), because its variance will inevitably increase.

7.7.
Application to protein signaling networks

Finally to complement our experimental results on simulated data, we also evaluated DAG-GFlowNet on real-world flow cytometry data
(
Sachs et al. 2005
)
. The data consists of continuous measurements of
d=11
phosphoproteins in individual T-cells. Out of all the measurements, we first selected the
N=853
observations corresponding to the first experimental condition of
Sachs et al. 2005
(control) as our dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
; we will see in
Section

7.7.2
how to also integrate the additional data from other experimental regimes. This is considered a standard dataset in the structure learning literature, and following prior work we used the DAG inferred by the authors of that study as our reference graph (“ground-truth”), containing
d=11
nodes and
17
edges. However, it should be noted that this “consensus graph” may not represent a realistic and complete description of the system being modeled here
(
Mooij et al. 2020
)
, biological systems often containing feedback processes that can’t be modeled as an acyclic graph; we will come back to this limitation in
Section

8.5.3
. We standardized the data, and used again the BGe score to compute
R(G)
.

7.7.1.
Inference from observational data

(

)

We compare the performance of DAG-GFlowNet on this protein signaling inference problem against the same baseline methods as the ones we considered in
Section

7.6.2
. In
Table

7.2
, we once again report the expected SHD & AUROC (based on the “ground-truth” graph), despite the comments we made in the previous section. In addition to those metrics, we also include the average number of edges present in the graphs to get a more comprehensive view; all the metrics are computed based on
1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}000
samples from each posterior approximation.

We observe that while BCD Nets and Bootstrap PC have a smaller
\mathbb{E}
-SHD, suggesting that the distribution is concentrated closer to the consensus graph than other methods, in reality they tend to be more conservative and sample graphs with fewer edges. Overall, DAG-GFlowNet offers a good trade-off between performance (as measured by the
\mathbb{E}
-SHD and the AUROC), and getting a distribution that assigns higher probability to DAGs with more edges, on par with Bootstrap GES. Interestingly, we also observed that
1.50\%
of the graphs samples with DiBS contained a cycle, validating empirically what we saw in
Section

7.1.2
(
i.e.
, methods based on continuous relaxations of the acyclicity constraint have a risk to sample cyclic graphs).

Figure 7.8
.
Coverage of the posterior approximations learned on flow cytometry data. Each point corresponds to a sampled Markov equivalence class, and its size represents the number of unique DAGs sampled from the posterior approximation (with
1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}000
samples) in the MEC. The x-axis is the BGe score of the MEC, and the y-axis is the marginal probability of the MEC (
i.e.
, the normalized count of DAGs falling in that MEC). Note the multiple points in light blue close to the
y=0
line (DiBS), and the 2 points in dark blue around
x=-10\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}850
(BCD Nets).

Beyond these metrics though, and in line with our comments in
Section

7.6.3
, we would like to test if the advantages of Bayesian structure learning are also reflected in the distribution induced by DAG-GFlowNet. In particular, we want to study (1) if this distribution covers multiple high-scoring DAGs, instead of being peaked at a single most likely graph, and (2) if DAG-GFlowNet can sample a variety of DAGs from the same Markov equivalence class (MEC; see
Section

1.1.2
), showing the inherent uncertainty over equivalent graphs (
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
itself assigning the same probability to equivalent DAGs since BGe is an equivalent score, and the prior is uniform). In
Figure

7.8
, we visualize the MECs of the graphs sampled with DAG-GFlowNet, along with other methods based on MCMC and variational inference. Each dot represents a MEC, and its size represents the number of unique DAGs in that MEC out of
1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}000
samples. The x-axis is the BGe score of the MEC (all equivalent DAGs sharing the same score), and the y-axis represents the marginal probability of the DAGs in that MEC (
i.e.
, the proportion of DAGs falling into the equivalence class).

We first observe that DAG-GFlowNet does not collapse to a single most-likely graph, and covers multiple MECs. Moreover, it is also capable of sampling different equivalent DAGs (larger dots), showing again that the distribution does not collapse to a single representative of the MECs with higher marginal probability. Its behavior is largely similar to methods based on MCMC (MC
3
& Gadget), and its maximal MEC reaches a high score, albeit lower than MC
3
and DiBS; as a point of reference, the score of the best MEC obtained with GES
(
Chickering 2002
)
is
-10\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}716.12
.

Moreover, despite comparing favorably in terms of
\mathbb{E}
-SHD & AUROC in
Table

7.2
, we can assess more precisely the quality of the posterior approximation given by BCD Nets & DiBS in
Figure

7.8
. The first observation is that out of
1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}000
samples from BCD Nets, the graphs belong to one of only two MECs (with a BGe score around
-10\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}850
and a higher marginal probability). Furthermore, as shown by the size of each point, those MECs happen to only contain
a single
unique DAG. Overall, this means that BCD Nets only returned
2
unique DAGs, showing a clear lack of diversity. On the other hand, DiBS sampled a significant number of low-scoring DAGs (
i.e.
, having a very low probability under
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
), with BGe scores as low as
-12\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}550
, which is not something any other method suffers from. We can also see that DiBS tends to return many graphs that belong to unique MECs (many unique points with a low marginal probability), but rarely multiple graphs from the same MEC. This shows that while DiBS benefits from a high diversity in terms of MECs (including ones covering low-scoring DAGs), it suffers from a lack of diversity within each MEC, which would be expected from a faithful approximation of the posterior distribution.

7.7.2.
Integration of experimental data

An advantage of working with biological data is that we can acquire
experimental data
. In addition to the observational data we used in the previous section,
Sachs et al. 2005
also provided flow cytometry data under different experimental conditions, where the T-cells were perturbed with some reagents; this effectively corresponds to
interventional data

(
Pearl 2009
)
, which we saw in
Section

1.5.2
is one possible ingredient for better causal identification. Although a molecular intervention may be imperfect and affect multiple proteins (
Eaton & Murphy 2007a
;
i.e.
, a molecule is used to “knock out” a protein, but this molecule may affect other proteins as well), we assume here that these interventions are perfect and the intervention targets are known. This time, we used a discretized dataset of
N=5\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}400
samples from
9
experimental conditions, of which
6
are interventions. Since the data is now discrete, we used a modification of the BDe score
(
Heckerman et al. 1995
)
to handle this mixture of observational and interventional data
(
Cooper & Yoo 1999
)
. This is done by effectively ignoring the terms corresponding to the intervened node in
Equation

B.1.4
(see also
Section

1.5.1
).

, we compare with the results reported by

, where they computed the AUROC of the exact posterior
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
itself using dynamic programming (at the expense of computing only the edge marginals, and not the whole distribution over DAGs), therefore working as an upper bound for what a posterior approximation can achieve. We observe that MC
3
predicts sparser graphs with a higher
\mathbb{E}

(

;

)
, and was designed specifically to showcase the ability of DAG-GFlowNet to also work with discrete (and interventional) data.

Chapter 8
Joint Inference of Structure and Parameters

This chapter contains material from the following papers:

•

Mizu Nishikawa-Toomey,
Tristan Deleu
, Jithendaraa Subramanian, Yoshua Bengio, Laurent Charlin (2023).
Bayesian learning of Causal Structure and Mechanisms with GFlowNets and Variational Bayes
. Graphs and More Complex Structures for Learning and Reasoning Workshop (AAAI).
\notecite
nishikawa2023vbg

•

Tristan Deleu
, Mizu Nishikawa-Toomey, Jithendaraa Subramanian, Nikolay Malkin, Laurent Charlin, Yoshua Bengio (2023).
Joint Bayesian Inference of Graphical Structure and Parameters with a Single Generative Flow Network
. Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS).
\notecite
deleu2023jspgfn

One of the major drawbacks of working with the (marginal) posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
over the structures alone is that it limits the scope of applications. Indeed, this assumes that the marginal likelihood
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)
can be computed efficiently (oftentimes analytically), which is only possible for a handful of models that we studied in the previous chapter. This notably rules out many Bayesian networks whose conditional probability distributions are defined with non-linear functions (
e.g.
, neural networks), which is sometimes essential to model the intricacies of real-world systems (
e.g.
, in systems biology). In this chapter, we will build upon the foundations of
Chapter

7
and present two different ways to model the posterior over entire Bayesian networks (
i.e.
, their structure
and
parameters) for complete flexibility with a GFlowNet: in
Section

8.2
we will introduce a direct extension of DAG-GFlowNet in conjunction with Variational Bayes, whereas in
Section

8.3
and onwards we will present a solution with
a single
GFlowNet that can be trained end-to-end.

8.1.
Posterior distribution over graphs & parameters

Chapter

7
was entirely dedicated to modeling the
marginal
posterior distribution
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
; we call it “marginal” to highlight the fact that the parameters of the conditional distributions
\theta
have been marginalized out. In this chapter, we will instead explicitly model the
joint
posterior distribution
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
over both the structure
G
and the parameters
\theta
of a Bayesian network. Again with Bayes’ rule, this joint posterior distribution is given by

(8.1.1)

P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})=\frac{P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P(\theta\mid G)P(G)}{P({\mathcal{D}})}\mathchar 59\relax

where
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
is now the likelihood function (as opposed to the
marginal
likelihood before), and
P(\theta\mid G)
is a prior over parameters that we will consider as known and fixed (just like we did with the prior over graphs
P(G)
). The fact that we don’t have to marginalize over
\theta
anymore and we can use the likelihood directly makes it applicable to any Bayesian network: we simply have to evaluate
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
with
Equation

1.1.2
. Modeling this joint posterior inherits many of the challenges that we mentioned in the previous chapter, namely that
G
is still a member of a discrete and combinatorially large space of DAGs, making
Equation

8.1.1
intractable.

But it also comes with its own set of challenges. The first one being that the sample space is now a mixed space with a discrete component
G
and a continuous one
\theta
. The second challenge is that the space of parameters
depends on the structure of the Bayesian network
. To see this, consider two Bayesian networks over two binary random variables
A
&
B
, with structures
G_{1}:A\phantom{\rightarrow}B
and
G_{2}:A\rightarrow B
. If we list the minimal set of parameters necessary to define both Bayesian networks

(8.1.2)

\displaystyle G_{1}

\displaystyle P(A=1)

\displaystyle=\theta_{1}

\displaystyle P(B=1)

\displaystyle=\theta_{2}

(8.1.3)

\displaystyle G_{2}

\displaystyle P(A=1)

\displaystyle=\theta_{1}

\displaystyle P(B=1\mid A=0)

\displaystyle=\theta_{2}

\displaystyle P(B=1\mid A=1)=\theta_{3}

all other conditional probabilities can be derived from those. We can see that
G_{1}
has 2 parameters, whereas
G_{2}
has 3. The space
\Theta_{G}
which the parameters
\theta
associated with a structure
G
belong to not only changes depending on the DAG, but its dimensionality changes as well. This makes the sample space
{\mathcal{X}}
of
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
highly complex, and we will give a formal definition in
Section

8.3.1
. This explains why joint Bayesian inference has received relatively little attention compared to its marginal counterpart.

8.1.1.
Reversible-jump MCMC

We saw in
Section

7.1.1
how we can build Markov chain over the space of DAGs with local moves
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
corresponding to adding, deleting, or reversing a single edge. How can we expect building a similar Markov chain on the joint sample space, moving
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
, when
\theta\in\Theta_{G}
and
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{G^{\prime}}
may not even be elements of the same space, let alone with the same dimensionality? This is something which is impossible within the standard framework of MCMC. A naive solution could be to run independent chains
(
Chib 1995
)
, one for each DAG
G
, and to query the appropriate chain after any move in the space of DAGs. This is clearly not appropriate for Bayesian structure learning, as the number of possible structures is extremely large.

A more flexible and principled option is to allow
trans-dimensional
moves, to move from any
\Theta_{G}
to any other
\Theta_{G^{\prime}}
and back.
Reversible-jump MCMC
methods (RJ-MCMC;
Green 1995
) is an extension of the Metropolis-Hastings scheme introduced in
Section

2.1.2
, but where the proposal is designed in such a way that moves between spaces of different dimensions are allowed, while preserving detailed balance (necessary to guarantee that the Markov chain will eventually converge to the target distribution, here
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
). This has been used for Bayesian model selection more generally
(
Hastie & Green 2012
)
, as well as Bayesian structure learning
(
Fronk 2002
)
.

As a simple alternative that doesn’t require the integration of complex trans-dimensional moves, we will consider in our experiments two extensions of structure MCMC (MC
3
, described in
Section

7.1.1
) studied by
Lorch et al. 2021
. The first one is using a Metropolis-Hastings scheme to jointly sample structures and parameters together, where the parameters belong to a shared sample space and are appropriately “masked out” depending on the adjacency matrix of
G
; this is called
Metropolis-Hastings MC
3
(M-MC
3
). The second is a
Metropolis-within-Gibbs
method (G-MC
3
) that alternates between proposing updates to the graph and parameters
(
Godsill 2001
;
Castelletti et al. 2024
)
.

8.1.2.
Variational inference

In this section, we give a more exhaustive comparison between various methods for Bayesian structure learning based on variational inference, for both joint inference and marginal inference, to complement
Section

7.1.2
. We give a comparison of various algorithms over multiple criteria in
Table

8.1
, where we also included methods based on GFlowNets since they are effectively variational methods as we saw in
Section

4.3

(
Malkin et al. 2023
;
Zimmermann et al. 2022
)
.

Table 8.1
