Joint
\bm{G}
&
\bm{\theta}

This category, of main interest in the context of this chapter, indicates whether the model can approximate the joint posterior
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
, or if they are limited to approximating the marginal posterior
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
. Both methods we presented in
Section

7.1.2
can also be adapted to model the joint posterior. In the case of BCD Nets
(
Cundy et al. 2021
)
, or another method based on the ordering of the random variables called BayesDAG
(
Annadani et al. 2023
)
, the model readily parametrizes a weighted adjacency matrix, whose non-zero elements encode the structure of the DAG, but their exact values also encode the parameters of a linear-Gaussian model. But this is not necessarily the case of other methods falling into this same family as well (
e.g.
, VI-DP-DAG, or DPM-DAG). In the case of DiBS
(
Lorch et al. 2021
)
, which uses a continuous relaxation of acyclicity as a soft prior, the posterior approximation can incorporate the parameters into the joint distribution
Q(Z\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
together with the latent variables
Z
used to determine the graphs downstream. On the other hand, another method also based on a continuous relaxation of acyclicity called VCN
(
Annadani et al. 2021
)
doesn’t include the parameters of the Bayesian network.

For methods based on GFlowNets, we saw in the previous chapter that DAG-GFlowNet was only capable of modeling the marginal posterior. We will see in this chapter two alternatives that do model the joint posterior: VBG
(
Nishikawa-Toomey et al. 2023
)
that we will introduce in
Section

8.2
, and JSP-GFN
(
Deleu et al. 2023
)
that we will introduce in the rest of this chapter. In the case of DynGFN
(
Atanackovic et al. 2023
)
, which is an adaptation of DAG-GFlowNet to learn the structure of
dynamic Bayesian networks

(
Friedman et al. 1998
;
Murphy 2002
)
, the parameters are estimated with maximum likelihood instead of having a completely Bayesian treatment.

Non-linear

This category indicates whether the model can be applied to Bayesian networks whose conditional probability distributions are parametrized by non-linear functions (
e.g.
, neural networks). While most methods approximating
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
may be applied to cases where the conditional probabilities are parametrized by Gaussian Processes (allowing for an explicit marginalization;
Kügelgen et al. 2019
), we only consider here methods that handle any non-linearity by design. It is natural to find models approximating
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
also being capable of working with non-linear conditional probability distributions, with the notable exception of BCD Nets which has an explicit “linear-Gaussian” assumption. VI-DP-DAG
(
Charpentier et al. 2022
)
, and DPM-DAG
(
Rittel & Tschiatschek 2023
)
which is based upon it, use maximum likelihood in order to estimate a point estimate of the parameters of a non-linear function. Finally even though VBG can be applied to non-linear cases, in practice
Nishikawa-Toomey et al. 2023
only studied linear-Gaussian models.

DAG support

This category indicates whether the posterior approximation is guaranteed to have support over the space of DAGs only. VCN and DiBS only encourage acyclicity via a prior term, meaning that those methods may return graphs containing cycles. AVICI
(
Lorch et al. 2022
)
uses a similar prior term in some cases, but in general this framework does not seek to enforce acyclicity by design (
e.g.
, to model feedback loop in certain domains, such as biological systems;
Sethuraman et al. 2023
).
Trust

(
Wang et al. 2022
)
, just like all other methods based on a variable ordering, guarantees acyclicity via a distribution over the variable orders that can be learned using sum-product networks
(
Poon & Domingos 2011
)
. On the other hand, methods based on GFlowNets are guaranteed to have a support over the space of DAGs by design; DynGFN considers dynamic Bayesian networks (without instantaneous edges), which are necessarily acyclic.

Discrete observations

This category indicates whether the posterior approximation may be applied to Bayesian networks with discrete random variables. Although a number of models make the explicit assumption to have a Bayesian network over continuous random variables (sometimes even requiring the variables to be normally distributed), some methods may in principle be applied to discrete Bayesian networks. The authors of VCN mention that their approach is also applicable to discrete observations. While there is no experiment in the discrete setting in
(
Lorch et al. 2021
)
, this extension can be found in the official code release for DiBS; this also transfers to
Trust
, which may use DiBS as its underlying routine for structure learning.

Maximum parents

This category indicates whether a maximum number of parents can be specified for each variable. Although this is a very common constraint in the structure learning literature to improve efficiency
(
Koller & Friedman 2009
)
, none of the variational methods for Bayesian structure learning allow for such a (hard) constraint. Some methods may introduce a sparsity-inducing prior
(
Annadani et al. 2021
;
Cundy et al. 2021
;
Lorch et al. 2021
;
Annadani et al. 2023
)
, or use post-processing of the sampled DAGs
(
Charpentier et al. 2022
)
to reduce the number of edges. Conversely, we saw in
Section

7.2.3
that this type of additional structural constraints can be naturally added to any GFlowNet over DAGs.

Sampler

The category indicates whether one can freely sample from the model once it is fully trained. While this seems like a basic requirement for Bayesian inference, a notable exception is DiBS which uses a particle-based approach
(
Liu & Wang 2016
)
to approximate the posterior (be it marginal, or joint), and therefore the number of particles is fixed ahead of time; once fully trained, it is impossible to sample new Bayesian networks
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
from this model anymore (distinct from the particles themselves).

Mini-batch

This category indicates whether the model can be updated with mini-batches of observations from
{\mathcal{D}}
, instead of the the full dataset. With only a few exceptions, most methods for Bayesian structure learning necessitate the whole dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
to learn the posterior approximation. DiBS uses mini-batch updates for their experiments on protein signaling networks, where the number of observations is large (
N=7\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}466
). We will see in
Section

8.4.2
that JSP-GFN can naturally leverage mini-batch training, while DAG-GFlowNet couldn’t (nor can VBG for that matter).

8.2.
Variational Bayes approach

Prior to our work extending generative flow networks to more general state spaces
(
Lahlou et al. 2023
)
that we presented in
Chapter

6
, the only way to model the joint posterior
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
with a GFlowNet (to leverage the tools developed in the previous chapter) was to treat the discrete component
G
and the continuous one
\theta
separately. We will see in this section how we can use
Variational Bayes
, that was introduced in
Section

1.3.2
, in conjunction with GFlowNets in order to approximate the joint posterior distribution. Since it takes inspiration from DAG-GFlowNet, we call this method
Variational Bayes-DAG-GFlowNet
(VBG;
Nishikawa-Toomey et al. 2023
).

8.2.1.
Evidence lower bound

To treat the graphs and parameters separately, we will approximate the joint posterior distribution
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
as the product of two distribution: a distribution
Q_{\phi}(G)
over DAGs, parametrized by
\phi
, and a distribution
Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)
, parametrized by
\psi

(8.2.1)

P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})\approx Q_{\phi}(G)Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G).

Our objective will be to find the best approximation of the posterior (
i.e.
, the best parameters
(\phi^{\star}\mathchar 59\relax\psi^{\star})
). We saw in
Section

1.3.2
that Variational Bayes approaches this problem by coordinate ascent on an
evidence lower bound
(ELBO) of the log-evidence
\log P({\mathcal{D}})
. The following proposition gives an explicit form for this ELBO.

Proposition 8.2.1

(ELBO)
.

Let
Q_{\phi}(G)
&
Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)
be two distributions such that their product is an approximation of the joint posterior distribution
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})\approx Q_{\phi}(G)Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)
. Then the
evidence lower bound

{\mathcal{J}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)
for this problem (i.e.,
\log P({\mathcal{D}})\geq{\mathcal{J}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)
) can be written as

(8.2.2)

{\mathcal{J}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)=\mathbb{E}_{G\sim Q_{\phi}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim Q_{\psi}}\big[\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big]-\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)\,\|\,P(\theta\mid G)\big)\right]-\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\phi}(G)\,\|\,P(G)\big).

Proof.

We start with writing the joint distribution between the data, the graph and parameters:

(8.2.3)

P({\mathcal{D}}\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G)=P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P(\theta\mid G)P(G).

The derivation of the ELBO follows the standard steps in variational inference (as in
Section

1.2.1
), except both the joint distribution
P({\mathcal{D}}\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G)
and the posterior approximation are more structured.

(8.2.4)

\displaystyle\log

\displaystyle P({\mathcal{D}})=\log\left(\sum_{G}\int_{\theta}P({\mathcal{D}}\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G)d\theta\right)

(8.2.5)

\displaystyle=\log\left(\sum_{G}\left[\int_{\theta}\frac{P({\mathcal{D}}\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G)}{Q_{\phi}(G)Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)}Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)d\theta\right]Q_{\phi}(G)\right)

(8.2.6)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{G}\left[\int_{\theta}\log\frac{P({\mathcal{D}}\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G)}{Q_{\phi}(G)Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)}Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)d\theta\right]Q_{\phi}(G)

(8.2.7)

\displaystyle=\sum_{G}\left[\int_{\theta}\log\frac{P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P(\theta\mid G)P(G)}{Q_{\phi}(G)Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)}Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)d\theta\right]Q_{\phi}(G)

\displaystyle=\sum_{G}\left[\int_{\theta}\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)d\theta+\int_{\theta}\log\frac{P(\theta\mid G)}{Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)}Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)d\theta+\log\frac{P(G)}{Q_{\phi}(G)}\right]Q_{\phi}(G)

(8.2.8)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{G\sim Q_{\phi}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim Q_{\psi}}\big[\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big]-\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)\,\|\,P(\theta\mid G)\big)\right]-\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\phi}(G)\,\|\,P(G)\big)\mathchar 59\relax

where we used Jensen’s inequality in
Equation

8.2.6
, and the decomposition of the joint distribution
Equation

8.2.3
in
Equation

8.2.7
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Although we didn’t give any detail on how these distributions are parametrized exactly, we will see in the next section that
Q_{\phi}(G)
in particular will be parametrized with a GFlowNet. On the other hand, the distribution
Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)
will be problem specific, depending on the form of the conditional distributions appearing in the Bayesian networks we are modeling.

8.2.2.
Modeling the distribution over DAGs with a GFlowNet

Since
Q_{\phi}(G)
is a distribution over DAGs, it is natural to model it as a GFlowNet, similar to the one we presented in
Section

7.2
. While this informs us about how the GFlowNet is structured (
Section

7.2.1
) and the loss being used (
Section

7.2.4
), we still need to specify its reward function. Recall that a GFlowNet models a target distribution which is proportional to that reward function. The question then becomes: what is this target distribution here? Given that Variational Bayes operates by coordinate ascent, the target distribution for a fixed
Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)
is the one maximizing the ELBO
{\mathcal{J}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)
we saw in the previous section.

Proposition 8.2.2
.

For a fixed distribution
Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)
, the distribution
Q_{\phi}^{\star}(G)
maximizing the ELBO
{\mathcal{J}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)
in
Proposition

8.2.1
satisfies for any DAG
G

(8.2.9)

\log Q_{\phi}^{\star}(G)=\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim Q_{\psi}}\big[\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big]-\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)\,\|\,P(\theta\mid G)\big)+\log P(G)+C\mathchar 59\relax

where
C
is a constant independent of
G
.

Proof.

There is a finite number of DAGs (albeit possibly extremely large) one can create with
d
nodes. We call them
\{G_{m}\}_{m=1}^{M}
. Maximizing the ELBO wrt. the distribution
Q_{\phi}(G)
is equivalent to solving the following constrained optimization problem

(8.2.10)

\displaystyle\max_{\{Q_{\phi}(G_{m})\}_{m=1}^{M}}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}(Q_{\phi}\mathchar 59\relax Q_{\psi})

(8.2.11)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{M}Q_{\phi}(G_{m})=1\mathchar 59\relax

where we make the dependence of the ELBO on the distributions
Q_{\phi}
&
Q_{\psi}
more explicit. The constraint only guarantees that
Q_{\phi}(G)
is a valid distribution over DAGs; there should be an additional non-negative constraint, which we ignore here for simplicity. To find solutions of this optimization problem, we an look at critical points of the Lagrangian, defined as

(8.2.12)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\big(\{Q_{\phi}(G_{m})\}_{m=1}^{M}\mathchar 59\relax\lambda)={\mathcal{J}}(Q_{\phi}\mathchar 59\relax Q_{\psi})+\lambda\left(\sum_{m=1}^{M}Q_{\phi}(G_{m})-1\right)

(8.2.13)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim Q_{\psi}}\big[\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G_{m}\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big]-\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G_{m})\,\|\,P(\theta\mid G_{m})\big)+\log\frac{P(G_{m})}{Q_{\phi}(G_{m})}+\lambda\Bigg]Q_{\phi}(G_{m})-\lambda.

Taking the derivative of the Lagrangian with respect to a particular
Q_{\phi}(G_{m})
, and equating it to zero to find the critical point, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial L}{\partial Q_{\phi}(G_{m})}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim Q_{\psi}}\big[\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G_{m}\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big]-\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G_{m})\,\|\,P(\theta\mid G_{m})\big)

(8.2.14)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\log P(G_{m})-\log Q_{m}^{\star}(G_{m})-1+\lambda=0

(8.2.15)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\qquad\log Q_{\phi}^{\star}(G_{m})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim Q_{\psi}}\big[\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G_{m}\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big]-\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G_{m})\,\|\,P(\theta\mid G_{m})\big)+\log P(G_{m})+C\mathchar 59\relax

where
C=\lambda-1
is a constant independent of
G_{m}
.
∎

The proposition above shows that the optimal
Q_{\phi}^{\star}(G)
for a fixed
Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)
is a distribution over DAGs defined up to a normalization constant. This is perfect application for generative flow networks, as we saw in
Chapter

7
. Unlike DAG-GFlowNet though, where the reward of the GFlowNet was fixed to
R(G)=P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)P(G)

(
Deleu et al. 2022
)
, the reward here is defined by

(8.2.16)

\log\widetilde{R}(G)=\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim Q_{\psi}}\big[\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big]-\mathrm{KL}\big(Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)\,\|\,P(\theta\mid G)\big)+\log P(G).

The key difference with DAG-GFlowNet is that the reward
\widetilde{R}(G)
now depends on
Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)
that may evolve (the reward function remains fixed while updating the GFlowNet though). In a different context, this relates to EB-GFN
(
Zhang et al. 2022a
)
and GFN-EM
(
Hu et al. 2023
)
where an iterative procedure is used to update both a GFlowNet and the reward function, instead of the latter being fixed. Interestingly, if the distribution
Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)=P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
matches the posterior distribution over parameters, given a DAG
G
, then the reward above simply reduces to the reward we used in DAG-GFlowNet

(8.2.17)

\displaystyle\log\widetilde{R}(G)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})}\big[\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big]-\mathrm{KL}\big(P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})\,\|\,P(\theta\mid G)\big)+\log P(G)

(8.2.18)

\displaystyle=\int_{\theta}\log\frac{P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P(\theta\mid G)}{P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})}P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})d\theta+\log P(G)

(8.2.19)

\displaystyle=\int_{\theta}\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})d\theta+\log P(G)

(8.2.20)

\displaystyle=\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)+\log P(G)=\log R(G).

Moreover, if we make the additional assumption that the distribution
Q_{\psi}(\theta)
also decomposes along the parameters
\theta_{i}
of the conditional distributions of the Bayesian network

(8.2.21)

Q_{\psi}(\theta)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}Q_{\psi}(\theta_{i})\mathchar 59\relax

then we can show that the delta-score
\log\widetilde{R}(G^{\prime})-\log\widetilde{R}(G)
appearing in the modified detailed balance loss for a transition
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
in the GFlowNet (
Section

7.4.1
) can also be easily computed without having to evaluate the whole log-reward of either graphs
(
Nishikawa-Toomey et al. 2023
)
.

Following the algorithm in
Section

1.3.2
, we can find the posterior approximation by interlacing the following steps: (1) for a fixed
Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)
, fit the parameters
\phi
of a GFlowNet over DAGs as described in
Section

7.2
, with a reward function
\widetilde{R}(G)
defined in
Equation

8.2.16
, and (2) for a fixed
Q_{\phi}(G)
, maximize the ELBO in
Proposition

8.2.1
wrt.
\psi
; see also
Algorithm

8
.

Algorithm 8

Training of Variational Bayes-DAG-GFlowNet (VBG).

1:

A dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
, and a model
P({\mathcal{D}}\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G)=P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P(\theta\mid G)P(G)
.

2:

An approximation of the joint posterior
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})\approx Q_{\phi}(G)Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)
.

3:

Initialization of the parameters
(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)

4:

loop

5:

repeat

6:

Update the parameters
\phi
of the GFlowNet (
Section

7.2
) with reward
\widetilde{R}(G)

\triangleright

Equation

8.2.16

7:

until
convergence (
e.g.
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DB}}(\phi_{t+1})-{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}\textrm{-}\mathrm{DB}}(\phi_{t})<\varepsilon
)

8:

Sample graphs from
Q_{\phi}(G)
modeled by the GFlowNet

9:

Update
\psi
to maximize
{\mathcal{J}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)

\triangleright

Proposition

8.2.1
; gradient updates, or analytical

10:

return

Q_{\phi}(G)Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)

8.2.3.
Update of the posterior approximation over parameters

The last ingredient for Variational Bayes is to determine how to parametrize
Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)
, and how to update its parameters
\psi
. Since
\theta
represents the parameters of a Bayesian network (continuous quantity), the posterior approximation will typically be represented as a Normal distribution, following
Kingma & Welling 2014
;
Rezende et al. 2014

(8.2.22)

Q_{\psi}(\theta_{i}\mid G)={\mathcal{N}}\big(\theta_{i}\mid\mu_{\psi}^{(i)}(G)\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{\psi}^{(i)}(G)\big)\mathchar 59\relax

where
\mu_{\psi}^{(i)}(G)
and
\Sigma_{\psi}^{(i)}(G)
are functions that depend on the graph
G
. To maximize the ELBO wrt.
\psi
, we can then apply gradient ascent. Since
Q_{\psi}(\theta\mid G)
appears as the distribution wrt. which we take an expectation in
Equation

8.2.2
, we can use the reparametrization trick (
Section

1.2.3
) to estimate the gradient of
{\mathcal{J}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)
. In some special cases, such as linear-Gaussian Bayesian networks, we can show that this maximization of
{\mathcal{J}}(\phi\mathchar 59\relax\psi)
wrt.
\phi
can be done analytically
(
Nishikawa-Toomey et al. 2023
)
, and does not require gradient updates.

8.3.
JSP-GFN: Joint inference with a single GFlowNet

Instead of taking directly DAG-GFlowNet and working around it to incorporate a distribution over parameters with variational Bayes as we did in VBG in the previous section, we can fully leverage the results of
Chapter

6
and define
a single
GFlowNet over a mixed discrete/continuous space capable of doing joint inference over the structure
G
and the parameters
\theta
. We call this model
JSP-GFN
, for Joint Structure and Parameters Bayesian inference with a GFlowNet.

8.3.1.
Structure of the GFlowNet

Unlike in
Chapter

7
, where we model a distribution only over DAGs, here we need to define a GFlowNet whose terminating states are pairs
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
, where
G
is a DAG and
\theta
is a set of (continuous-valued) parameters whose dimension may depend on
G
. These terminating states are obtained through two phases, shown in
Figure

8.1
: (1) the DAG
G
is first constructed one edge at a time, following our description in
Section

7.2.1

(
Deleu et al. 2022
)
, and then (2) the corresponding parameters
\theta
are generated, conditioned on
G
. We denote by
(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)
states where the DAG
G
has no parameters associated to it yet (
i.e.
, states in blue in
Figure

8.1
); they are intermediate states during the first phase of the GFlowNet, and do not correspond to valid samples. Using our notations of
Section

2.2.2
,
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\in{\mathcal{X}}
, whereas
(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\in{\mathcal{S}}\backslash{\mathcal{X}}
.

Starting at the empty graph
(G_{0}\mathchar 59\relax\ast)
, the DAG is constructed one edge at a time during the first phase, following the forward transition probabilities
P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)
; the same considerations as in
Section

7.2.2
also hold here, namely that the edges added must not be already present nor introduce a cycle. This first phase ends when a special “
\mathrm{stop}
” action is selected with
P_{F}
, indicating that we stop adding edges to the graph and we are entering the second phase. Then during the second phase, we generate
\theta
conditioned on
G
, following the forward transition probabilities
P_{F}(\theta\mid G)
. For a fixed
G
, all the terminating states
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
can be seen as forming an (infinitely-wide) tree rooted at
(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)
.

Figure 8.1
.
Structure of JSP-GFN to approximate the joint posterior distribution
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
. A graph
G
and the parameters
\theta
are constructed as follows: starting from the empty graph
G_{0}
, (1) the graph
G
is first generated one edge at a time (blue), as in
(
Deleu et al. 2022
)
. Then once we select the “
\mathrm{stop}
” action indicating that we stop adding edges to the graph, (2) we generate the parameters
\theta
(red), conditioned on the graph
G
. Finally given
G
and
\theta
, (3) we receive a reward
R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
(green).

Note that since the states of the GFlowNet are really
pairs
of objects,
P_{F}(\theta\mid G)
(resp.
P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)
) is an abuse of notations, and actually represents
P_{F}\big((G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\mid(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\big)
(resp.
P_{F}\big((G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\mid(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\big)
). In particular, it is important to see that
P_{F}(\theta\mid G)
is
not
a distribution over
\theta
, in the sense that it is not normalized to
1
, since

(8.3.1)

\int_{\theta}P_{F}(\theta\mid G)d\theta+\sum_{G^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(G)}P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)=1\mathchar 59\relax

where
\underline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(G)
corresponds to the children states of
G
during the first phase only (
i.e.
, children DAGs
G^{\prime}
being the result of adding a single edge to
G
).

Reward function

Since our objective is to approximate the joint posterior distribution
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})\propto P({\mathcal{D}}\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G)
, it is natural to define the reward function of a terminating state
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
as

(8.3.2)

R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)=P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P(\theta\mid G)P(G)\mathchar 59\relax

where the likelihood model
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
can be completely arbitrary now (
e.g.
, with conditional distributions parametrized by neural networks),
P(\theta\mid G)
is the prior over parameters, and
P(G)
is the prior over graphs.

Measurable pointed graph perspective

While the description of the GFlowNet’s structure above is perfectly intuitive, we saw in
Section

6.3
that some care must be taken when it comes to defining GFlowNets over general state spaces. This includes in particular mixed spaces with discrete and continuous components, as is the case here. Recall that for some DAG structure
G
, the space of parameters associated with
G
is denoted by
\Theta_{G}
. In particular, we saw in
Section

8.1.1
that the dimensionality of
\Theta_{G}
may depend on
G
. For example in a linear-Gaussian model, the space of parameters is
\Theta_{G}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{K}
, where
K
is the number of edges in
G
.

With this, we can formally define the state space
{\mathcal{S}}
and the space of terminating states
{\mathcal{X}}
as being the finite union over all the DAGs over
d
nodes of product spaces

(8.3.3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{G\in\mathrm{DAG}}\{G\}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{G}

and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{G\in\mathrm{DAG}}\{G\}\times\Theta_{G}\mathchar 59\relax

where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{G}=\Theta_{G}\cup\{\ast\}
is the space of parameters, augmented with the symbol
\ast
indicating that we are still in the first phase.

We now have to define the structure of the GFlowNet, in terms of a forward and backward reference kernels. For a DAG
G
, the measure
\kappa_{F}\big((G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\mathchar 59\relax\cdot\big)
is the sum of a discrete measure (to transition to another intermediate state
(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\ast)
during the first phase) and a continuous measure (to transition to a terminating state
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
in the second phase). We can write this measure as

(8.3.4)

\kappa_{F}\big((G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\mathchar 59\relax\cdot\big)=\sum_{G^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathrm{Ch}}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(G)}\delta_{(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\ast)}(\cdot)+\big(\delta_{G}\otimes\lambda_{\Theta_{G}}\big)(\cdot)\mathchar 59\relax

where
\delta
is the Dirac measure, and
\lambda_{\Theta_{G}}
is the Lebesgue measure over
\Theta_{G}
. Moreover, we also have
\kappa_{F}\big((G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\mathchar 59\relax\cdot\big)=\delta_{\bot}(\cdot)
for all the terminating states
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\in{\mathcal{X}}
; in other words, there is no transition from a terminating state other than to the terminal state
\bot
. This clearly shows in particular that the measurable pointed graph will be finitely absorbing
Equation

6.3.2
, with a maximum length
d(d-1)/2+1
. Unlike RJ-MCMC, this approach does not require trans-dimensional steps from
\Theta_{G}
to another
\Theta_{G^{\prime}}
because once we select a graph
G
during the first phase, we are committing to that space
\Theta_{G}
; to get another sample, we need to start again the process from scratch. On the other hand, the backward reference kernel is always discrete, regardless of the state

(8.3.5)

\displaystyle\kappa_{B}\big((G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\mathchar 59\relax\cdot\big)

\displaystyle=\delta_{(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)}(\cdot)

and

\displaystyle\kappa_{B}\big((G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\mathchar 59\relax\cdot\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{G\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(G)}\delta_{(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)}(\cdot).

We can observe that
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
only has one “parent”, which will be useful in the next section to simplify the flow matching condition we will use here. Finally, even though the reward and “forward transition probabilities” should really be measures (
R(G\mathchar 59\relax d\theta)
and
P_{F}(d\theta\mid G)
), we will only work with their Radon-Nikodym derivatives throughout this chapter, without mentioning it again.

8.3.2.
Sub-trajectory balance condition

To obtain a generative process that samples pairs
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
proportionally to the reward, the GFlowNet needs to satisfy some flow matching condition, such as the ones described in
Section

3.2
. Unlike DAG-GFlowNet though, we can’t use the modified detailed balance of
Proposition

7.2.4
directly as not all the states are terminating here. But we can take inspiration from it, and consider a condition between two terminating states
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
&
(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
instead, where
G^{\prime}
is the result of adding an edge to
G
. We can achieve this with the
sub-trajectory balance condition
we presented in
Section

3.2.4

(
Madan et al. 2023
;
Malkin et al. 2022
)
, and in particular with its generalization to undirected paths going “backward then forward” in
Equation

3.2.54
. For an undirected path of length 3 of the form
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\leftarrow(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
(illustrated in red in
Figure

8.2
), we can rewrite
Equation

3.2.54
as

(8.3.6)

F(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P_{B}(G\mid\theta)P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{F}(\theta^{\prime}\mid G^{\prime})=F(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})P_{B}(G^{\prime}\mid\theta^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{F}(\theta\mid G)\mathchar 59\relax

where we used a similar abuse of notation
P_{B}(G\mid\theta)
to denote
P_{B}\big((G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\mid(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big)
, and
F(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
is a state flow function. In fact, since the terminating state
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\in{\mathcal{X}}
has only a single parent state
(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)
, we necessarily have
P_{B}(G\mid\theta)=1
(and similarly for
(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
). Furthermore, we can use the same observation we made in
Equation

7.2.7
in the proof of
Proposition

7.2.4
to write the state flow
F(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
at a terminating state as a function of its reward:

(8.3.7)

F(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)=\frac{R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)}{P_{F}\big(\bot\mid(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big)}.

We can further simplify this equation by observing that in the GFlowNet described in the previous section,
\bot
is the
only
child of the terminating state
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
and therefore we necessarily have
P_{F}\big(\bot\mid(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big)=1
(hence
F(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)=R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
). With all these simplifications, the sub-trajectory balance condition
Equation

8.3.6
can be written as

(8.3.8)

R(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{F}(\theta\mid G)=R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{F}(\theta^{\prime}\mid G^{\prime}).

This particular sub-trajectory balance condition happens to be very similar to the modified detailed balance we used in DAG-GFlowNet (
Proposition

7.2.4
), where the probability of terminating
P_{F}(\bot\mid G)
has been replaced by
P_{F}(\theta\mid G)
, in addition to the reward now depending on both
G
&
\theta
. This is not a coincidence, as we will see later in
Section

8.3.4
.

The downside of using the sub-trajectory balance condition (instead of the detailed balance condition, as a basis for
Proposition

7.2.4
) is that we saw in
Section

3.2.4
that it was not sufficient to define a valid Markovian flow. Despite having no guarantee in general that satisfying the sub-trajectory balance conditions would yield a flow (let alone a distribution proportional to the reward), the following theorem shows that this GFlowNet does induce a distribution
\propto R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
if the sub-trajectory balance condition in
Equation

8.3.8
is satisfied for all undirected paths
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\leftarrow(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
.

Theorem 8.3.1

(Sub-trajectory balance condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be the GFlowNet described in
Section

8.3.1
, with a reward function
R
, a forward transition probability
P_{F}
, and a backward transition probability
P_{B}
. If the following
sub-trajectory balance condition

(8.3.9)

R(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{F}(\theta\mid G)=R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{F}(\theta^{\prime}\mid G^{\prime})

is satisfied for all undirected paths of length 3 between any
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
and
(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
of the form
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\leftarrow(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
, then the terminating state distribution is proportional to the reward:
P_{F}^{\top}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\propto R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
.

Proof.

Let
G\neq G_{0}
be a fixed DAG distinct from the empty graph, and
\theta\in\Theta_{G}
a set of corresponding parameters. Let
\tau=(G_{0}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax G_{T-1}\mathchar 59\relax G)
be an arbitrary trajectory from
G_{0}
to
G
during the first phase described in
Section

8.3.1
, where we use the convention
G_{T}\equiv G
. For any
t<T
, if
\theta_{t}\in\Theta_{G_{t}}
is a fixed set of parameters associated with
G_{t}
, then the sub-trajectory balance condition
Equation

8.3.9
can be written for any timestep as

(8.3.10)

R(G_{t+1}\mathchar 59\relax\theta_{t+1})P_{B}(G_{t}\mid G_{t+1})P_{F}(\theta_{t}\mid G_{t})=R(G_{t}\mathchar 59\relax\theta_{t})P_{F}(G_{t+1}\mid G_{t})P_{F}(\theta_{t+1}\mid G_{t+1})\mathchar 59\relax

again using the convention
\theta_{T}\equiv\theta
. Taking the product of the ratio between
P_{F}
and
P_{B}
over the trajectory
\tau
, we get by telescoping

(8.3.11)

\prod_{t=0}^{T-1}\frac{P_{F}(G_{t+1}\mid G_{t})}{P_{B}(G_{t}\mid G_{t+1})}=\prod_{t=0}^{T-1}\frac{P_{F}(\theta_{t}\mid G_{t})R(G_{t+1}\mathchar 59\relax\theta_{t+1})}{R(G_{t}\mathchar 59\relax\theta_{t})P_{F}(\theta_{t+1}\mid G_{t+1})}=\frac{P_{F}(\theta_{0}\mid G_{0})R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)}{R(G_{0}\mathchar 59\relax\theta_{0})P_{F}(\theta\mid G)}.

By definition of the terminating state distribution:

(8.3.12)

\displaystyle P_{F}^{\top}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)

\displaystyle=P_{F}(\theta\mid G)\sum_{\tau:G_{0}\rightsquigarrow G}\prod_{t=0}^{T-1}P_{F}(G_{t+1}\mid G_{t})

(8.3.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{F}(\theta_{0}\mid G_{0})}{R(G_{0}\mathchar 59\relax\theta_{0})}R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\sum_{\tau:G_{0}\rightsquigarrow G}\prod_{t=0}^{T-1}P_{B}(G_{t}\mid G_{t+1})

(8.3.14)

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{F}(\theta_{0}\mid G_{0})}{R(G_{0}\mathchar 59\relax\theta_{0})}R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used
Lemma

3.1.6
in
Equation

8.3.14
. Moreover, we can prove that
P_{F}(\theta_{0}\mid G_{0})/R(G_{0}\mathchar 59\relax\theta_{0})
is independent of
\theta_{0}
(albeit depending on
G_{0}
, which is the fixed initial state) with
Lemma

B.2.1
. Therefore, this shows that
P_{F}^{\top}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\propto R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
.
∎

This is a remarkable result, and was only possible thanks to the particular structure of the GFlowNet we consider here. Similar to
Section

7.2.5
, we will use a fixed backward transition probability
P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})
as being uniform over the parents of
G^{\prime}
for convenience.

8.3.3.
Parametrization of the forward transition probabilities

Since
P_{B}
is fixed, the only quantity that we will learn is once again the forward transition probability
P_{F}
, similar to
Section

7.3
. We will therefore call it
P_{\phi}
to match the conventions of the previous chapter, where
\phi
are the parameters of the forward transition probability. In the case of JSP-GFN though, we need to parametrize both
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)
the probability to transition to a new graph
G^{\prime}
by adding an edge to
G
(first phase), and
P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)
the probability of generating the parameters given
G
(second phase). We will largely follow the hierarchical representation of
Section

7.3.3
, where we will parametrize 3 quantities: (1) the probability of terminating the first phase
P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)
, (2) the probability of transitioning to the next graph given that we continue the first phase
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})
, and (3) the distribution over parameters given that we are in the second phase
P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop})
. The forward transition probabilities are then defined as a combination of all these quantities:

(8.3.15)

\displaystyle P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)

\displaystyle=\big(1-P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)\big)P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})

(8.3.16)

\displaystyle P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)

\displaystyle=P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop}).

We already saw in
Section

7.3.4
how to parametrize both
P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)
&
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})
. To take the example of a graph neural network parametrization (which is the one we use in practice;
Deleu et al. 2023
), recall that we have a shared backbone from which we extract an embedding
{\bm{g}}
of the graph
G
, along with 3 embeddings
{\bm{u}}_{i}
,
{\bm{v}}_{i}
&
{\bm{w}}_{i}
for each node
X_{i}
in
G

(8.3.17)

{\bm{g}}\mathchar 59\relax\{{\bm{u}}_{i}\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{v}}_{i}\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{w}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}=\mathrm{SelfAttention}_{\phi}\big(\mathrm{GraphNet}_{\phi}(G)\big).

The embedding
{\bm{g}}
was already used to define
P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)
, and the embeddings
{\bm{u}}_{i}
&
{\bm{v}}_{i}
to define
P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})
. Finally, we will use the embeddings
{\bm{w}}_{i}
to define the distribution
P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop})
; it is important to note that unlike
P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)
, this is a well-defined probability distribution over
\theta
, and it will effectively be an approximation of the marginal posterior
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
. Under the same assumption of modularity of the prior
P(\theta\mid G)
that was already made in the previous chapter (and standard in structure learning), we can show that the posterior
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
also decomposes along the variables
X_{i}
. In turn, this motivates our modularization of
P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop})
along the variables

(8.3.18)

P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop})=\prod_{i=1}^{d}P_{\phi}(\theta_{i}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop}).

Finally, these distributions over
\theta_{i}
of the conditional probability distributions of
X_{i}
are parametrized as a multivariate Normal distribution with diagonal covariance (unless specified otherwise), following the literature on Bayesian neural networks
(
Graves 2011
;
Blundell et al. 2015
)

(8.3.19)

P_{\phi}(\theta_{i}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop})={\mathcal{N}}\big(\theta_{i}\mid\mu_{\phi}({\bm{w}}_{i})\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{\phi}({\bm{w}}_{i})\big)\mathchar 59\relax

where
\mu_{\phi}
and
\Sigma_{\phi}
are two neural networks, with appropriate non-linearities to guarantee that
\Sigma_{\phi}({\bm{w}}_{i})
is a well-defined diagonal covariance matrix. In addition to providing an approximation of the marginal posterior
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
via this learned distribution, JSP-GFN also provides an approximation of the marginal posterior
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
, by only following the first phase of the generation process (to generate
G
) until the “
\mathrm{stop}
” action is selected, and not continuing into the generation of the parameters.

8.3.4.
DAG-GFlowNet as a special case

Despite the similarities between the modified detailed balance condition and the sub-trajectory balance condition we saw in
Section

8.3.2
, this new GFlowNet seems quite distinct from DAG-GFlowNet at first glance. However, suppose in this section only that we are in a scenario where we
can
compute the marginal likelihood
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)
(recall that we introduced VBG & JSP-GFN specifically because we
couldn’t
compute it). In that case, we also know the marginal posterior
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
as a byproduct, since by Bayes’ rule

(8.3.20)

P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})=\frac{P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P(\theta\mid G)}{P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)}=\frac{R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)}{P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)P(G)}.

If we know
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
, this means in particular that we don’t have to learn an approximation of it anymore, and the probability of generating
\theta
during the second phase now becomes

(8.3.21)

P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)=P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})\mathchar 59\relax

based on our discussion in the previous section, and the hierarchical representation in
Equation

8.3.16
. If we write the sub-trajectory balance condition from
Theorem

8.3.1
for some undirected path
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\leftarrow(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
more precisely based on the equation above

(8.3.22)

\displaystyle R(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)

\displaystyle=R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle R(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})

\displaystyle=R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G^{\prime})P(\theta^{\prime}\mid G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})

(8.3.23)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}(G^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{R}(G)P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G^{\prime})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used
Equation

8.3.20
in
Equation

8.3.23
, and the notation
\widetilde{R}(G)=P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)P(G)
to denote the reward of DAG-GFlowNet. We observe that in that case, the sub-trajectory balance condition is in fact
exactly
equivalent to the modified detailed balance condition of
Proposition

7.2.4
, based on the parametrization
Equation

7.3.2
for
P_{\phi}(\bot\mid G)\equiv P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G)
in DAG-GFlowNet. In that sense, we can treat JSP-GFN as a direct generalization of DAG-GFlowNet, the latter being recovered with the parametrization
Equation

8.3.21
.

8.3.5.
Limitations of JSP-GFN

In light of what we saw in the previous section, JSP-GFN inherits from many of the limitations that DAG-GFlowNet suffers from, namely that the system is fully observed (
Section

7.4.4
) and the difficulties when
{\mathcal{D}}
becomes large (
Section

7.4.3
). But there are also challenges specific to JSP-GFN itself. In particular, we made the choice in
Section

8.3.3
to parametrize the posterior approximation over parameters
P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop})
as a Normal distribution (with diagonal covariance, except for
JSP-GFN (full)
in
Section

8.5.1
). This choice was made purely for simplicity, but it limits its expressivity to unimodal distributions only; this is an assumption which is commonly made with Bayesian neural networks
(
Hinton & Van 1993
)
. However in general, the posterior distribution
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
may be
highly multimodal
, especially when the model is non-linear. To see this, consider a Bayesian network whose conditional probability distributions are parametrized with 2-layer MLPs (as in
Section

8.5.2
). The weights and biases of both layers can be transformed (through an equivariant transformation) in such a way that the hidden units may be permuted, while preserving the values of the outputs. In other words, there are many sets of parameters
\theta
leading to the same likelihood
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
, and under mild assumptions on the priors they would have the same posterior probability
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
.

To address this issue of unimodality, we can use a more expressive posterior approximation
P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop})
, such as ones parametrized with a normalizing flow
(
Rezende & Mohamed 2015
)
; this is a drop-in replacement in JSP-GFN since their likelihood can be evaluated. We could even imagine combining it with other generative models, such as diffusion
(
Song & Ermon 2019
)
or a flow-matching models
(
Lipman et al. 2023
)
, although parametrizing a score function/flow in those cases might be challenging since this would require the definition of a vector field over the space of parameters
\Theta_{G}
, and would probably necessitate something akin to a hypernetwork
(
Ha et al. 2017
)
. Alternatively, we could also consider multiple steps of a continuous GFlowNet (
Chapter

6
) instead of a single one to generate
\theta
.

8.4.
Learning objective of JSP-GFN

Inspired by how we turned flow matching conditions into objectives that can then be optimized using gradient methods in order to approximately satisfy them, we could do the same with the sub-trajectory balance condition of
Theorem

8.3.1
here. We saw this type of
sub-trajectory balance loss
already in
Section

5.2.1
when we established the equivalence between this loss and Path Consistency Learning (PCL;
Nachum et al. 2017
) in MaxEnt RL. This would again take the form of a non-linear least-square objective
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\big[\widetilde{\Delta}^{2}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime};\phi)\big]
, where
\widetilde{\Delta}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime};\phi)
is a residual derived from
Equation

8.3.9

(8.4.1)

\widetilde{\Delta}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime};\phi)=\frac{R(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)}{R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{\phi}(\theta^{\prime}\mid G^{\prime})}.

The behavior policy
\pi_{b}
is a distribution over
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
with full support, whose details will be given in the next section.

Figure 8.2
.
Sub-trajectory balance conditions for undirected paths of length
3
(red) &
2
(blue) considered here, with an illustration of these paths on an excerpt of the pointed DAG of
Figure

8.1
.

But we can go one step further. The sub-trajectory balance condition we work with here only considers undirected paths of length 3 of the form
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\leftarrow(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
. However, there are other types of undirected paths that we might be able to also make use of. Consider for example an undirected path of length 2 this time, of the form
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\leftarrow(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G\mathchar 59\relax\bar{\theta})
with
\theta\neq\bar{\theta}
(see
Figure

8.2
); instead of transitioning to another graph, we directly go down the second phase again from
(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)
. In that case, we can show that the corresponding sub-trajectory balance condition can be written in simple form as

(8.4.2)

R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P_{\phi}(\bar{\theta}\mid G)=R(G\mathchar 59\relax\bar{\theta})P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G).

Equivalently, we can say that the function
f_{G}(\theta)=\log R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)-\log P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)
is constant (
i.e.
, independent of
\theta
, but with a constant that depends on
G
). Since this function is clearly differentiable (wrt.
\theta
), another way to write this condition is
\nabla_{\theta}f_{G}(\theta)=0
. This gives us a
differential formulation
of the sub-trajectory balance condition
Equation

8.4.2
over undirected paths of length 2:

(8.4.3)

\nabla_{\theta}R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)=\nabla_{\theta}P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G).

Interestingly, this formulation is equivalent to the notion of
score matching

(
Hyvärinen 2005
)
to model unnormalized distributions, since
R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
here corresponds to the unnormalized (marginal) posterior distribution
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
, where the normalization is over
\theta
.

Another interesting point is that by
Lemma

B.2.1
, we show that
Equation

8.4.3
is effectively redundant when the sub-trajectory condition of
Theorem

8.3.1
is satisfied for all undirected paths of length 3. Although satisfying them exactly for
all
undirected paths of length 3 is unlikely in practice, they will eventually be satisfied approximately over the course of training. Another way to encourage this is to “embed” the condition
Equation

8.4.3
into our loss function. Going back to the residual
Equation

8.4.1
, using the notation
\widetilde{\Delta}(\phi)
for clarity, since
\theta
&
\theta^{\prime}
may depend on
\phi
via the reparametrization trick (we will see in
Section

8.4.1
that they are sampled on-policy), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{d\phi}\widetilde{\Delta}^{2}(\phi)=\widetilde{\Delta}(\phi)\frac{d}{d\phi}\Big[\log R(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\log P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)

(8.4.4)

\displaystyle-\log R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)-\log P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)-\log P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)\Big].

Isolating one term in particular, by the law of total derivatives

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\phi}\Big[\log P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)-\log R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\Big]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\log P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)-\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\log R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\right]}_{=\,0}\frac{d\theta}{d\phi}+\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\log P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)

(8.4.5)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\log P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

and similarly for the other term in
(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
. We integrated the differential formulation of the sub-trajectory balance condition
Equation

8.4.3
over undirected paths of length 2 directly into the computation of the gradient. This derivative of the objective then becomes

(8.4.6)

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{d\phi}\widetilde{\Delta}^{2}(\phi)=\widetilde{\Delta}(\phi)\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\Big[\log P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)-\log P_{\phi}(\theta^{\prime}\mid G^{\prime})-\log P_{\phi}(G^{\prime}\mid G)\Big].

An alternative way to obtain the same derivative in
Equation

8.4.5
is to take
d\theta/d\phi=0
instead, meaning that we would not differentiate through
\theta
(and
\theta^{\prime}
). Using the stop-gradient operator
\bot(\cdot)
(to not confuse with the terminal state!), this means that we can define the new sub-trajectory balance objective as
{\mathcal{L}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\big[\Delta^{2}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime};\phi)\big]
, where the residual now is

(8.4.7)

\Delta(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime};\phi)=\log\frac{R\big(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bot(}\theta^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0})}\big)P_{B}\big(G\mid G^{\prime}\big)P_{\phi}\big({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bot(}\theta{\color[rgb]{1,0,0})}\mid G\big)}{R\big(G\mathchar 59\relax{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bot(}\theta{\color[rgb]{1,0,0})}\big)P_{\phi}\big(G^{\prime}\mid G\big)P_{\phi}\big({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bot(}\theta^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0})}\mid G^{\prime}\big)}\mathchar 59\relax

whose gradient matches exactly
Equation

8.4.6
. While optimizing this objective alone leads to eventually satisfying the sub-trajectory balance conditions over undirected paths of length 2, it may be advantageous to explicitly encourage the behavior, especially in cases where
d
is larger and/or for non-linear models. We can incorporate some penalty to the loss function, such as

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(\phi)={\mathcal{L}}(\phi)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\Big[\|\nabla_{\theta}\log P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G)

\displaystyle-\nabla_{\theta}\log R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\|^{2}

(8.4.8)

\displaystyle+\|\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\log P_{\phi}(\theta^{\prime}\mid G^{\prime})-\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\log R(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})\|^{2}\Big].

Finally, the same considerations about the use of a target network that we highlighted in
Section

7.4.2
also apply here when we evaluate
P_{\phi}\big(\bot(\theta^{\prime})\mid G^{\prime}\big)
.

8.4.1.
Behavior policy

In DAG-GFlowNet, we used a combination of
\varepsilon
-sampling with a replay buffer in order to encourage exploration during training (
Section

4.2.2
). If we were to do the same thing here, we would be confronted with two problems. First, the notion of
\varepsilon
-sampling is not as well-defined for continuous quantities such as the parameters
\theta
as it is with discrete ones; this can be addressed by adding an extra variance term to
Equation

8.3.19

(
Malkin et al. 2023
)
. But the second issue, more practical this time, is that storing experience in the replay buffer here would mean storing tuples
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
, which could be extremely large since
\theta
represents all the parameters of the Bayesian network.

Instead, we will adopt a “lazy” strategy where the behavior policy
\pi_{b}
is a combination of off-policy and on-policy. Taking inspiration from DAG-GFlowNet
(
Deleu et al. 2022
)
, we will still store transitions
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
in the replay buffer, following
Section

4.2.2
, whereas their corresponding parameters
\theta
&
\theta^{\prime}
are sampled on-policy using our current
P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop})
. Therefore, a key difference with
Deleu et al. 2022
is that the reward
R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
is computed “lazily” when the loss is evaluated (
i.e.
, only once
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
are known), as opposed to being computed during the interaction with the state space and stored in the replay buffer alongside the transitions.
Algorithm

9
gives a description of the training algorithm of JSP-GFN.

Algorithm 9

JSP-GFN training

1:

Initialize the trajectory at
G_{0}
the empty graph

2:

loop

3:

\triangleright

Interactions with the environment

\triangleleft

4:

for

T
steps
do

5:

Sample whether the trajectory continues or not:
a\sim P_{\phi}(\mathrm{stop}\mid G_{t})

6:

if

a=1

then

7:

Reset the trajectory:
G_{t+1}=G_{0}
is the empty graph

8:

else

9:

Sample a new graph
G_{t+1}\sim P_{\phi}(G_{t+1}\mid G_{t}\mathchar 59\relax\neg\mathrm{stop})

\triangleright
see also
Algorithm

6

10:

Store the transition
G_{t}\rightarrow G_{t+1}
in the replay buffer

11:

A blank line

12:

\triangleright

Update of the parameters
\phi

\triangleleft

13:

Sample a mini-batch
{\mathcal{B}}
of transitions
G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
from the replay buffer

14:

Sample
\theta\sim P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop})
(and similarly for
\theta^{\prime}
) for each transition in
{\mathcal{B}}

15:

Evaluate the rewards
R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
and
R(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})

16:

Evaluate the loss
{\mathcal{L}}(\phi)
based on
{\mathcal{B}}

\triangleright

Equation

8.4.7

17:

\phi\leftarrow\phi-\beta\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}(\phi)

\triangleright
Update the parameters with gradient methods

18:

return
The forward transition probability
P_{\phi}(\cdot\mid G)

8.4.2.
Mini-batch training

So far when we talked about Bayesian structure learning in general, we always assumed that inference was done over the whole dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
at once. As the size of the dataset grows, computing the reward becomes more and more expensive since it is linear in the size of
{\mathcal{D}}
, regardless of our discussion in
Section

7.4.3
. Taking inspiration from how deep learning models are trained, it would be far more convenient if we could also train our GFlowNet not based on the whole dataset, but on mini-batches of data. This proves to impossible though for DAG-GFlowNet (and more generally to approximate the marginal posterior
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
). The reason being that if we have 2 datapoints
{\bm{x}}
&
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}
from
{\mathcal{D}}
, then they are
not
independent given
G
alone:
{\bm{x}}\not\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\mid G
; there exists an open path through
\theta
in the (higher-level) Bayesian network shown in
Figure

8.3(b)
describing the distribution
P({\mathcal{D}}\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G)
.

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 8.3
.
Conditional dependencies between the DAG
G
, the parameters
\theta
, and datapoints
{\bm{x}}
&
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}
. (a) The conditional dependencies represented as a (higher level) Bayesian network. (b) The effect of conditioning only on
G
; this leaves an open path between
{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}
through
\theta
, making them conditionally
dependent
. (c) The effect of conditioning on both
G
&
\theta
this time, closing all paths from
{\bm{x}}
to
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}
and thus making them conditionally
independent
. Conditional independencies are read via d-separation presented in
Definition

1.1.2
.

If we model the joint posterior
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
though, there is no such open path anymore, and we do have
{\bm{x}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta
. Therefore beyond the capacity to model arbitrary (
e.g.
, non-linear) likelihood models, another advantage of approximating the joint posterior is that we can train the GFlowNet using mini-batches of observations. Concretely, for a mini-batch
{\mathcal{B}}=\{{\bm{x}}^{(m)}\}_{m=1}^{M}
of
M
observations sampled uniformly at random from the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
, we can define

(8.4.9)

\log\widehat{R}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)=\log P(\theta\mid G)+\log P(G)+\frac{N}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\log P\big({\bm{x}}^{(m)}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\big)\mathchar 59\relax

which is an unbiased estimate of the log-reward. We can likewise define a (stochastic) loss function
\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)
by simply replacing the reward by this estimate in the residual
Equation

8.4.7
. The following proposition shows that minimizing this estimated loss wrt. the parameters
\phi
also minimizes the original loss function that uses the whole dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
.

Proposition 8.4.1
.

Suppose that
{\mathcal{B}}
is a mini-batch of observations sampled uniformly at random from the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
, and let
\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)
be the loss where the reward has been replaced by the estimate
\widehat{R}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
in
Equation

8.4.9
in the residual
Equation

8.4.7
. Then we have
{\mathcal{L}}(\phi)\leq\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\big[\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)\big]
.

Proof.

We will first show that
\log\widehat{R}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
is an unbiased estimate of the log-reward
\log R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
under a uniform distribution of the mini-batches
{\mathcal{B}}
. By conditional independence of the observations
{\bm{x}}^{(n)}
given
G
and
\theta
, we have

(8.4.10)

\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\log P\big({\bm{x}}^{(n)}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\big)=N\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}}\big[\log P({\bm{x}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big]\mathchar 59\relax

where the expectation is taken over the uniform distribution over
{\mathcal{D}}=\{{\bm{x}}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
. It is important to note that the independence of the observations is critical here to get this decomposition. Similarly, we have

(8.4.11)

\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Bigg[\sum_{{\bm{x}}^{(m)}\in{\mathcal{B}}}\log P\big({\bm{x}}^{(m)}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\big)\Bigg]=M\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{x}}}\big[\log P({\bm{x}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big].

Combining these two equations together, we can show that the estimate of the log-reward is indeed unbiased:

(8.4.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\big[\log\widehat{R}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{M}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Bigg[\sum_{{\bm{x}}^{(m)}\in{\mathcal{B}}}\log P\big({\bm{x}}^{(m)}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\big)\Bigg]+\log P(\theta\mid G)+\log P(G)

(8.4.13)

\displaystyle=\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)+\log P(\theta\mid G)+\log P(G)=\log R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta).

Recall that the stochastic loss is defined as
\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\big[\widehat{\Delta}^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime};\phi)\big]
, where the residual is

(8.4.14)

\widehat{\Delta}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime};\phi)=\log\frac{\widehat{R}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\big(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\bot(\theta^{\prime})\big)P_{B}\big(G\mid G^{\prime}\big)P_{\phi}\big(\bot(\theta)\mid G\big)}{\widehat{R}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(G\mathchar 59\relax\bot(\theta)\big)P_{\phi}\big(G^{\prime}\mid G\big)P_{\phi}\big(\bot(\theta^{\prime})\mid G^{\prime}\big)}.

Taking the expectation of the stochastic loss wrt. a random batch
{\mathcal{B}}
, we get

(8.4.15)

\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\big[\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)\big]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\big[\widehat{\Delta}^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)\big]\Big]\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\big[\widehat{\Delta}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)\big]^{2}\Big]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}\big[\Delta^{2}(\phi)\big]={\mathcal{L}}(\phi)\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the convexity of the squared function and Jensen’s inequality, and the unbiasedness of
\log\widehat{R}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
(as well as
\log\widehat{R}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
).
∎

Note that we only used the convexity of the squared loss in the proof above to conclude, not its exact form. This is interesting because as we mentioned in
Section

7.3.1
, we use the Huber loss in practice for stability, which is also convex. In the case of the squared loss, we can actually show a stronger result in terms of unbiasedness of the gradient estimator.

Proposition 8.4.2
.

The mini-batch gradient estimator is unbiased:
\nabla_{\phi}{\mathcal{L}}(\phi)=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\widehat{{\mathcal{L}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)\big]
. Therefore, the local and global minima of the expected mini-batch loss coincide with those of the full-batch loss.

Proof.

We can first observe that
\nabla_{\phi}\Delta(\phi)=\nabla_{\phi}\widehat{\Delta}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)
, since only the terms corresponding to the reward differ between
\Delta(\phi)
&
\widehat{\Delta}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)
, and they do not depend on
\phi
(in particular thanks to the stop-gradient operator). Therefore

\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\big[\nabla_{\phi}\widehat{\Delta}_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{2}(\phi)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\big[\widehat{\Delta}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)\nabla_{\phi}\widehat{\Delta}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\big[\widehat{\Delta}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\phi)\big]\nabla_{\phi}\Delta(\phi)=\Delta(\phi)\nabla_{\phi}\Delta(\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\phi}\Delta^{2}(\phi).

Taking the expectation over the behavior policy
\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{b}}[\cdot]
of both sides of the equation above concludes the proof.
∎

This proposition implies that the expected mini-batch loss and the full-batch loss only differ by a constant factor
(
Deleu et al. 2023
)
. Note that mini-batch training alone does not address the limitations mentioned in
Section

7.4.3
for Bayesian inference when the dataset is large, since the estimate of the log-reward
Equation

8.4.9
still scales with the total number of observations
N
.

8.5.
Experimental results

To validate the effectiveness of both VBG and JSP-GFN to model the joint posterior over DAGs and the parameters of the conditional probability distributions, we will revisit the experiments we performed in
Chapter

7
. We will first test these methods on simulated data, both on small graphs to allow comparison with
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
directly and on medium scale, and then we will study two real-world biological applications.

For comparison with existing algorithms, we use a similar set of baseline methods as the one we already used throughout
Chapter

7
: 2 methods based on MCMC
(
Giudici & Castelo 2003
)
, 2 based on bootstrapping
(
Friedman et al. 1999
)
, and 2 based on variational inference. The ones based on variational inference, DiBS
(
Lorch et al. 2021
)
& BCD Nets
(
Cundy et al. 2021
)
, are already approximations of the joint posterior as we saw in
Section

8.1.2
, although BCD Nets is only applicable on linear-Gaussian models. However the other 4 methods are by default approximating the
marginal
posterior
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
, and they need to be adapted for joint inference. In the case of bootstrapping, still using either GES
(
Chickering 2002
)
or the PC algorithm
(
Spirtes et al. 2000
)
, we approximate
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
by a Dirac distribution placed at the maximum likelihood estimate
\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{MLE}}
for each sampled graphs
(
Agrawal et al. 2019
)
. For MCMC, we enhance structure MCMC (MC
3
) described in
Section

7.1.1
with the ability to also sample parameters
\theta
using two options
(
Lorch et al. 2021
)
: either (1) the proposal distribution sample jointly
(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
for the Metropolis-Hastings step (
MH-MC
3
), or (2) new parameters
\theta^{\prime}
are only proposed once we have already proposed to move to a new
G^{\prime}
with structure MCMC (
Gibbs-MC
3
).

8.5.1.
Joint posterior over small graphs

Following
Section

7.5.2
, we first evaluate the quality of the posterior approximation for both VBG (
Section

8.2
) and JSP-GFN (
Section

8.3
) on small simulated problems, in order to allow direct comparison with the exact posterior
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
. Computing this distribution can only be done exactly in limited cases, where (1)
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
can be computed analytically, and (2) for a small enough number of variables
d
such that all the DAGs can be enumerated to compute
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
. Therefore, we use the same experimental setup described in
Section

7.5
with graphs over
d=5
nodes, keeping a linear-Gaussian assumption to compute the posterior over parameters analytically. We once again generated
20
different datasets of
N=100
observations from randomly generated linear-Gaussian Bayesian networks
(G^{\star}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\star})
. The likelihood function
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
is described in
Equation

1.1.3
, and we place a unit Normal distribution as the prior over parameters
P(\theta_{ij}\mid G)={\mathcal{N}}(0\mathchar 59\relax 1)
; this differs from the BGe score we used in
Section

7.5
in that
\sigma^{2}
is treated as a hyperparameter here, instead of being a parameter of the Bayesian network, and therefore the resulting log-reward is not
score-equivalent
anymore (
i.e.
, placing the same reward for Markov equivalent DAGs;
Koller & Friedman 2009
). We use a uniform prior over graphs
P(G)
.

(X_{i}\mathchar 59\relax X_{j})

nodes, either based on the exact posterior
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})

7.5.1
). The Pearson correlation coefficient
r
is included at the bottom-right corner of each plot.

The quality of the joint posterior approximation is evaluated separately for
G
and
\theta
. For the DAGs, we compare the approximation and the exact posterior on different marginals of interest, also called
features
(
Friedman & Koller 2003
; see also
Section

7.5.2
). We show a comprehensive evaluation of JSP-GFN on the 3 categories of features in
Figure

8.4
, with two versions that only differ in the way
P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop})
is parametrized: both use a Normal distribution, but “
JSP-GFN (diag)
” uses a diagonal covariance matrix
\Sigma_{\phi}
in
Equation

8.3.19
, whereas “
JSP-GFN (full)
” uses a full covariance matrix. We consider both parametrizations since it is known that in the case of a linear-Gaussian model the posterior
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
, which is approximated by
P_{\phi}(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{stop})
, is a Normal distribution with a full covariance matrix in general
(
Murphy 2023
;
Nishikawa-Toomey et al. 2023
)
, “JSP-GFN (full)” hence serving as a possibly more faithful approximation. Similar to DAG-GFlowNet in
Figure

7.5
, we observe that both versions of JSP-GFN are capable of accurately approximating the marginal posterior
P(G\mid{\mathcal{D}})
based at least on these structural features.

But to get a complete view of how accurate this approximation is, we also evaluate the performance of the posterior approximation over
\theta
. This time we not only compare JSP-GFN, but also VBG and all other baseline methods described in the previous section. In addition to quantitative results of edge features (purely structural quantities as we saw above),
Table

8.2
also shows the cross-entropy between the posterior approximations (over which the expectation is taken) and the exact posterior
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
. This measures how likely sample parameters from the approximations are under the exact posterior (lower is better). We observe that out of all methods, JSP-GFN is capable of sampling parameters
\theta
that are significantly more probable under
P(\theta\mid G\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{D}})
. On the other hand, VBG fares relatively well, being on par with bootstrapping methods (only surpassed by BCD Nets).

8.5.2.
Gaussian Bayesian networks from simulated data

To evaluate whether our observations hold on medium-size graphs, we also compare the performance of VBG & JSP-GFN on data simulated from larger Gaussian Bayesian networks with
d=20
variables, following the experimental setup described in
Section

7.6
. In addition to linear Gaussian models though, this time we also experimented with models whose conditional distributions are non-linear functions parametrized by neural networks. Following
Lorch et al. 2021
, we parametrized the conditional probability distributions of each variable with a 2-layer MLP, for a total of
|\theta|=2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}220
parameters. For both experimental settings, we generated
20
datasets of
N=100
observations from randomly generated Bayesian networks
(G^{\star}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\star})
. We use the same priors over parameters
P(\theta\mid G)
and over graphs
P(G)
as the ones described in the previous section.

(b)

{\mathcal{D}}
. (c) Linear correlation between the log-reward (x-axis) and the terminating state log-probability (y-axis, estimated with

) for

In light of the discussion of
Section

7.6.3
, we opted for evaluating all methods based on the negative log-likelihood on held-out data
(
Eaton & Murphy 2007
)
(
i.e.
,
N^{\prime}=100
new observations from the same
(G^{\star}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\star})
), as opposed to structural measures with the ground-truth graphs
G^{\star}
such as the
\mathbb{E}
-SHD or the AUROC. This provides a more representative perspective of the eventual performance on downstream tasks. In
Figure

8.5(a)
we show the negative log-likelihood for linear-Gaussian models, and observe that JSP-GFN achieves the best performance out of all other methods, with VBG being a close contender. In
Figure

8.5(b)
we show the negative log-likelihood, this time on non-linear models, and therefore ruling out the methods based on bootstrapping and BCD Nets, as well as VBG since it was primarily introduced for linear-Gaussian models
(
Nishikawa-Toomey et al. 2023
)
, despite our discussion in
Section

8.2.3
. We can observe that JSP-GFN achieves this time a performance similar to DiBS and competitive with MH-MC
3
.

To complement these metrics, and in line with
Figure

7.7(c)
for DAG-GFlowNet, we also assess the quality of the posterior approximation for JSP-GFN by comparing the terminating state log-probability and the log-reward of sampled
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
. Recall that ideally, the terminating state probability distribution approximates the joint posterior, meaning that

(8.5.1)

\log P_{\phi}^{\top}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\approx\log P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})=\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mathchar 59\relax G)-\log P({\mathcal{D}})\mathchar 59\relax

where
\log P_{\phi}^{\top}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
is once again estimated using the technique described in
Section

7.6.1
, and
P({\mathcal{D}})
is a constant offset. In
Figure

8.5(c)
, we can see that there is indeed a strong linear correlation across multiple samples
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
from JSP-GFN, with a slope
\Delta
close to
1
, suggesting again that it accurately approximates the joint posterior distribution at least around the modes it captures.

8.5.3.
Learning biological structures from real data

We finally evaluate JSP-GFN on real-world biological data for two separate tasks: the discovery of protein signaling networks from flow cytometry data
(
Sachs et al. 2005
)
following
Section

7.7
, and the discovery of small gene regulatory networks from gene expression data.

Protein signaling network

We use the data described in
Section

7.7.2
, consisting of a discretized version of flow cytometry data with both observational and interventional data
(
Sachs et al. 2005
;
Eaton & Murphy 2007
)
. In preparation for the evaluation based on negative log-likelihood, we split the data into a dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
of
N=4\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}200
measurements of the activity of
d=11
phosphoproteins from
7
experimental settings (including control), leaving out
N^{\prime}=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}200
measurements from
2
separate experimental settings; in other words, we will evaluate the performance of JSP-GFN on completely unseen interventions (via the
interventional
negative log-likelihood). To account for the complexity of biological systems, we model it as a non-linear discrete Bayesian network, where the conditional probability distributions are parametrized using a 2-layer MLP with
16
hidden units,
i.e.
,
P\big(X_{i}\mid\mathrm{Pa}_{G}(X_{i});\theta_{i}\big)=\mathrm{Categorical}(\pi_{i})
, with

(8.5.2)

\pi_{i}=\mathrm{MLP}({\bm{M}}_{i}{\bm{X}};\theta_{i})\mathchar 59\relax

where
{\bm{X}}
encodes the inputs as one-hot vectors,
{\bm{M}}_{i}
is an appropriate mask depending on the structure of
G
, and the MLP has a softmax activation function for the output layer. In total, the model has
|\theta|=6\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}545
parameters. The priors over parameters
P(\theta\mid G)
and over graphs
P(G)
are the same as the ones described in
Section

8.5.1
. The purpose of this experiment is to show that JSP-GFN can work with a flexible model on discrete data.

(a)

Sub-sampled dataset (
N=100
)

(b)

Full dataset (
N=4\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}200
)

(

)
. (a) Linear correlation between the log-reward (x-axis) and terminating state log-probability (y-axis) on a model trained with a subsampled dataset. Each of the

of the linear fit obtained with RANSAC
(

)
is shown at the bottom-right corner.

To measure the quality of the posterior approximation, we again compare in
Figure

8.6
the terminating state log-probability with the log-reward, similar to the previous section. We observe there is a linear correlation between these two quantities. However unlike
Figure

8.5(c)
, the slope
\Delta=0.7817
in
Figure

8.6(b)
is not as close to
1
, suggesting that JSP-GFN under-estimates the probability of
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
. We also observe that that the samples are “clustered” together, which can be explained by the fact that the posterior approximation is concentrated at only a few graphs, since the size of the dataset
{\mathcal{D}}
is larger. To confirm this observation, we show in
Figure

8.6(a)
a similar plot but this time on a dataset of
N=100
datapoints randomly sampled from
{\mathcal{D}}
, matching the experimental setup we considered in prior sections. We observe a much closer linear fit this time, with a slope
\Delta
close to
1
. We also report in
Table

8.3
the performance of JSP-GFN as the negative log-likelihood on held-out interventional data (with new unseen interventions).
