Gene regulatory networks

Taking inspiration from prior works using Bayesian networks to model gene regulatory networks
(
Friedman et al. 2000
;
Friedman 2004
;
Pe’er et al. 2006
;
Kaderali & Radde 2008
;
Lopez et al. 2022
)
, we also tested JSP-GFN on a similar inference task. Following
Sethuraman et al. 2023
, we used a subset of
N=2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}628
datapoints corresponding to expression data from
d=61
genes
(
Dixit et al. 2016
)
under various experimental settings (both experimental & control). The data is pre-processed following
Lopez et al. 2022
;
Sethuraman et al. 2023
. Gene expression data is composed of either non-zero continuous data (when a gene is expressed) or
exactly
zero values (when the gene is inhibited). Therefore, we model this system as a non-linear Bayesian network, where the conditional probability distributions are
zero-inflated Normal
distributions

(8.5.3)

P\big(X_{i}\mid\mathrm{Pa}_{G}(X_{i});\theta_{i}\big)=\alpha_{i}\delta_{0}(X_{i})+(1-\alpha_{i}){\mathcal{N}}(X_{i};\mu_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\sigma_{i}^{2})\mathchar 59\relax

where
\mu_{i}
is the result of a 2-layer MLP with
16
hidden units similar to the one described above for protein signaling networks. The parameters of the conditional probability distributions contain the parameters of the MLPs, as well as the mixture parameters
\alpha_{i}
and the variances of the observation noise
\sigma_{i}^{2}
, for a total of
|\theta|=61\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}671
parameters.

The purpose of this experiment is again to show the complete flexibility of the modeling for the Bayesian network, with non-standard distributions, but also to test JSP-GFN on a larger system of
d=61
variables. In
Table

8.3
we report the negative log-likelihood of held-out data, with unseen interventions. We observe that JSP-GFN achieves a lower interventional negative log-likelihood than both methods based on MCMC.

Biological plausibility of the acyclicity assumption

One of the strengths of JSP-GFN, and DAG-GFlowNet before it, is the capacity to obtain a distribution over the DAG structure of a Bayesian network (and its parameters). The acyclicity assumption is necessary to properly define the likelihood model in
Equation

1.1.2
. Although the flow cytometry dataset of
Sachs et al. 2005
is a well established dataset in the structure learning literature, and there is a growing interest in using those techniques for inferring gene regulatory networks, it is important to note that biological systems often exhibit some feedback processes that cannot be captured by acyclic graphs
(
Mooij et al. 2020
)
. Therefore, the graphs found by JSP-GFN (or any structure learning algorithm) must be carefully interpreted. As a general framework though, GFlowNets can be adapted to ignore the acyclic nature of the graphs sampled by ignoring parts of the mask
{\bm{M}}
defined in
Section

7.2.2
. Alternatively, we can view the generation of a cyclic graph by unrolling it
(
Murphy 2002
)
, and use a GFlowNet similar to the one defined by
Atanackovic et al. 2023
but with a full Bayesian treatment.

8.5.4.
Scaling to larger graphs

Throughout this chapter, and more generally the second part of this thesis, we carefully evaluated the performance of DAG-GFlowNet, VBG and JSP-GFN on a range of experimental settings, from small-scale problems where we could compare against the exact posterior, to medium and larger scale problems, up to Bayesian networks over
d=61
variables in the previous section. This is the order of magnitude the community of (Bayesian) structure learning has been mostly focused on. A question one might ask is: what does it take to scale these models to larger problems? For example, gene regulatory networks at the human genome level would require systems over about
20\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}000
variables, far beyond the scale we considered in this thesis.

The theoretical framework we devised in
Chapters

7
and
8
does not limit the scale at which they can be applied a priori. But of course the challenge now becomes practical, as larger networks will inevitably require more computational resources and larger neural networks to model the forward transition probability
P_{\phi}
. The advantage of GFlowNets in general though is that they acquire their own data via interactions with the pointed DAG
{\mathcal{G}}
(just like online reinforcement learning), meaning that they are in principle not bound by the amount of “training data” they have access to. However the quality of this data is of utmost importance, and this requires an extensive
exploration
of the state space to possibly capture multiple modes of the posterior distribution. This is largely an open problem, not only for GFlowNets but also in the reinforcement learning literature.

Finally another challenge of scaling to larger graphs is the problem of
evaluation
: how can we make sure that even if we can train JSP-GFN on larger graphs, the posterior approximation we find is faithful? Considering the limitations of structural metrics mentioned in
Section

7.6.3
, throughout this chapter we saw three evaluation methods: (1) the comparison with the exact posterior (
Figure

8.4
), (2) the comparison between
\log P_{\phi}^{\top}(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
and
\log R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
(
e.g.
,
Figure

8.5(c)
), and (3) the evaluation of the (interventional) negative log-likelihood on held-out data (
e.g.
,
Figure

8.5(a)
). The first evaluation is clearly limited to small graphs and specific conditional distributions in order to compute
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
analytically. The second one requires the estimation of the terminating state log-probability as described in
Section

7.6.1
, which is reliable only up to a certain scale (the limiting factor here being the number of edges
K
more than the number of nodes
d
, since we are estimating a sum with
K!
terms). The third one is the most universally applicable, but it only serves as a comparative measure with other methods; it is not a measure of the intrinsic quality of JSP-GFN
by itself
as an approximation of
P(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta\mid{\mathcal{D}})
. But even as a comparison, does a good predictive power (even on new interventions) necessarily reflect something about the posterior distribution over the
structure
of the Bayesian network? This question of robust evaluation in Bayesian structure learning is again an open problem.

Conclusion

In this thesis, we presented a comprehensive view of the theory of
generative flow networks
, with a particular accent on their applications to
Bayesian structure learning
. GFlowNets offer a new set of tools to model distributions over discrete and compositional objects, which has been a relatively under-represented area of (modern) generative modeling. We saw that they originated from addressing the shortcomings of using maximum entropy reinforcement learning to sample from these distributions. We also showed throughout the first part how they eventually unify many domains of machine learning and statistics, such as variational inference (
Section

4.3
), reinforcement learning (
Chapter

5
), and Markov chains (
Section

6.2
). But despite their apparent generality, it should be emphasized that the ambition of GFlowNets is
not
to supersede any other class of generative models, but to complement them and play to their (GFlowNets’) strengths. This is precisely why modeling the posterior distribution over the full characterization of a Bayesian network, in particular with its directed acyclic graph structure, arises as a natural fit for GFlowNets.

However, modeling a distribution over models is not an end in itself. Going back to the work of George E. P. Box, he argued that scientific investigation is an iterative process interlacing
deduction
(
i.e.
, using hypotheses to make predictions) and
induction
(
i.e.
, inferring hypotheses from new observations)
(
Box 1976
)
. Taking a Bayesian perspective for model selection fits this vision by design, since the posterior can be used for predictions and subsequently be updated with new actively acquired evidence to refine our model of the real-world
(
Buntine 1991a
)
; this update may be over the whole system
(
Tong & Koller 2001
;
Murphy 2001
;
Scherrer et al. 2021
;
Tigas et al. 2022
;
Toth et al. 2022
)
, or only part of it
(
Agrawal et al. 2019
)
. This interplay between scientific discovery and active learning has been a major driving force in the GFlowNet literature
(
Jain et al. 2022
;
Jain et al. 2023
;
Jain et al. 2023a
;
Hernandez-Garcia et al. 2023
)
.

More generally, GFlowNets have come a long way since their origins as a model to generate diverse small molecules
(
Bengio et al. 2021
)
, and our work laying their theoretical foundations as a generative model
(
Bengio et al. 2023
)
. They have found applications in a wide spectrum of domains, including combinatorial optimization
(
Zhang et al. 2023
;
Zhang et al. 2023a
;
Kim et al. 2024
)
, symbolic regression and neural architecture search
(
Li et al. 2023
;
Chen & Mauch 2024
)
, reasoning in large language models
(
Hu et al. 2024
;
Song et al. 2024
)
, AI safety
(
Lee et al. 2024
)
, probabilistic inference
(
Falet et al. 2024
)
, astrophysics
(
Bunao et al. 2024
)
, genetics
(
Zhou et al. 2024
)
, materials science
(
Mila et al. 2023
;
Nguyen et al. 2023
;
Cipcigan et al. 2024
)
, and computational chemistry for drug design
(
Volokhova et al. 2024
;
Cretu et al. 2024
)
.

The future is bright for GFlowNets, but some outstanding challenges still remain. Chief among them is the problem of efficient
exploration
of the state space. We saw in
Section

4.2.2
that GFlowNets are inherently off-policy methods, allowing for more flexibility in the way data is acquired, and providing clear benefits empirically (
Section

7.5.3
). But with massive state spaces, such as the ones we experience in structure learning and scientific discovery in general
(
Fink et al. 2005
)
, naive strategies such as
\varepsilon
-sampling will most likely not be sufficient to effectively explore all the modes of the target distribution. Some methods borrowed from the reinforcement learning literature have been proposed to address this difficult question
(
Pan et al. 2023a
;
Rector-Brooks et al. 2023
;
Morozov et al. 2024
)
, but further inspiration from reinforcement learning (where the question of exploration is predominant) is certainly necessary.

One characteristic of GFlowNets, once again inherited from their close ties with reinforcement learning, is that they require
simulation
through the state space
{\mathcal{G}}
: data is acquired by rolling out a behavior policy
\pi_{b}
(possibly storing the experience in a replay buffer) always starting from the initial state; there are notable exceptions though
(
Zhang et al. 2022a
;
Kim et al. 2024b
)
. This is in stark contrast with other modern generative models such as diffusion models
(
Song & Ermon 2019
)
or flow-matching models
(
Lipman et al. 2023
;
Tong et al. 2024
)
which tend to go towards
simulation-free
objectives where a noisy version of the data is obtained directly without going through an iterative process; this is arguably what makes them so successful. While the situation remains quite different for GFlowNets (we never have to backpropagate through the simulation), we could leverage the fact that unlike most RL environments
{\mathcal{G}}
is known (at least locally). This means that we could get training data starting from
any state
without having to systematically go back to
s_{0}
, which could again help for exploration
(
Ecoffet et al. 2021
)
.

The synergies between GFlowNets and those other modern generative models should also be further exploited. These have already been the subject of some works using GFlowNets-like objectives to train diffusion models for example
(
Zhang et al. 2024
;
Zhang et al. 2024a
;
Sendera et al. 2024
;
Venkatraman et al. 2024
)
. An exciting direction of research is to use a GFlowNet not as a way to
tune
these models, but rather to
guide
an a priori completely unstructured generative model like a diffusion model (
e.g.
, for de novo molecule generation) with the highly structured and often discrete GFlowNet on top of it (
e.g.
, to enforce synthesizability at low cost). The use of these powerful generative models can also have major benefits in conjunction with a GFlowNet like JSP-GFN (
Chapter

8
) to capture the multi-modeality of the target posterior distribution.

Finally, specifically for the application of GFlowNets to Bayesian structure learning, the next frontier is to learn from low-level observations (
e.g.
, images), as opposed to directly observing the values of the random variables. In this setting, the Bayesian network would be defined over
latent variables
, the values of which now would have to be inferred (based on low-level observations) along with the structure and the parameters of the model, the latter two being covered in
Chapter

8
. This is largely an unexplored area, apart from the seminal work of
Friedman 1998
in (non-Bayesian) structure learning with latent variables
(
Murphy 2002
)
. Although some of our preliminary findings extending DAG-GFlowNet to latent variable models can be found in
(
Manta 2024
)
, substantial additional work is necessary to make it broadly applicable.

Index

Appendices

Appendix A
Generative Flow Networks

A.1.
Generative flow networks

A.1.1.
Alternative conditions

In this section, we will prove an alternative formulation of the flow matching condition stated in
Proposition

6.1.1
. We recall this proposition here and prove it; the proof follows the same pattern as the one of
Theorem

4.1.1
.

Proposition 6.1.1

(Flow matching condition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{A}})
be a pointed DAG. A function
F:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
defines the state flow, and
P_{F}:{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathcal{G}}})
the forward transition probabilities of a unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
if and only if they satisfy the following condition for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
:

(A.1.1)

F(s^{\prime})=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s).

Moreover, under these conditions, the unique Markovian flow
F^{\star}
whose state flow and forward transition probabilities match
F
and
P_{F}
respectively is defined, for any complete trajectory
\tau=(s_{0}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T}\mathchar 59\relax\bot)
, by

(A.1.2)

F^{\star}(\tau)=F(s_{0})\prod_{t=0}^{T}P_{F}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t})\mathchar 59\relax

with the convention
s_{T+1}=\bot
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
: If
F^{\star}
is a Markovian flow, then by
Proposition

3.1.2
its edge and state flows satisfy the following identity for all
s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
:

(A.1.3)

F^{\star}(s^{\prime})=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime}).

Moreover, by definition of the forward transition probability induced by a flow (
Definition

3.1.7
), we know that
F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F^{\star}(s)P_{F}^{\star}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
for any
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
. Therefore, this proves the necessity of the condition for the state flow and the forward transition probabilities of a Markovian flow.

\Leftarrow
: Let
F
and
P_{F}
be functions satisfying the condition in
Equation

6.1.1
for all state
s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
, and let
F^{\star}
be the trajectory flow defined in
Equation

6.1.2
. Since
F(s_{0})
is constant and
P_{F}
is a forward transition probability consistent with
{\mathcal{G}}
, the flow
F^{\star}
is Markovian by
Proposition

3.1.10
. Moreover, this also shows that
P_{F}
matches the forward transition probabilities of
F^{\star}
(again, by
Proposition

3.1.10
). To show the sufficiency of the condition in
Equation

6.1.1
, we then need to prove that (1)
F
matches the state flow of
F^{\star}
, and that (2) the Markovian flow
F^{\star}
is unique.

(1)

We can prove that the function
F
matches the state flow of
F^{\star}
by strong induction. For any state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, let
d_{s}
denote the maximum length of a partial trajectory from the initial state
s_{0}
to
s
.

Base case:
The initial state is the unique state such that
d_{s_{0}}=0
. By
Proposition

3.1.10
and the form of the Markovian flow in
Equation

6.1.2
, we know that the total flow of
F^{\star}
is given by
Z^{\star}=F(s_{0})
. Furthermore, by
Proposition

3.1.4
, we know that the total flow is equal to the state flow at the initial state:
F^{\star}=F^{\star}(s_{0})
. Therefore, this shows that
F^{\star}(s_{0})=F(s_{0})
.

Induction step:
Suppose that
F^{\star}(s)=F(s)
for all states
s
such that
d_{s}\leq d
, for some
d>0
. Since
{\mathcal{G}}
is a pointed DAG, there exists a state
s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
such that
d_{s^{\prime}}=d+1
; it is clear that
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
. By
Proposition

3.1.2
&
Definition

3.1.7
, we then have

(A.1.4)

\displaystyle F^{\star}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F^{\star}(s)P_{F}^{\star}(s^{\prime}\mid s)

(A.1.5)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{Pa}_{{\mathcal{G}}}(s^{\prime})}F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=F(s^{\prime})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we used the fact that
P_{F}^{\star}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
and the induction assumption in
Equation

A.1.5
, as well as the condition in
Equation

6.1.1
. This shows that the function
F
matches the state flow of
F^{\star}
.

(2)

The proof of unicity of the Markovian flow is identical to the proof of
Theorem

3.2.2
. Suppose that there exists another Markovian flow
F^{\prime\star}
such that
F
matches its state flow and
P_{F}
matches its forward transition probabilities. We denote by
P_{F}^{\prime\star}
the forward transition probabilities associated with
F^{\prime\star}
. By definition, we then have for any transition
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
(where
s^{\prime}
may be equal to the terminal state
\bot
)

(A.1.6)

F^{\prime\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})=F^{\prime\star}(s)P_{F}^{\prime\star}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=F^{\star}(s)P_{F}^{\star}(s^{\prime}\mid s)=F^{\star}(s\rightarrow s^{\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the fact that
F^{\star}
and
F^{\prime\star}
have both their state flows and forward transition probabilities equal to
F
and
P_{F}
respectively. Since this is valid for all transitions
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{G}}
, by definition of equivalent flows we have
F^{\prime\star}\sim F^{\star}
. By
Proposition

3.1.12
, this means that the two flows are equal, showing the unicity of
F^{\star}
.

∎

A.1.2.
Convergence guarantees

In this section, we will prove a general upper-bound on the log-expectation-exp based on the maximum element. This lemma is useful for proving multiple results for the convergence guarantees of GFlowNets (
e.g.
,
Propositions

4.2.3
and
4.2.4
).

Lemma A.1.1

(Bound on log-expectation-exp)
.

Let
{\bm{p}}=(p_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax p_{n})
be a vector of probabilities (i.e.,
p_{i}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i}p_{i}=1
), and
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax x_{n})
be an arbitrary vector. Then

(A.1.7)

\left|\log\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\exp(x_{i})\right|\leq\max_{i}|x_{i}|.

Proof.

For any vector
{\bm{y}}=(y_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax y_{n})
, and since
p_{i}\geq 0
, we have the following inequalities

(A.1.8)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\Big(\min_{j}y_{j}\Big)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}y_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\Big(\max_{j}y_{j}\Big).

Since
\sum_{i}p_{i}=1
, both sides of these inequalities can be further simplified, only involving the minimum and maximum of
{\bm{y}}
. We can apply these inequalities to
y_{i}=\exp(x_{i})
, and observe that
\min_{j}\exp(x_{j})=\exp(\min_{j}x_{j})
(and similarly for the
\max
), because the exponential is a monotonically increasing function

(A.1.9)

\exp\Big(\min_{j}x_{j}\Big)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\exp(x_{i})\leq\exp\Big(\max_{j}x_{j}\Big).

Taking the logarithm of the inequalities above, and again using the fact that
\log
is monotonically increasing

(A.1.10)

\min_{j}x_{j}\leq\log\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\exp(x_{i})\leq\max_{j}x_{j}.

Another way to write
Equation

A.1.10
is

(A.1.11)

\displaystyle\log\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\exp(x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i}x_{i}\leq\max_{i}|x_{i}|

(A.1.12)

\displaystyle-\log\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\exp(x_{i})

\displaystyle\leq-\min_{i}x_{i}\leq\max_{i}|x_{i}|\mathchar 59\relax

which concludes the proof.
∎

A.1.3.
GFlowNets & variational inference

In this section, we recall a well-known result called the
data processing inequality
, that relates the
f
-divergence between joint distributions to the
f
-divergence between their marginals. This result, taken from
(
Zhang et al. 2019
)
and recalled for completeness, plays an important role in proving
Proposition

4.3.1
, which establishes a bound on the
f
-divergence between the terminating state probability distribution of a GFlowNet and the target Gibbs distribution, in terms of the
f
-divergence between distributions over complete trajectories
(
Malkin et al. 2022
)
.

Proposition A.1.2

(Data processing inequality)
.

Let
f
be a convex function. Let
P(x\mathchar 59\relax z)
and
Q(x\mathchar 59\relax z)
be two distributions. The
f
-divergence of the marginals is bounded by the
f
-divergence of the joint distributions:

(A.1.13)

D_{f}\big(P(x)\,\|\,Q(x)\big)\leq D_{f}\big(P(x\mathchar 59\relax z)\,\|\,Q(x\mathchar 59\relax z)\big).

Proof.

The proof is taken from
(
Zhang et al. 2019
)
, and recalled here for completeness. Using the general decomposition
Q(x\mathchar 59\relax z)=Q(x)Q(z\mid x)
we have

(A.1.14)

\displaystyle D_{f}\big(P(x\mathchar 59\relax z)\,\|\,Q(x\mathchar 59\relax z)\big)

\displaystyle=\iint Q(x\mathchar 59\relax z)f\left(\frac{P(x\mathchar 59\relax z)}{Q(x\mathchar 59\relax z)}\right)dxdz

(A.1.15)

\displaystyle=\int Q(x)\int Q(z\mid x)f\left(\frac{P(x\mathchar 59\relax z)}{Q(x\mathchar 59\relax z)}\right)dzdx

(A.1.16)

\displaystyle\geq\int Q(x)f\left(\int q(z\mid x)\frac{P(x\mathchar 59\relax z)}{Q(z\mid x)Q(x)}dz\right)dx

(A.1.17)

\displaystyle=\int Q(x)f\left(\frac{P(x)}{Q(x)}\right)dx=D_{f}\big(P(x)\,\|\,Q(x)\big)\mathchar 59\relax

where we used Jensen’s inequality in
Equation

A.1.16
.
∎

A.2.
GFlowNets over General State Spaces

A.2.1.
Generative flow network as a Markov chain

The following proposition shows that the terminating state probability distribution defined purely over Markov chains in
Definition

6.1.3
(in the discrete case) is a properly defined distribution over
{\mathcal{S}}
. This result is analoguous as
Proposition

2.2.7
in the case of discrete GFlowNets.

Proposition A.2.1
.

The terminating state distribution
P_{F}^{\top}
is a well-defined probability distribution over
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

It is clear that for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
P_{F}^{\top}(x)\geq 0
. Moreover, using
Proposition

6.1.4
, we know that
P_{F}^{\top}(x)=\lambda(x)P_{F}(s_{0}\mid x)
where
\lambda
is the unique invariant measure of
P_{F}
such that
\lambda(s_{0})=1
(
Theorem

6.1.2
). Therefore

(A.2.1)

\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{S}}}P_{F}^{\top}(x)=\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{S}}}\lambda(x)P_{F}(s_{0}\mid x)=\lambda(s_{0})=1\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the invariance of
\lambda
in the second equality.
∎

A.2.2.
From invariance back to flow matching

The Markov chain perspective we devised in
Section

6.1.3
is elegant, but in practice the objective is to find a transition probability
P_{F}
and a measure
F
, which may be parametrized by neural networks, that satisfy the conditions of
Theorem

6.1.5
:
F
must satisfy the boundary conditions and must be invariant for
P_{F}
. For this, we can take inspiration from the loss functions introduced in
Section

4.2.1
. The summation in
Equation

6.1.7
, necessary for checking the invariance of
F
, is typically inexpensive for most states if the state space is well structured (
i.e.
, if the objects have a clear compositional nature, as described in
Section

2.1.3
), since we only have to sum over the “parents” of
s^{\prime}
(all the states with non-zero probability of transitioning to
s^{\prime}
).

However, checking the invariance of
F
at the initial state
s_{0}
proves to be as difficult as computing the partition function itself, since the parent set of
s_{0}
is the whole sample space
{\mathcal{X}}
. Fortunately, the following proposition shows that we only have to check that
F
satisfies
Equation

6.1.7
for any state
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
.

Proposition A.2.2
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
be an irreducible and positive recurrent Markov chain over
{\mathcal{S}}
, with initial state
s_{0}
and transition probability
P_{F}
. A measure
F
is invariant for
P_{F}
if and only if for all
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
, we have

(A.2.2)

F(s^{\prime})=\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}F(s)P_{F}(s^{\prime}\mid s).

Proof.

The necessity of this proposition is immediate. For sufficiency, we can first show (by induction) that if
Equation

A.2.2
is satisfied for all
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
, then

(A.2.3)

F(s)=F(s_{0})\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n<\sigma_{s_{0}})\mathds{1}(X_{n}=s)\big].

Since the Markov chain is irreducible and positive recurrent, then by
Theorem

6.1.2
it admits an invariant measure. The only non-trivial statement one must show is that if
Equation

A.2.2
is satisfied for any
s^{\prime}\neq s_{0}
, then it must also hold for
s^{\prime}=s_{0}
. Since the Markov chain is positive recurrent, it must eventually return to
s_{0}
in finite time. In other words

(A.2.4)

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(\sigma_{s_{0}}=n)\big]=1.

Furthermore, it is clear that at any point in time
n\geq 0
,
X_{n}
must be in one of the states of
{\mathcal{S}}
, meaning that
\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\mathds{1}(X_{n}=s)=1
. Therefore

(A.2.5)

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}F(s)P_{F}(s_{0}\mid s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}F(s_{0})\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(n<\sigma_{s_{0}})\mathds{1}(X_{n}=s)\big]P_{F}(s_{0}\mid s)

(A.2.6)

\displaystyle=F(s_{0})\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(\sigma_{s_{0}}=n)\mathds{1}(X_{n-1}=s)\big]

(A.2.7)

\displaystyle=F(s_{0})\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\Bigg[\mathds{1}(\sigma_{s_{0}}=n)\underbrace{\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\mathds{1}(X_{n-1}=s)}_{=\,1}\Bigg]

(A.2.8)

\displaystyle=F(s_{0})\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}}\big[\mathds{1}(\sigma_{s_{0}}=n)\big]=F(s_{0})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where we substituted
F(s)
by
Equation

A.2.3
in the first step
Equation

A.2.5
, we used
Equation

6.1.14
from the proof of
Proposition

6.1.4
in the second step
Equation

A.2.6
, and finally
Equation

A.2.4
to conclude in
Equation

A.2.8
. This concludes the proof, showing that invariance also holds at
s_{0}
.
∎

This results mirrors exactly the practical implementation of flow networks as a pointed DAG (
Proposition

6.1.1
), where the flow matching condition is never checked at the terminal state
\bot
since it is an abstract state outside of the state space
{\mathcal{S}}
.

A.2.3.
Operations on transitions kernels

We saw in
Definition

6.2.2
that a transition kernel
\kappa
was defined with a single measurable state space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma)
. This effectively means that we are transitioning from an element
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
to another element of
{\mathcal{S}}
with measure
\kappa(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)
. But we could easily extend this definition to different spaces. In this section, we will use the notations
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{S})
,
({\mathcal{T}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{T})
and
({\mathcal{U}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{U})
to denote three measurable spaces (some may be equal); note that in this context
{\mathcal{T}}
denotes a state space, and is not the set of complete trajectories in
Definition

2.2.3
. We first revisit the definition of a transition kernel in this general setting

Definition A.2.3

(Markov kernel)
.

Let
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{S})
&
({\mathcal{T}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{T})
be two measurable state spaces. A function
\kappa:{\mathcal{S}}\times\Sigma_{T}\rightarrow[0\mathchar 59\relax\infty)
is called a positive
\sigma
-finite
transition kernel
if

(1)

For any
B\in\Sigma_{T}
, the mapping
s\mapsto\kappa(s\mathchar 59\relax B)
is measurable;

(2)

For any
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, the mapping
B\mapsto\kappa(s\mathchar 59\relax B)
is a positive
\sigma
-finite measure on
({\mathcal{T}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{T})
.

Furthermore, if the mappings
\kappa(s\mathchar 59\relax\cdot)
are probability distributions (
i.e.
,
\kappa(s\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{T}})=1
), the transition kernel is called a
Markov kernel
.

With this broader definition of a transition kernel, we are now ready to define two important operations over kernels: the product and the composition. These operations are used throughout
Chapter

6
, and especially for the practical aspects in
Section

6.3
.

Product kernel

Before defining the product between two kernels, we first need to understand what it means to have a product of measurable spaces. We will say that the product space
{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{T}}
is associated with the
product
\sigma
-algebra

\Sigma_{S}\otimes\Sigma_{T}
, corresponding to the
\sigma
-algebra of product sets

(A.2.9)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{productsigmaalgebra}{{{}}\Sigma_{S}\otimes\Sigma_{T}}\triangleq\sigma\big(\{B_{S}\times B_{T}\mid B_{S}\in\Sigma_{S}\mathchar 59\relax B_{T}\in\Sigma_{T}\}\big).

Definition A.2.4

(Product kernel)
.

Let
\kappa_{1}
be a transition kernel from
{\mathcal{S}}
to
{\mathcal{T}}
(
i.e.
,
\kappa_{1}:{\mathcal{S}}\times\Sigma_{T}\rightarrow[0\mathchar 59\relax\infty)
), and
\kappa_{2}
a transition kernel from
{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{T}}
to
{\mathcal{U}}
(
i.e.
,
\kappa_{2}:({\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{T}})\times\Sigma_{U}\rightarrow[0\mathchar 59\relax\infty)
). We define the
product kernel

\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}
from
{\mathcal{S}}
to
{\mathcal{T}}\times{\mathcal{U}}
as

(A.2.10)

\big[\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}\big](s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\int_{{\mathcal{T}}}\kappa_{1}(s\mathchar 59\relax dt)\int_{{\mathcal{U}}}\kappa_{2}\big((s\mathchar 59\relax t)\mathchar 59\relax du\big)\mathds{1}_{B}(t\mathchar 59\relax u)\mathchar 59\relax

for
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
B\in\Sigma_{T}\otimes\Sigma_{U}
.

The definition of a product kernel was useful when we defined the Markov kernel of the split chain in
Section

6.2.1
. In particular, if both
\kappa_{1}
&
\kappa_{2}
are Markov kernel, then it is easy to show that the product
\kappa_{1}\otimes\kappa_{2}
is also a Markov kernel (over
{\mathcal{T}}\times{\mathcal{U}}
). This definition naturally extends to the case where
\kappa_{2}
is a transition kernel from
{\mathcal{T}}
to
{\mathcal{U}}
(since we could define
\bar{\kappa}_{2}((s\mathchar 59\relax t)\mathchar 59\relax B)=\kappa_{2}(t\mathchar 59\relax B)
for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
). This is especially interesting to define recursive products of the same Markov kernel. Let
P_{F}
be a Markov kernel over
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{S})
(into itself, the setting studied in
Chapter

6
). For
T>0
, we define the product kernel
P_{F}^{\otimes T}
from
{\mathcal{S}}
into
{\mathcal{S}}^{T}
recursively by

(A.2.11)

P_{F}^{\otimes T+1}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)\triangleq\big[P_{F}\otimes P_{F}^{\otimes T}\big](s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}^{T+1}}P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})P_{F}^{\otimes T}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax ds_{1:T})\mathds{1}_{B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T})\mathchar 59\relax

where
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
B\in\Sigma_{S}^{\otimes T}
(an immediate generalization of
Equation

A.2.9
), and where
P_{F}^{\otimes 0}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\delta_{s}(B)
is the Dirac measure at
s
. This product Markov kernel is used in the definition of the generalized trajectory balance condition in
Theorem

6.3.10
. Finally, we can also define the product of a measure
\nu
over
{\mathcal{S}}
with a transition kernel
\kappa
from
{\mathcal{S}}
to
{\mathcal{T}}
as

(A.2.12)

\big[\nu\otimes\kappa\big](B)=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\nu(ds)\int_{{\mathcal{T}}}\kappa(s\mathchar 59\relax dt)\mathds{1}_{B}(t)\mathchar 59\relax

where
B\in\Sigma_{T}
; this is a consequence of
Definition

A.2.4
, since a measure
\nu
can be seen as a (trivial) transition kernel that ignores where it transitions from (
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}(s\mathchar 59\relax B_{S})=\nu(B_{S})
, for
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
B_{S}\in\Sigma_{S}
). This is necessary in our definition of a measurable pointed graph in
Definition

6.3.1
to guarantee that the reference kernels are “inverse” of one another.

Composition kernel

In addition to the product kernel, we can also define another operation between two kernels called the
composition kernel
.

Definition A.2.5

(Composition kernel)
.

Let
\kappa_{1}
be a transition kernel from
{\mathcal{S}}
to
{\mathcal{T}}
(
i.e.
,
\kappa_{1}:{\mathcal{S}}\times\Sigma_{T}\rightarrow[0\mathchar 59\relax\infty)
), and
\kappa_{2}
a transition kernel from
{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{T}}
to
{\mathcal{U}}
(
i.e.
,
\kappa_{2}:({\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{T}})\times\Sigma_{U}\rightarrow[0\mathchar 59\relax\infty)
). We define the
composition kernel

\kappa_{1}\cdot\kappa_{2}
from
{\mathcal{S}}
to
{\mathcal{U}}
as

(A.2.13)

\big[\kappa_{1}\cdot\kappa_{2}\big](s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\int_{{\mathcal{T}}}\kappa_{1}(s\mathchar 59\relax dt)\int_{{\mathcal{U}}}\kappa_{2}\big((s\mathchar 59\relax t)\mathchar 59\relax du\big)\mathds{1}_{B}(u)\mathchar 59\relax

for
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
B\in\Sigma_{U}
.

Similar to the product kernel, it is easy to show that if both
\kappa_{1}
&
\kappa_{2}
are Markov kernels, then
\kappa_{1}\cdot\kappa_{2}
is also a Markov kernel. Aligned with our discussion for product kernels above, it is also easy to define the composition kernel if
\kappa_{2}
is a transition kernel from
{\mathcal{T}}
to
{\mathcal{U}}
. This allows us in particular to also have a recursive definition of a transition kernel (similar to
Equation

A.2.11
). Let
\kappa
be a transition kernel over
{\mathcal{S}}
(into itself). For
T>0
, we define the composition kernel
\kappa^{T}
from
{\mathcal{S}}
into itself recursively by

(A.2.14)

\kappa^{T+1}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)\triangleq\big[\kappa\cdot\kappa^{T}\big](s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\kappa(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\kappa^{T}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime\prime})\mathds{1}(s^{\prime\prime}\in B)\mathchar 59\relax

where
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
B\in\Sigma_{S}
, and where
\kappa^{0}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\delta_{s}(B)
is the Dirac measure at
s
. This definition is useful throughout
Section

6.3
, and in particular when we introduce the notion of
finitely absorbing
measurable pointed graph
Equation

6.3.2
, and to define the invariant measure of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{P}_{F}
in the generalized trajectory balance condition in
Theorem

6.3.10
.

Marginalization

The product kernel in
Equation

A.2.11
and the composition kernel
Equation

A.2.14
are related to one another by
marginalization
. We will use this marginalization property in the proof of the generalized trajectory balance condition
Theorem

6.3.10
.

Proposition A.2.6
.

Let
P_{F}
be a Markov kernel over a measurable space
({\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 59\relax\Sigma_{S})
(into itself), and let
T>0
. The marginalization of the product kernel
P_{F}^{\otimes T+1}
corresponds to the composition kernel: for
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
B\in\Sigma_{S}
,

(A.2.15)

P_{F}^{T}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}\mathds{1}_{B}(s^{\prime\prime})\int_{{\mathcal{S}}^{T-1}}P_{F}^{\otimes T}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime}ds^{\prime\prime})\mathchar 59\relax

where the outer integral is over
s^{\prime\prime}
and the inner one over
s^{\prime}
.

Proof.

For
B\in\Sigma_{S}
, we will consider the product set
{\mathcal{S}}^{T-1}\times B
, which is an element of
\Sigma_{S}^{\otimes T}
. Another way to write
Equation

A.2.15
is therefore as
P_{F}^{T}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=P_{F}^{\otimes T}(s\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{S}}^{T-1}\times B)
. We will prove this statement by induction.

Base case:
If
T=1
, then we clearly have
P_{F}^{\otimes 1}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=P_{F}^{1}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)
. This satisfies the statement above, since
{\mathcal{S}}^{0}=\emptyset
.

Induction step:
Suppose that
P_{F}^{T}(s\mathchar 59\relax B)=P_{F}^{\otimes T}(s\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{S}}^{T-1}\times B)
for some
T
. Then using the recursions
Equation

A.2.11
&
Equation

A.2.14
, we get

(A.2.16)

\displaystyle P_{F}^{\otimes T+1}(s\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{S}}^{T}\times B)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}^{T+1}}P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})P_{F}^{\otimes T}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax ds_{1:T})\mathds{1}_{{\mathcal{S}}^{T}\times B}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T})

(A.2.17)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})\int_{{\mathcal{S}}^{T}}P_{F}^{\otimes T}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax ds_{1:T})\mathds{1}_{{\mathcal{S}}^{T-1}\times B}(s_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax s_{T})

(A.2.18)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})P_{F}^{\otimes T}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathcal{S}}^{T-1}\times B)

(A.2.19)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})P_{F}^{T}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax B)

(A.2.20)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}P_{F}(s\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime})\int_{{\mathcal{S}}}P_{F}^{T}(s^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax ds^{\prime\prime})\mathds{1}_{B}(s^{\prime\prime})

(A.2.21)

\displaystyle=P_{F}^{T+1}(s\mathchar 59\relax B).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Bayesian Structure Learning

B.1.
Bayesian Structure Learning with Generative Flow Networks

B.1.1.
Bayesian scores

In
Chapter

7
, we considered two types of Bayesian networks: either (1) linear-Gaussian models, or (2) discrete Bayesian networks with a Dirichlet prior over parameters. These two settings give rise to two different ways to compute the marginal likelihood
P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)
, respectively the
BGe score
and the
BDe score
that we will define here. Both of these have the remarkable property of giving the same value for two Markov equivalent graphs
(
Koller & Friedman 2009
)
. Recall that in general, the marginal likelihood is given by

(B.1.1)

P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)=\int_{\theta}P({\mathcal{D}}\mid\theta\mathchar 59\relax G)P(\theta\mid G)d\theta\mathchar 59\relax

which is intractable, unless we put some careful assumptions on the likelihood model and the prior.

BGe score

The
Bayesian Gaussian equivalent score

(
Geiger & Heckerman 1994
;
Kuipers et al. 2014
)
can be used to compute the marginal likelihood of a linear-Gaussian model, as described in
Example

1.1.1
. We place a Normal prior over the linear parameters, and a Wishart prior over the variance parameters. If the data is
{\mathcal{D}}=\{{\bm{x}}^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{N}
, the (log-)marginal likelihood is given by

(B.1.2)

\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}C+\log\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{N+\alpha_{w}-d+l+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{\alpha_{w}-d+l+1}{2}\big)}+lt+\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{|{\bm{R}}_{\pi_{i}}|^{N+\alpha_{w}-d+l}}{|{\bm{R}}_{i}|^{N+\alpha_{w}-d+l+1}}\mathchar 59\relax

where
\Gamma
is the Gamma function,
C
is a constant term independent of
i
,
l
is the number of parents of
X_{i}
in
G
, and
{\bm{R}}
is the matrix defined by

(B.1.3)

{\bm{R}}=t{\bm{I}}+\sum_{n=1}^{N}({\bm{x}}^{(n)}-\bar{{\bm{x}}})^{\top}({\bm{x}}^{(n)}-\bar{{\bm{x}}})+\frac{N\alpha_{\mu}}{N+\alpha_{\mu}}\bar{{\bm{x}}}^{\top}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\mathchar 59\relax

where
\bar{{\bm{x}}}
is the average observation in
{\mathcal{D}}
, and
t=\alpha_{\mu}(\alpha_{w}-d-1)/(\alpha_{\mu}+1)
is a constant scalar. The matrix
{\bm{R}}_{\pi_{i}}
corresponds to the sub-matrix
{\bm{R}}
where only the rows and columns of
\mathrm{Pa}_{G}(X_{i})
are selected; similarly,
{\bm{R}}_{i}
is the sub-graph where the rows and columns of
\mathrm{Pa}_{G}(X_{i})
and
X_{i}
are selected. The strength of the prior is defined by the hyperparameters
\alpha_{\mu}>0
and
\alpha_{w}>d+1
.

BDe score

The
Bayesian Dirichlet equivalent score

(
Heckerman et al. 1995
)
can be used to compute the marginal likelihood of a Bayesian network over discrete random variables (with Categorical distributions), whose parameters organized as conditional probability
tables
have a Dirichlet prior. We will assume for simplicity that all the random variables
X_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax X_{d}
may take values in
\{1\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax K\}
. In that case, the (log-)marginal likelihood is given by

(B.1.4)

\log P({\mathcal{D}}\mid G)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{{\bm{u}}_{i}\in\Pi^{G}_{i}}\log\frac{\Gamma\big(N^{\prime}[{\bm{u}}_{i}]\big)}{\Gamma\big(N^{\prime}[{\bm{u}}_{i}]+N[{\bm{u}}_{i}]\big)}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\log\frac{\Gamma\big(N^{\prime}[k\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{u}}_{i}]+N[k\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{u}}_{i}]\big)}{\Gamma\big(N^{\prime}[k\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{u}}_{i}]\big)}\mathchar 59\relax

where
\Pi^{G}_{i}=\mathrm{Val}(\mathrm{Pa}_{G}(X_{i}))\simeq K^{|\mathrm{Pa}_{G}(X_{i})|}
is the set of all the possible values the parents of
X_{i}
in
G
may take, and
\Gamma
is the Gamma function.
N[k\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{u}}_{i}]
represents the number of observations in
{\mathcal{D}}
where
X_{i}=k
&
\mathrm{Pa}_{G}(X_{i})={\bm{u}}_{i}
, and
N[{\bm{u}}_{i}]=\sum_{k=1}^{K}N[k\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{u}}_{i}]
. The strength of the prior in the BDe score is controlled by a scalar hyperparameter
N^{\prime}
called the
equivalent sample size
, from which we define
N^{\prime}[k\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{u}}_{i}]=N^{\prime}\cdot P^{\prime}\big(X_{i}=k\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{Pa}_{G}(X_{i})={\bm{u}}_{i}\big)
where
P^{\prime}
is a joint distribution over
X_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots X_{d}
that can be defined as a Bayesian network, and is often chosen to be the uniform distribution
(
Buntine 1991
)
; similarly, we have
N^{\prime}[{\bm{u}}_{i}]=\sum_{k=1}^{K}N^{\prime}[k\mathchar 59\relax{\bm{u}}_{i}]
.

B.1.2.
Estimation of the terminating state probability

In
Section

7.6.1
, we saw a method to approximate the terminating state probability of a specific graph
G
using a combination of beam-search to extract high-scoring complete trajectories, and Monte-Carlo. We provide in
Algorithm

10
a pseudo-code of the beam-search procedure for completeness.

Algorithm 10

Beam-search algorithm for finding high-probability trajectories
G_{0}\rightsquigarrow G
.

1:

A DAG
G
with
K
edges, the forward transition probability
P_{F}
of the GFlowNet, the beam width
B
.

2:

B
complete trajectories
\{\tau_{m}\}_{m=1}^{B}
with high-probability
P_{F}(\tau_{m})
.

3:

Initialization of the trajectories:
G_{0}^{(m)}\leftarrow G_{0}

\triangleright

\forall m\in\{1\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax B\}

4:

Initial log-probabilities:
\ell_{0}^{(m)}\leftarrow 0

\triangleright

\forall m\in\{1\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax B\}

5:

for

k=0

to

K-1

do

6:

for

m=1

to

B

do

7:

for all
edge
e\in G

do

8:

s(m\mathchar 59\relax e)\leftarrow\ell_{k}^{(m)}+\log P_{F}(G^{(m)}_{k}\cup\{e\}\mid G^{(m)}_{k})

9:

10:

for

m=1

to

B

do

11:

Find
(b\mathchar 59\relax e)
such that
s(b\mathchar 59\relax e)
is the
m
-th largest score

12:

Save the partial trajectory
b
:
\widetilde{G}^{(m)}_{0:k}\leftarrow G^{(b)}_{0:k}

13:

Add the new graph to the trajectory
m
:
G^{(m)}_{k+1}\leftarrow G^{(b)}_{k}\cup\{e\}

14:

Update the log-probabilities:
\ell_{k+1}^{(m)}\leftarrow s(b\mathchar 59\relax e)

15:

Re-assign the partial trajectories:
G^{(m)}_{0:k}\leftarrow\widetilde{G}^{(m)}_{0:k}

\triangleright

\forall m\in\{1\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax B\}

16:

return
trajectories
\{\tau_{m}=G^{(m)}_{0:K}\}_{m=1}^{B}

B.2.
Joint Inference of Structure and Parameters

B.2.1.
Sub-trajectory balance condition

The following lemma shows that if the sub-trajectory balance condition in
Equation

8.3.8
is satisfied for all undirected paths of length 3 in JSP-GFN, then the different between the log-reward and the forward transition log-probability is independent of the parameters
\theta
. This is useful to show that satisfying these sub-trajectory balance condition alone is sufficient to have JSP-GFN sample proportionally to the reward (unlike the general case in
Section

3.2.4
).

Lemma B.2.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{G}}
be the GFlowNet described in
Section

8.3.1
, with a reward function
R
, and a forward transition probability
P_{F}
. For two DAGs
G
&
G^{\prime}
, if the sub-trajectory balance condition
Equation

8.3.8
is satisfied for all undirected paths of the form
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)\leftarrow(G\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\ast)\rightarrow(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
(for any
\theta
&
\theta^{\prime}
), then the following function

(B.2.1)

f_{G}(\theta)\triangleq\log R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)-\log P_{F}(\theta\mid G)

is constant (i.e., independent of
\theta
, but a constant depending on
G
).

Proof.

Let
\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{G^{\prime}}
be a fixed set of parameters associated with
G^{\prime}
, and
\theta
&
\bar{\theta}\in\Theta_{G}
two arbitrary sets of parameters associated with
G
. We can write the sub-trajectory balance condition for the undirected paths between
(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)
&
(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
and between
(G\mathchar 59\relax\bar{\theta})
&
(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})
:

(B.2.2)

\displaystyle R(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{F}(\theta\mid G)

\displaystyle=R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{F}(\theta^{\prime}\mid G^{\prime})

(B.2.3)

\displaystyle R(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})P_{F}(\bar{\theta}\mid G)

\displaystyle=R(G\mathchar 59\relax\bar{\theta})P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{F}(\theta^{\prime}\mid G^{\prime})

Using these two equations, we have

(B.2.4)

\frac{R(G\mathchar 59\relax\theta)}{P_{F}(\theta\mid G)}=\frac{P_{F}(G^{\prime}\mid G)P_{F}(\theta^{\prime}\mid G^{\prime})}{R(G^{\prime}\mathchar 59\relax\theta^{\prime})P_{B}(G\mid G^{\prime})}=\frac{R(G\mathchar 59\relax\bar{\theta})}{P_{F}(\bar{\theta}\mid G)}.

Taking the
\log
of both sides of this equation, we can conclude that
f_{G}(\theta)
in
Equation

B.2.1
is constant since this is true for any arbitrary
\theta
&
\bar{\theta}
.
∎
